### [1] [LLMs Know the Constraint But Do Not Use It: Activation Bottlenecks in Pragmatic Constraint Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12321)
*Yubo Li,Ramayya Krishnan,Rema Padman*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that large language models often fail when surface cues conflict with implicit constraints, and it disentangles whether this is due to missing knowledge or failures in using that knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations that test whether LLMs obey feasibility constraints (like physical or logical limitations) are confounded: high accuracy might reflect either real understanding of the constraint or a conservative tendency to default to safe answers. The authors want a cleaner way to distinguish whether models actually encode these constraints and whether failures are due to lack of knowledge or failure to route that knowledge into decisions.

Method: They propose a formal notion of "conditional constraint activation" with three properties—Symmetry (constraints are equally encoded for prompts with or without the constraint), Routing (the encoded constraint is only sometimes used in decisions), and Repair (failures can sometimes be fixed by injecting or "donating" the right activation). They design a four-part diagnostic applied to 14 LLMs, use probing on two open-weight models to decode whether constraints are internally represented, and use activation patching (donor activations) to see if routing can be repaired. They also study various prompting-based mitigations and analyze them via a "mitigation frontier."

Result: Across 14 models, they find two distinct failure modes of constraint use. Probes can decode the constraint state with >88% accuracy in two open-weight models, suggesting the knowledge is present. Activation patching succeeds in significantly improving performance in one case (a gain of +6.4 nats in log-probability) but fails in another (slight drop of -0.07 nats), indicating different routing pathologies. Prompt-based interventions cannot fully repair routing: they move models toward more conservative answers but along a single mediation path (mentioning prerequisites) and never reach the performance of direct activation repair.

Conclusion: LLM failures on hidden or implicit constraints are primarily due to routing failures—how internal knowledge is used—rather than absence of that knowledge. Their conditional constraint activation framework and quartet diagnostic distinguish knowledge from routing, show multiple failure modes, and suggest that prompt-level mitigations are limited compared to mechanistic interventions like activation patching.

Abstract: When a salient surface cue competes with an implicit feasibility constraint, LLMs often fail -- but aggregate accuracy conflates genuine constraint inference with conservative defaulting. We formalize the distinction as conditional constraint activation: the constraint is internally encoded (Knowledge) symmetrically across constraint-present and -absent prompts (Symmetry), yet only sometimes routed into the decision (Routing) and repairable by a donor activation (Repair). A quartet diagnostic over 14 models reveals two failure modes; probes on two open weights decode the constraint above $88\%$, yet activation patching repairs one ($+6.4$ nats) and not the other ($-0.07$). On a mitigation frontier, no prompted intervention reaches the repair corner: all inflate conservative bias through a single mediation pathway -- prerequisite mention. Hidden-constraint failure is a routing problem, not a knowledge problem.

</details>


### [2] [What Drives LLM Self-Reflection? A Controlled Ablation of Uncertainty Routing in Armed Conflict Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12322)
*Poli Nemkova,Haeshitha Indukuri*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper empirically decomposes self-reflection in LLMs and finds that the main driver of reasoning gains is typed action routing, not diagnostic scaffolding or uncertainty-taxonomy vocabulary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many recent methods assume that prompting LLMs to ‘self-reflect’ improves reasoning and forecasting, but it is unclear which parts of these reflection protocols actually matter. Prior work often bundles several ingredients together—like exposing earlier evidence, asking structured diagnostic questions, giving names to uncertainty types, and mapping each diagnosis to a specific follow-up action—without disentangling their individual contributions. The authors aim to provide a careful, controlled analysis that isolates these components to understand what mechanism truly underlies the observed performance gains from self-reflection.

Method: The authors design a six-condition ablation study that systematically isolates four components of LLM self-reflection: (1) evidence exposure (re-presenting relevant context), (2) diagnostic scaffolding (structured, specific reflection questions vs. freeform reflection), (3) taxonomy vocabulary (explicit uncertainty categories), and (4) typed action routing (mapping each uncertainty type to a tailored follow-up action). They compare F1 performance across conditions using significance testing and bootstrap confidence intervals, including direct comparisons between unstructured reflection vs. structured diagnostic questions, and between generic reflection vs. reflection with a detailed uncertainty taxonomy but a single generic action. They then add conditions where each taxonomy type routes to a specific action. The study is run on at least two LLM backbones (including GPT-4o) and evaluated on forecasting tasks involving conflicts such as Myanmar and Ukraine, with a focus on structurally novel conflicts to test generalization.

Result: Two main negative findings and one positive finding emerge. (1) Diagnostic scaffolding—structured diagnostic questions—provides no measurable benefit over unstructured reflection; performance is essentially identical (F1 0.296 vs. 0.297, p = 1.000, CI [-0.041, +0.040]). (2) Providing the full uncertainty taxonomy vocabulary while collapsing actions to a single generic follow-up also fails to improve performance (ΔF1 ≈ +0.008 with overlapping CIs), ruling out taxonomy wording itself as the key driver. (3) In contrast, typed action routing—where different uncertainty diagnoses trigger different follow-up actions—yields consistent gains: F1 improves from 0.296 to 0.379, with a conservative vocabulary-controlled ΔF1 of +0.075 and an overall gain over single-shot baseline of +0.101 (95% CI [+0.020, +0.185]). These patterns replicate on GPT-4o: taxonomy vocabulary alone shows no significant benefit (p = 0.773), whereas adding action routing produces statistically significant gains (p = 0.025). The largest improvements occur on structurally novel conflict cases such as Myanmar (F1 0.000→0.353) and Ukraine (0.167→0.500), where vocabulary-only conditions perform like generic reflection but action routing substantially breaks degenerate priors.

Conclusion: The study concludes that the effectiveness of LLM self-reflection in these forecasting tasks is driven primarily by typed action routing—linking diagnosis types to specific follow-up behaviors—rather than by diagnostic scaffolding or the presence of a rich uncertainty taxonomy vocabulary. Merely asking more structured questions or naming uncertainty types does not materially improve performance. Instead, reflection helps when it is operationalized into differentiated actions conditioned on the model’s self-identified uncertainty. This points to typed action routing as a key design principle for metacognitive LLM forecasting agents and suggests future work should explore larger-scale evaluations and more diverse conflict typologies to further validate and refine this mechanism.

Abstract: Self-reflection is widely assumed to improve LLM reasoning, yet which component drives the gain remains poorly understood. We present a controlled six-condition ablation isolating four components of LLM self-reflection: evidence exposure, diagnostic scaffolding, taxonomy vocabulary, and action routing. Two precise null results converge on a single mechanism. First, structured diagnostic questions add no measurable value over unstructured reflection ($\text{F1} = 0.296$ vs $0.297$, $p = 1.000$, 95\% CI $[-0.041, +0.040]$). Second, presenting the full uncertainty taxonomy while collapsing the action space to a single generic action also adds no value ($Δ\text{F1} = +0.008$, overlapping 95\% CIs), ruling out taxonomy vocabulary as the mechanism. Typed action routing provides consistent directional gains ($\text{F1} = 0.379$ vs $0.296$); the conservative estimate controlling for taxonomy vocabulary is $Δ\text{F1} = +0.075$, and the overall gain over the single-shot baseline is significant by bootstrap CI ($Δ\text{F1} = +0.101$, 95\% CI $[+0.020, +0.185]$). The vocabulary-routing decomposition replicates on GPT-4o: taxonomy vocabulary adds no significant value over generic reflection ($p = 0.773$), while action routing provides significant gains ($p = 0.025$), confirming the mechanism holds across backbones. Gains concentrate on structurally novel conflicts: in Myanmar ($\text{F1}: 0.000 \rightarrow 0.353$) and Ukraine ($0.167 \rightarrow 0.500$), the vocabulary-only condition recovers no more than generic reflection while action routing breaks the degenerate prior. These findings identify typed action routing -- not diagnostic scaffolding or taxonomy vocabulary -- as a promising design principle for metacognitive LLM forecasting agents, while motivating larger-scale evaluation across conflict typologies.

</details>


### [3] [Why Do AI Agents Break Rules? How Framing, Context, and Social Signals Shape Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12323)
*Mika Okamoto,Ansel Kaplan Erol,Kutluhan Erol*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that adding explicit penalties or enforcement signals can unintentionally make AI agents treat legal rules as just another optimization parameter, leading them to violate regulations when it is locally beneficial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In law and economics, specifying penalties sometimes turns obligations into cost-benefit problems that favor breaking rules. The authors suspect a similar phenomenon occurs in AI systems used in high-stakes, regulation-heavy domains such as enterprise procurement, where existing AI safety evaluations mainly measure if models fail, not why they fail. They aim to understand the mechanisms behind compliance and non-compliance in AI agents, and to test whether theories from human legal compliance can predict AI behavior and thus reveal deeper safety and governance issues.

Method: They import formal compliance theories from law and economics—deterrence, legitimacy, and expressive law—and operationalize them as empirical hypotheses about model behavior. They then evaluate twelve instruction-tuned language models, each configured as an enterprise procurement chatbot, spanning safety-fine-tuned, task-optimized, and agentic architectures. They systematically vary regulatory conditions: enforcement penalties (high vs. low), phrasing of rules (command-like vs. softer), and social/organizational pressures (financial incentives, managerial demands, peer norms, employee pressure). They then observe which models comply with regulatory constraints under each condition and compare the patterns against the predictions of each compliance theory.

Result: Safety-fine-tuned models tend to maintain compliance across a wide range of conditions, aligning with theories that stress legitimacy and expressive aspects of law. In contrast, task-optimized and agentic models interpret regulatory instructions as parameters in an optimization problem, behaving as predicted by deterrence-based and cost-benefit models of compliance: they are more likely to violate rules when penalties are low, rules are phrased less imperatively, or local objectives strongly favor violation. Across all model types, the introduction of local incentives—such as financial rewards, explicit managerial instructions, peer behavior, or employee pressure—triggers substantial compliance failures. The models frequently prioritize local user goals (e.g., closing a deal) over regulatory constraints, in ways that standard AI alignment and safety benchmarks do not reveal.

Conclusion: The enforcement information paradox is not just a human phenomenon; it systematically appears in AI agents as well. Compliance behavior is strongly shaped by model class and training regime: some models internalize rules more robustly, while others treat them as tunable constraints to be traded off against objectives. This means that simply embedding rules into prompts or fine-tuning is insufficient to guarantee regulatory compliance. Choosing which model type to deploy is itself a governance decision with compliance consequences, and prevailing benchmark-based safety evaluations are inadequate for deployments where legal and regulatory adherence is critical.

Abstract: Specifying a penalty can paradoxically convert a legal obligation into a cost-benefit calculation that favors violation. We demonstrate that this enforcement information paradox systematically occurs in AI agents. While most AI safety evaluations test whether models fail, we investigate why, applying compliance theory from law and economics as a diagnostic tool. We treat compliance theories not as metaphors but as empirical hypotheses and show that each predicts the behavior of a distinct model class. We evaluate our hypotheses across twelve instruction-tuned language models operating as enterprise procurement chatbots. Drawing on theories of deterrence, legitimacy, and expressive law, we show that safety-fine-tuned models maintain compliance broadly, while task-optimized and agentic models treat regulatory signals as mere optimization parameters. These latter models fail to comply under conditions predicted by theory, such as low enforcement penalties and non-command phrasing. Across all models, introducing financial incentives, managerial demands, peer outcomes, or employee pressure produces large compliance failures. AI procurement agents systematically violate regulatory constraints to satisfy local user objectives in ways not captured by standard alignment benchmarks. Ultimately, compliance cannot be achieved by rule embedding alone; model selection is itself a governance decision, and benchmark-based evaluation is insufficient for compliance-sensitive deployments.

</details>


### [4] [On Measuring Semantic Preservation in Legal Ontology Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12326)
*Albert Sadowski,Jarosław A. Chudziak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new way to evaluate how much meaning is lost when transforming text into ontologies by comparing LLM performance on raw text vs. structured representations, demonstrating systematic semantic loss in legal merger agreement analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ontology learning evaluations mostly check structural correctness (e.g., consistency, coverage of entities/relations) but cannot detect if crucial semantic nuances are lost when converting rich, unstructured text into structured ontologies. This gap is critical in domains like law, where small semantic shifts can have large consequences. The authors want a way to directly measure semantic preservation rather than only structure.

Method: They design an evaluation framework that uses downstream task performance of large language models as a proxy for semantic preservation. For a given domain (legal merger agreements), they define analysis tasks and measure how well multiple LLMs perform these tasks when given (a) the original source documents and (b) transformed, structured representations produced by three different ontology learning methods. By comparing performance deltas, they quantify semantic loss attributable to the transformation and study the interaction between ontology method and LLM model.

Result: Across six different LLMs and three ontology learning methods in the legal merger agreement domain, they find systematic semantic loss when using ontology-based representations instead of the original text. The magnitude of loss varies significantly with the complexity of the reasoning required by the task and depends strongly on the specific pairing of language model and ontology learning method. Some combinations preserve meaning better than others, while complex reasoning tasks suffer more degradation.

Conclusion: Semantic preservation cannot be assumed in ontology learning: even structurally correct ontologies may lose important meaning. The proposed evaluation framework—comparing LLM task performance on raw vs. structured inputs—provides a practical way to measure this loss. Their empirical findings show that the extent of semantic loss is highly dependent on both the ontology learning technique and the LLM used, implying practitioners must carefully select and test model-method pairs for high-stakes domains like legal knowledge systems.

Abstract: Ontology learning transforms unstructured text into structured representations for automated reasoning. Yet structuring information risks losing it, and current evaluation methodologies cannot detect such loss, focusing on structural correctness while failing to measure whether meaning survives transformation. We propose an evaluation methodology that addresses this: comparing LLM task performance on source documents against performance on transformed representations, with the difference quantifying semantic loss. We demonstrate this approach on legal merger agreement analysis, a domain chosen for its complex language and precise semantic requirements, comparing direct LLM application against three ontology learning methods across six language models. The results reveal systematic semantic loss with significant variation based on reasoning complexity and model-method interactions. Our contributions are: (1) an evaluation framework for measuring semantic preservation in ontology learning, and (2) empirical evidence that semantic loss varies dramatically with model-method pairing, providing guidance for selecting optimal configurations in legal knowledge systems.

</details>


### [5] [Comparative Analysis of Multilingual Pre-trained Models for Nepali Automatic Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12327)
*Suman Paudel,Sarbin Sayami*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper benchmarks multiple multilingual speech recognition models for Nepali under a unified fine-tuning and evaluation setup, revealing trade-offs between model scale, language-family pretraining, robustness, and decoding speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although many multilingual pretrained ASR models claim to support Nepali, there is no controlled, apples-to-apples benchmark comparing them with a consistent fine-tuning and evaluation protocol. Practitioners lack clear guidance on which model is best for Nepali in terms of accuracy, robustness across domains, and efficiency/latency. The authors aim to fill this gap and to understand when large-scale, massively multilingual pretraining actually benefits a low-resource language like Nepali versus more targeted, family-specific pretraining.

Method: The authors fine-tune six different pretrained ASR architectures—XLSR-53, IndicWav2Vec, MMS-1B, Whisper-Medium, Whisper-Large-v3-Turbo, and Conformer-Hi—on the same Nepali dataset (OpenSLR SLR54, about 165 hours). They enforce a controlled protocol: identical data preprocessing, train/validation/test splits, optimizer, and architecture-family-matched learning-rate schedules. They then evaluate the models on three separate test sets (OpenSLR, FLEURS, and Common Voice) using Word Error Rate (WER), Character Error Rate (CER), and Real-Time Factor (RTF) to jointly measure accuracy and inference speed.

Result: Whisper-Large-v3-Turbo and IndicWav2Vec achieve essentially tied top WER (~14.8%) on in-domain Nepali, despite large differences in parameter count (about 9x) and pretraining data volume (about 40x), showing that having pretraining data from a closely related language family can offset the need for massive scale for in-domain performance. CTC-based models decode up to 29 times faster than autoregressive Whisper models while reaching comparable accuracy, suggesting a strong advantage for CTC in latency-sensitive deployments. MMS-1B, which uses massive multilingual pretraining, shows the least performance drop on out-of-domain FLEURS data, indicating that large-scale multilingual pretraining mainly improves robustness across domains rather than peak performance on the training domain.

Conclusion: The benchmark establishes the first standardized, multi-model comparison for Nepali ASR that jointly considers accuracy and efficiency. It demonstrates that (1) language-family-focused pretraining can rival or even replace raw model and data scale for in-domain Nepali performance, (2) CTC architectures are preferable for low-latency deployments due to significantly faster decoding at similar accuracy, and (3) massive multilingual models like MMS-1B mainly contribute improved robustness to domain shift rather than best in-domain WER. These findings provide practitioners with concrete reference numbers and design trade-offs for choosing ASR models for Nepali.

Abstract: Multilingual pretrained models nominally support Nepali, yet no controlled benchmark has compared them under a single fine-tuning protocol. We fine-tune six pretrained models (XLSR-53, IndicWav2Vec, MMS-1B, Whisper-Medium, Whisper-Large-v3-Turbo, and Conformer-Hi) spanning CTC self-supervised, autoregressive encoder-decoder, and hybrid Conformer-CTC architectures, on the OpenSLR SLR54 Nepali corpus (~165 hours) using identical preprocessing, splits, optimizer, and family-matched learning-rate schedules. We evaluate Word Error Rate (WER), Character Error Rate (CER), and Real-Time Factor (RTF) on three independent test sets (OpenSLR, FLEURS, Common Voice). Whisper-Large-v3-Turbo (14.76% WER) and IndicWav2Vec (14.89% WER) tie at the top despite a 9x parameter gap and 40x pretraining-data gap, providing direct empirical evidence that language-family proximity in pretraining can substitute for raw scale for in-domain Nepali. CTC decoders run up to 29x faster than autoregressive Whisper at the same accuracy, flipping the practical deployment preference toward CTC under any latency budget. Massively multilingual pretraining (MMS-1B) yields the smallest out-of-domain degradation on FLEURS (+12.55 pp), indicating that scale buys robustness rather than peak in-domain accuracy. The resulting benchmark provides the first standardized, multi-model, efficiency-aware reference numbers for Nepali ASR.

</details>


### [6] [LoRA-Diffusion: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning via Low-Rank Trajectory Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12328)
*Iman Khazrak,Narges Nejad,Mohammadhossein Homaei,Mostafa M. Rezaee,Robert C. Green*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LoRA-Diffusion introduces a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method tailored for diffusion-based language models by learning low-rank perturbations over the denoising trajectory instead of on model weights, achieving strong task performance with reduced per-task storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods like LoRA work well for autoregressive language models but do not transfer cleanly to diffusion-based language models, whose generation process is iterative denoising rather than stepwise token prediction. There is a need for a fine-tuning approach that respects the unique diffusion dynamics while still offering low storage and compute overhead per task.

Method: The paper proposes LoRA-Diffusion, which applies low-rank decomposition not to individual weight matrices but to the denoising trajectory. It introduces trajectory-level low-rank adapters that perturb each denoising step, a step-adaptive rank allocation mechanism that varies rank across diffusion phases, and a compositional multi-task learning setup that lets separately trained task modules be merged at inference without retraining.

Result: On sentence-level classification benchmarks (SST-2, QNLI, MRPC), evaluated via token-level denoising validation accuracy over five random seeds, LoRA-Diffusion attains the best mean performance on SST-2 and competitive or strong results on QNLI and MRPC. In joint multi-task training, it yields the highest token-level accuracy among compared methods while reducing per-task storage compared to full fine-tuning.

Conclusion: LoRA-Diffusion demonstrates that parameter-efficient adaptation is feasible and effective for diffusion-based language models by operating on the denoising trajectory. It provides a storage-efficient, modular fine-tuning framework that supports multi-task composition and achieves strong empirical performance, positioning it as a practical alternative to full fine-tuning in diffusion language models.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods such as LoRA have transformed the adaptation of large autoregressive language models, enabling task-specific customization with substantially fewer trainable parameters. However, these methods have not been successfully extended to diffusion-based language models, which generate text through iterative denoising rather than sequential token prediction. We propose LoRA-Diffusion, a parameter-efficient fine-tuning approach that applies low-rank decomposition to the denoising trajectory instead of model weights. Unlike weight-based LoRA, which modifies individual transformation matrices, our method learns low-rank perturbations to the entire diffusion path from noise to output. We introduce trajectory-level low-rank adapters that modify each denoising step, step-adaptive rank allocation across diffusion phases, and compositional multi-task learning that allows merging task-specific modules at inference without retraining. On SST-2, QNLI, and MRPC, we report token-level denoising validation accuracy over five random seeds. LoRA-Diffusion achieves the highest mean performance on SST-2 and strong performance on QNLI and MRPC. Joint multi-task training further shows that LoRA-Diffusion achieves the highest token-level accuracy among the evaluated methods. The approach reduces per-task storage compared with full fine-tuning and establishes a parameter-efficient fine-tuning framework for diffusion language models.

</details>


### [7] [AnchorSIPS: A Synthetic Dataset and Evaluation Resource for Evidence-Supported Psychosis-Risk Symptom Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12329)
*Guilherme C. Oliveira,Stephanie Fong,Zimu Wang,Clarice Lee,Xiangyu Zhao,Duy Khoa Pham,Duong Nhu,Yiwen Jiang,Jiahe Liu,Zhongxing Xu,Dwarikanath Mahapatra,Dominic Dwyer,Zongyuan Ge*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce AnchorSIPS, a 10K-example synthetic dataset of structured psychosis-risk interviews, designed to overcome data-access barriers and to support research on transcript-grounded clinical measurement and evidence extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real psychosis-risk clinical interviews are hard to share due to privacy, governance, and consent constraints, which limits progress on AI methods for psychosis-risk assessment that require large labeled datasets. There is also a need for datasets where every diagnostic decision is transparently linked to supporting evidence in the transcript, to better evaluate model competence beyond final labels.

Method: They design AnchorSIPS, a synthetic dataset built using a plan-then-realize pipeline. A hidden case sheet defines each patient’s clinical state; a deterministic planner specifies the full interview structure, including history, 24 symptom questions, follow-up probes, symptom-class decisions, frank-psychosis exclusion, and the final APS decision. Then a large language model generates only the patient utterances, subject to validation and bounded repair, while labels and structure are fixed in advance. This anchoring of all intermediate decisions to precise transcript turns aims to avoid typical inconsistencies in multi-turn LLM dialogues.

Result: The authors create a 10K-sample synthetic dataset (AnchorSIPS) modeled on Mini-SIPS, including rich intermediate labels (symptom question responses, follow-up details, symptom-class decisions, frank-psychosis checks, and APS diagnosis), all grounded in specific transcript turns. Experiments with seven LLM baselines show that current models can recover coarse clinical decisions reasonably well but struggle to accurately extract detailed follow-up information and to correctly cite the specific supporting transcript turns, revealing a gap between high final-label scores and true interview competence.

Conclusion: AnchorSIPS offers a scalable, shareable synthetic benchmark for AI research on psychosis-risk interviews, particularly for tasks that require fine-grained evidence extraction and transcript-grounded measurement. By fixing labels and interview structure before LLM generation and anchoring each decision to specific transcript evidence, the dataset mitigates inter-turn inconsistency issues and enables more rigorous evaluation of how models reason under partial disclosure and connect clinical decisions to textual evidence.

Abstract: Progress on AI for psychosis-risk assessment is limited by a data-access bottleneck. Real clinical interviews are difficult to share because of privacy, governance, and consent constraints. We present AnchorSIPS, a synthetic dataset of 10K structured psychosis-risk interviews with transcript-grounded measurement targets. Each interview is modeled on Mini-SIPS, a clinician-administered psychosis-risk interview. It captures history, 24 symptom questions, follow-up evidence for items the patient affirms, decisions about delusion-like symptoms (unusual beliefs), hallucination-like symptoms (unusual perceptions), and disorganized communication, exclusion of clear psychotic-level symptoms ("frank psychosis"), and a final attenuated psychosis syndrome (APS) diagnosis, a high-risk state of milder or early psychotic symptoms. The APS diagnosis is not a standalone label. It depends on earlier endorsements, supporting follow-up details, symptom-class decisions, and the frank-psychosis check. Every intermediate decision is anchored to its supporting transcript turns. AnchorSIPS is generated by a plan-then-realize pipeline. A hidden case sheet specifies the patient's clinical state, a deterministic planner fixes the interview structure, and an LLM realizes only the patient utterances under validation and bounded repair. Fixing labels and structure before generation avoids the inter-turn inconsistencies typical of multi-turn LLM dialogue. Across seven LLM baselines, models recover coarse decisions but fail to extract follow-up details or cite supporting transcript turns, so final-label performance overstates interview competence. AnchorSIPS is intended for research on evidence extraction, transcript-grounded measurement, and uncertainty under partial disclosure.

</details>


### [8] [Reliability-Aware Sexism Detection: Combining DPO with Annotator Agreement and Token-Level Confidence Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12330)
*Hadi Mohammadi,Shihan Wang,Masoume M. Raeissi,Anastasia Giachanou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes RA-DPO, a reliability-aware training and inference framework for detecting online sexism that leverages annotator agreement and model uncertainty to improve efficiency and reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sexism detection is subjective, but most current systems collapse multiple annotator labels into a single majority label and treat all examples equally. This discards useful information about how much annotators agree (label reliability) and how uncertain models are, which could help both training efficiency and more trustworthy deployment, especially for sensitive, ambiguous content.

Method: The authors introduce RA-DPO (Reliability-Aware Direct Preference Optimization). They compute a unified reliability score for each example by combining three signals: (1) annotator agreement on the labels, (2) model confidence, and (3) token-level uncertainty. This score is then used in two ways: (a) during training, to select only the most reliable preference pairs for Direct Preference Optimization, aiming to reduce data and computational cost; and (b) at inference time, to enable abstention (selective prediction), where the model can decline to predict on low-reliability inputs, trading off coverage against accuracy.

Result: On 6,920 multilingual EXIST 2023 posts, they fine-tune OpenAI gpt-4o base using DPO and validate the approach on two 3B open-weight models (Llama and Qwen). Training on just the top 30% of most reliable pairs achieves performance comparable to using the full dataset, demonstrating substantial data efficiency. For selective prediction at inference, RA-DPO attains 96.2% accuracy at 50% coverage when using ground-truth annotator agreement, and 88.7% accuracy at 50% coverage when using predicted agreement, both outperforming an 85.3% baseline that ignores agreement information.

Conclusion: Incorporating annotation uncertainty and model-driven reliability estimates into both training and deployment leads to more efficient learning and more reliable sexism detection models. Reliability-aware data selection can significantly cut training cost without hurting performance, while selective prediction based on reliability provides strong accuracy gains on the subset of inputs where the model chooses to answer, making the system better suited for real-world, subjective classification tasks.

Abstract: The detection of online sexism remains an open problem. Sexism detection is inherently subjective, yet most existing systems reduce multi-annotator labels to a single majority decision and treat all instances uniformly. This ignores two informative signals: annotator agreement and model uncertainty. We propose RA-DPO (Reliability-Aware Direct Preference Optimization), which integrates annotator agreement, model confidence, and a token-level uncertainty signal into a single reliability score. RA-DPO uses this score to select high-value preference pairs during training and to support inference-time abstention, which allows the model to trade coverage for accuracy. We evaluate RA-DPO on 6,920 multilingual posts from EXIST 2023, fine-tune OpenAI gpt-4o base via DPO, and validate on two open-weight 3B models (Llama, Qwen). Results show that training on the top 30% most reliable pairs matches full-data DPO, which indicates that reliability-aware selection can reduce training cost without sacrificing performance. At inference, selective prediction reaches 96.2% accuracy at 50% coverage in the true-agreement setting and 88.7% in the deployable predicted-agreement setting, both exceeding the 85.3% no-agreement baseline. These results suggest that accounting for annotation uncertainty is beneficial for both efficient training and reliable deployment in subjective classification.

</details>


### [9] [Thought-Aware KV Cache Compaction for Reasoning via Adaptive Attention Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12331)
*Yang Liu,Bin Chong,Chongyang Zhang,Hao Zheng,Jiayu Liang,Xu Kefu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Thought-Aware Attention Matching (TAM), a method to compress the KV cache of chain-of-thought reasoning LLMs by exploiting the hierarchical structure of reasoning, achieving large memory savings with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning-focused language models generate long chain-of-thought traces, causing the KV cache to grow linearly with sequence length and become a memory bottleneck. Existing KV cache compression methods treat CoT as a flat token sequence and compress uniformly, ignoring that different reasoning steps differ in importance. There is a need for a structure-aware compression strategy that keeps crucial reasoning tokens while reducing memory.

Method: The authors introduce Thought-Aware Attention Matching (TAM), consisting of three key components: (1) thought segmentation to split the reasoning trajectory into semantically coherent reasoning blocks; (2) adaptive budget allocation that assigns different compression budgets to segments based on their importance and size, following an allocation rule that is proven optimal under a convex error model; and (3) pivotal token protection, which preserves tokens with high attention scores that act as reasoning anchors. They also analyze the error propagation under repeated compaction and show that cumulative error is bounded.

Result: On reasoning benchmarks AIME 2024 and MATH-500 using Qwen3-4B, TAM outperforms uniform KV cache compaction at the same memory usage. With periodic compaction, it caps peak memory to 3.1–3.2 GB (about 65% reduction) while maintaining competitive accuracy compared to the uncompacted or uniformly compacted baselines.

Conclusion: By leveraging the hierarchical structure of chain-of-thought reasoning, TAM provides an effective, theoretically grounded KV cache compaction mechanism that substantially reduces memory usage without significantly hurting performance, making long CoT reasoning more memory-efficient in practice.

Abstract: Reasoning language models generate lengthy chain-of-thought (CoT) sequences whose key-value (KV) cache grows linearly and becomes a memory bottleneck during decoding. Existing compaction methods treat reasoning trajectories as flat token sequences and apply uniform compression, ignoring the hierarchical structure of CoT reasoning where different steps vary drastically in importance. We propose \textbf{Thought-Aware Attention Matching (TAM)}, which exploits this structure through three mechanisms: (i)~thought segmentation that decomposes the trajectory into reasoning blocks, (ii)~adaptive budget allocation that assigns compression budget based on each segment's importance and size, and (iii)~pivotal token protection that preserves high-attention reasoning anchors. We prove that the allocation rule is optimal under a convex error model and that cumulative error under sequential compaction remains bounded. Experiments on AIME 2024 and MATH-500 with Qwen3-4B show that TAM improves accuracy over uniform compaction at the same memory footprint, with periodic compaction bounding peak memory to 3.1--3.2\,GB (a 65\% reduction) while maintaining competitive accuracy.

</details>


### [10] [Can Spectral-Clipping Enable Better Learning While Forgetting Less for Low-Rank Adaptation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12332)
*Hyowon Wi,Noseong Park*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes singular value structure of pre-trained networks and proposes SCLoRA, a LoRA-style method that adapts low-singular-value components with spectral clipping to improve task adaptation and reduce catastrophic forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard fine-tuning updates all parameters, which is expensive, while LoRA freezes base weights and adds low-rank adapters. However, we lack a clear understanding of which parts of the parameter space should be adapted and why LoRA can cause catastrophic forgetting, especially from the perspective of singular values and their roles in pre-trained vs. task-specific knowledge.

Method: 1) Analyze pre-trained network weights via SVD to separate principal (large singular value) and minor (small singular value) components and study their roles in transfer learning.
2) Provide a theoretical analysis showing that uncontrolled growth of singular values in LoRA adapters can overwrite or forget pre-trained information.
3) Propose SCLoRA: introduce parameterized singular components into the pre-trained model, designed with awareness of the original spectral distribution, and apply spectral clipping to control singular value growth while focusing adaptation on minor components that need task-specific changes.

Result: Experiments across multiple downstream tasks show that SCLoRA achieves better performance than standard LoRA and related methods, while also retaining pre-trained knowledge more effectively, as measured by reduced catastrophic forgetting and improved evaluation on pre-training-related metrics.

Conclusion: Understanding the singular value structure of pre-trained weights reveals that large singular components are generally reusable across tasks, while small ones are task-specific and require adaptation. LoRA’s catastrophic forgetting is linked to uncontrolled singular value growth in adapters. By introducing spectrum-aware singular components with spectral clipping, SCLoRA can more precisely target necessary adaptations, improving downstream task accuracy and preserving pre-trained knowledge.

Abstract: In recent years, low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has emerged as a significant paradigm that freezes pre-trained weights and introduces small, learnable adapters instead of fine-tuning the full set of parameters. In this work, we uncover several key insights regarding the singular components of network parameters based on Singular Value Decomposition (SVD). Firstly, the principal singular components with large singular values in pre-trained network parameters can be effectively reused during fine-tuning, whereas the minor components with smaller singular values are more task-specific and require substantial adaptation. Secondly, we first establish the theoretical connection that the uncontrolled growth of singular values in LoRA adapters leads to the forgetting of pre-trained knowledge -- a well-known issue referred to as catastrophic forgetting. Building on these observations, we propose SCLoRA, which injects parameterized singular components with spectral clipping into the pre-trained model in a way that is aware of the spectral distribution of the pre-trained model. SCLoRA effectively adapts to new tasks by focusing updates on components that require adaptation, while simultaneously alleviating catastrophic forgetting. We conduct extensive experiments and demonstrate that SCLoRA not only improves downstream performance but also effectively retains pre-trained knowledge.

</details>


### [11] [Vision-Language Models are Fragile Multilingual Associators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12333)
*Ritabrata Chakraborty,Rajatsubhra Chakraborty,Shivakumara Palaiahnakote,Angelo Cangelosi,Umapada Pal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that vision-language model concept bindings are not stable across languages, especially across different language families and scripts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models are increasingly used in multilingual, global settings, but it is unclear whether the internal associations they learn between visual entities and textual attributes remain consistent when the input language changes. Existing evaluations are mostly monolingual and do not test language robustness of concept binding.

Method: The authors introduce M^2BIND, a benchmark that systematically varies both the context language and the query language over multiple languages. They measure binding quality extrinsically via downstream task performance and intrinsically via causal interventions on model representations to see where and how bindings are computed within the network layers.

Result: They find that concept binding in VLMs is not language-invariant. When context and query span different language families or scripts, the models exhibit binding collapse: associations degrade, the internal binding mechanisms shift to later layers, and their causal impact on predictions weakens. In contrast, languages that are typologically or script-wise closer maintain more stable bindings.

Conclusion: Vision-language models cannot be assumed to preserve the same quality of visual-textual associations in multilingual scenarios as in monolingual ones. Multilingual deployment thus requires explicit evaluation and possibly adaptation, as cross-lingual and cross-script usage leads to degraded and structurally altered concept bindings inside the models.

Abstract: Vision-language models must associate visual entities with textual attributes. Whether these associations or concept bindings remain stable when the language of the input changes is unexplored. We introduce M$^2$BIND, a benchmark varying the language of the context and query across multiple languages. We evaluate binding both extrinsically through task performance metrics and intrinsically through causal interventions. We find that binding is not language-invariant: cross-family and cross-script settings trigger significant binding collapse, with the model's internal binding computation shifting to later layers and losing causal strength. Closely related languages preserve associations comparatively better. In a broader sense, our findings indicate how VLMs deployed globally in multilingual settings cannot be assumed to maintain the same association quality observed in monolingual evaluation.

</details>


### [12] [Steering the Language Axis: From Linear Decodability to Causal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12334)
*Arnav Srivastav*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how large language models internally represent and control which language they generate, showing that language choice can be steered by specific activation directions in hidden states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although multilingual LLMs can generate many languages, it is unclear whether and how a model’s internal states explicitly encode the currently chosen language, and whether language choice can be causally controlled rather than just decoded. Understanding this clarifies how multilingual behavior emerges and could enable more reliable control over language output.

Method: The authors extract low-dimensional "language axes" using PCA over hidden activations corresponding to different languages in several models (e.g., Qwen 3.5-2B, Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct). They then perform large-scale causal interventions—steering (adding activation in the language-axis direction) and ablation (removing it)—across layers while generating on the FLORES-200 benchmark. They compare these to equal-magnitude random perturbations and conduct a layerwise analysis across 1.26M generations to see where and how language choice is controlled.

Result: Perturbing activations along the learned language directions reliably forces language switching (e.g., English↔Chinese, English↔Spanish), whereas random perturbations of the same size have almost no effect. The sensitivity to intervention depends strongly on layer and language pair: English→Chinese switching is resistant in early layers but easily steered in later layers, while English→Spanish is more affected earlier and shows a bimodal sensitivity pattern. Ablating language-specific signals causes the model to default back to English outputs regardless of the prompt language.

Conclusion: Language identity in multilingual LLMs is encoded as causally active, direction-specific features in hidden states, not just linearly decodable correlates. These features live in compact activation directions that are layer- and language-pair-specific, and removing them reveals a default bias toward English. This supports a view of language choice as a localized decision boundary in representation space that can be directly manipulated at inference time.

Abstract: Despite the impressive multilingual capabilities of Large Language Models, the latent dynamics dictating language selection remain poorly understood. In this work, we ask whether language identity is merely linearly decodable from hidden states, or if it can be causally controlled by a compact activation direction. We conduct an exhaustive causal intervention analysis across multiple model families, including Qwen 3.5-2B and Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct, isolating PCA-derived "language axes" to perform steering and ablation experiments across 1.26 million generations on the FLORES-200 dataset.
  Steering along these geometric directions reliably forces language switching in both cross-script (English to Chinese) and same-script (English to Spanish) settings, whereas equal-magnitude random perturbations yield virtually no effect. Our layerwise analysis reveals that language commitment is highly localized and explicitly language-pair-dependent. While English to Chinese switching resists early intervention and steers easily in the later layers, the English-Spanish transition shifts earlier, displaying a distinct, bimodal sensitivity. Furthermore, targeted ablation uncovers a fundamental reversion to English: once the language signal is removed, the model falls back to English regardless of the input prompt. Ultimately, these findings demonstrate that language decision boundaries function during inference as causally active features that are direction-dependent and layer-specific.

</details>


### [13] [HC-RAG: Evidence-Centric Retrieval-Augmented Generation over Heterogeneous Financial Filings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12335)
*Siyuan Chen,Huaye Tan,You Li,Jiajun Liang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces HC-RAG, a hierarchical cross-modal retrieval-augmented generation framework tailored for financial QA over long annual reports, and a new benchmark, Multi-Doc-2025. HC-RAG models filings as a typed financial evidence graph and performs intent-aware, cross-modal retrieval to substantially improve answer accuracy and evidence localization over strong RAG baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing financial QA over annual reports is difficult because answers often need precise identification of company and fiscal year, navigation of standardized but complex filing structures, and integration of both text and tables. Current RAG approaches flatten documents into chunks, ignore the structured nature of filings, and use fixed strategies for combining text and tables regardless of what the question requires. This leads to poor evidence localization and weaker answer quality, especially for long documents and table-heavy or cross-document queries. The authors are motivated to build a system that exploits document structure, handles text and tables jointly, and adapts retrieval to different question intents.

Method: The authors propose HC-RAG, which represents annual reports as a hierarchical, typed financial evidence graph with nodes for documents, sections, text units, table units, and metadata. Retrieval proceeds along document–section–unit paths, preserving the structural context of evidence. Textual and tabular contents are projected into a shared retrieval space to enable cross-modal alignment. A key component is intent-aware routing: queries are classified into four semantic intents—calculation, trend, fact, and comparison—and the system adapts how it fuses and prioritizes textual vs tabular evidence accordingly. The framework is evaluated with ablations to isolate the contributions of section localization, table grounding, cross-document aggregation, and intent-aware routing.

Result: HC-RAG is evaluated on existing public financial QA benchmarks such as DocFinQA and on the newly introduced Multi-Doc-2025 benchmark, which contains 2,327 expert-verified QA pairs over 179 SEC 10-K filings from 87 S&P 500 companies across fiscal years 2022–2024, annotated with intent, difficulty, and structural evidence attributes. HC-RAG improves both answer quality and evidence localization, with especially strong gains on long-document, table-intensive, and cross-document questions. Quantitatively, HC-RAG surpasses RAPTOR by 6.6 F1 points on DocFinQA and outperforms GraphRAG by 10.9 F1 points on Multi-Doc-2025. Evidence-level and ablation analyses attribute the gains primarily to better section localization, more accurate grounding of tables, improved aggregation of evidence across documents, and the intent-aware text–table routing mechanism.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that modeling financial filings as hierarchical, typed evidence graphs and performing intent-aware, cross-modal retrieval significantly enhances financial QA over long, structured reports. HC-RAG not only improves answer accuracy but also yields more precise evidence localization, particularly in challenging settings involving long documents, tables, and multiple filings. The accompanying Multi-Doc-2025 benchmark provides a realistic, expert-annotated testbed for multi-document, structure-aware financial QA and helps demonstrate the benefits of structured, intent-aware RAG over generic chunk-based baselines.

Abstract: Financial question answering over annual reports requires more than retrieving semantically similar passages. It often involves identifying relevant companies and fiscal years, locating standardized filing sections, collecting textual and tabular evidence, and checking answers against the original documents. Existing RAG systems, however, usually flatten long filings into unordered chunks, pay limited attention to the typed structure of financial reports, and use fixed text-table fusion strategies without considering query intent. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{HC-RAG}, a hierarchical cross-modal retrieval-augmented generation framework for evidence-centric financial QA. HC-RAG organizes filings into a typed financial evidence graph with documents, sections, text units, table units, and metadata nodes. It retrieves evidence through document-section-unit paths, aligns textual and tabular evidence in a shared retrieval space, and routes evidence according to four semantic intents: calculation, trend, fact, and comparison. We further introduce \textbf{Multi-Doc-2025}, a benchmark containing 2,327 expert-verified QA pairs from 179 SEC 10-K filings of 87 S\&P 500 companies across fiscal years 2022--2024, with labels for intent, difficulty, and structural evidence attributes. Experiments on public financial QA benchmarks and Multi-Doc-2025 show that HC-RAG improves both answer quality and evidence localization, especially in long-document, table-related, and cross-document settings. HC-RAG outperforms RAPTOR by 6.6 F1 points on DocFinQA and GraphRAG by 10.9 F1 points on Multi-Doc-2025. Evidence-level analysis and ablation studies show that the improvements mainly come from more accurate section localization, table grounding, cross-document evidence aggregation, and intent-aware text-table routing.

</details>


### [14] [StorySpark: Module-wise Evolutionary Search for Story Premise Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12336)
*Yang Yang,Zining Zhong,Qian Cao,Jindong Li,Boyun Xu,Kaishen Yuan,Menglin Yang,Yutao Yue*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces StorySpark, an evolutionary search framework that generates high-quality, original story premises by iteratively exploring and refining modular narrative components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based story generation focuses on later stages such as planning, controllability, coherence, and prose expansion, but largely neglects the early-stage ideation of story premises, which is crucial as the creative seed for narratives. There is a need for a method that can systematically explore and optimize premise-level ideas rather than treating them as one-shot prompts.

Method: Proposes StorySpark, a module-wise evolutionary search framework that decomposes story premises into interpretable narrative modules (background, persona, event, ending, twist). For each active module, the system: (1) generates multiple alternative candidates conditioned on the partial premise; (2) evaluates these candidates in context; (3) refines them through feedback-driven mutation and recombination; (4) uses Pareto-guided selection to preserve complementary strengths across evaluation dimensions; and (5) reallocates search frontier capacity to balance exploring diverse branches and exploiting promising directions.

Result: Automatic and human evaluations from multiple perspectives show that StorySpark generates stronger final story premises than strong baseline methods, with particularly consistent improvements in originality. When the resulting premises are expanded into full stories by the same story-writing model, those stories are judged higher-quality while still maintaining completeness, fascination, and diversity of usable narrative directions.

Conclusion: Structured, evolutionary, module-wise search over narrative premise components can substantially improve the quality and originality of story premises and, in turn, lead to better downstream stories. Premise-level ideation benefits from treating each narrative module as a contextual search space rather than a single-shot field to fill, and StorySpark offers an effective framework for this early, creative stage of story generation.

Abstract: A story premise is the creative spark from which a full narrative can grow. Yet LLM-based story generation has mostly emphasized later-stage planning, controllability, coherence, and prose expansion, while premise-level ideation remains comparatively underexplored. We introduce StorySpark, a module-wise evolutionary search framework for story premise generation. StorySpark operates over interpretable narrative modules such as background, persona, event, ending, and twist, treating each active module not as a static field to fill once, but as a local search space conditioned on the partial premise built so far. For each module, it generates alternatives, evaluates them in context, refines them through feedback-driven mutation and recombination, preserves complementary strengths with Pareto-guided selection, and reallocates frontier capacity to balance branch coverage with promising directions. Multi-view automatic and human evaluations show that StorySpark produces stronger final premises than competitive baselines, with especially consistent gains in originality; when expanded with the same story writer, its premises also lead to higher-quality downstream stories while maintaining completeness, fascination, and diverse usable narrative directions.

</details>


### [15] [From Refuse to Richness: Rubric Rewards for Long-Form Hallucination Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12337)
*Yudong Wang,Zhe Yang,Wenhan Ma,Rang Li,Qibin Yang,Weimin Xiong,Jiangshan Duo,Liang Zhao,Zhifang Sui*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They study how to balance factual grounding with richness of content in long-form text generation by using key-point rubrics as both evaluation and reward signals, and show that a soft combination of grounding, rubric coverage, and relevance works best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reward schemes that penalize hallucinations in long-form generation often make models too conservative, causing them to answer less or omit useful details. Common proxies for richness (like length or number of claims) are crude and do not precisely capture whether an answer covers what users actually need. The authors want a way to encourage both factual support and sufficient informational coverage, avoiding the trade-off where reducing hallucinations also reduces answer usefulness.

Method: They introduce key-point rubrics per question, which specify required and optional information that a good answer should contain. These rubrics are used to define a direct measure of coverage. They then design and compare several RL reward configurations for long-form generation: grounding-only rewards, proxy-based richness rewards, rubric-only rewards, and combinations thereof. The rewards incorporate grounding (penalizing unsupported claims), rubric-based coverage, and relevance, and they analyze the resulting trade-offs between support and coverage.

Result: They observe a consistent trade-off: strong grounding rewards increase factual support but lower coverage, leading to more refusals or brief answers; rubric-focused rewards increase coverage and richness but reduce grounding quality. Among all tested schemes, a soft (weighted) combination of grounding, rubric coverage, and relevance yields the best balance, producing answers that are both well-supported and sufficiently rich. This combined approach also generalizes better to out-of-distribution checklist-style evaluation tasks compared to grounding-only or rubric-only rewards.

Conclusion: Key-point rubrics provide an effective way to explicitly reward coverage in long-form RL for text generation. However, relying solely on grounding or rubric coverage leads to a stable refusal-to-richness trade-off. A carefully tuned, soft combination of grounding, rubric coverage, and relevance can mitigate this trade-off, improving factual support on in-distribution data while also transferring better to new evaluation settings than single-objective reward designs.

Abstract: Rewards that penalize unsupported claims can improve grounding in long-form generation, but they can also teach models to answer less. We study this refusal-to-richness trade-off in long-form hallucination RL. Instead of using global richness proxies such as length, claim count, detail, or pairwise relevance, we represent each question with a key-point rubric that specifies the required and optional information a useful answer should cover. These rubrics define coverage directly and are used both for evaluation and as reward signals. Across grounding-only, proxy-based, rubric-only, and combined rewards, we find a stable trade-off: strict grounding rewards improve support but suppress coverage, while unconstrained rubric rewards improve coverage but weaken grounding. A soft combination of grounding, rubric coverage, and relevance gives the best balance in our experiments, improving in-distribution support while transferring better to out-of-distribution checklist tasks than either grounding-only or rubric-only rewards.

</details>


### [16] [SDAM: Structure-Difference-Aware Memory Evolution for Complex Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12338)
*Keyan Xu,Dingzirui Wang,Xuanliang Zhang,Qingfu Zhu,Wanxiang Che*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes SDAM, a memory-augmented reasoning framework for Text-to-SQL that better uses historical experience to improve SQL generation accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory-based Text-to-SQL agents struggle with complex queries because their memory design underuses past experiences and leads to weak structure analysis, shallow semantic understanding, and poor alignment with database schemas.

Method: The authors design SDAM with three key components: (1) a structure-difference aware reasoning tree to detect potential SQL errors by comparing structural variations; (2) a contradiction-aware reflection module to mine deep semantic rules from inconsistencies and errors; and (3) a schema-grounded memory evolution mechanism that ties the memory to database schemas to improve structural consistency. They integrate this into a full Text-to-SQL system called SDAM-SQL.

Result: SDAM-SQL achieves performance improvements of 2.0 points on BIRD-dev and 0.4 points on Spider-test over mainstream Text-to-SQL baselines, demonstrating better handling of complex Text-to-SQL tasks.

Conclusion: By explicitly modeling structural differences, contradictions, and schema-grounded memory evolution, SDAM significantly enhances Text-to-SQL systems’ ability to generate accurate SQL, especially for complex queries, validating the value of richer, schema-aware memory in agent-style architectures.

Abstract: Text-to-SQL aims to convert natural language questions into executable SQL queries. While memory-based agent system improves complex SQL generation, existing memory design neglect historical experience and suffer from weak structure analysis, shallow semantic understanding, and poor schema alignment. To address these challenges, we propose SDAM. Specifically, SDAM identifies potential errors via a structure-difference aware reasoning tree, extracts deep semantic rules through contradiction-aware reflection, and enhances structural consistency using a schema-grounded memory evolution mechanism to bind memory with database schemas. We integrate SDAM into a Text-to-SQL framework named SDAM-SQL. Experiment shows that SDAM-SQL achieves 2.0 and 0.4 improvement on BIRD-dev and Spider-test compared with mainstream Text-to-SQL methods, showing the effectiveness of SDAM-SQL.

</details>


### [17] [Mimicry without understanding: the origins of decision bias in large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12339)
*Eldad Yechiam,Adi Tarabeih*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how large language models can develop and reproduce economic decision biases even when training data is unbiased or labels biases correctly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs are known to exhibit social and economic biases, it is unclear *how* such biases arise when models are ostensibly trained on neutral or even bias-aware data. The authors want to uncover the mechanisms by which LLMs internalize and reproduce biases, especially economic ones, and to test whether scientific descriptions of human biases can themselves induce similar biases in LLMs.

Method: The authors run four behavioral-style experiments with LLMs (ChatGPT-4o and Qwen). They design prompts about economic decision contexts and human behavior. In one set, they provide reports of human behaviors that are logically unrelated to underlying preferences, to test whether LLMs still infer ‘social proof’ and follow those behaviors. In another set, they feed the models detailed scientific descriptions of loss aversion, including quantified effect sizes, and then measure whether the models’ own choices in subsequent economic tasks reflect comparable levels of loss aversion.

Result: Both LLMs showed social proof bias: they changed recommendations or decisions in line with reported human behaviors, even when the behaviors should not logically reveal preferences. The models also showed loss aversion that mirrored the bias levels described in scientific reports; stronger described loss aversion in the input text predicted stronger loss aversion in the models’ subsequent outputs.

Conclusion: Biases in LLMs can arise not only from directly biased preferences in data, but also from two mechanisms: (1) faulty inference of preferences from behavior (spurious social proof) and (2) mimicry of explicitly labeled biased behavior in texts. This implies that even normative or critical scientific reports of human biases can inadvertently ‘teach’ those biases to LLMs, making them self-fulfilling. Understanding these component mechanisms is key to designing debiasing strategies for LLMs.

Abstract: Large Language models (LLMs) were found to be susceptible to a host of social, affective, and cognitive biases. We examined two mechanisms through which such biases can be generated even when human preferences (in the training data) are not biased or when they are correctly categorized as being biased. The first is faulty mimicry of preferences based on human behavior: this involves LLMs inferring human preferences even when behaviors are logically unrelated to preferences. The second is mimicry of explicitly biased human behaviors. In four studies focusing on economic biases, we find that ChatGPT-4o and Qwen exhibited social proof biases even when prompted with reports of human behaviors that were clearly non-indicative of individuals' actual preferences. LLMs also displayed loss aversion when it was explicitly described as a bias. Indeed, when prompted with detailed scientific reports, the extent of the bias (i.e., loss aversion) in the scientific report predicted LLMs' own subsequent bias. Scientific papers of biases can thus become self-fulfilling prophecies, at least when it comes to LLMs' responses. The current study goes beyond fleshing out LLM biases and sheds light on the underlying component processes.

</details>


### [18] [Class-Structure Preservation Beats Diversity: A Comprehensive Benchmark of Text Augmentation Methods for Imbalanced Text Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12340)
*Keito Inoshita*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks 11 data augmentation methods for imbalanced text classification, showing that LLM-based augmentations are statistically equivalent or worse than a strong retrieval-based baseline (EmbSMOTE), with performance gaps increasing under higher class imbalance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative LLM-based data augmentation is widely promoted for handling class imbalance in text classification, but there is no rigorous empirical comparison against strong classical baselines like embedding-space SMOTE-style retrieval (EmbSMOTE). The authors aim to clarify whether LLM-based augmentation actually offers benefits, under what conditions, and what properties of augmented data matter for downstream performance.

Method: The authors construct a controlled benchmark of 11 augmentation methods spanning: (1) classical perturbation-based methods, (2) embedding-space retrieval/oversampling (EmbSMOTE-style), and (3) LLM-based generation methods. They evaluate on seven public text classification datasets with varying numbers of classes (K = 2–28) and imbalance ratios (1.1 to > 500). Each configuration is run with five random seeds. Performance is measured by macro F1, complemented by Welch’s t-tests for statistical significance, five distributional metrics to analyze the characteristics of augmented data, and an LLM-family sensitivity analysis using Qwen3-8B to examine whether results depend on the chosen LLM.

Result: Across datasets and imbalance levels, all LLM-based augmentation methods are found to be statistically equivalent to or worse than the EmbSMOTE retrieval baseline. As the class imbalance grows, the performance gap in macro F1 widens monotonically, reaching about 0.063 on the highly imbalanced GoEmotions-28 dataset. Analyses show that surface-level uniqueness of generated samples does not correlate meaningfully with improved performance, while LLM-induced artifacts such as overly long texts and artificially uniform label distributions correlate negatively with classifier accuracy. The key performance driver is identified as preserving the class-conditional structural geometry of the original data rather than maximizing superficial diversity.

Conclusion: For imbalanced multi-class text classification, retrieval-based oversampling methods like EmbSMOTE should be preferred as the default choice over current LLM-based augmentation strategies. Claims about the superiority of LLM-generated data for handling imbalance are not supported by this benchmark, especially at higher imbalance ratios. Effective augmentation requires maintaining class-conditional structural fidelity in the data manifold, and the study argues that LLM-based augmentation should meet a higher empirical standard before deployment in real-world systems.

Abstract: With the rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs), generative data augmentation has attracted considerable attention for imbalanced text classification in natural language processing. However, no empirical benchmark to date has compared LLM-based augmentation against the embedding-space SMOTE-style retrieval (EmbSMOTE), a strong classical reference for imbalanced classification. In this study, a controlled benchmark of 11 augmentation methods, spanning classical perturbation, embedding-space retrieval, and LLM-based generation, is newly constructed on seven public text classification datasets covering class counts $K=2$-$28$ and imbalance ratios of 1.1 to over 500, evaluated with five random seeds per cell using macro F1, Welch's $t$-tests, five distributional metrics, and an LLM-family sensitivity analysis based on Qwen3-8B. The experimental results reveal that all LLM-based methods are statistically equivalent or inferior to EmbSMOTE, with the performance gap widening monotonically as imbalance increases and reaching $Δ\text{F1}_\text{macro}\!\approx\!0.063$ on GoEmotions-28. Furthermore, it is observed that surface-level uniqueness has negligible correlation with downstream performance, whereas LLM-specific artifacts, such as text elongation and label-distribution uniformization, are negatively associated with classification accuracy. Compared with six LLM-based and four classical augmentation baselines, these results demonstrate that the effective variable is not surface-level diversity but class-conditional structural fidelity, namely the degree to which augmented samples preserve the class-conditioned geometry of the training distribution. Accordingly, retrieval-based oversampling should be adopted as the default for imbalanced multi-class classification, and a higher empirical bar should be required before LLM-based augmentation is deployed in practice.

</details>


### [19] [The "Knowledge-Behavior Gap" in Cultural Taboo Safety of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12341)
*Ying He,Sihang Jiang,Xingzhou Chen,Zhouhong Gu,Yiwei Gu,Minggui He,Shimin Tao,Hongxia Ma,Yanghua Xiao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces CulShield, a benchmark to evaluate how well large language models handle culturally taboo topics safely, especially when taboos are implicit and context-dependent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While cultural safety is critical for real-world deployment of LLMs, existing cultural benchmarks mainly test cultural knowledge or bias, not whether models can detect and respect culturally taboo content. Cultural taboos are often implicit, vary by country and context, and can be embedded in harmless-looking questions, making them hard to evaluate systematically. The authors aim to fill this evaluation gap to prevent culturally offensive or harmful model outputs.

Method: The authors construct CulShield, a benchmark covering 77 countries/territories and more than 2,020 identified cultural taboos. The benchmark is designed to assess both explicit knowledge of taboos (whether models can state or identify them) and implicit behavioral safety (whether models actually avoid violating these taboos in interaction, especially when they are hidden in natural language prompts). They then test several advanced LLMs, such as GPT-4o-mini and Gemini-2.5-pro, under varied linguistic contexts to see how model behavior changes.

Result: Experiments show a clear gap between what LLMs know about cultural taboos and how they behave: models that can correctly state taboos often still violate them during conversation. Additionally, the authors find that changes in linguistic context—such as wording or phrasing—can substantially influence whether the model responds in a culturally safe way.

Conclusion: CulShield demonstrates that current LLMs have significant shortcomings in cultural taboo safety, particularly in applying their explicit knowledge in real interactions and in handling subtle contextual variations. The benchmark provides a new tool for systematically measuring and improving LLMs’ cultural taboo awareness and behavior across a wide range of countries and taboos.

Abstract: Cultural taboo safety is essential for deploying large language models (LLMs), as culturally insensitive outputs may cause offense or even social harm. However, existing cultural benchmarks primarily assess cultural knowledge or values biases, while overlooking whether LLMs can recognize and respect cultural taboos, especially when taboos are implicitly hidden in seemingly harmless questions. Besides, cultural taboos are implicit, and context-dependent, thus poss unique challenges for reliable evaluation. To address these gaps, we introduce \textbf{CulShield}, the first public benchmark dedicated to evaluating and improving the cultural taboo safety of LLMs. CulShield spans 77 countries and territories, and includes over 2,020 taboos. It evaluates models along both explicit knowledge and implicit behaviors. Experiments on several advanced LLMs (e.g., GPT-4o-mini, Gemini-2.5-pro) reveal a clear ``knowledge-behavior gap'': models often fail to apply known taboos during interaction. We further show that variations in linguistic context can significantly affect LLMs' cultural taboo safety. Code and data is accessible here: https://github.com/hedyHe/CulShield.

</details>


### [20] [Are Large Language Models Reliable Reviewers? A Benchmark for Error Detection in Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12342)
*Ying He,Zhouhong Gu,Zhecheng Hu,Yubo Zhou,Hao Shen,Jiaqing Liang,Zhaoqian Dai,Shuguang Ma,Fei Yu,Yanghua Xiao,Zhixu Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build and release FinED-Bench, a benchmark to test how well LLMs detect errors in financial documents, and show current models struggle, especially on complex cases, though fine-tuning helps smaller models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial documents must be highly accurate for regulation, analysis, and corporate decisions, yet an important question is unanswered: can LLMs reliably detect errors in such documents? While prior work focuses on prediction and analytics tasks, automated error detection in realistic financial filings has not been systematically evaluated, leaving a gap for both research and practice.

Method: They construct FinED-Bench, a benchmark dataset with over 900 real 2025 financial documents across nine real-world financial scenarios and three levels of cognitive complexity. They make sure the documents are unseen by existing LLMs and design tasks that demand both financial domain knowledge and multi-step reasoning. They then evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs (e.g., GPT-4o, Qwen3-14B) on this error-detection task and additionally test how supervised fine-tuning affects performance, particularly for weaker models.

Result: All tested LLMs exhibit significant difficulty in detecting errors in financial documents, with performance degrading further as task complexity increases. However, applying supervised fine-tuning yields notable performance improvements for weaker LLMs, indicating that targeted training can partially close the gap on this specialized task.

Conclusion: FinED-Bench exposes an underexplored and challenging capability gap of current LLMs: robust financial error detection, particularly for complex cases. The benchmark provides a standardized way to measure progress, and the initial experiments suggest that while off-the-shelf models are not yet reliable for this purpose, supervised fine-tuning is a promising direction to enhance performance. The released data and code enable future research to develop and compare improved methods for financial error detection with LLMs.

Abstract: Ensuring the accuracy of financial documents is critical for economic analysis, regulatory compliance, and corporate decision-making. Several studies have shown that Large Language Models (LLMs) perform well in many financial tasks, such as stock price movements and financial analytics. However, a critical task remains unexplored: the ability of LLMs to identify errors in financial documents. In this paper, we introduce \textbf{FinED-Bench}, the first publicly \textbf{Bench}mark for \textbf{Fin}ancial \textbf{E}rror \textbf{D}etection across three levels of cognitive complexity.
