FinED-Bench covers nine real-world financial scenarios, and includes over 900 documents reported in 2025 that are unseen by existing language models. We detail the benchmark construction process and evaluate several advanced LLMs (e.g., GPT-4o, Qwen3-14B) on this tasks, which requires both financial domain knowledge and reasoning capabilities. Experimental results show that current LLMs still struggle with this task, especially in high-complexity cases. Besides, supervised fine-tuning can significantly improve the performance of weaker LLMs on this task. Our data and code are available at https://github.com/hedyHe/FinED-Bench.

</details>


### [21] [Large Language Models Pass the History Exam But Miss the <<History>>: A Polish High School Exit Exam Matura Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12343)
*Adrian Trzoss,Kacper Dudzic,Wiktor Werner,Marcin Moskalewicz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates leading LLMs on Polish high school history exams (Matura), finding they far exceed human performance but show unstable rankings and systematic weaknesses, and introduces a new benchmark aligned with the Polish curriculum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Students already use AI chatbots as historical knowledge sources, yet existing LLM benchmarks rarely test the kind of interpretative, curriculum-grounded historical reasoning required in real exams, especially in underrepresented languages and contexts like Polish. The authors aim to understand how well LLMs actually handle this kind of history reasoning and where they fail.

Method: The authors test eight state-of-the-art LLMs on three official Polish Matura history exams from 2023–2025, which include both short-answer questions and extended essays. They compare model scores to the distribution of human student scores, analyze performance across task types, source modalities, and geographical scope (Polish vs global history), and conduct qualitative error analysis to identify systematic failure patterns in model reasoning.

Result: All evaluated LLMs score far above the average human examinee on the Matura history exams. However, models exhibit different strengths and weaknesses, leading to unstable rankings across question type, source format, and historical scope. A consistent performance penalty appears on questions about Polish history compared to global history. Qualitative analysis identifies two main recurring failure modes: (1) source conflation, where models treat source texts as factual content to reason from instead of objects to be critically analyzed; and (2) temporal disorientation, where answers are placed in the wrong historical period or context.

Conclusion: Despite outperforming human students in aggregate scores, current LLMs have non-uniform, brittle competence in historical reasoning, with systematic weaknesses in handling Polish history and interpretative source analysis. The new benchmark, based on the Polish national curriculum and official Matura exams, exposes these limitations and provides a more realistic, culturally specific testbed for evaluating and improving LLMs’ historical reasoning abilities.

Abstract: AI chatbots are widely used by students as knowledge sources, yet LLM benchmarks rarely assess interpretative historical reasoning. We evaluate eight leading LLMs on the Polish high school exit exams (Matura) in history - three official papers from 2023-2025, comprising short-answer questions and extended essays - comparing model performance against the human examinee population. Every model dramatically outperforms human examinees, yet aggregate scores mask distinct competency profiles: rankings are unstable across task type, source modality, and geographical scope, with a consistent penalty on Polish versus Global history content. Qualitative error analysis reveals two recurring failure modes - source conflation, in which models reason from source content rather than treating it as an object of analysis, and temporal disorientation, in which responses are historically misplaced. This study introduces the first LLM history benchmark grounded in Polish national curriculum.

</details>


### [22] [Predicting consumer-technology ownership without a diffusion history](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12344)
*Irina Vartanova,Niels Selling,Jennifer Viberg Johansson,Pontus Strimling*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether perceived attributes of consumer technologies, as rated by humans and language models, can predict how widely those technologies are owned, and finds that attribute-based models—especially using LM ratings—improve prediction over a simple age-based baseline for cross-sectional ownership, but not for short-term changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and forecast the adoption and ownership prevalence of consumer technologies, researchers often rely on technology acceptance models like UTAUT2. However, collecting large-scale human perception data is costly, and it is unclear whether modern language models can substitute for or augment human ratings in predicting real-world adoption levels. The paper aims to test whether perceived attributes of technologies, as judged by both humans and advanced LMs, meaningfully predict ownership prevalence and whether LMs can be used as a scalable surrogate for human judgments.

Method: The authors run a 2022 survey on Prolific with 678 US adults, asking them to rate 65 consumer technologies along six perceived attributes, including four drawn from the UTAUT2 acceptance framework. They obtain parallel attribute ratings for the same 65 technologies from two frontier language models, Anthropic Claude Opus 4.7 and OpenAI GPT-5.5. They then estimate sign-constrained penalized regressions of ownership prevalence on four UTAUT2 attributes plus a log-age covariate (years since launch). Model performance is assessed using leave-one-technology-out prediction. They compare attribute-based models with a baseline model using only years since launch, and also test short-term predictive performance from 2022 to 2025 against a no-change baseline. Finally, they demonstrate a deployment-style use case by predicting 2027 ownership for eleven products launched in 2025–2026.

Result: For cross-sectional ownership levels in 2022, models that incorporate perceived attributes significantly outperform a baseline model that only uses years-since-launch. With human-provided attributes, the mean absolute error in predicting ownership prevalence falls by 17% relative to the age-only baseline. When using LM-provided attribute ratings, the improvement is even larger, with the best performance achieved using Anthropic Claude Opus 4.7. However, when predicting ownership changes over the short 2022–2025 period—during which ownership levels for many technologies changed little—attribute-based models do not beat a simple no-change baseline. The authors also generate illustrative forecasts of 2027 ownership for eleven new products launched in 2025–2026, based on their attribute ratings, but these are not yet validated.

Conclusion: Perceived attributes of consumer technologies, captured via UTAUT2-style ratings, meaningfully predict cross-sectional ownership prevalence beyond what can be explained by age alone. Advanced language models can provide attribute ratings that are at least as predictive as human ratings in this context, suggesting a scalable tool for forecasting technology adoption. However, the approach has limitations: it does not improve short-run change predictions over a no-change baseline in a period of relatively stable ownership, and LM-based ratings may encode prior knowledge about specific technologies rather than independent attribute reasoning. The method is promising for long-run or cross-sectional adoption forecasting and deployment-style exercises, but requires caution in interpretation and further validation over longer horizons and with genuinely novel technologies.

Abstract: We test whether the perceived attributes of a consumer technology predict how widely it is owned. In a 2022 Prolific survey of US adults (n = 678), respondents rated 65 consumer technologies on six attributes. We then elicited the same ratings from two frontier language models, Anthropic Claude Opus 4.7 and OpenAI GPT-5.5. We regress ownership prevalence on four UTAUT2 acceptance attributes plus a log-age covariate with a sign-constrained penalized regression and evaluate it by holding out one technology at a time. The attribute model improves on a baseline of years-since-launch: mean absolute error falls by 17% with the human ratings, and by more with either model, most with Opus 4.7. Over the short 2022-to-2025 window, where ownership moved little, the same attributes do not improve on a no-change baseline. We set out the limitations of the approach, including the possibility that language-model ratings reflect prior knowledge of these technologies rather than independent attribute reasoning. We include a deployment illustration: 2027 ownership predictions for eleven products launched in 2025 and 2026.

</details>


### [23] [New Terms, New Toxicity: Consensus-based Chinese Neologism Toxicity Detection via Search-Augmented LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12361)
*Shiyao Cui,QingLin Zhang,Di Wang,Yida Lu,Zhexin Zhang,Jinhua Gao,Jinglin Yang,Min He,Han Qiu,Minlie Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how to detect toxic meaning hidden in seemingly benign new words (neologisms) by combining a taxonomy, a lexicon of toxic neologisms, and a search-augmented LLM framework called SeTox.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing toxicity detection systems struggle with new or repurposed terms that look harmless on the surface but are understood by the public as toxic (e.g., coded slurs or rebranded insults). These neologisms change quickly and are underexplored, yet they are widely used to spread hate or harassment while evading moderation.

Method: 1) Define a taxonomy describing how toxic neologisms arise and how their toxic usage is validated by public consensus. 2) Build a lexicon of toxic neologisms across multiple risk categories, based on that taxonomy. 3) Propose SeTox, a search-augmented framework that lets otherwise static LLMs query the web in real time, retrieve current contextual information about a neologism, and then decide whether its usage is toxic based on retrieved evidence and consensus signals.

Result: Experiments comparing SeTox (with relatively small 3B-parameter LLMs) against recent larger models show that SeTox achieves better performance on toxic neologism detection, demonstrating that adding real-time search and consensus-grounded context is more effective for this task than simply scaling model size.

Conclusion: Toxic neologisms represent a distinct, challenging threat for content moderation because they are implicit, dynamic, and consensus-based. By providing a taxonomy, a curated lexicon, and the SeTox search-augmented detection framework, the paper shows that integrating external, up-to-date web knowledge enables smaller LLMs to reliably identify toxic neologisms, offering a scalable and practical solution for moderating emerging toxic language.

Abstract: Neologisms, emerging terms in meaning or form, can serve as new vehicles for toxic expression, like "country girl" as a stigmatizing label targeting feminism. Such toxic neologisms appear benign but have evolved into toxic usage in public consensus, posing challenges to moderation systems and remaining underexplored. In this paper, we investigate how to detect implicit toxicity expressed via neologisms. We first propose a taxonomy that captures the origins and consensus-verification criteria of toxic neologisms, followed by the construction of a lexicon spanning widely observed risk categories. To capture toxicity grounded in public consensus, we introduce SeTox, a search-augmented framework that enables static large language models (LLMs) to incorporate real-time web context for neologism toxicity detection. Experiments show that SeTox, even with 3B-scale models, outperforms recent large-scale models, demonstrating its scalability to incorporate real-world knowledge for toxic neologism detection. Disclaimer: this paper has offensive contents that may be disturbing to some readers.

</details>


### [24] [Are you Talking Logic to Me? Assessing Language Models Syllogistic Reasoning Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12374)
*Hanna Abi Akl,Fabien Gandon,Catherine Faron,Pierre Monnin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how different formal knowledge representation notations affect language models’ ability to perform syllogistic reasoning, introduces a categorization method and prompts to improve small models, and releases a library for generating KR-formatted syllogisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models, especially small ones, perform poorly on logical reasoning tasks like syllogisms. Prior work suggests that the way knowledge is represented strongly influences reasoning performance, but there is little systematic analysis of how formal KR notations (e.g., logical forms beyond natural language) affect LM reasoning or how to exploit these notations to make small models both more accurate and efficient.

Method: The authors extend existing syllogistic reasoning datasets (FOLIO and P-FOLIO) by translating problems into multiple formal KR notations. They run experiments on small language models under supervised fine-tuning and zero-shot prompting, comparing performance across input notations against natural language baselines and measuring inference speed. They also design a syllogistic categorization scheme called SEF and use it to augment zero-shot prompts with logical definitions tailored to each category. Finally, they provide the Common Logic Grammar Construction (CLGC) Python framework to automatically generate syllogisms in KR notations and assign SEF categories.

Result: Experiments show that, for small language models, certain formal KR input notations achieve performance comparable to or better than natural-language formulations of syllogistic tasks, while enabling faster inference due to more compact or structured inputs. Enriching zero-shot prompts with SEF-based logical definitions further improves syllogistic reasoning accuracy in small models.

Conclusion: Formal knowledge representation choices significantly influence small language models’ syllogistic reasoning capabilities, and well-designed KR notations can match natural language performance while improving efficiency. Structured syllogistic categorization and definition-based prompting (SEF) provides an additional accuracy boost. The released CLGC library facilitates future work by enabling automatic generation of KR-encoded syllogisms and their categorizations.

Abstract: Language models (LMs) struggle with logical tasks like reasoning on syllogisms. It has been shown that Knowledge Representation (KR) plays a crucial role in expressing input information to help models solve tasks. This observation motivates our study of the impact of different formal KR notations on syllogistic reasoning by extending the FOLIO and P-FOLIO datasets. Our experiments on Small Language Models (SLMs) in Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Zero-Shot (ZS) settings show that the choice of input notation can yield performances competitive with natural language while enabling faster inference. We also propose a syllogistic categorization method (SEF) and use it to enrich ZS prompts with logical definitions, which boost reasoning in small models. We open-source our framework, Common Logic Grammar Construction (CLGC), as the first Python library for automatically generating syllogisms in KR notations and defining their SEF categories.

</details>


### [25] [Query Timing Produces Opposite Positional Biases Between LLMs and Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12387)
*Jasin Cekinmez,Addison J. Wu,Thomas L. Griffiths*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies positional biases (primacy and recency) in large language models and compares their mechanisms to human judgment updating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although primacy and recency effects in LLMs are known, we do not yet understand *how* LLMs aggregate sequential evidence and whether this resembles human belief updating, which is known to depend on when people update (incrementally vs. at the end).

Method: The authors systematically vary the order and timing of evidence presented to LLMs, analyze how models’ evaluations change with different positional arrangements, and compare across model generations and with human data on belief-updating timing.

Result: LLMs display primacy and recency biases whose strength depends on the position of evidence, but their pattern of dependence on updating timing diverges from human behavior; newer LLMs exhibit stronger positional biases than older ones.

Conclusion: LLMs’ evaluation mechanisms over sequences differ in important ways from human belief-updating processes, and architectural or training advances have not removed but instead amplified positional biases in newer models.

Abstract: Positional biases such as recency and primacy effects have been documented in large language models (LLMs), yet the underlying mechanism by which these models make their evaluations remains poorly understood. Both primacy and recency biases have been observed in human judgments in response to evidence, but recent work suggest that \emph{when} the listener updates their beliefs -- during the presentation of evidence or only at the end -- influences the presence of such effects. We investigate whether a similar phenomenon holds for LLMs, finding divergence from human behavior. These biases are more exacerbated in newer models compared to their predecessors.

</details>


### [26] [Unified Multi-Dimensional Benchmark for Complex Graph Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12391)
*Fali Wang,Ali Al-Lawati,Iliyas Bektas,Jinxuan Fang,Alek Melenski,Tianxiang Zhao,Yao Ma,Suhang Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a semi-automatic, LLM-driven framework to build complex, scalable graph reasoning benchmarks that stress-test LLMs in both text and code settings, revealing limitations that prior benchmarks miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph reasoning benchmarks for LLMs are narrow in complexity, often handcrafted, and do not systematically evaluate both text-based and code-based reasoning, limiting our understanding of LLMs’ true graph reasoning abilities, especially on large and structurally complex inputs.

Method: The authors design a five-stage semi-automatic benchmark-construction framework that uses LLMs to generate graph tasks, descriptions, data, reference solutions, graph-loading scripts, question formats, and evaluation scripts, while incorporating human checks at critical stages. The framework explicitly varies five complexity dimensions: graph size, task complexity, task description, graph loading modality, and task source. They then instantiate this framework to create a 202-task benchmark and evaluate multiple LLMs under text-only reasoning, code-based reasoning, and retrieval-augmented settings.

Result: The constructed benchmark exposes model weaknesses that are not evident on existing datasets: fine-tuned models generalize poorly to this new benchmark, and retrieval-augmented approaches only help in some scenarios, reliably improving text-based reasoning but showing inconsistent benefits for code-based reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed framework yields a challenging, diagnostic benchmark for graph reasoning that better highlights LLM limitations across multiple complexity dimensions. It also shows that current fine-tuning and retrieval-augmentation strategies are insufficiently robust, offering empirical guidance for designing more effective future enhancement methods for LLM graph reasoning.

Abstract: Graph reasoning provides a promising testbed for evaluating the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs), as graph instances can be programmatically generated, structurally controlled, and naturally scaled to long-input settings. However, existing graph reasoning benchmarks have limited coverage of data complexity, rely heavily on manual construction, and lack unified evaluation across text-based and code-based reasoning modes. To address these limitations, we propose {\dataset}, a five-stage \textit{semi-automatic} framework for constructing complex graph reasoning benchmarks. It expands benchmark coverage along five dimensions: \textit{Graph Size}, \textit{Task Complexity}, \textit{Task Description}, \textit{Graph Loading}, and \textit{Task Source}. The framework uses an LLM-based data generator to automatically produce task descriptions, graph data, reference solutions, graph-loading scripts, question forms, and evaluation scripts, while retaining human validation at key quality-control stages. Based on it, we construct a benchmark with $202$ tasks and evaluate LLMs under text-based, code-based, and augmented reasoning settings. Experiments show that the complexity dimensions reveal model limitations that are less visible in existing benchmarks; existing fine-tuned models struggle to generalize to GraphGym, whereas retrieval-augmented methods show scenario-dependent adaptability, improving textual reasoning but not consistently improving coding reasoning. These findings suggest that ours serves as a challenging and diagnostic benchmark for graph reasoning and provides empirical guidance for future enhancement methods. Code and dataset will be published soon.

</details>


### [27] [DIVE: Unlocking Self-Improvement in Frozen Language Models Through Diversity-Driven Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12486)
*Siheng Xiong,Ali Payani,Oguzhan Gungordu,Faramarz Fekri*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DIVE is a diversity-driven framework that lets frozen LLMs self-improve by evolving multiple natural-language "skills" from experience and verifier feedback, outperforming existing reasoning and prompt-optimization methods without changing model parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LLMs cannot accumulate and retain post-deployment experience unless their parameters are updated, which is costly, slow, and often impractical. Existing approaches to improve reasoning—such as fine-tuning, RL-based methods, prompt optimization, or external memories—either require parameter changes, teacher models, many rollouts, or are brittle and opaque. The authors want an interpretable, parameter-free way for LLMs to continually self-improve from experience while avoiding overfitting and suboptimal convergence during skill evolution.

Method: They propose DIVE, which treats reusable reasoning procedures as explicit natural-language “skills.” The LLM is kept frozen, and from task experience plus verifier feedback, it stochastically generates and revises these skills. To handle the non-convex, high-variance search over skills, DIVE maintains multiple independent skill populations created from bootstrapped experience. Each population is adaptively transformed (e.g., refined, diversified, or pruned) and then a complementary subset of skills is jointly selected for deployment. The same LLM executes the skills (by using them in prompting) and edits them during evolution; no teacher model is used. The framework is evaluated across several mathematical and logical reasoning tasks and with multiple model families.

Result: Across six math and logic benchmarks and different LLM families, DIVE consistently beats standard reasoning strategies, prompt-optimization methods, other skill-development frameworks, and memory baselines. It self-improves quickly from limited rollouts and achieves larger performance gains per sample than parameter-based fine-tuning methods like SFT and GRPO, as well as prompt optimization like GEPA. The learned skills transfer well across both model scales and families, allowing smaller models (e.g., GPT-5-nano) to match or surpass larger models (e.g., GPT-5) under ordinary prompting by reusing the evolved skill set.

Conclusion: DIVE demonstrates that evolving diverse populations of natural-language skills is an effective way for frozen LLMs to self-improve. By leveraging diversity-driven evolution and verifier feedback, the method offers fast, sample-efficient, and interpretable gains in reasoning ability without any parameter updates or teacher supervision. The successful transfer of skills across models suggests that such explicit skill libraries can serve as a powerful, model-agnostic layer for continual improvement in LLM systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) cannot retain post-deployment experience without parameter updates. We introduce DIVE, a diversity-driven framework that enables frozen LLMs to improve by evolving persistent natural-language skills from task experience and verifier feedback. These skills encode reusable reasoning procedures, verification strategies, common failure modes, and output constraints and are both executed and revised by the same underlying model without access to a teacher model. Since natural-language skill evolution is a stochastic, non-convex search process, optimizing a single skill trajectory can overfit to sampled experience or converge to a suboptimal solution. DIVE mitigates this optimization variance by independently evolving multiple skill populations from bootstrapped experience, adaptively refining them through diverse transformations, and jointly selecting a complementary set of skills. Across six mathematical and logical reasoning tasks and multiple model families, DIVE consistently outperforms existing reasoning methods, prompt-optimization approaches, skill-development frameworks, and memory-based baselines. It achieves rapid self-improvement from accumulated experience, obtaining substantially larger performance gains with fewer rollouts than parameter-based methods such as SFT and GRPO, and prompt optimization with GEPA. Further, the resulting skills transfer across model scales and families, enabling smaller models such as GPT-5-nano to match or outperform larger counterparts, i.e., GPT-5, under conventional prompting. These results establish diversity-driven skill evolution as an effective, interpretable, and parameter-free approach to LLM self-improvement.

</details>


### [28] [Intensional Anaphora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12598)
*Ezra Keshet,Steven Abney*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that pronoun reference in intensional contexts (with operators like 'might') cannot be fully explained by traditional value-based accounts and instead requires a description-based, presuppositional treatment, formalized in a new logical system (PIP).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semantic theories often model intensional operators (like 'might', 'believe', etc.) as quantifiers over possible worlds, analogously to how determiners quantify over individuals. However, data show that individuals introduced in intensional contexts behave differently from those introduced in simple quantificational contexts with respect to anaphora: pronouns frequently cannot pick them up as antecedents, contrary to expectations. Prior accounts try to solve this by requiring that pronouns refer only to entities that actually exist in the evaluation world, which rules out some intensional anaphora. But these accounts overgenerate and undergenerate: there are cases where anaphora is blocked even though the referent clearly exists, and cases where it is allowed despite possible non-existence. This mismatch motivates a more refined theory of intensional anaphora.

Method: The authors conduct a semantic and logical analysis of intensional anaphora, focusing on contrasts between sentences like 'Everyone is eating a cheeseburger' vs. 'Andrea might be eating a cheeseburger' and subsequent pronouns such as 'it' or 'they'. They critically evaluate value-based accounts from Stone (1999), Stone & Hardt (1999), and Brasoveanu (2010) that tie anaphora to the existence of referents in the world of evaluation. Based on empirical counterexamples, they propose a description-based account where pronouns presuppose the instantiation of their antecedent descriptions in each world of the context set. They then formalize this approach using a new logical system called Plural Intensional Presuppositional predicate calculus (PIP), which extends work by Keshet (2018) and Abney & Keshet (2022). PIP is designed so that every formula has a direct translation into classical first-order predicate calculus with set abstraction, ensuring a rigorous foundational backing.

Result: They derive a new semantics for intensional anaphora in which pronouns are not tied to specific individuals (values) but to descriptive conditions that must be satisfied across all candidate worlds. This yields correct predictions for when pronouns can or cannot refer back to NPs introduced under intensional operators, capturing data that value-based, existence-based accounts mishandle. The PIP system provides a formal implementation of this theory, demonstrating that the description-based, presuppositional treatment can be encoded in a logic that remains compatible with classical first-order predicate calculus via a straightforward translation. The result is a more empirically adequate and formally well-founded theory of intensional anaphora and its constraints.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that intensional anaphora cannot be adequately modeled by simply requiring that pronoun referents exist in the actual world or world of evaluation. Instead, pronouns in intensional contexts are better understood as carrying presuppositions about the cross-world instantiation of their antecedent descriptions. Anaphora after sentences like 'Andrea might be eating a cheeseburger' is felicitous only if, in every world in the context set, there is a cheeseburger being eaten by Andrea. This description-based, presuppositional approach is formally captured in the PIP logic, which both refines the semantics of intensional operators and pronouns and aligns neatly with classical first-order logic, offering a robust framework for further work on intensionality and anaphora.

Abstract: Intensional operators are often treated as quantifiers over possible worlds, parallel to the treatment of determiners as quantifiers over individuals. Yet individuals introduced in intensional contexts cannot serve as antecedents to later pronouns as easily as those introduced in merely quantificational contexts. For instance, "Everyone is eating a cheeseburger" may be followed by "They are large", where "they" refers to the cheeseburgers being eaten. However, as Stone (1999) points out, the similar "Andrea might be eating a cheeseburger" does not support later anaphoric references such as "It is large" or "They are large". Stone (1999), Stone and Hardt (1999), and Brasoveanu (2010) address this by requiring a pronoun's value (its referents) to exist in the world of evaluation, ruling out anaphora from non-veridical intensional contexts. We show, however, both cases where such anaphora is disallowed even when the pronoun's referents clearly exist and cases where it is allowed even though they might not exist. We argue that intensional anaphora is best captured using a description-based rather than value-based account. A pronoun presupposes that its corresponding antecedent description is instantiated in each world of the context set. Thus, there must be a cheeseburger being eaten by Andrea in every candidate world for "It is large" to be felicitous after "Andrea might be eating a cheeseburger". We implement our proposal via a new logic, building on Keshet (2018) and Abney and Keshet (2022), called Plural Intensional Presuppositional predicate calculus (PIP). Each PIP formula translates directly into standard first-order predicate calculus with set abstraction, providing a classical foundation for this work.

</details>


### [29] [When Explanations Betray Backdoors: Black-Box Auditing for Language Model Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12623)
*Yang Liu,Ran Zou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new way to audit language model classifiers using their explanations, introducing a metric called Groundedness Drift that detects backdoor attacks more effectively than prior methods, plus an escalated test called Unsupported Groundedness for harder, adaptive attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language model classifiers are widely used for safety-critical tasks like moderation and routing, but they can harbor hidden backdoor triggers. Existing black-box auditing methods typically assume access to trigger-like data or focus only on labels, not on explanations. The authors want a method that works in realistic settings where auditors only have clean data and can query both the model’s label and its short rationale, and that can robustly detect multiple common backdoor attack families.

Method: The authors treat the model as a black-box that returns a label and an explanation (summary, rationale, or quoted evidence) for each input. They introduce Groundedness Drift, a score that measures how much the explanation remains grounded in the actual input text, and use this as a signal for detecting backdoor activations. They test this across two 7B model backbones, five datasets, and four non-adaptive OpenBackdoor-style attack families, comparing its AUROC and residual attack success rate (ASR) at a fixed clean false positive rate. For more stressful cases where explanations are manipulated to hide the attack (explanation camouflage), they propose Unsupported Groundedness, a multi-probe escalation that issues several queries or probes to check for inconsistencies and unsupported content in the explanations.

Result: Groundedness Drift consistently outperforms baseline detectors: it yields higher AUROC and lower residual target attack success rate (ASR) at a nominal 5% clean false-positive rate across all tested settings (two 7B backbones, five datasets, four backdoor attack families). Unsupported Groundedness further strengthens detection signals in cases where attackers try to camouflage the backdoor by manipulating explanations, although adaptive attackers can still partially evade detection.

Conclusion: Explanation-grounded auditing is a promising direction for black-box backdoor detection in language model classifiers. The proposed Groundedness Drift metric is simple yet effective at detecting several families of non-adaptive backdoor attacks under realistic auditing constraints, outperforming existing detectors. The more intensive Unsupported Groundedness strategy provides additional robustness for explanation-camouflage scenarios but does not fully solve adaptive attacks, leaving an open challenge for future work.

Abstract: Language model classifiers with explanations are used for moderation, routing, topic triage, and low-resource annotation. We study black-box auditing when the defender has only clean calibration data without trigger information but can ask the classifier for a label plus a short rationale or quoted evidence. We introduce Groundedness Drift, a lightweight score measuring whether the answer summary remains grounded in the input. Across two 7B backbones, five datasets, and four common non-adaptive OpenBackdoor-style attack families, Groundedness Drift achieves higher AUROC and lower residual target ASR than every compared detector in all cases at a nominal 5\% clean-FPR budget. We then evaluate Unsupported Groundedness, a multi-probe escalation for explanation-camouflage stress cases. Unsupported Groundedness improves signals but does not close the adaptive gap.

</details>


### [30] [LLMs Are Not Good Strategists, Yet Memory-Enhanced Agency Boosts Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12626)
*Yi Wu,Zhimin Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EpicStar is a framework that equips LLM-based agents with a learned memory-as-policy mechanism to sustain coherent long-horizon strategies, demonstrated in StarCraft II with better performance and far fewer tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with long-horizon strategic tasks because limited context and attention cause them to lose track of coherent long-term plans, leading to inconsistent subgoals and strategic drift. Existing methods often rely on simple retrieval or longer context windows, which do not fully address the need for stable, cross-episode strategic memory in dynamic environments. The paper aims to build an LLM agent that can execute robust, consistent strategies over thousands of steps in complex games like StarCraft II.

Method: The authors propose EpicStar, an architecture where an LLM agent learns to treat memory as part of its policy. The agent stores a bank of successful past episodes, which serve as high-level strategic heuristics, and maintains a separate working memory for short-term, step-wise environmental changes. At inference time, a dynamic gating mechanism chooses between (1) directly executing an action retrieved from similar past successful episodes, or (2) invoking fresh reasoning by contextually fusing retrieved episodes with the current working memory. This creates a structured cross-episode memory that the policy can use adaptively over long horizons.

Result: In StarCraft II experiments against a variety of opponent styles and difficulties, EpicStar substantially outperforms baseline LLM-agent methods, achieving higher win rates while using roughly an order of magnitude fewer tokens. Its performance advantage is stable across different difficulty levels and opponent strategies, indicating that the memory-as-policy approach yields more robust and efficient long-term strategic play.

Conclusion: The study concludes that structured cross-episode memory, where agents explicitly learn to use stored successful episodes as part of their policy, is crucial for enabling LLM agents to perform consistent, long-horizon strategic reasoning. EpicStar demonstrates that combining episodic memory with a learned gating mechanism and working memory can reduce token usage while improving strategic robustness in dynamic, autonomous environments such as StarCraft II.

Abstract: Strategic reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs) within long-horizon environments is often limited by inconsistent subgoals. In these settings, finite attention resources prevent the model from maintaining strategic coherence over thousands of steps. This limitation leads to strategic drift, where localized decisions fail to sustain a coherent trajectory across reasoning. To address this, we introduce EpicStar, a framework that enables agents to learn memory as policy to tackle long-horizon reasoning. Specifically, the agent maintains a bank of successful past episodes as a heuristic alongside a working memory to track short-term environmental changes. During inference, a dynamic gating mechanism determines whether to execute a retrieved action directly or to perform new reasoning through a contextual fusion of the retrieved episodes and current working memory. Utilizing StarCraft II as the testbed, we evaluated EpicStar against diverse opponent styles. It significantly outperforms baseline methods, achieving higher win rates while consuming an order of magnitude fewer tokens, and it maintains this advantage consistently across difficulty levels and opponent strategies. Our findings provide compelling evidence that structured cross-episode memory is essential for enabling LLM agents to perform robust, long-term strategic execution in dynamic, autonomous settings.

</details>


### [31] [Novels generated by language models show compressed formal variation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12630)
*Mehdy Sedaghat Payam,Justin Quinn*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares stylistic variation in AI-generated and human-written novels and finds that, across many books, AI outputs occupy a much narrower formal range than human fiction, even when single AI novels can look human-like.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is extensive discussion of detecting whether a single text is AI-generated, but little understanding of how much stylistic diversity large language models can sustain across many long outputs. The authors want to know whether repeated generations from LLMs can match the breadth of formal variation seen in human literary corpora, rather than just producing isolated human-like passages.

Method: The authors build six corpora: (1) 20 GPT-5.5 Thinking novels in 19th‑century British realist style, (2) 20 Qwen3‑14B novels in the same historical style, (3) 20 GPT‑5.5 novels in a contemporary “zero style,” (4) 20 Qwen3‑14B novels in zero style, (5) 205 19th‑century human British novels, and (6) 65 contemporary human zero‑style novels. At the document level, they compute formal metrics: MATTR-500 (lexical diversity), Shannon entropy, average sentence length, readability indices, punctuation rate, and measures of variability in sentence length. They then compare means, variances, and cross‑measure correlations across AI and human corpora to assess how wide a stylistic space each corpus occupies and how internal measures co-vary.

Result: AI-generated novels show clear compression of sentence structure: across different books, their sentence-level features vary much less than in human corpora. This compression also appears in readability scores, punctuation behavior, and within-novel sentence length variability. Lexical diversity metrics are also generally compressed, except for Qwen in zero style, whose MATTR is closer to human variation. While GPT and Qwen have distinct mean stylistic profiles, they do not exhibit a stable pattern of correlations among the different stylistic measures, unlike humans. These patterns reveal a restricted, over‑regularized formal range in AI outputs compared with human fiction.

Conclusion: The authors introduce the concepts of variance overclosure (LLM outputs spanning a limited formal range across novels) and correlational overclosure (lack of rich, stable cross-measure relationships seen in human writing). They conclude that even if one AI-generated novel can closely resemble human fiction, a collection of such novels occupies a much narrower and more rigid stylistic space than human corpora. This has implications for literary diversity, detection of AI writing at the corpus level, and our understanding of how LLMs represent style.

Abstract: While large language models can generate entire novels, there is little information about the level of formal variation in their output over many generations. Rather than asking whether individual passages can be identified as AI-generated, this study asks whether repeated AI generation can produce the same range of diversity which is found across human corpora. This paper contrasts six corpora based on generation source and target style: twenty novels generated using GPT-5.5 Thinking in a nineteenth-century British realist style, twenty novels generated using Qwen3-14B in a nineteenth-century British realist style, twenty novels generated using each of these models in a contemporary zero style, 205 nineteenth-century human-written British novels, and sixty-five contemporary human-written Zero-Style novels. At the document level, the research includes MATTR-500, Shannon entropy, average sentence length, readability, and punctuation rate measurements. The most robust and reliable result is compression of sentence structure. Repeated generations produce novels that vary far less from one another in sentence structure than human novels do. Compression is also present in the measures of readability, punctuation, and sentence length variability within novels. Lexical measures tend to be similarly compressed, with the exception of Qwen Zero-Style MATTR. Despite having distinct mean stylistic profiles, GPT and Qwen lack a stable pattern of cross-measure correlation. This article therefore distinguishes between variance overclosure, which represents a limited formal range between novels, and a more specific phenomenon of correlational overclosure. This means that an individual AI-generated novel may resemble human fiction stylistically, while a collection of AI-generated novels occupies a much narrower formal range.

</details>


### [32] [Excess Separability: Nuisance-Controlled Residual-Stream Probing for Benchmark Contamination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12652)
*Florian Braun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper critiques and refines a linear-probe-based method for detecting benchmark contamination in large language models, proposing a carefully controlled protocol that avoids high false positive rates and unreliable conclusions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for detecting benchmark contamination (n-gram overlap, likelihood-based membership inference, canaries) require impractical resources such as full training data, specially chosen statistics, or prior planning. A newer idea—using linear probes on internal activations to detect familiarity—seems attractive but naive implementations fail. There is a need for a reliable, statistically sound probing protocol that works under realistic constraints without producing many false alarms.

Method: The authors systematically evaluate and discard simpler probing setups, then propose a refined protocol: (1) train linear probes across model depths to distinguish a suspect set from a reference set that is twice as large; (2) compute a zero-sum contrast on the depth profile of probe accuracy, recentred on a level-matched placebo baseline that accounts for generic differences between item sets; (3) assess significance via a label-permutation null that refits probes, instead of item bootstrapping with a fixed probe; and (4) summarize contamination via the overall level of excess separability rather than the detailed shape of the depth profile. They analyze error rates and power under various design choices using simulated and empirical data.

Result: They find that naive or simpler choices cause severe statistical pathologies: false positive rates can range from 0.03 to 0.99 under a true null depending on the analyst’s control set; using a flat depth-profile baseline misbehaves in both directions—rejecting 72% of the time when decodability increases with depth and losing power when it decreases; item bootstrapping with a fixed probe yields false positive rates up to 0.09 compared to 0.02 with a proper permutation null; and using a half-sized baseline triples error. On real transformer models, baseline depth profiles are significantly non-flat (up to 29.1 accuracy points across depths on a temporal split), and this non-flatness strongly correlates (0.87 across six audits) with surface differences between item sets, showing the necessity of the placebo baseline. In practical audits on Pile subsets and a temporal split, all four well-matched Pile subsets show no contamination, and the method declines to give a verdict on the temporal split instead of issuing an overconfident claim.

Conclusion: A straightforward application of linear probes to detect benchmark contamination is unreliable and can be dominated by artefacts of dataset differences and model depth structure. A carefully designed protocol—using depth-profile contrasts relative to a placebo baseline, appropriate reference-set sizing, and permutation-based null tests—substantially stabilizes inference and avoids inflated false positives, although it still does not answer whether models truly encode a generic 'familiarity direction.' The method is better viewed as a cautious auditing tool whose reliability depends on exchangeability between suspect and reference sets, not as a simple, universally valid contamination detector.

Abstract: Benchmark contamination is diagnosed today with n-gram overlap, with likelihood-based membership inference, or with canary strings, and each needs something usually unavailable: the training corpus, a well-chosen test statistic, or foresight at dataset release. A recent alternative reads contamination off a linear probe on internal activations. We show that the natural way to do this does not work, and specify one that survives measurement.
  The protocol reports a zero-sum contrast on the depth profile of probe accuracy, recentred on a level-matched placebo baseline, tested against a label-permutation null, with the reference set twice the size of the suspect set. Each choice replaces a simpler alternative we measured and rejected. Reporting the level of excess separability rather than its shape makes the false positive rate track the size of the analyst's own control set, from 0.03 to 0.99 under a true null. Contrasting against a flat depth profile fails in both directions, rejecting a true null 0.72 of the time when surface decodability rises with depth and losing all power when it falls. An item bootstrap holds the fitted probe fixed and rejects up to 0.09 of the time where a permutation null that refits it holds 0.02. A half-size baseline triples the error rate.
  On real transformers, baseline depth profiles are measurably not flat, spanning up to 29.1 accuracy points on a temporal split, and their non-flatness tracks the surface difference between the item sets (correlation 0.87 over 6 audits), so the correction is largest exactly where it is needed. All 4 well-matched Pile arms return null, and the protocol refuses a verdict on the temporal split rather than reporting one. What this does not establish is whether transformers carry a familiarity direction at all: the only positive sits on the split where exchangeability fails. Implementation, tests and audits are released.

</details>


### [33] [ERSkill: Evolving for Skill-Guided Adaptive Memory Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12720)
*Haolong Chen,Liang Zhang,Zhuo Li,Lei Xue,Guanrxu Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ERSkill is a retrieval-centric, self-evolving framework that lets LLM agents dynamically learn and select specialized memory-retrieval skills, significantly boosting performance on long-term memory tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents use long-term memory with mostly fixed retrieval mechanisms, which struggle with heterogeneous queries that need different ways of constructing evidence. The authors aim to make retrieval itself adaptive and learnable so agents can better answer diverse, complex memory-based questions over time.

Method: ERSkill first structures interaction histories into a memory store. It models retrieval behaviors as executable "skills" built from basic retrieval primitives. A trained router selects the best skill for each incoming query at inference time to build tailored evidence. During training, both the skill set and the router co-evolve: an experience trie records explored retrieval paths, and a double-frontier mechanism separates experimental skill expansion from the stable skill set exposed to the router, ensuring safe, incremental deployment.

Result: On several agent memory benchmarks, ERSkill outperforms strong static and self-evolving baselines. Using Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Instruct, it improves the overall average of F1, BLEU-1, and LLM-judge metrics by 31.3%, and with GPT-5.4-nano by 28.1%, indicating robust gains across models and evaluation types.

Conclusion: Treating retrieval as an evolvable set of skills, routed per query and refined over time, leads to substantially better long-term memory use in LLM agents. ERSkill’s structured skills, co-evolution with a router, experience trie, and double-frontier design provide an effective and stable way to continually improve retrieval strategies for persistent agent interactions.

Abstract: While Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on long-term memory for persistent interactions, the retrieval mechanisms governing this memory are rarely treated as evolvable components. This static approach limits performance on heterogeneous memory queries, which often demand diverse evidence construction strategies. To address this, we introduce \textbf{ERSkill}, a retrieval-centric framework for self-evolving, skill-guided memory access. ERSkill compiles interaction histories into a structured memory store and represents retrieval behaviors as executable skills composed of fundamental primitives. At inference time, a trained router dynamically matches each query to the optimal skill to construct tailored evidence for answer generation. To enable continuous improvement, ERSkill co-evolves the skill set and the router during training. It employs an experience trie to efficiently record explored retrieval paths, alongside a double-frontier mechanism that safely decouples the expansion of new skill capabilities from stable, router-facing deployment. Experiments across multiple agent memory benchmarks demonstrate that ERSkill substantially outperforms strong non-evolving and self-evolving baselines. Notably, it improves the overall average across F1, BLEU-1, and LLM-judge scores by 31.3\% with Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Instruct and by 28.1\% with GPT-5.4-nano.

</details>


### [34] [PatientAct: Theory-Grounded Mental Health Client Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12750)
*Sahand Sabour,TszYam NG,Yaqian Chen,Guanqun Bi,Jialu Zhao,Minlie Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PatientAct is a framework for simulating psychotherapy clients with realistic resistance, memory gating, and clinically grounded profiles, improving over existing LLM-based client simulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based simulated therapy clients behave unrealistically: they are overly cooperative, disclose sensitive information too easily, quickly accept therapeutic interventions, and resolve complex issues within a single session. This limits their usefulness for training counselors, evaluating LLM therapists, and generating realistic clinical data. The authors aim to create a simulation framework that better reflects real clinical dynamics, especially resistance, gradual trust-building, and causal psychological structure.

Method: The authors design PatientAct, a client-simulation framework grounded in established clinical theories. They construct client profiles using the 5Ps clinical case formulation (presenting problem, predisposing, precipitating, perpetuating, and protective factors) to give each simulated client causal depth independent of any specific therapy school. In the simulator, they add a dynamic memory layer where pieces of information have different trust thresholds—more surface-level content (e.g., symptoms) is accessible early, while deeper material (e.g., formative memories) requires sustained alliance to be revealed. At each conversational turn, they explicitly model the client’s emotional reaction and behavioral tendencies before producing a text response. When the therapist probes gated content, the system generates resistance along multiple dimensions—how much the client says, what they say, and the style or tone of their response—rather than defaulting to compliance or a single resistance template. They then test PatientAct across 40 different clinical situations and compare it to baseline simulators.

Result: PatientAct produces a wide variety of client profiles judged to have high clinical plausibility. In evaluations on 40 clinical scenarios, it significantly outperforms baseline LLM-based client simulators, particularly in the richness and appropriateness of resistance behaviors and in overall behavioral realism.

Conclusion: Grounding LLM-based client simulators in structured clinical formulations and explicit models of trust, memory gating, and emotional-behavioral dynamics leads to more realistic and clinically plausible simulated clients. PatientAct offers a more faithful representation of real therapy interactions, especially client resistance and gradual disclosure, thereby improving tools for counselor training, LLM therapist evaluation, and synthetic clinical data generation. The framework and resources are made available to the community via the associated GitHub repository.

Abstract: LLM-based simulated clients are increasingly used to train novice counselors, evaluate LLM therapists, and generate synthetic data. However, current simulators produce overly cooperative clients that disclose too readily, accept therapeutic reframes without resistance, and resolve core issues within a single session. We trace these issues to profiles that lack causal depth and behavioral mechanisms that treat all content as equally accessible. We present PatientAct, a framework for client simulation grounded in established clinical theories. Our profiles integrate the 5Ps clinical case formulation, providing causal depth without tying the design to any single therapeutic modality. During simulation, profiles include a dynamic memory layer in which items carry trust thresholds (e.g., symptoms are available early, whereas formative memories require a sustained therapeutic alliance). At each turn, the client's emotional reaction and behavior are modeled before generating a response. If the therapist approaches gated content, PatientAct expresses resistance in terms of quantity, content, and style rather than defaulting to cooperation or a single resistance pattern. We evaluate our framework on 40 clinical situations and demonstrate that it generates diverse profiles with high clinical plausibility. Moreover, PatientAct significantly outperforms the baselines, yielding substantial gains in resistance quality and behavioral realism. Our code and data will be publicly available via github.com/Sahandfer/PatientHub.

</details>


### [35] [ReconSpan: Reconstruction-Guided Adaptive Latent Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12756)
*Lixing Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces ReconSpan, an adaptive latent tokenization method that compresses text into reconstructable chunks using a backward decoder and contextual prefix codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create shorter, more efficient latent sequences of text that still preserve important information, enabling adaptive tokenization that is finer-grained than fixed subword units yet more compact than the original sequence.

Method: They propose ReconSpan, which segments text into chunks such that each chunk can be reconstructed by a backward decoder from a single contextual prefix code. During training, an autoencoder learns to form chunks using a reconstruction-based criterion, and one prefix code per chunk is kept as the latent token. They explore different average chunk lengths, from about 6.5 to 12.2 tokens, and compare reconstruction quality with random chunk boundaries at the same average length.

Result: The trained autoencoder can adaptively produce chunks with controllable average lengths between 6.5 and 12.2 tokens. For a fixed average chunk length, ReconSpan’s reconstruction-guided chunk boundaries preserve more of the original text content than randomly assigned chunk boundaries. Human readers of the compressed latent sequences can reliably infer the topic of the original text but find it difficult to recover fine-grained details.

Conclusion: ReconSpan offers an effective adaptive latent tokenization scheme that compresses text into shorter sequences of latent tokens while better preserving semantic content than random segmentation. The representation reliably maintains topic-level information but sacrifices detailed, exact content, highlighting a trade-off between compression and fidelity.

Abstract: Adaptive latent tokenization maps a fine-grained input to a shorter sequence of continuous representations associated with input-dependent spans. We introduce ReconSpan, which divides text into chunks that a backward decoder can reconstruct from a single contextual prefix code and retains one such code as the latent token for each chunk. The reconstruction criterion is applied when chunks are formed, allowing one trained autoencoder to produce average chunk lengths from 6.5 to 12.2. At matched average length, reconstruction-guided boundaries preserve more text than random boundaries. Readers of the resulting latent sequence recover topic information reliably but struggle to extract exact details.

</details>


### [36] [ViTOED: A Dataset for Target-Oriented Emotion Detection on Vietnamese Social Media Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12776)
*Chanh Vo,Son T. Luu,Ngan Luu-Thuy Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces ViTOED, a Vietnamese dataset and baseline models for target-oriented emotion detection, exposing language-specific challenges and leaving room for model improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of high-quality, fine-grained resources for emotion detection targeting specific entities in Vietnamese social media. Existing datasets often focus on sentiment rather than emotion, or are not tailored to Vietnamese linguistic characteristics, limiting research and model development in this area.

Method: The authors construct ViTOED with 10,985 Vietnamese social media comments and 21,244 manually annotated opinion quadruples (source, target, expression, polarity), following strict annotation guidelines that capture Vietnamese-specific phenomena such as implicit sources/targets and ambiguous vocabulary. They then design a baseline approach using structured sentiment graphs and systematically evaluate several Vietnamese pre-trained language models on tasks including span detection and relation extraction.

Result: The resulting ViTOED dataset exposes challenging aspects of Vietnamese target-oriented emotion detection, especially in accurately detecting spans and extracting relations among opinion elements. Experimental evaluations show that current Vietnamese pre-trained language models perform suboptimally on these tasks, evidencing high difficulty and significant performance gaps.

Conclusion: ViTOED provides a new, challenging benchmark for Vietnamese target-oriented emotion detection, reflecting real-world linguistic complexities. The observed performance limitations of existing models underscore the need for more advanced architectures and methods to better handle span detection, relation extraction, and Vietnamese-specific phenomena in emotion analysis.

Abstract: This paper introduces ViTOED, a novel dataset for target-oriented emotion detection in Vietnamese social media texts. The ViTOED comprises 10,985 user comments and 21,244 manually annotated opinion quadruples (source, target, expression, polarity) that follow strict guidelines. The dataset reveals Vietnamese-specific phenomena, such as implicit sources and targets and vocabulary ambiguities, enabling deeper analysis of user emotions toward entities. We propose a baseline using structured sentiment graphs and evaluate various Vietnamese pre-trained language models. The empirical results highlight challenges in span detection and relation extraction and indicate substantial room for model improvement in Vietnamese Target-Oriented Emotion Detection tasks.

</details>


### [37] [CRAFT: LLM-Based Iterative Refinement for Temporal Reasoning over Clinical Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12779)
*Chengyang He,Tahreem Arif,Marko Zivkovic,Lijing Wang,Yue Ning,Ping Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents CRAFT, an LLM-based generator–verifier framework to reconstruct stage-wise symptom timelines from temporally sparse clinical narratives, evaluated on a new MedTempo benchmark of vaccine adverse-event reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Temporal reasoning over clinical narratives is essential for monitoring disease progression, assessing safety, and determining causal relationships, but most notes lack explicit timestamps or anchors. Existing work mainly classifies pairwise temporal relations in multi-visit, timestamp-rich records and does not directly reconstruct coherent symptom trajectories from single, anchor-sparse narratives, leaving a key gap for many real-world clinical documents.

Method: The authors introduce CRAFT, a large language model framework that couples a timeline generator with a constraint-based verifier. The generator proposes stage-wise symptom timelines from free-text narratives, while the verifier enforces temporal and logical constraints, providing targeted feedback that guides iterative refinement of the generated timelines. They also construct MedTempo, a benchmark of 5,347 COVID-19 vaccine adverse-event narratives with expert-validated temporal stage annotations for a subset of 3,166 reports, and test CRAFT across four different LLM backbones with ablation studies to separate the effects of the generator and verifier components.

Result: On the MedTempo benchmark, CRAFT consistently improves temporal ordering accuracy compared with baseline LLM approaches across all four backbone models. Ablation experiments show how much each component (generator vs. constraint-based verifier) contributes to performance gains at different underlying model capability levels, indicating that the verifier especially enhances weaker base models while still benefiting stronger ones.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that pairing an LLM generator with a constraint-based verifier is an effective strategy for reconstructing structured symptom timelines from temporally sparse clinical narratives. CRAFT advances temporal reasoning beyond pairwise relation classification toward full trajectory reconstruction, supported by the new MedTempo dataset. This framework can improve disease monitoring and safety surveillance in settings where explicit temporal anchors are limited, and the analysis suggests it is broadly applicable across different LLM backbones.

Abstract: Understanding the temporal progression of symptoms in clinical narratives is critical for disease monitoring, safety surveillance, and causality assessment. Clinical narratives, however, rarely provide explicit temporal anchors. Current approaches to temporal information reasoning focus predominantly on pairwise relation classification across multi-visit and timestamp-rich records, leaving the reconstruction of structured symptom trajectories from individual anchor-sparse reports largely unaddressed. We propose CRAFT, an LLM framework that pairs a generator with a constraint-based verifier to iteratively produce and refine stage-wise symptom timelines through targeted feedback. We conduct evaluation on MedTempo, a new benchmark of 5,347 vaccine adverse-event narratives spanning three COVID-19 vaccine types, with expert-validated temporal stage annotations for 3,166 reports. Experiments across four LLM backbones demonstrate that CRAFT consistently improves temporal ordering accuracy, with ablation analysis isolating the contribution of generator and verifier components across model capability levels.

</details>


### [38] [FastThaiG2P: Lightning-fast Thai Grapheme-to-phoneme Conversion for Voice Agent Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12814)
*Charin Polpanumas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FastThaiG2P is an ultra-fast Thai grapheme-to-phoneme converter optimized for TTS pipelines, providing sub-millisecond latency and enabling efficient training and inference of Thai speech models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for highly efficient and accurate Thai grapheme-to-phoneme (G2P) conversion to support modern text-to-speech (TTS) systems, especially for real-time or resource-constrained applications. Existing Thai G2P tools may be slower, less standardized, or not tightly integrated with contemporary TTS conventions like IPA and Kokoro-TTS, limiting practical deployment and experimentation.

Method: The paper introduces FastThaiG2P, which uses PyThaiNLP-based tokenization, an extensible pronunciation dictionary, and rule-based text normalization tailored for common Central Thai speech. It supports phoneme outputs in IPA and Kokoro-TTS formats. The authors benchmark latency on a large synthetic utterance set, profile time spent on tokenization, normalization, and out-of-vocabulary (OOV) fallback handling, and then integrate the G2P into a TTS training pipeline (Som-TTS dataset + StyleTTS 2 model).

Result: FastThaiG2P achieves an average of 0.15 ms per utterance on 27,242 synthetic utterances, with processing time distributed as 30% tokenization, 12% normalization, and 58% OOV fallback, despite a low OOV rate of 0.5%. When used to phonemize the 20-hour Som-TTS dataset and train an 82M-parameter StyleTTS 2 model (Kokoro-TTS recipe), the resulting TTS system can generate intelligible Thai speech at about 0.25 real-time factor using ONNX inference on CPU.

Conclusion: FastThaiG2P is a practical, extremely low-latency Thai G2P solution that integrates smoothly into modern TTS pipelines, supporting both IPA and Kokoro-TTS phoneme conventions. Its performance enables real-time or faster-than-real-time Thai TTS on CPUs, making it useful for prototyping and development and showing that lightweight, rule-based-plus-dictionary G2P can be effective for Thai in contemporary TTS systems.

Abstract: FastThaiG2P provides sub-millisecond Thai grapheme-to-phoneme conversion for text-to-speech pipelines (International Phonetic Alphabet and Kokoro-TTS conventions) using a PyThaiNLP-tokenized, extensible dictionary and normalization rules for common Central Thai speech. The approach achieves an average latency of 0.15 ms per utterance on a benchmark of 27,242 synthetically generated utterances, of which 30\% is spent on tokenization, 12\% on normalization, and 58\% on out-of-vocabulary fallbacks (0.5\% OOV rate). To demonstrate its effectiveness, we used FastThaiG2P to phonemize Som-TTS, an open dataset containing 20 hours of grapheme-and-audio pairs, then trained an 82M-parameter StyleTTS 2 model based on a Kokoro-TTS recipe. The resulting model vocalizes intelligible Thai speech suitable for prototyping and development at 0.25 real-time factor (4x real-time) with ONNX inference on CPU.

</details>


### [39] [From Atomic Evidence to Logical Composition: Structured Compositional Reasoning over Compound Answer Options](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12836)
*Obed Junias,Maria Leonor Pacheco*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a framework that lets language models handle multiple-choice answers with logical operators (AND, OR, NEITHER/NOR) by decomposing options into atomic pieces, scoring them separately, and recombining scores via an ILP, giving large F1 gains on logical QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often mis-handle answer choices that use explicit logic (e.g., "A and B", "neither B nor C") even when they correctly understand each atomic statement. The authors want to diagnose and fix this compositional failure so models can better handle logically-structured multiple-choice questions, especially in commonsense and reading comprehension settings.

Method: 1) Decompose each compound answer option into atomic propositions.
2) Have the model independently score contrastive hypotheses for each atomic proposition, so it never directly processes the full compound option.
3) Calibrate these atomic scores.
4) Use an operator-constrained integer linear program (ILP) that encodes the semantics of AND, OR, and NEITHER/NOR to recombine atomic scores into a globally optimal choice among options.
They evaluate on LOGICAL-COMMONSENSEQA and a new benchmark LOGICAL-SATA derived from SATA-Bench.

Result: Their framework substantially improves multiple-choice logical reasoning: Macro-F1 jumps from 48.3 to 77.0 on the human-validated LOGICAL-COMMONSENSEQA split and from 47.0 to 75.6 on LOGICAL-SATA. The largest performance increases occur on options involving NEITHER/NOR operators, where models previously struggled most.

Conclusion: Explicitly decomposing compound logical options into atomic judgments and then recombining them with a constrained optimization layer can greatly enhance LLM performance on tasks requiring logical composition, especially for tricky operators like NEITHER/NOR. This suggests that part of LLMs' apparent logical weakness stems from how options are presented and aggregated, and that structured post-processing can recover much of their latent capability.

Abstract: Large language models often fail when answer options require combining atomic judgments under explicit logical operators, even when they judge the individual atoms correctly. We study compound options connected by AND, OR, and NEITHER/NOR, introducing a framework that decomposes each option into atomic answers and scores contrastive hypotheses about each one, so the model never sees a compound option. An operator-constrained integer linear program then composes the calibrated scores into a single prediction. We evaluate on LOGICAL-COMMONSENSEQA and introduce LOGICAL-SATA, a reading-comprehension benchmark derived from SATA-Bench. Our framework improves Macro-F1 from 48.3 to 77.0 on the human-validated LOGICAL-COMMONSENSEQA split and from 47.0 to 75.6 on LOGICAL-SATA, with the largest gains on NEITHER/NOR.

</details>


### [40] [AQuA: Recursively Self-Improving Quantitative Trading Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12841)
*Jiacheng Guo,Suozhi Huang,Yunlong Gao,Zihao Li,Jian Ge,Xu Kuang,Mengdi Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces AQuA, an autonomous, language-model-driven framework that uses recursive self-improvement to design and refine quantitative trading signals and models, achieving profitable, out-of-sample trading performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether recursive self-improvement—an AI system autonomously using feedback from its own experiments to improve future hypotheses—can be practically realized and yield meaningful gains in quantitative-investment research, while remaining safely sandboxed and constrained.

Method: They build two separate, sandboxed research loops powered by language models: (1) a symbolic factor-discovery system that uses a manager-mediated multi-agent pipeline to propose, test, and refine alpha factors; and (2) a trainable-model system that iteratively adjusts a hybrid time-series model via configuration changes. Each loop retains validated evidence and uses it to guide subsequent proposals, but they do not share agents, memories, or candidate spaces. Performance is evaluated via information coefficient and downstream trading strategy metrics on crypto and US equity universes with fixed data splits and evaluation protocols.

Result: The factor-discovery system discovers and combines factors that yield a signal with an information coefficient of about 0.190 on a cryptocurrency universe. The model-development system achieves a per-stock information coefficient of +0.0843 on US equities and, when turned into a threshold long/short strategy, attains a held-out Sharpe ratio of up to +2.50 (after two-leg transaction costs), with the strategy being profitable every year from 2021 to 2025.

Conclusion: Autonomous, language-model-driven research loops can implement bounded recursive self-improvement in quantitative finance: by retaining and leveraging experimental evidence, such systems can iteratively generate better factors and models that translate into robust, out-of-sample trading performance, all while operating within tightly controlled, sandboxed environments.

Abstract: We study recursive self-improvement at the level of quantitative-investment research: whether an autonomous system can use evidence from earlier experiments to improve the hypotheses and candidates proposed in later iterations. We present AQuA, which comprises two separate language-model-driven research systems: one for symbolic factor discovery and one for trainable model development. The two systems do not share agents, memories, candidate spaces, or research state. Instead, each independently closes its own research loop by retaining validated evidence and using it to guide subsequent proposals. In this bounded sense, both systems implement recursive self-improvement at the level of the research process. Each system also uses its own sealed sandbox, which fixes the data splits, feature and label definitions, and evaluator while allowing the model to act only through constrained factor expressions or configuration diffs. The factor system, a manager-mediated multi-agent pipeline, discovers and combines factors into a signal that reaches a combined information coefficient of about $0.190$ on a crypto universe. The model system, a config-driven loop over a hybrid time-series architecture, reaches a per-stock information coefficient of $+0.0843$ on US equities and converts it into a threshold long/short strategy with a held-out Sharpe of up to $+2.50$ at a two-leg cost. The strategy is positive in every year from 2021 to 2025.

</details>


### [41] [Falsehood and Impossibility Are Different Directions in an AI's Representation of Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12852)
*Yoon Pyo Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how a small multimodal LLM internally represents different kinds of falsehoods and impossibilities, finding that its internal activations distinguish necessary falsehoods from contingent falsehoods—even though its outputs often do not.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Philosophers distinguish between contingent falsehoods (could have been true but aren’t), necessary falsehoods (could not possibly be true), and semantic anomalies (meaningless or category mistakes). It is unknown whether contemporary LLMs internally track these distinctions or just treat all non-true statements alike. This paper aims to empirically test whether a particular open-weight model’s internal representations reflect these philosophical categories, beyond what is apparent from its verbal classifications.

Method: The author uses Gemma 3 4B IT, a small open-weight multimodal model, and constructs 85 prompts from 17 philosophical topic families with 15 topics. Each topic is instantiated in five ways: truth, contingent falsehood, improbable claim, semantic anomaly, and necessary falsehood. The model’s verbal labels (e.g., calling a statement a contradiction) are recorded, and its internal activations are analyzed layer by layer. Linear probes are trained to distinguish: (a) true vs impossible statements (truth probe), (b) necessary vs contingent falsehoods (impossibility probe), and (c) semantic anomalies. The geometry of these probe directions is examined (angles, overlaps), and sparse autoencoder features are learned at the key layer to see whether feature-level selectivity mirrors the probe geometry. Performance is evaluated with AUC, balanced accuracy, and statistical tests (Bonferroni-corrected p-values).

Result: Behaviorally, the model mostly conflates contingent falsehoods with contradictions, labeling most false statements (12/15) simply as “contradiction,” so its outputs don’t respect the philosophical distinctions. In contrast, its internal activations do: a linear truth probe cleanly separates impossible from true statements (AUC 0.93) but fails to separate impossible from (merely) false ones (AUC 0.20).
