An impossibility probe, tested on held-out topic families, cleanly separates necessary from contingent falsehoods with AUC 1.00, peaking at layer 15 with balanced accuracy 0.97 and Bonferroni-adjusted p = 0.018. Geometrically, the “truth” and “impossibility” directions are nearly orthogonal, while the impossibility direction partially overlaps but remains distinguishable from a semantic anomaly direction. Sparse autoencoder features at the key layer replicate this geometry: features tuned to impossibility activate strongly for impossible and anomalous sentences but rarely for contingent falsehoods.

Conclusion: Gemma 3 4B IT’s internal representation space clearly distinguishes necessary from contingent falsehoods and aligns necessary falsehoods more closely with semantic anomalies than with ordinary falsehoods. Thus, necessary falsehoods are not merely “extreme” falsehoods in this model’s activation geometry. However, these internal distinctions are not transparently reflected in its overt labels, which tend to lump many falsehood types together. The results are correlational, derived from a single small model, and do not entail that impossible statements are meaningless; they instead provide an empirically grounded nuance to classical philosophical distinctions about impossibility, contradiction, and semantic anomaly in the context of LLM representations.

Abstract: Language can describe states of affairs that are false and states of affairs that could not be the case at all. Whether an AI model internally distinguishes these failures remains unclear. I report an exploratory activation study of the multimodal open-weight model Gemma 3 4B IT using 85 prompts from 17 philosophical families and a topic-matched modality set of 15 topics, each expressed as a truth, contingent falsehood, improbable claim, semantic anomaly, and necessary falsehood. In its answers, the model conflates contingent falsehood with contradiction, labeling 12 of 15 false statements "contradiction." Its activations show a different pattern. A linear truth probe separates impossible from true statements (AUC 0.93) but not impossible from false statements (AUC 0.20). An impossibility probe evaluated on held-out topic families separates necessary from contingent falsehood at AUC 1.00, peaking at layer 15 with balanced accuracy 0.97 (Bonferroni-adjusted P=0.018). The truth and impossibility directions are close to orthogonal, whereas the impossibility direction partially overlaps a semantic anomaly direction while remaining distinguishable from it. Sparse autoencoder features at the same layer repeat this geometry. Features selective for impossibility also fire on anomalous sentences but rarely on contingent falsehoods. In this model's activation space, necessary falsehoods are not extreme cases of contingent falsehood but lie closer to the experimentally defined category of semantic anomaly. This representational proximity does not imply that impossible statements are intrinsically meaningless. These correlational observations from one small model offer an empirical footnote to an old philosophical distinction.

</details>


### [42] [The Embedder's Dilemma: LLMs Are Better, but at What Cost?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12875)
*Adnan El Assadi,Niklas Muennighoff,Jinhyuk Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares large language models (LLMs) used directly for semantic tasks against dedicated text-embedding models, finding they perform similarly overall but LLMs are far more expensive and slower.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs improve, practitioners wonder whether they can simply replace traditional embedding models for tasks like retrieval, classification, and clustering, but there is limited systematic, cost-aware evidence to guide this choice.

Method: The authors evaluate 10 LLMs from six model families and 26 embedding models ranging from 118M to 14B parameters on 37 benchmarks covering classification, semantic textual similarity, clustering, pair classification, and retrieval. They measure not only accuracy but also computational cost, latency, and the impact of reasoning tokens via controlled ablations of reasoning budgets.

Result: Overall performance between the best LLM and best embedding model is nearly tied (77.6 vs. 77.2). LLMs perform better on reasoning-heavy retrieval tasks, embedding models perform better on classification, and they are comparable on clustering, STS, and pair classification. However, LLMs can cost up to 1,431 times more per benchmark run and are 2.5–736 times slower on the same hardware. Reasoning tokens make up 28–81% of LLM inference costs, and reducing reasoning budgets often maintains or improves retrieval results. Only one LLM, Gemini 3.1 Pro, lies on the cost–quality Pareto frontier alongside the best embedding models.

Conclusion: LLMs do not universally dominate embedding models; rather, there is a clear division of labour. For most similarity, classification, and clustering workloads, specialized embedding models offer far better cost–performance trade-offs, while LLMs should be reserved for retrieval scenarios that genuinely require complex reasoning. The paper provides open-source code and data to help practitioners make informed architecture choices.

Abstract: Should you replace your text-embedding pipeline with a large language model? We answer this with a controlled, cost-aware comparison of ten LLMs across six families and 26 embedding models (118M to 14B parameters) on 37 tasks spanning classification, semantic textual similarity (STS), clustering, pair classification, and retrieval. In aggregate the two paradigms are effectively tied: the best LLM (Gemini 3.1 Pro, 77.6) and the best embedding model (77.2) differ by 0.4 points. Their strengths differ by task: LLMs lead on reasoning-heavy retrieval, embedding models lead on classification, and the two match on clustering, STS, and pair classification. Reaching that parity is expensive. An LLM costs up to 1,431x more than an embedding model of comparable quality (USD 154 vs. USD 0.11 per benchmark pass), and the open LLMs tested process tokens 2.5 to 736x more slowly on the same GPU. Reasoning tokens account for 28 to 81% of LLM inference cost; lower reasoning budgets preserve or improve retrieval quality for most models in our ablation. The Pareto frontier contains the leading embedding models and one LLM, Gemini 3.1 Pro. These results support a division of labour: use embedding models for similarity, classification, and clustering, and reserve LLMs for reasoning-intensive retrieval. Our code, datasets, and results are publicly available at https://github.com/embeddings-benchmark/embedders-dilemma.

</details>


### [43] [When Your Agent Opens the Chat App: Agent-Controlled Search over Raw Chat Logs Rivals Structured Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12888)
*Ruizhe Li,Licheng Zhang,Benfeng Xu,Mingxuan Du,Zheren Fu,Weidong Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ReFind, an agent-driven lexical search interface that operates over raw chat logs without building semantic memory structures, yet outperforms graph- and tree-based memory systems on conversational-memory benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent-memory systems rely heavily on constructing structured representations (summaries, graphs, trees, embeddings) of conversation histories to support retrieval, but it is unclear how much of their benefit comes from these structures versus from the retrieval process itself. The authors want to isolate and quantify the value of explicit structure by comparing it to a strong, controllable retrieval system that uses only the unmodified conversation archive.

Method: The authors propose ReFind, which keeps conversation histories unaltered and indexed only lexically at the level of conversation turns. An agent iteratively conducts keyword-based searches with four chat-oriented controls derived from empirical refinding research: (1) session-aware rank fusion to combine results across sessions, (2) local context expansion to bring in surrounding turns, (3) temporal narrowing to focus on specific time windows, and (4) skipping sessions already inspected. A separate reasoning stage then answers user queries based on the retrieved evidence. They evaluate ReFind on MemoryAgentBench (≈2,800 questions across single-/multi-hop QA, event ordering, and fact consolidation) and on LongMemEval-S/M, using matched LLM backbones to enable fair comparison to baselines including graph/tree memory systems and various BM25 and dense/hybrid retrieval controls.

Result: On MemoryAgentBench under an incremental multi-turn setting, ReFind achieves the highest mean accuracy (58.2), surpassing strong structured-memory baselines such as HippoRAG 2 (53.2), when all systems share a GPT-4o-mini backbone. Ablation and controlled comparisons to single-shot BM25, a generic agentic BM25 variant, component-removal versions of ReFind, and dense/hybrid retrieval agents show that agent control, the specific chat-native controls, and lexical retrieval each contribute significantly to performance. On LongMemEval-S/M with GPT-5-mini, ReFind attains 93.2 ± 3.3 and 89.3 ± 6.0 accuracy, respectively.

Conclusion: For precise, evidence-grounded querying over chat archives, most of the gains often attributed to sophisticated memory structures (graphs, trees, LLM-built indices) can instead be realized by empowering an agent with controllable, iterative lexical search over the raw conversation history. This suggests that expensive LLM-based index construction is not necessary to achieve high retrieval and reasoning performance in many conversational-memory tasks, and that well-designed agentic search interfaces over unstructured logs are a strong and competitive alternative.

Abstract: Agent-memory systems increasingly buy retrieval quality with structure, transforming raw conversation histories into summaries, embeddings, trees, or knowledge graphs before any question is asked. We ask how much of that benefit comes from the structure itself, rather than from competent retrieval over the raw history. We present ReFind, an agent-controlled search interface that builds no semantic structure at all: it leaves the conversation archive unmodified, indexes it lexically at turn granularity, and combines a generic iterative keyword-search loop with four chat-native controls grounded in empirical refinding work: session-aware rank fusion, local context expansion, temporal narrowing, and skipping already-inspected sessions. A separate reasoning stage answers from the collected evidence. Across a broad suite of conversational-memory tasks (single- and multi-hop QA, event ordering, and fact consolidation), roughly 2,800 questions on precise-retrieval and fact-tracking capabilities evaluated under the incremental multi-turn setting of MemoryAgentBench, ReFind attains the highest mean accuracy (58.2) of any system compared, above the strongest graph- and tree-based memory systems (HippoRAG 2, 53.2), all under a GPT-4o-mini backbone matched to every reused baseline. Controlled comparisons to single-shot BM25, a matched generic-agentic BM25 control, component removals, and agentic dense/hybrid variants separately support the roles of agent control, chat-native controls, and lexical retrieval. On LongMemEval-S/M, the same interface reaches 93.2 +/- 3.3 and 89.3 +/- 6.0 with GPT-5-mini. The results indicate that for precise, evidence-grounded questions over chat archives, much of the benefit credited to elaborate memory structures is recoverable by giving an agent controllable search over the unmodified record, with no LLM-based index construction at all.

</details>


### [44] [BavGround: A Benchmark for Regional Cultural Grounding and Dialect Competence in Bavarian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12894)
*Jophin John,Michael Hoffmann,Jan Fillies,Michael A. Hedderich,Barbara Plank*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BavGround is a new benchmark to test how well language models handle Bavarian regional culture and dialect across English, German, and Bavarian, showing that models struggle particularly with dialect and localized knowledge and that evaluation protocol choices strongly affect model rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM cultural evaluations focus on high-resource, standardized languages and overlook regional cultures and dialects like Bavarian. This leaves a gap in understanding how well models represent and reason about localized cultural knowledge and non-standard language varieties. The authors aim to fill this gap by creating a systematic way to test regional cultural grounding and dialect competence, and to investigate how different evaluation protocols influence conclusions about model performance.

Method: The authors build BavGround, a benchmark with 206 multiple-choice questions per language (English, German, Bavarian), across eight cultural domains, giving 618 parallel instances. Questions span general Bavarian-related cultural knowledge and more source-grounded regional knowledge derived from journalism, historical documents, and specialist literature. They then evaluate fifteen 7B–10B open-weight, instruction-tuned LLMs plus one closed reference model. Multiple evaluation protocols are compared: raw answer-letter scoring, shuffled-letter scoring, option-text likelihood, parsing of generated answers, and semantic matching. They also conduct an exploratory analysis of checkpoints from GENBA-10B during continued pretraining to inspect how answer likelihood and dialect competence evolve by domain.

Result: Multilingual models achieve the highest overall scores, yet all models show a marked drop in performance on items written in Bavarian and on questions requiring detailed, source-grounded regional knowledge. The choice of evaluation protocol substantially changes both absolute scores and the relative ranking of models, with particularly strong effects for regionally adapted models. In the GENBA-10B checkpoint study, continued pretraining improves answer-content likelihood in some cultural domains but fails to substantially enhance dialect handling; dialect competence remains relatively weak and uneven.

Conclusion: BavGround demonstrates that current LLMs, even strong multilingual ones, struggle with Bavarian dialect and localized regional cultural knowledge, highlighting underrepresentation of such varieties in training data and evaluation. It also shows that cultural evaluation outcomes are highly sensitive to the scoring protocol, making protocol design a crucial methodological choice. The benchmark offers a tool for localized, protocol-aware assessment of cultural representation and dialect competence in LLMs, and the GENBA-10B analysis suggests that naive continued pretraining is insufficient to robustly improve dialect skills across domains.

Abstract: Cultural evaluation of large language models (LLMs) often focuses on high-resource standard languages, leaving regional culture and dialect communities underrepresented. We introduce BavGround, a benchmark for evaluating Bavarian regional cultural grounding and dialect competence across English, German and Bavarian. BavGround contains 206 multiple-choice source questions across eight cultural domains per language, yielding 618 multi-parallel instances, with items covering both broadly accessible cultural knowledge and source-grounded regional knowledge from journalism, historical sources, and specialist literature. We evaluate fifteen 7B-10B open-weight instruction-tuned models and one closed-model reference. Strong multilingual models perform best overall, but performance drops on Bavarian items and source-grounded questions, indicating persistent difficulty with dialectal and localized cultural knowledge. We further show that conclusions depend strongly on evaluation protocol: raw answer-letter scoring, shuffled-letter scoring, option-text likelihood, generated-answer parsing, and semantic matching can produce different absolute scores and rankings, especially for regionally adapted models. Finally, an exploratory analysis of GENBA-10B checkpoints suggests that continued pretraining improves answer-content likelihood unevenly across domains, while dialect competence remains comparatively weak. BavGround supports localized, protocol-aware evaluation of cultural representation in LLMs.

</details>


### [45] [Prompts in the Wild: A Large Analyzed Collection of Transactional Prompts in Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12905)
*Victoria Basmov,Yoav Goldberg,Reut Tsarfaty*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper builds and analyzes a large, structured dataset of real-world LLM prompts embedded in software, showing their diversity across languages, domains, tasks, and modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although prompts are central to the behavior of LLMs, they are usually treated as ad-hoc text snippets rather than systematic, analyzable linguistic objects. There is little empirical, quantitative understanding of how prompts are actually written and used in real software projects. The authors aim to make prompts a first-class object of scientific study, enabling better understanding, comparison, and potentially design of prompts at scale.

Method: The authors mine GitHub repositories and collect 57.5K unique transactional prompts—reproducible natural language instructions that are programmatically integrated into software. They design a structured ontology describing prompt properties and their formal and semantic components. Using this ontology, they annotate and transform raw prompt texts into richly structured representations. They then quantitatively analyze these structured prompts to study usage patterns. Finally, they conduct a detailed error analysis across all ontology fields to assess annotation quality.

Result: The authors obtain a large corpus of 57.5K prompts from real software projects, each annotated according to their ontology. Their analysis reveals substantial diversity in prompt usage across programming languages, application domains, tasks, and modalities. The distribution of prompt types and patterns follows a Zipf-like long-tail behavior: a few patterns are very frequent, while many are rare and diverse. The error analysis quantifies the reliability and limits of the ontology-based annotations across all fields.

Conclusion: Prompts can and should be treated as structured linguistic objects suitable for empirical study. The proposed ontology, dataset, and interface enable systematic analysis of how prompts are constructed and used in practice, revealing diverse and long-tail usage patterns. The released resources provide a foundation for future research on prompt design, classification, and understanding in real-world LLM-integrated software systems.

Abstract: The behavior of contemporary generative Large Language Models (LLMs) is directly shaped by prompts, unstructured texts that describe the desired output and model behavior. In this paper we argue that prompts are linguistic objects that merit investigation in their own right. To this end, we collect 57.5K unique samples of prompts from GitHub. Specifically, we focus on transactional prompts: reproducible natural language instructions that are integrated into software. To enable the empirical, quantitative study of prompts, we introduce a structured ontology, capturing the properties of prompts as well as their formal and semantic components. Based on this ontology, we transform prompts from unstructured raw texts into richly structured linguistic objects. Analysis of these structured data reveals significant diversity of usage patterns across languages, domains, tasks, and modalities, in a typical Zipf-like distribution where some clearly prevail and others, more diverse, appear in the long tail. To validate the reliability of the ontology-based annotation of the prompts, we perform a comprehensive error analysis across all fields, providing a detailed assessment of annotation quality. We release the dataset together with a browsing and exploration interface (https://github.com/OnlpLab/transactionalPromptsCollection ).

</details>


### [46] [Decoupled Contrastive Decoding via Expert-Aligned Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12913)
*Zhixuan Liu,Zhichen Dong,Yuanfu Wang,Chao Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how to make Contrastive Decoding (CD) faster using speculative decoding, and finds that keeping the contrastive (amateur) model only in verification while using an expert-aligned drafter yields better, faster performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contrastive Decoding improves text generation quality but is computationally expensive because it requires passes through both an expert and an amateur model. Using speculative decoding can speed things up, but it raises a design question: should the contrastive signal influence the drafter model or be confined to the verification stage? Understanding this is important for making high-quality CD practical at scale.

Method: The authors focus on a lightweight, feature-level drafter regime and run two main controlled diagnostics: (1) matched Cross-alpha training, where they train drafters under comparable conditions with and without contrastive awareness; and (2) an Approximate Dual-Drafter decomposition to tease apart the roles of expert-aligned and contrastive-aware components. They compare systems where the drafter is contrastive-aware versus purely expert-aligned and analyze error, correction strength, and reconstruction effects. Based on these insights, they design Decoupled Contrastive Decoding (DCD), which uses an expert-aligned lightweight proposer (drafter) and keeps the amateur model only for standard CD-style verification.

Result: Both diagnostics show that contrastive-aware drafting usually does not outperform expert-aligned drafting: the contrastive correction tends to be weaker than the drafter’s own error, and reconstruction can exacerbate that error. In empirical evaluations on 8B-parameter model settings, DCD, built on EAGLE3-style speculative decoding, achieves 1.65–1.95× average greedy decoding speedups over vanilla CD. It also cuts MMLU proposal-path latency by roughly 5–12× compared to designs where the amateur model is coupled into the proposal path.

Conclusion: Contrastive signals are better confined to the verification stage rather than used to shape the drafter in speculative Contrastive Decoding. By decoupling the amateur model from drafting and using an expert-aligned lightweight proposer with unchanged CD verification, Decoupled Contrastive Decoding achieves substantial speedups while preserving the original CD output distribution, making high-quality CD more computationally efficient.

Abstract: Contrastive Decoding (CD) improves generation quality, but its amateur-model pass makes decoding expensive. Accelerating CD with speculative decoding raises a proposal-alignment question: should the contrastive signal shape the drafter, or should it remain only in verification? We study this question in the lightweight feature-level drafter regime. Two controlled diagnostics, matched Cross-alpha training and an Approximate Dual-Drafter decomposition, give the same diagnosis: contrastive-aware drafting does not consistently improve over expert-aligned drafting because the contrastive correction is usually weaker than drafter error, and reconstruction can amplify that error. We introduce Decoupled Contrastive Decoding (DCD), which drafts with an expert-aligned lightweight proposer and applies the amateur only in unchanged CD verification. Standard speculative verification preserves the vanilla-CD output distribution. Across the main 8B settings, EAGLE3-based DCD achieves average greedy speedups of 1.65 to 1.95x over vanilla CD and reduces MMLU proposal-path latency by about 5 to 12x relative to amateur-coupled proposal paths.

</details>


### [47] [Unifying Depth and Width Pruning for LLMs via Binary Knapsack Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12953)
*Palaash Goel,Ayan Sengupta,Akshay Nambi,Tanmoy Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SNIPER is a two-stage structured pruning framework for LLMs that formulates pruning as a knapsack optimization to get better budget adherence and performance retention than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing structured pruning methods for large language models often use greedy heuristics that make locally optimal but globally suboptimal choices, and they frequently fail to hit precise target compression ratios, causing either under- or over-pruning and degraded performance.

Method: SNIPER performs pruning in two stages: (1) a coarse-grained stage that formulates pruning as a knapsack optimization problem over larger components (e.g., blocks or groups of parameters), using fixed importance estimates to allocate pruning budgets in a conditionally optimal way; and (2) a fine-grained pruning stage that further prunes within the selected components to exactly satisfy strict global compression budget constraints. The paper also introduces a metric, CRAFT (Compression Ratio Adherence Factor), to quantify how closely the achieved compression matches the target ratio.

Result: SNIPER achieves near-exact adherence to specified compression ratios, with a reported CRAFT score of 0.98, while prior structured pruners can deviate from target ratios by up to 33%. On four diverse architectures and 18 tasks spanning five domains, SNIPER consistently improves average performance retention and task-level stability compared to six state-of-the-art pruning baselines and attains a strong mean rank of 1.25 across all pruning configurations.

Conclusion: By casting structured pruning as a knapsack optimization and adding a fine-grained adjustment stage, SNIPER delivers both accurate budget adherence and robust performance across models and tasks, outperforming existing heuristic-based pruners and demonstrating strong generalizability and reliability for compressing large language models.

Abstract: Structured pruning is a promising approach for compressing large language models (LLMs), yet existing methods rely heavily on greedy heuristics that produce myopic decisions, and often fail to precisely meet target compression budgets. We present SNIPER, a two-stage structured pruning framework that solves a knapsack optimization over coarse-granularity components to yield conditionally optimal parameter allocations with respect to fixed importance estimates, followed by a fine-grained pruning stage to meet strict budget constraints. We introduce the Compression Ratio Adherence Factor (CRAFT) to quantify budget fidelity, showing that while existing pruners deviate from target compression ratios by up to 33%, SNIPER achieves near-exact adherence with a CRAFT score of 0.98. Evaluations across four diverse architectures over a set of 18 tasks spanning five domains demonstrate SNIPER's consistent improvements in average performance retention and task-level stability over six state-of-the-art pruners. Across all pruning configurations, SNIPER achieves an excellent mean rank of 1.25, indicating its robust cross-architectural generalizability and excellent reliability.

</details>


### [48] [LycheeMemory V2: Efficient Long-Term Memory for LLM Agents via Semantic Segment-Level Consolidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12990)
*Dongfang Li,Zixuan Liu,Junmai Wang,Jiahe Huang,Fuhao Li,Bonian Jia,Baotian Hu,Min Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes LycheeMemory V2, a more efficient and accurate long-term memory framework for LLM-based agents that consolidates interactions at semantic segment level instead of per turn, achieving state-of-the-art performance with far fewer construction tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon LLM agents need to remember information over many interactions without incurring prohibitive token and computation costs. Existing memory systems typically perform eager, turn-level consolidation, requiring LLM calls after every interaction to extract and summarize memories. This becomes increasingly expensive as conversations grow and often forces coarse summarization or heavy query-time reasoning, both of which hurt efficiency or accuracy. There is a need for a memory mechanism that maintains fine-grained contextual evidence while significantly reducing cost.

Method: LycheeMemory V2 introduces semantic segment-level memory consolidation. Instead of invoking an LLM after each user-agent turn, the system batches multiple exchanges into segments determined by semantic boundary detection, aiming to align with coherent events and temporal units. Once a segment is finalized, it is encoded into context-independent, typed memory records (structured representations of salient information). These records are stored in lightweight structured indexes that support query-planned retrieval of relevant evidence. This design reduces the number of LLM calls for memory construction and maintains event-level coherence better than fixed-window batching, while keeping query-time context sizes stable.

Result: Using GPT-4.1-Mini as the backbone, LycheeMemory V2 achieves state-of-the-art results on long-term memory benchmarks, reaching 89.22% on LoCoMo and 92.20% on LongMemEval-S. Relative to A-Mem, it reduces construction token usage by 86.0% on LoCoMo and 75.9% on LongMemEval-S, without increasing query-time token consumption. This demonstrates that the framework provides both high accuracy and substantial efficiency gains in memory construction.

Conclusion: The study concludes that LycheeMemory V2 effectively improves the efficiency and accuracy of long-term memory for LLM agents by consolidating information at the semantic segment level rather than per interaction. The work highlights that the accuracy–cost trade-off in long-term agent memory is not solely about what information is stored but also about the granularity of consolidation. Semantic segment-level batching with structured, context-independent records and lightweight indexes can drastically cut construction costs while preserving or improving retrieval quality.

Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents must preserve information from past interactions to support future tasks. Existing memory systems typically rely on eager consolidation, invoking LLMs after each interaction to extract, summarize, or update memories. This design makes memory construction increasingly costly as conversations grow. Coarse summarization can reduce construction cost but risks discarding fine-grained contextual evidence, whereas larger retrieval contexts or multi-hop LLM reasoning shift the overhead to query time. We present LycheeMemory V2, an efficient long-term memory framework that replaces turn-level consolidation with semantic segment-level consolidation. Instead of consolidating every interaction, LycheeMemory batches multiple exchanges into segments and encodes each finalized segment into context-independent typed memory records. Segment-level batching lowers LLM encoding frequency, while semantic boundary detection helps preserve coherent event-level and temporal evidence compared with fixed-window batching. The resulting records are organized with lightweight structured indexes for query-planned evidence retrieval. Experiments using GPT-4.1-Mini show that LycheeMemory achieves state-of-the-art performance, reaching 89.22% on LoCoMo and 92.20% on LongMemEval-S. Compared with A-Mem, it reduces construction tokens by 86.0% on LoCoMo and 75.9% on LongMemEval-S without increasing query-time token usage. More broadly, our results suggest that the accuracy--cost trade-off of long-term agent memory depends not only on what information is retained, but also on the granularity at which it is consolidated.

</details>


### [49] [HybridRAG-BN: A Retrieval-Augmented Framework with Fine-Tuned Verification for Bangla KBQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13004)
*Rathijit Aich,Nirjhar Das,Mahfuzulhoq Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents HybridRAG-BN, a retrieval-augmented Bangla KBQA system that combines hybrid retrieval, large language models, and answer verification to achieve state-of-the-art performance in a competition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable knowledge-base question answering for low-resource languages like Bangla is difficult due to limited retrieval research, scarce language resources, and challenges in grounding generated answers in external knowledge. The authors aim to build a robust, accurate KBQA system specifically tailored for Bangla that can overcome these limitations and perform competitively.

Method: The framework, HybridRAG-BN, uses hybrid retrieval by combining BM25 (sparse retrieval) with BGE-M3 (dense retrieval) to fetch relevant knowledge. It employs the GGUF version of Gemma-4-31B-Instruct for answer generation, then applies a LoRA-fine-tuned Gemma-4-31B-Instruct model for answer verification and refinement. Additionally, a post-processing module handles difficult or unresolved cases using fallback answer replacement and extra retrieval via DuckDuckGo.

Result: On competition datasets, the system attains token-level F1 scores of 0.71654 on the public leaderboard and 0.72912 on the private leaderboard, ranking first overall.

Conclusion: Integrating hybrid retrieval, strong generation models, and a verification/refinement stage in a retrieval-augmented framework effectively improves Bangla KBQA performance, demonstrating that carefully designed RAG pipelines can overcome some limitations of low-resource languages and achieve state-of-the-art results.

Abstract: Knowledge-base question answering (KBQA) systems rely on effective retrieval and reasoning mechanisms to generate accurate answers from external knowledge sources. However, developing reliable KBQA systems for low-resource languages such as Bangla remains challenging due to limited retrieval-focused research, scarce language resources, and difficulties in grounding generated responses in external knowledge. In this work, we propose HybridRAG-BN, a retrieval-augmented framework for Bangla KBQA that integrates hybrid retrieval using BM25 and BGE-M3, answer generation using the GGUF version of Gemma-4-31B-Instruct, and a LoRA-fine-tuned Gemma-4-31B-Instruct model for answer verification and refinement. To further improve robustness, the framework incorporates a post-processing stage that addresses unresolved cases through fallback answer replacement and DuckDuckGo-assisted retrieval. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework, achieving token-level F1 scores of 0.71654 and 0.72912 on the public and private leaderboards, respectively, securing first place in the competition.

</details>


### [50] [EviReform: Evidence-Guided Query Reformulation for Multi-Hop Graph Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13006)
*Xinlong Xu,Yoshua Y. Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes EviReform, a graph-based multi-hop retrieval method that dynamically reformulates queries using already retrieved passages to better find complementary evidence, achieving state-of-the-art improvements on several QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard multi-hop retrieval struggles because later hops often depend on information uncovered in earlier hops, but retrieval signals are usually derived only from the original question. Even graph-based methods largely propagate this initial signal, forcing the system to rely on pre-defined corpus links instead of direct semantic cues from retrieved evidence. There is a need for a retrieval framework that can explicitly revise the information need once partial evidence is observed and then guide graph traversal toward remaining missing pieces of the reasoning chain.

Method: The authors propose EviReform, a retrieval framework that disentangles two processes: (1) revising the retrieval request and (2) aggregating evidence via a graph. First, passages are retrieved using the original question. These retrieved passages are then used to formulate residual queries that capture the unresolved information needs not satisfied by the already observed evidence. The system computes separate retrieval signals for the original query and the residual queries, normalizes them independently, and then combines them. These combined scores are propagated across a graph whose nodes represent propositions (passages or entities) and edges connect propositions sharing entities. This allows the retrieval to be guided both by the initial question and by dynamically updated signals informed by newly retrieved evidence.

Result: On three multi-hop QA benchmarks—2WikiMultiHopQA, HotpotQA, and MuSiQue—EviReform outperforms strong graph-retrieval baselines. It improves Recall@5 by up to 5.59 points and end-to-end QA F1 by up to 4.50 points, demonstrating better recovery of complete supporting evidence chains and improved answer quality.

Conclusion: EviReform shows that treating retrieved passages as sources for query reformulation—rather than only as nodes in a fixed graph—significantly improves multi-hop retrieval. By separating residual query construction from graph-based evidence aggregation and combining their signals, the method effectively directs search toward underspecified parts of the reasoning chain. This validates the idea that observed evidence can and should directly steer subsequent retrieval steps in multi-hop question answering systems.

Abstract: Multi-hop retrieval must recover passages that provide sufficient evidence together. An initial passage often resolves an entity or relation implicit in the question, making the missing evidence easier to describe only after retrieval begins. Graph retrieval improves access to related evidence through stored corpus structure, but its retrieval signal is commonly derived from the original question. Complementary evidence must then be reached through stored relations even when an observed passage provides a more direct semantic cue. We introduce EviReform, which separates revising the retrieval request from aggregating evidence in the graph. Retrieved source passages formulate residual queries for the unresolved information need. The original and residual retrieval signals are normalized separately, combined, and propagated between propositions that share entities. On 2WikiMultiHopQA, HotpotQA, and MuSiQue, EviReform exceeds the strongest baseline by up to 5.59 Recall@5 points and 4.50 F1 points. These results show that observed evidence can guide graph retrieval toward the part of a supporting chain left underspecified by the original question. Code is available at https://github.com/XrazyMee/EviReform.

</details>


### [51] [RAGSieve: Self-Referenced Local Contrast for Knowledge-Poison Detection in Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13010)
*Xinlong Xu,Yoshua Y. Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: RAGSieve is a self-referenced detection framework that identifies and mitigates poisoned documents in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems without relying on trusted corpora or poison labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrieval-augmented generation systems treat external corpora as evidence for answering queries. This makes them vulnerable to data poisoning attacks where adversaries inject malicious documents that push specific false claims. Existing defenses generally require trusted reference corpora, are tuned to specific attack traces, or depend on brittle global thresholds that can fail when the corpus structure changes. There is a need for a general, scalable, and self-contained detection method that can protect RAG systems both when building the corpus and at query time, without external references.

Method: The paper proposes RAGSieve, a self-referenced detection framework with two complementary components. (1) RAGSieve-Query (RSQ) is a query-time detector that operates locally per query. It looks at the retrieval results for a query and compares the top-5 candidate documents (those most likely to influence the answer) to documents ranked 6–20 in the same retrieval. By doing this query-local contrast, it scores phenomena like answer-anchor concentration (suspicious dominance of particular answer-supporting content) and carrier transitions (sudden shifts in which documents carry the answer signal). (2) RAGSieve-Graph (RSG) is a corpus-time detector that builds a graph over the corpus based on semantic similarity while ensuring lexical diversity. For each document, it compares the document and its local neighbors to a local baseline, looking for abnormally dense or coordinated clusters that indicate poisoning before any queries are asked. Both components are self-referenced: they build their detection signals solely from the structure and statistics of the RAG system’s own retrieval and corpus graph, without external labels or reference corpora.

Result: On three QA datasets and six different poisoning constructions, RAGSieve significantly outperforms prior defenses. RSQ achieves 95.2% AUROC and detects 82.2% of poisoned documents at the operating point where only 5% of clean documents are removed, compared to 81.1% AUROC and 52.5% poison detection for the baseline GMTP. RSG, the corpus-level component, achieves 93.3% AUROC and 79.8% poison detection at 5% clean removal, versus 79.4% AUROC and 37.6% poison detection for CleanBase. When RSQ and RSG are jointly deployed, the attack success rate is reduced from 67.4% to 14.0%, while retaining 41.3% F1 on unpoisoned retrieval, indicating strong robustness with moderate impact on normal system performance.

Conclusion: RAGSieve demonstrates that self-referenced, contrastive analysis of retrieval rankings and corpus similarity structure can effectively detect poisoned content in RAG systems without relying on trusted external corpora or poison labels. Its two components—RSQ for query-local, rank-based anomaly detection and RSG for corpus-local, graph-based density detection—provide complementary protection at both ingestion and query time. The reported AUROC, detection rates, and reduced attack success show that RAGSieve offers practical, deployable defenses against a wide range of poisoning strategies while preserving much of the system’s utility on clean data.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation treats an external corpus as inference evidence, allowing injected documents to promote attacker-chosen claims. Existing detectors depend on trusted references, specific attack artifacts, or global thresholds sensitive to corpus topology. We present RAGSieve, a self-referenced detection framework that constructs its reference from the inspected system. RAGSieve-Query (RSQ) performs query-local contrast, scoring top-five candidates against ranks 6-20 of the same retrieval to detect answer-anchor concentration and carrier transitions. RAGSieve-Graph (RSG) performs corpus-local contrast, comparing each document's semantically similar but lexically distinct neighbors with its local baseline to detect coordinated density before queries arrive. Across three QA datasets and six poisoning constructions, RSQ achieves 95.2% AUROC and detects 82.2% of poison at 5% clean-document removal, versus 81.1%/52.5% for GMTP. RSG achieves 93.3%/79.8%, versus 79.4%/37.6% for CleanBase. Joint deployment reduces attack success from 67.4% to 14.0% while retaining 41.3% F1 on unpoisoned retrieval, demonstrating practical protection at both corpus ingestion and query time without poison labels or trusted corpora. Source code is available at https://github.com/XrazyMee/RAGSieve.

</details>


### [52] [CASA: Content-Acoustic Speaking Assessment with Speech Encoder and Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13101)
*Nhan Phan,Ilona Lähteenmäki,Anna von Zansen,Olli-Pekka Pauna,Yaroslav Getman,Tamás Grósz,Mikko Kurimo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CASA is a simpler, multimodal ASA system (Whisper + Qwen3.5-2B) that achieves state-of-the-art scoring accuracy with fewer parameters and clearer separation between speech delivery and content, and includes analysis of modality contributions, stability, and LLM-based content validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic speaking assessment is increasingly using multimodal speech LLMs, but current work offers limited understanding of how acoustic vs. content information each influence scoring and how stable model performance is across runs. There is also a need for architectures that are both strong and interpretable while remaining lightweight and easily adaptable across corpora.

Method: The authors propose CASA, a two-stage architecture that combines Whisper-medium for acoustic processing with Qwen3.5-2B for language reasoning. The design explicitly separates speech delivery (acoustics, fluency) from content assessment and augments the model with three handcrafted fluency features. They train and evaluate CASA on the Speak & Improve Corpus 2025, then run ablation studies (removing or altering acoustic/content components and handcrafted features) and repeated runs to assess variability. They also explore using the LLM component for training-free content validation via its reasoning abilities.

Result: On the Speak & Improve Corpus 2025, CASA attains a root mean square error (RMSE) of 0.358, surpassing previously reported best results, while using about half the estimated inference parameters of competing systems. The ablation experiments show distinct and complementary contributions from acoustic and content information, and repeated-run experiments quantify performance variability. The LLM reasoning experiments demonstrate that CASA can perform content validation without additional supervised training.

Conclusion: CASA delivers state-of-the-art automatic speaking assessment with a simpler, more parameter-efficient architecture that cleanly separates delivery and content assessment. The findings indicate that both acoustic and content modalities are important and complementary, that the model’s performance is relatively stable, and that large language model reasoning can support training-free content validation. The architecture is general-purpose and can be adapted to other ASA datasets without structural changes, requiring only a small set of handcrafted fluency features.

Abstract: Research on automatic speaking assessment (ASA) has increasingly adopted multimodal speech large language models to assess learners' speaking performance. However, existing studies provide limited analysis of how acoustic and content information contribute to predictions and how stable the resulting performance is. We propose CASA, a simpler architecture combining Whisper-medium and Qwen3.5-2B that achieves state-of-the-art performance while providing a more interpretable separation between speech delivery and content.
  On the Speak & Improve Corpus 2025, CASA achieves a root mean square error (RMSE) of 0.358, improving on the previous best RMSE while using approximately half the estimated inference parameters. The general-purpose architecture is designed for adaptation to other ASA corpora without structural changes and relies on three handcrafted fluency features. Through ablations and repeated runs, we analyze the individual and complementary contributions of acoustic and content information, examine performance variability, and demonstrate the potential of large language model reasoning for training-free content validation.

</details>


### [53] [LigBench: A Unified and Human-Aligned Benchmark for LLM-based Research Idea Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13136)
*Chenrun Wang,Mingxuan Zhu,Tiancheng Huang,Wenjie Li,Yujie Zhang,Zichen Zhu,Zhiying Zou,Kai Yu,Lu Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper introduces LigBench, an automated benchmark and PAIR-IQ dataset for objective evaluation of AI-generated research ideas, improving alignment with expert judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Idea generation with LLMs is growing, but evaluation is fragmented, often uses direct LLM scoring, and lacks unified, objective, and reliable standards across different idea distributions.

Method: Propose LigBench, a fine-grained automated evaluation benchmark for AI research ideas, applicable across varying generation distributions; and create PAIR-IQ, a pairwise idea judgment dataset to train comparison models and serve as an auxiliary reference for more objective evaluations.

Result: Experiments show LigBench yields stable, interpretable scores that align well with expert evaluations, and models trained on PAIR-IQ achieve higher ranking accuracy and robustness in judging research ideas.

Conclusion: LigBench and PAIR-IQ together provide a principled, scalable, and more objective framework for evaluating AI-generated research ideas, improving consistency and expert-alignment over prior ad-hoc methods.

Abstract: With the rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs), research idea generation has attracted increasing attention. Existing approaches enable LLMs to retrieve relevant literature and propose novel ideas for research areas. However, current evaluation practices for idea generation remain fragmented and lack objective standards, often relying on direct LLM scoring, which limits their ability to provide unified and reliable assessments across a coherent distribution of generated ideas. To address this challenge, we propose LigBench, an automated evaluation benchmark that enables fine-grained and reliable evaluation of AI research ideas, consistently applicable across different generation distributions. In addition, we introduce PAIR-IQ, a dataset tailored for training pairwise idea judgment models and serving as an auxiliary reference to support more objective comparative evaluation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LigBench achieves stable and interpretable evaluations, significantly improving alignment with expert judgments. Furthermore, models trained on PAIR-IQ exhibit enhanced ranking accuracy and robustness, establishing a principled standard for scalable and objective research idea assessment.

</details>


### [54] [Better Decomposition, Free Aggregation: A Synthesizer-Folding Framework for Multilingual Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13160)
*Yilin Wang,Yuchun Fan,Weidong Bao,Zili Wei,Shi Feng,Tong Xiao,Zhengtao Yu,Jingbo Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Syfer is a multilingual RAG framework that performs controlled question decomposition in the original language, only falling back to translation and bilingual alignment when needed, to improve accuracy and reduce cost for multilingual multi-hop QA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual RAG systems either translate all retrieved documents or queries, losing language-specific information, adding translation noise, and increasing cost, or they greedily decompose complex questions, generating redundant sub-questions that propagate and amplify reasoning errors. There is a need for a method that both respects native-language information and controls decomposition quality while still enabling effective multi-hop reasoning across languages.

Method: Syfer introduces a synthesizer-folding framework. It first uses a format-constrained decomposer to generate a graph of sub-questions directly in the original (target) language. A decomposition-quality check is then applied. If the decomposition passes this check, the system answers sub-questions sequentially using a retrieve-then-answer policy entirely in the target language. If the check fails, Syfer activates an English translation pathway: the content is translated, bilingual alignment of the sub-question graph is performed, and reasoning continues in this fallback mode. Translation is thus deferred and conditional rather than default. The method is evaluated on multilingual multi-hop QA benchmarks across several languages.

Result: Across multiple languages, Syfer achieves accuracy that is competitive with or better than prior multilingual RAG baselines, while reducing unnecessary translation and associated computational cost. It maintains a good trade-off between performance and efficiency, demonstrating that conditional, quality-checked decomposition and deferred translation can match strong baselines without always operating in English.

Conclusion: Deferred and conditional translation, combined with controlled question decomposition in the original language, can mitigate translation noise, preserve culturally and linguistically specific information, and avoid error compounding in multi-hop reasoning. Syfer’s synthesizer-folding framework provides an effective and more cost-efficient approach for multilingual multi-hop QA than methods that rely on blanket translation or uncontrolled decomposition and aggregation.

Abstract: Multilingual retrieval-augmented generation (mRAG) equips large language models with access to globally distributed external knowledge for complex multilingual question answering. Recent approaches either translate retrieved documents into English or the query language to bridge the cross-lingual semantic gap, or decompose a complex query into sub-questions and aggregate the intermediate reasoning process. However, both lines of work suffer from two limitations. First, one-size-fits-all translation alignment, blanket translation discards culturally and linguistically native information unique to the target language, introduces translation noise, and inflates system cost. Second, greedy decomposition and aggregation, uncontrolled decomposition produces redundant sub-questions that compound errors during step-wise reasoning, and the final aggregation over reasoning paths further amplifies these errors. We address both with our method Syfer, a synthesizer-folding framework for multilingual multi-hop question answering that defers translation rather than applying it by default. Syfer first invokes a format-constrained decomposer to produce a sub-question graph in the original language, followed by a decomposition-quality check; when the check passes, sub-questions are answered sequentially under a retrieve-then-answer policy in the target language, and the English translation pathway with bilingual sub-question graph alignment is activated only when the check fails. Experiments across multiple languages show that Syfer attains competitive accuracy while striking a favourable balance between performance and computational cost.

</details>


### [55] [Which LLM Is Your Ideal Companion? Evaluating Emotional Companion Capabilities of LLMs Based on Adult Attachment Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13168)
*Junkai Zhou,Shiting Guan,Zhaoyi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a psychology-grounded way to evaluate large language models used for emotional companionship, focusing on their attachment styles and behavior in intimate-like interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM evaluations mostly measure general abilities or broad personality traits, but they say little about how models behave in emotionally sensitive, intimate relationship contexts where they are increasingly being deployed. There is a need for systematic, theory-based evaluation of LLMs as emotional companions, especially in how they handle closeness, dependence, and emotional support.

Method: The authors import adult attachment theory into LLM evaluation by adapting the Experiences in Close Relationships-Revised (ECR-R) psychological scale to measure two key attachment dimensions—anxiety and avoidance—in LLM outputs. They construct an Emotional Companionship Benchmark (ECBench) with realistic multi-turn scenarios in four domains (emotional support, collaborative tasks, conflict resolution, social guidance) and across both friendship and romantic relationship framings. They then evaluate 32 LLMs on ECBench using 11 dialogue-quality metrics and three different evaluation approaches, and further analyze selected models to see how attachment tendencies appear in contextualized conversations and how prompts can alter these tendencies.

Result: The study characterizes each of 32 LLMs in terms of attachment anxiety and avoidance using the adapted ECR-R scale, revealing distinct attachment-like profiles across models. On ECBench, the models show varied performance across scenarios and relationship types, and their attachment tendencies influence how they respond in emotionally charged, multi-turn dialogues. The authors observe that carefully designed prompts can shift or modulate a model’s apparent attachment style and its behavior as an emotional companion.

Conclusion: By integrating adult attachment theory and creating ECBench, the paper offers a new, psychologically grounded framework for evaluating LLMs as emotional companions. This framework helps reveal nuanced behavioral patterns in intimate-like interactions, supports more informed model selection and configuration for emotional companionship use cases, and shows that prompting can be used to steer attachment-related tendencies, improving alignment with desired relational behaviors.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied for emotional companionship, evaluating their behavior and capabilities in intimate relationships has become a pressing issue. However, existing assessments primarily characterize general personality traits, providing limited insight into model behavior within intimate and emotionally sensitive contexts. Therefore, we introduce adult attachment theory into LLM evaluation and use the Experiences in Close Relationships-Revised (ECR-R) scale to characterize attachment anxiety and avoidance. To evaluate emotional companionship capabilities of LLMs in realistic interaction scenarios, we present an emotional companionship benchmark, ECBench, spanning four scenarios including emotional support, collaborative tasks, conflict resolution, and social guidance, across friendship and romantic relationships. ECBench is utilized to assess model behavior using 11 dialogue-quality metrics and three evaluation methods. We evaluate the attachment tendencies of 32 LLMs and select representative models to investigate how these tendencies manifest in contextualized multi-turn interactions and whether they can be shaped through prompting. Our study provides a theoretical lens from psychology, along with practical tools to understand and select LLMs for emotional companionship.

</details>


### [56] [GEM: A Generative Embedding Model Bridging Reasoning and Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13200)
*Zhili Shen,Craig Macdonald*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces GEM, a generative embedding model that improves information retrieval by explicitly reasoning about user intent and relevance before producing embeddings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern large language models allow users to specify complex, reasoning-heavy, and instruction-style information needs, but existing retrieval models still rely mostly on shallow lexical or semantic matching. This mismatch creates a gap: retrievers fail to capture the rich intent and nuanced relevance criteria expressed in natural language queries. The authors aim to close this gap by making the retriever itself capable of reasoning about the query, instead of treating it as a static text string to embed.

Method: The authors propose GEM, a model that unifies text generation and embedding in a single architecture. Given a query, GEM first performs explicit reasoning to elaborate on user intent and relevance criteria. After this reasoning step, it appends a special embedding token whose representation is computed in the enriched context. The hidden state of this token is then used as the query embedding for retrieval. The model is trained so that this reasoning-augmented embedding is optimized for retrieval quality, and its generative interface supports flexible prompting and test-time compute scaling (e.g., longer or more detailed reasoning chains).

Result: On benchmarks that require substantial reasoning and instruction following for effective retrieval, GEM outperforms a non-reasoning version of itself and reaches performance comparable to baselines that rely on much larger models. It also benefits from increased test-time compute via prompting, further improving retrieval performance by allowing more extensive reasoning before embedding creation.

Conclusion: Reasoning directly within the retriever, rather than only in a separate LLM, significantly improves alignment between user intent and retrieval behavior. GEM shows that integrating generation and embedding—by reasoning first and then encoding an enriched context into an embedding token—yields more effective retrieval on complex queries, while enabling scalable improvements at test time through prompting-based compute scaling.

Abstract: Modern LLMs excel at reasoning and instruction following, enabling users to express complex and diverse information needs. However, conventional retrievers largely rely on surface-level matching between queries and documents, resulting in a growing gap between how users express their needs and how retrievers interpret them. In this paper, we present GEM, a generative embedding model that augments retrieval through its own knowledge by explicitly reasoning about user intent and relevance criteria. GEM unifies generation and embedding within a single model: it first reasons over the query, then appends an embedding token to encode the enriched context for retrieval. \zhili{Evaluated on reasoning-intensive and instruction-following retrieval tasks, GEM demonstrates the effectiveness of its reasoning-augmented retrieval, outperforming its non-reasoning variant and matching baselines using substantially larger models.} Furthermore, GEM's generative nature allows test-time compute scaling via prompting to further enhance retrieval performance. Our code is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/GEM.

</details>


### [57] [Localize, Then Reason: Visual Latent Structural Reasoning for Molecular Properties and Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13244)
*Xingqiao Lin,Junmei Wang,Haocheng Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Visual Latent Structural Reasoning (VLSR), an end-to-end framework that first localizes chemically meaningful regions in molecular images and then reasons over them in a latent space to predict properties, achieving much higher throughput than textual LLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based molecular reasoning methods either depend on SMILES plus explicit local motif descriptions or reason directly from whole molecular images, which prevents the model from explicitly focusing on chemically important substructures before reasoning. There is a need for a unified framework that can automatically discover and attend to local chemical regions while performing property reasoning efficiently.

Method: The authors propose VLSR, which implements a localize-then-reason pipeline on molecular images. The model first learns to identify and localize chemically meaningful regions (e.g., functional groups or substructures) in the image. These localized regions are then encoded into a compact latent workspace where the model performs property reasoning. The entire process—from localization to reasoning to final answer generation—is trained end-to-end on molecular images.

Result: Under the same inference conditions as a strong textual reasoning baseline, VLSR attains 9.6× higher throughput, implying much faster inference while maintaining competitive reasoning capabilities for molecular property prediction.

Conclusion: By explicitly decoupling localization from reasoning in an end-to-end framework, VLSR enables more efficient and focused chemical property reasoning from molecular images. The localize-then-reason design allows the model to concentrate on chemically meaningful regions, leading to significant throughput gains over text-based LLM approaches while preserving the ability to reason about structure–property relationships.

Abstract: Local chemical perception and property reasoning are both essential for understanding how molecular structure determines properties. Current LLM-based chemical reasoning methods either receive SMILES/molecular images together with descriptions of local motifs, or reason directly from molecular images. Neither approach enables the model to focus on chemically meaningful regions before reasoning. To address this gap, we propose Visual Latent Structural Reasoning (VLSR), an end-to-end framework that jointly learns localization and reasoning from molecular images. Central to our approach is a localize-then-reason strategy. VLSR first learns to locate chemically meaningful regions in a molecular image. It then reasons about their property effects in a compact latent workspace before producing the final answer. Under the same inference setup, this design achieves 9.6X higher throughput than a comparable textual-reasoning baseline.

</details>


### [58] [Self-Referential Induction Increases Response Instability Relative to Unresolvable and Verifiable Questions in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13258)
*Paras Balani,Subhrakanta Panda*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper quantifies how consistent LLM “subjective experience” self-reports are, showing they are significantly less stable than answers to other philosophical or factual questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows that self-referential prompts can induce LLMs to produce first-person, experience-like reports, but it is unclear whether these reports are stable across multiple independent runs. The authors want to know if such induced reports behave like ordinary uncertain reasoning or form a distinct, less reliable mode of output.

Method: They define a metric called response instability: one minus the mean pairwise cosine similarity between sentence embeddings of compressed core claims from each response. They compare this instability across three groups of questions: (1) self-referential prompts asking for subjective-experience style reports, (2) unresolvable but non-self-referential philosophy questions, and (3) questions with a verifiable correct answer. Using Gemini at temperature 0.7, they generate 30 independent responses for each of four questions per group (360 total) and compute instability scores.

Result: Self-referential subjective-experience questions have the highest instability (0.343 ± 0.047), unresolvable philosophy questions show intermediate and tightly clustered instability (0.192 ± 0.008), and verifiable questions have the lowest instability (0.105 ± 0.058). Thus, self-referential reports vary much more from run to run than other question types.

Conclusion: Induced subjective-experience reports form a distinct, relatively unstable region of the model’s output space, differing even from other open-ended philosophical uncertainty. This quantitative baseline suggests that such self-referential reports should not be interpreted like stable beliefs, but as a fragile, distributionally special mode of generation.

Abstract: Self-referential prompting has been shown to reliably induce large language models to produce first-person reports resembling subjective experience, but no prior work measures how consistent these reports are across repeated, independent trials, or how that consistency compares to the model's behavior on other kinds of open-ended questions. We measure response instability, defined as one minus the mean pairwise cosine similarity of sentence embeddings computed over a compressed core claim extracted from each response, for three groups of questions: self-referential prompts eliciting a subjective-experience report, unresolvable philosophical questions unrelated to self-reference, and questions with a verifiable correct answer. Using 30 independent responses per question (360 responses total, Gemini API, temperature 0.7) across four questions per group, we find that self-referential questions show the highest instability (0.343 +/- 0.047), unresolvable philosophy questions show intermediate and tightly clustered instability (0.192 +/- 0.008), and verifiable questions show the lowest instability (0.105 +/- 0.058). This provides a quantitative baseline for the induced subjective-experience report, showing that it occupies a distinct, less stable position in the model's output distribution than ordinary open-ended philosophical uncertainty.

</details>


### [59] [How Do VLMs Behave When Blind or Misled? Behavioral Evaluation of VLMs on Scientific Figures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13267)
*Paul Osemudiame Oamen,Owusu-Banahene Osei,Ananya Mukherjee,Christian Greisinger,Steffen Eger,Pius Onobhayedo,Wei Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SciFigBench is a new benchmark and framework to test not only how well vision-language models understand scientific figures, but also how reliably they behave under uncertainty and misleading visual/contextual cues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLM benchmarks focus mainly on perception and reasoning—how accurately models describe and reason about images—while largely ignoring how models behave when visual evidence is incomplete, unreadable, or misleading. In scientific settings, such behavioral reliability (e.g., not hallucinating details, properly expressing uncertainty, resisting biased context) is critical because erroneous confident outputs can mislead researchers. The paper aims to fill this evaluation gap for scientific figure understanding.

Method: The authors build SciFigBench, a diagnostic benchmark of 250 scientific figures with detailed human annotations covering perception, reasoning, and behavioral reliability. They massively expand these via transformations, tailored reasoning questions, resistance probes, caption-bias probes, and selective blurring to create over 34,000 stressed evaluation instances. They also introduce the Admittance-Resistance-Inductance (A-R-I) evaluation framework: (1) Admittance—does the model admit insufficient evidence instead of hallucinating? (2) Resistance—does it resist misleading or biased contextual cues? (3) Inductance—does it infer cautiously and appropriately from partial information. They then comparatively evaluate multiple advanced VLMs with this framework.

Result: Experiments show notable behavioral differences between state-of-the-art VLMs despite similar perceptual and reasoning capabilities. For example, GPT-5.2 attains the highest figure description quality (MQM 91.6) and strong reasoning accuracy (78.4%) but hallucinates unreadable content 96% of the time. In contrast, Gemini 3.1 Pro, with similar perception and slightly better reasoning (MQM 90.2, reasoning 81.0%), admits uncertainty in 71% of cases where content is unreadable and scores highest on resistance (0.91). These results empirically demonstrate that current top models vary widely in behavioral reliability even when their raw accuracy is comparable.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that standard perception and reasoning metrics are insufficient to capture how trustworthy VLMs are for scientific figure understanding. Behavioral reliability under uncertainty and misleading context is a distinct, critical dimension for real-world deployment in scientific workflows. SciFigBench and the A-R-I framework provide tools to systematically stress test and compare models along this dimension, highlighting the need for future VLM development to explicitly target uncertainty-aware, non-hallucinatory behavior rather than accuracy alone.

Abstract: Existing vision-language model (VLM) benchmarks emphasize perception and reasoning accuracy (how well VLMs describe and reason about what they see in an image), with limited attention to behavioral reliability under uncertainty (how they behave when visual evidence is missing or misleading). We introduce SciFigBench, a diagnostic VLM benchmark for scientific figure understanding that jointly evaluates perception, reasoning, and behavioral reliability under uncertainty. It contains 250 figures with high-quality human annotations across three evaluation aspects, totaling 600+ hours of annotation effort. We further extend these figures via image transformations, reasoning questions, resistance probes, caption-bias probes, and confirmed selective-blur targets, producing over 34,000 evaluation setups for stress testing.
  We further propose the Admittance-Resistance-Inductance (A-R-I) framework to evaluate whether models acknowledge insufficient evidence, resist misleading context, and infer cautiously from partial information. Our results reveal substantial behavioral differences among models. GPT-5.2 achieves the highest description quality (MQM 91.6) with strong reasoning accuracy (78.4%), yet hallucinates unreadable content in 96% of cases, whereas Gemini 3.1 Pro, a comparably capable model (MQM 90.2, reasoning 81.0%), admits uncertainty in 71% of such cases and achieves the strongest resistance score (0.91). These findings show that high perception and reasoning accuracy alone do not guarantee behavioral reliability, a dimension critical for deployment in scientific workflows.

</details>


### [60] [Mixture of Training: Recombining Small-Scale Scaffolded Pretraining Runs into a Larger Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13277)
*Mohammed Sabry,Sean Augenstein,Keith Rush,Lucio Dery*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Mixture of Training (MoT), a modular pre‑training method that trains disjoint layer blocks of a Transformer separately within a frozen aligner scaffold and then recomposes them into a full language model, achieving parity with standard monolithic pre‑training on a 1.3B model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard language-model pre-training is done as one large, monolithic optimization process, which is compute- and time-intensive and hard to parallelize across depth or reuse partial work. The authors want to know if pre-training can be decomposed into smaller, independently trainable units (layer blocks) that can later be recombined, enabling potential parallelism, modularity, and reusability of sub-runs.

Method: They propose Mixture of Training (MoT): (1) partition a target Transformer into contiguous blocks of layers; (2) insert each block into a frozen, pretrained aligner scaffold that provides the surrounding context and stability; (3) train each block independently inside this scaffold; (4) recompose the full model by stitching the trained blocks together; and (5) optionally run a short end-to-end adaptation (fine-tuning) pass on the recomposed model. Experiments are done on a 1.3B-parameter Gemma-style model trained on C4, with different training schedules and analysis of token usage and compute paths.

Result: They show a proof-of-mechanism: independently trained depth slices can be recombined into a functional language model. With an appropriately chosen “quality-parity” training schedule, the recomposed model matches the perplexity of a standard monolithic baseline on the 1.3B Gemma-style model. This setting consumes more total (aggregate) tokens but yields a shorter idealized layer-equivalent critical training path, assuming the aligner preparation is done once and reused.

Conclusion: MoT demonstrates that modular, scaffolded pre-training of Transformer depth slices is feasible and can attain parity with conventional pre-training in a small-scale setting. However, because it requires more aggregate tokens and its compute advantage depends on reusing the aligner across runs, the authors do not present MoT as a drop-in replacement for standard pre-training. Instead, they frame it as a framework for exploring whether reusable, scaffolded sub-runs can serve as modular training units in future language-model development.

Abstract: We ask whether language-model pre-training can be decomposed into smaller, independently trainable jobs that can later be recomposed into a coherent larger model. We introduce Mixture of Training (MoT), a scaffolded modular pre-training procedure that partitions a target Transformer into contiguous layer blocks, trains each block inside a frozen pretrained aligner scaffold, and then recomposes the trained blocks with an optional short end-to-end adaptation pass. On a 1.3B-parameter Gemma-style model trained on C4, MoT provides a small-scale proof of mechanism: independently trained depth slices can be recomposed into a usable language model, and a quality-parity schedule reaches the same reported perplexity as the monolithic baseline. This parity setting processes more aggregate tokens and has a shorter idealized layer-equivalent critical path after aligner preparation; its effective compute advantage depends on reusing the aligner across runs. We therefore present MoT not as a general replacement for monolithic pre-training, but as a small-scale framework for studying whether scaffolded sub-runs can act as reusable training units.

</details>


### [61] [Refusing Intent, Not Form: Wrapper-Based Intent-Group Supervision for LLM Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13304)
*Ping Wu,Haibo Tong,Feifei Zhao,Han Shen,Yu Shi,Yilin Zhao,Sicheng Shen,Guobin Shen,Yun Luo,Yi Zeng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces WIFA, a data augmentation framework that pairs structurally similar harmful and benign wrapped prompts to improve safety tuning without excessive over-refusal, and proposes two fine-tuning methods (WIFA-Boost and A-GCRT) that enhance refusal of transformed harmful content while limiting unnecessary refusals of benign content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-tuned language models often pick up superficial patterns, such as specific wrappers or formats, instead of genuinely understanding user intent. This leads to two problems: attackers can bypass safety by wrapping harmful prompts in certain ways, and the model may over-refuse benign queries that share similar surface forms. Existing approaches require manual labeling of wrapper intent or external teacher models, which is costly and brittle. The paper aims to create a more robust and scalable way to make models sensitive to true intent rather than surface form, while controlling over-refusal.

Method: They propose Wrapper-Based Intent-Form Augmentation (WIFA), which automatically constructs intent groups by pairing wrapped harmful prompts with structurally matched wrapped benign prompts. This pairing is done without external teachers or manual per-wrapper intent labels. On top of this shared data layer, they design two fine-tuning strategies: (1) WIFA-Boost, a two-stage high-safety training recipe that first strengthens safety against harmful content and then leverages WIFA pairs to refine behavior; and (2) Anchored Group-Consistent Refusal Training (A-GCRT), which enforces consistency of refusal/compliance scores across different wrappers corresponding to the same intent, and applies a margin-based objective that pulls harmful and benign groups to opposite sides of a decision boundary.

Result: In experiments on Qwen models, WIFA-Boost achieves the highest refusal rates for transformed or wrapped harmful prompts, indicating strong robustness to wrapper-based attacks.
