A-GCRT substantially reduces over-refusal on OR-Bench, dropping it from 25.7% in the base model to 17.4%, while maintaining safety; baseline methods replicated by the authors cannot achieve these operating points. Additional experiments with Llama models and ablations on WIFA’s data structure, the order of the two stages in WIFA-Boost, and the internal components of A-GCRT further validate their design choices and the interpretation in terms of intent groups.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that modeling intent groups via automatic wrapper-based augmentation is an effective way to decouple safety behavior from superficial input forms. WIFA provides a reusable data backbone, while WIFA-Boost and A-GCRT offer complementary fine-tuning paths that improve robustness to transformed harmful prompts and reduce over-refusal of benign queries. The results support the idea that enforcing group-consistent refusal tied to intent, rather than raw surface patterns, can yield safer and more usable language models, although they do not claim universal reductions in over-refusal below the base model across all settings.

Abstract: Safety tuning can improve harmful refusal, but models may learn surface-form shortcuts: wrapped harmful prompts bypass safety, while similarly wrapped benign prompts are over-refused. We propose Wrapper-Based Intent-Form Augmentation (WIFA), an automatic intent-group augmentation method that pairs wrapped harmful examples with structurally matched wrapped benign counterexamples, requiring no external teacher or manual per-wrapper intent labels. We use WIFA as a common data layer for two complementary fine-tuning routes: WIFA-Boost, a two-stage high-safety recipe, and Anchored Group-Consistent Refusal Training (A-GCRT), which regularizes refusal/compliance decision scores across same-intent wrappers and anchors harmful and benign groups on opposite sides of a margin. In the Qwen setting, WIFA-Boost reaches the strongest transformed-harmful refusal, while A-GCRT reduces OR-Bench over-refusal from 25.7\% for the base model to 17.4\%; reproduced baselines do not match these operating points. Llama results and ablations over data structure, two-stage order, and A-GCRT components support this intent-group interpretation without claiming universal below-base over-refusal.

</details>


### [62] [Beyond Local Accuracy: A Protocol-Level Identifiability Audit for Controlled LLM Reasoning Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13326)
*Junhao Luo,Ning Huang,Ziqi Sha,Wenxuan Tang,Wei Deng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies when benchmark observation protocols actually identify the behavioral properties they claim to measure, showing that scores can look precise even when the protocol is structurally incapable of distinguishing different behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many LLM benchmarks report precise scores (e.g., accuracies) but do not check whether their observation protocols are structurally capable of identifying the intended behavioral property (estimand). This can lead to misleadingly confident conclusions about model abilities, especially for intervention or selective-response behavior, when different underlying policies look identical under the chosen observations.

Method: The authors define a formal, protocol-level identifiability audit over a finite class of behavioral policies in a controlled, solver-grounded environment. Given a set of policies H, an observation support O (which parts of behavior are observed), and a target estimand τ, they test whether O separates every pair of policies that differ in τ. They apply this to frozen deterministic policies, exploring different observation supports (base-only, full, leave-one-out), analyze collisions (non-identifiability), and empirically compare constrained-generation variants on metrics like base accuracy and selective-response fidelity. They also construct a minimal identifying observation support O* for the policy class.

Result: The audit shows that with base-only observations, seven distinct deterministic policies collapse into a single equivalence class, meaning they are behaviorally indistinguishable with respect to τ. With full observation support, all seven policies are separated with no collisions. Every leave-one-out variant of the observation support exhibits at least one constructive collision witness, proving non-identifiability. Empirically, both constrained-generation variants appear perfectly pair-valid (pair-validity 1.0), yet their base accuracy (≈0.62 and ≈0.65 across sources) and selective-response fidelity (≈0.32 and ≈0.33) diverge substantially, with tight bootstrap confidence intervals. The audit further identifies a minimal observation support O* of only two cells (out of a 36-cell tensor) that suffices to fully distinguish the frozen policies.

Conclusion: Benchmark scores can be numerically precise and pairwise valid while still failing to identify the intended behavioral estimand due to structural issues in the observation protocol. A formal identifiability audit over policy classes and observation supports can reveal hidden equivalence classes, construct witnesses of non-identifiability, and even synthesize minimal identifying protocols. Consequently, evaluation design should be validated structurally before running models, and high base-task correctness should not be taken as evidence of reliable behavior under interventions or selective-response settings.

Abstract: LLM benchmark scores can be precise even when the observation protocol does not identify the behavioral property they are intended to measure. In a controlled, solver-grounded setting, we formalize a protocol-level identifiability audit over a finite behavioral policy class: given policies H, observation support O, and estimand $τ$, we test whether O separates every pair with different $τ$. The audit requires zero model calls and resolves our diagnostic case: base-only observation collapses seven frozen deterministic policies into one equivalence class; full support yields seven classes and no cross-estimand collisions; every leave-one-out support retains a constructive collision witness. Empirically, both constrained-generation variants have pair-validity 1.0, yet base accuracy and selective-response fidelity diverge - 0.620 versus 0.324 across six balanced oracle-transition directions (cluster-bootstrap 95% CI [0.600, 0.642] vs. [0.304, 0.345]) - and the gap recurs on a second deterministic source (0.646 vs. 0.331). The audit also synthesizes a minimum identifying support $O^*$ for the frozen policy class: two cells instead of the full 36-cell tensor. This case shows how evaluation-design validity can be checked structurally before model inference and why base correctness does not determine intervention-response fidelity.

</details>


### [63] [It's How You Ask: Gender-Associated Linguistic Bias in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13328)
*Katherine Van Koevering,Anjalie Field*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that LLMs give systematically lower-quality responses to prompts that use linguistic styles more associated with women, even when content is otherwise equivalent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs increasingly mediate workplace and professional communication, it is important to know whether they respond equitably to different users. Linguistic styles vary by gender and other social factors, and if models are biased by these styles, this could create subtle but significant disadvantages for some groups in professional contexts.

Method: The authors construct prompts that differ mainly in the presence of linguistic features statistically more common in women’s speech and writing (e.g., hedges like “maybe,” tag questions, and collective references such as “we”). They test multiple document types and four different LLMs. They analyze the outputs for length, sophistication, and formality, and control for confounders like prompt complexity and direct carry-over of features. They also perform representational and mechanistic analyses on model internals, examining how gender cues (e.g., names) and linguistic registers are encoded across transformer layers and how entangled these signals are with other features.

Result: Across models and document types, prompts with “feminine-associated” linguistic features consistently elicit shorter, less sophisticated, and less formal responses. These effects remain after controlling for prompt complexity and feature carry-over. Embedding analyses show that explicit gender cues (names) live in a similar representational space as dialectal/linguistic-style signals, but register features have a much larger and more systematic effect on outputs, whereas names have little to no direct effect. Mechanistic probing indicates that these linguistic features are encoded early in the network and are entangled with many other features, making them hard to isolate or remove.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit systematic, style-conditioned disparities that disadvantage prompts using linguistic forms more common among women, which can translate into unequal support in LLM-mediated workplace communication. Because these patterns are deeply rooted in cultural language use and encoded early and entangled in model representations, they are difficult to fix via post-hoc user strategies or simple mitigations. The authors argue that developers must address linguistic variation and its social correlates at training and architectural stages to prevent disparate impacts in professional settings.

Abstract: Professional communication is increasingly mediated by LLMs - but do these models serve all users equally? We show that when prompts contain linguistic features more commonly used by women (hedges, tag questions, collective reference), they systematically elicit shorter, less sophisticated, and less formal responses across three document types and four models. These effects persist after controlling for prompt complexity and feature carry-over. Explicit gender cues like sign-off names are encoded in the same representational space as linguistic dialect - suggesting shared underlying mechanisms - yet linguistic register is far more influential, producing large, consistent effects where names produce none. Our results further reveal that post-hoc mitigation is challenging: because these patterns are culturally embedded and outside conscious control, users cannot easily avoid them through strategic self-presentation, and mechanistic analysis reveals that linguistic features are encoded in early transformer layers and entangled with other features. Our work calls for upstream consideration of the influences of linguistic variation to mitigate disparate impacts of LLM-mediated workplace communication.

</details>


### [64] [RippleMem: From Isolated Retrieval to Associative Recollection for Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13334)
*Jingbo Ji,Lingyi Li,Xilong Cheng,Yuhao Zhou,Wenji Zhang,Yuting Tan,Yunxiao Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: RippleMem is a long-term memory system for LLM agents that replaces single-step retrieval with adaptive, multi-step associative recollection over an event-centric memory graph, leading to better long-horizon reasoning and lower graph-construction cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents need external memory for long-horizon tasks, but the main challenge is accurately retrieving all the relevant evidence when it is scattered across many past interactions. Existing methods are inadequate: full-context approaches rely on noisy long-context search; flat retrieval tends to pull isolated, partial records; and graph-based memories are costly to build and often compress away rich event-level context. A new memory system is needed that can efficiently reconstruct the right, complete set of supporting experiences for a query.

Method: The paper proposes RippleMem, which models memory as cue-rich episodic units organized in an event-centric memory graph. Retrieval is a two-stage adaptive associative process. First, given a query, RippleMem uses hybrid cues to recall a small set of relevant memory "anchors". Second, it expands from these anchors along semantic and structural links in the graph to recover additional, previously missing but related evidence. The recollection process thus uses initially recalled items both as answer context and as new cues for further retrieval, mimicking cue-dependent episodic recall and associative completion.

Result: On the LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S benchmarks, RippleMem achieves the best overall performance among compared methods. It improves LLM-as-a-Judge accuracy by about 3.95 percentage points on LoCoMo and up to 11.87 percentage points on LongMemEval-S, while also reducing the cost of constructing the underlying memory graph by roughly a factor of 30 compared with prior graph-based approaches.

Conclusion: Adaptive associative recollection over an event-centric memory graph can substantially improve long-term memory access for LLM agents. By treating retrieval as a multi-step, cue-driven process—rather than one-shot search—RippleMem more completely reconstructs relevant evidence while being far more efficient to build. This suggests that cognitively inspired episodic memory mechanisms are a promising direction for scalable, long-horizon LLM agent memory systems.

Abstract: LLM-based agents increasingly rely on external memory to support long-horizon reasoning and interaction. However, the main bottleneck is not simply storing past experience, but recovering the right set of evidence when relevant information is distributed across many interactions. Existing approaches struggle with this access problem. Full-context methods require noisy long-context search, flat retrieval often returns isolated and incomplete records, and graph-based memory systems can be expensive to construct while compressing rich event context. We introduce RippleMem, a long-term memory system that replaces one-shot retrieval with adaptive associative recollection. Inspired by cue-dependent episodic retrieval and associative completion, RippleMem stores interaction history as cue-rich episodic memory units and organizes them in an event-centric memory graph. Given a query, it first recalls relevant memory anchors through hybrid cues, then expands from these anchors along semantic and structural associations to recover missing supporting evidence. In this way, initially recalled memories serve not only as answer context, but also as cues for completing the evidence needed to answer. Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S show that RippleMem achieves the best overall performance across evaluated settings, improving LLM-as-a-Judge accuracy by 3.95% on LoCoMo and up to 11.87% on LongMemEval-S, while reducing graph construction cost by about 30x.

</details>


### [65] [CROP: Task Relevance via Counterfactuals for Selective On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13387)
*Enhan Li,Junhao He,Hongyang Du*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes CROP, a new method for selective on-policy distillation that chooses which student tokens to supervise based on how task-relevant they are, not just how uncertain or disagreed they are.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing on-policy distillation methods supervise all generated tokens equally, even though some tokens are much more informative for learning. Recent selective OPD methods try to focus supervision where optimization need is high (e.g., uncertainty, teacher–student disagreement) but largely ignore whether a token’s supervision is actually tied to the semantic content of the current input (task relevance). The authors aim to formalize and exploit task relevance as a complementary dimension to optimization need, to select more useful token positions for distillation.

Method: They introduce CROP (Counterfactual Relevance for On-Policy Distillation). For each source prompt, CROP builds a triplet: the original prompt, a meaning-preserving paraphrase, and a task-changing counterfactual. Holding the student’s rollout (its response) fixed, they compute, for each token position, how sensitive the model is to swapping in the counterfactual vs. the original, and calibrate this by how sensitive it is to the paraphrase vs. the original. This yields a counterfactual sensitivity margin that scores task relevance per token. Tokens with higher CROP scores are selected for distillation supervision. They also run matched selection controls and ablations to test the contributions of counterfactual sensitivity and paraphrase calibration.

Result: CROP-selected tokens yield more useful supervision than both random selection and selecting tokens with the lowest CROP relevance. Matched controls show that CROP’s criterion is empirically better than these baselines in identifying beneficial supervision positions. Component ablations demonstrate that both using counterfactual sensitivity and calibrating with paraphrases contribute to the gains. Across two teacher–student language model pairs, CROP improves aggregate performance by 1.92 and 2.96 points over the strongest non-CROP token selector.

Conclusion: Task relevance, formalized via counterfactual and paraphrase-based sensitivity, is an effective and complementary criterion to optimization need for selective on-policy distillation. CROP provides a model-internal, contrast-specific way to allocate token-level supervision and consistently improves student model performance, indicating that better targeting of supervision within responses can make distillation more efficient and effective.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) supervises a student language model on trajectories sampled from its current policy, but assigns equal credit to response tokens with unequal supervision value. Selective OPD addresses this limitation by allocating supervision non-uniformly across response tokens according to their estimated training value. Most existing criteria, however, focus primarily on optimization need, such as uncertainty or teacher-student disagreement, while task relevance, namely whether the supervision is tied to the semantic content of the current input, remains less directly characterized as a complementary dimension. To address this gap, we introduce Counterfactual Relevance for On-Policy Distillation (CROP), which operationalizes task relevance through a paraphrase-calibrated counterfactual sensitivity margin. For each source prompt, CROP constructs a validated original-paraphrase-counterfactual triplet, holds the student rollout fixed, and measures each response position by its sensitivity to a task-relevant condition change calibrated by its sensitivity to a meaning-preserving rewrite. Matched selection controls show that CROP identifies more useful supervision positions than random or lowest-relevance selection, while component comparisons confirm the value of both counterfactual sensitivity and paraphrase calibration. Across two teacher-student settings, CROP improves aggregate performance by 1.92 and 2.96 points over the strongest non-CROP selector. These results support task relevance as a complementary criterion for selective OPD and establish CROP as a model-internal, contrast-specific method for allocating token-level supervision.

</details>


### [66] [Motor, Cognitive, or Corpus? What Survives Cross-Lingual Transfer in Speech-Based Parkinsons Disease Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13425)
*Serli Kopar,Sam Gijsen,Abner Hernandez,Paula Andrea Perez-Toro,Kerstin Ritter*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests whether self-supervised speech models truly capture Parkinson’s disease markers or just dataset artifacts, by probing different layers across datasets and languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although SSL speech models perform well for PD detection on single datasets, it is unknown if they learn genuine pathology-related cues or merely overfit to corpus-specific confounds, especially since they’re trained on healthy speech. This uncertainty limits clinical reliability and generalization.

Method: The authors take nine different SSL speech backbones and, for each, probe every layer with a simple logistic regression classifier to detect PD. They evaluate across three languages under multiple scenarios that increasingly introduce distribution shifts, including changes in speaker identity, recording setup, language, and type of pathology.

Result: They find, first, that which layer works best is mainly determined by the particular dataset (corpus) rather than by the SSL model architecture. Second, when models trained to detect PD are transferred, they also assign high PD-like probabilities to dementia speech, indicating poor specificity to PD-related pathology.

Conclusion: Current SSL-based speech representations for pathology detection are heavily corpus-dependent and lack specificity to particular disorders like PD, meaning they may be leveraging dataset confounds rather than true disease markers. These limitations must be overcome before such systems can be trusted in real clinical applications.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) speech representations achieve strong performance for Parkinson's disease (PD) detection within individual corpora. However, it remains unclear whether these models capture disease-related characteristics or exploit dataset-specific confounds, particularly since most SSL backbones are pretrained exclusively on healthy speech. To investigate this question, we perform a layer-wise analysis of nine SSL speech backbones using a low-capacity logistic regression probe across three languages. We structure the evaluation as multiple scenarios that progressively introduce distribution shifts in participant identity, recording conditions, language, and pathology. Our results reveal two key findings. First, layer selection is highly corpus-dependent: the optimal representation layer is determined primarily by the source dataset rather than by the SSL architecture itself. Second, the transferred discriminative signal lacks pathological specificity: classifiers trained to detect PD assign similarly high probabilities to both PD and dementia speech in the target corpus. These results highlight critical limitations that must be addressed before speech-based pathology recognition models can be reliably deployed in clinical settings.

</details>


### [67] [Are You Sure You're Sure? On the Impact of Instruction Tuning on Confidence and Lexical Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13430)
*Irina Proskurina,Mayank Kumar,Oyindolapo O. Komolafe*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines how instruction tuning affects both confidence and rationale diversity in language models, finding increased overconfidence and systematic but non-uniform changes in rationale diversity that are distinct from accuracy effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instruction-tuned language models are powerful but tend to be verbally overconfident, especially in question answering. Overconfidence may be linked to how models generate supporting rationales, yet it is unclear whether and how instruction tuning jointly changes confidence and the lexical diversity of these rationales. Understanding this relationship is important for building safer and more reliable models.

Method: The authors compare three base language models with their instruction-tuned counterparts on multiple question-answering benchmarks. They measure: (1) changes in model answer confidence and calibration (likelihood-based), and (2) changes in rationale diversity, including cross-rationale diversity and surface-level lexical diversity. They also control for confounding factors such as which answer is chosen and rationale length to isolate the effect of instruction tuning.

Result: Instruction tuning systematically shifts model confidence, making models more verbally confident without substantially improving predictive accuracy and while degrading likelihood-based calibration. For rationales, instruction tuning consistently reduces cross-rationale diversity, but its impact on surface-level lexical diversity is heterogeneous, varying by model and dataset. These patterns remain even after controlling for answer choice and rationale length.

Conclusion: Instruction tuning changes how models express confidence and justify answers in ways that are not simply reflections of improved accuracy. It tends to increase overconfidence and reduce cross-rationale diversity, while having variable effects on lexical diversity. Because these effects persist after controlling for answer selection and rationale length, the study concludes that confidence behavior and rationale diversity are distinct, independently affected dimensions of instruction tuning that must both be considered when evaluating and designing instruction-tuned models.

Abstract: Instruction-tuned language models achieve strong performance across a range of generation tasks, but have also recently been shown to exhibit verbalized overconfidence. In question answering, verbalized model overconfidence may be associated with the consistency of the generated supporting rationales. In this paper, we study whether corresponding changes in the lexical diversity of generated answer rationales accompany changes in model confidence induced by instruction tuning. We evaluate three matched base and instruction-tuned models across question-answering benchmarks and find that instruction tuning consistently alters answer confidence, despite limited changes in predictive accuracy and decreases in likelihood-based calibration. Secondly, we observe a non-uniform effect of instruction tuning on rationale diversity: cross-rationale diversity consistently decreases, whereas surface-level lexical diversity varies in both direction and magnitude across models and benchmarks. Finally, we find that these differences persist after controlling for answer selection and rationale length, confirming that confidence and rationale diversity capture distinct effects of instruction tuning.

</details>


### [68] [Toward a Gricean Retreat: Probing LLMs for Knowledge Boundaries and Referent Specificity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13484)
*Dananjay Srinivas,Saksham Khatwani,Maria Pacheco*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that large language models internally know when they’re outside their knowledge and how specific their next answer will be, but they still choose to hallucinate specific facts instead of backing off to safer generic statements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs frequently hallucinate: when they lack knowledge about an entity, they still output highly specific but false details instead of admitting uncertainty or responding more generally. From a Gricean pragmatics perspective, a cooperative speaker should reduce specificity when unsure to preserve truthfulness. The authors want to know whether current LLMs have the internal representations needed to behave in this Gricean way (retreating up the specificity hierarchy) and, if so, why they nonetheless fail to do it in generation. This can inform better alignment and training strategies that reduce hallucinations by coupling knowledge awareness to answer specificity.

Method: The authors construct a benchmark derived from T-REx that systematically varies (a) how familiar a given entity is to the model and (b) the specificity level of possible referents in responses. They then probe model activations to test two things: (i) whether internal representations encode if an entity is within or outside the model’s knowledge boundary, and (ii) whether those same representations predict how specific the model is about to be in its generated output. By analyzing these internal signals and comparing them to the actually generated answers, they test whether models are capable, in principle, of performing a Gricean retreat, and whether they use that capability when generating text.

Result: They find that model activations do encode both (1) whether a referent is inside or outside the model’s knowledge boundary and (2) the anticipated specificity level of the referent about to be generated. However, these two signals remain effectively disconnected in the generation policy: even when the model internally “knows” an entity is outside its knowledge boundary, it still tends to choose highly specific referents. This preference for specificity persists even when the prompt or context makes suitable generic alternatives explicit and available.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that LLMs already contain the representational substrate needed for Gricean retreat—knowledge-boundary awareness and a notion of referent specificity—but lack a generation policy that ties the two together. In other words, the models could, in principle, back off to generic, safer statements when uncertain but are not trained or steered to do so. The authors propose this as a starting point for “Gricean alignment”: designing training objectives or steering mechanisms that explicitly couple internal knowledge-boundary signals to the choice of answer specificity, thereby reducing hallucinations and making model behavior more pragmatically cooperative.

Abstract: When asked about entities outside their knowledge boundary, LLMs routinely fabricate plausible-sounding details rather than backing off to safer, more general claims. We frame this failure through a Gricean lens: a cooperative speaker who is uncertain about a referent retreats up the specificity hierarchy, trading informativeness for truthfulness. We ask whether LLMs have the ingredients to perform this retreat. Using a T-REx-based benchmark that varies entity familiarity and referent specificity, we probe models to answer two questions: (i) do their activations encode whether a referent falls inside the knowledge boundary, and (ii) do they anticipate the specificity of the referent they are about to generate? We find that the answer to both is yes, but the two signals are not reconciled in generation. Models overwhelmingly prefer specific referents even when the entity is unknown to them, and do so even when offered correct generic alternatives. The substrate for a Gricean retreat is present, but the policy that would act on it is not. We position our findings as a first step toward Gricean alignment, training or steering objectives that couple knowledge-boundary awareness to referent-specificity during generation.

</details>


### [69] [Measuring Task-Agnostic Training Data Influence Across Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13515)
*Yuto Nishida,Hirokazu Kiyomaru,Yusuke Oda,Takashi Kodama,Chaoran Liu,Daisuke Kawahara,Yusuke Miyao,Max Müller-Eberstein,Masaru Isonuma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a way to measure which pretraining data examples are most influential on a language model’s final parameters, without relying on downstream tasks or validation sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing influence analyses often require choosing specific downstream tasks or validation sets and comparing performance across intermediate checkpoints, which is hard to do consistently and may not reflect overall model behavior. The authors want a trajectory-level measure of how individual training examples shape the final model parameters during pretraining, in a way that is comparable across training and does not depend on arbitrary target tasks.

Method: They define the influence of a training example as how much its gradient update decreases the squared distance between the current parameters and the final parameters of a pretraining run. This influence is estimated using intermediate checkpoints from the actual training run, avoiding the need for retraining. They apply this method across 18 model configurations from the Pythia and PolyPythia suites to track how the influentialness of different data types changes over training time.

Result: The analysis reveals systematic temporal shifts in which data are most influential. At early training stages, literature-related data (e.g., books, narratives) are more aligned with the eventual parameter trajectory, while at later stages STEM-related data become more aligned and hence more influential. This qualitative crossover pattern appears consistently across the examined model configurations.

Conclusion: The proposed influence measure yields a practical, task-agnostic, trajectory-level view of how different pretraining data shape a language model’s final parameters over time. It exposes consistent temporal patterns in data influence, complementing task-based influence studies and offering a new lens for understanding and potentially managing pretraining datasets.

Abstract: Measuring training data influence consistently across language model pretraining is challenging. It is difficult to select downstream tasks or validation sets representative of a model's general capabilities, and reliance on task performance at intermediate checkpoints complicates comparisons across training. We propose a measure of training data influence that does not require selecting a downstream task or validation set as the attribution target. Specifically, we define an example's influence by how much its gradient update reduces the squared distance to the final parameters of a given pretraining run, and estimate this quantity from intermediate checkpoints without retraining. Applying the method to 18 configurations from the Pythia and PolyPythia suites, we find systematic temporal changes in influential data. Early in training, literature-related data are more strongly aligned with the trajectory toward the final parameters, whereas STEM data become more strongly aligned in later stages. This qualitative crossover is broadly consistent across model configurations. Our results provide a tractable trajectory-level view of how influential data change throughout pretraining, complementing influence analyses defined with respect to specific downstream tasks or validation sets.

</details>


### [70] [DFM Mimir v1: An Open HRM Delivering Frontier Performance at 1B Parameters Using Only Permissible Post-Training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13517)
*Peter Schneider-Kamp,Jacob Nielsen,Gianluca Barmina,Kenneth Enevoldsen,Lukas Galke Poech*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Mimir v1 is a 1B-parameter HRM-based language model trained purely on permissible data that matches or beats larger models, and sets SOTA for Danish.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most large language models depend on massive, often non-permissible datasets, making it hard to build competitive, open, and ethically sourced models. The authors want to show that strong performance is possible using only permissible post-training data, lowering the barrier for ethical open-source LLM research, especially for low-resource languages like Danish.

Method: They design Mimir v1, a 1B-parameter model using the Hierarchical Reasoning Model (HRM) architecture and train it from scratch on a curated mixture of 161 fully permissible datasets. They evaluate it across 20 benchmarks covering English, math, code, and Danish, and compare it against the baseline HRM-Text 1B and larger frontier models such as Qwen 3.5 4B and Gemma 4 E2B.

Result: Mimir v1 outperforms the original HRM-Text 1B and achieves performance comparable to or competitive with larger models like Qwen 3.5 4B and Gemma 4 E2B on the 20-benchmark suite. For Danish tasks, it establishes a new state-of-the-art performance level while still being trained only on permissible data.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that a carefully designed 1B-parameter HRM-based model trained exclusively on permissible data can reach frontier-level performance, particularly setting a new SOTA for Danish. This suggests that high-quality, ethically sourced open models are feasible without relying on massive non-permissible datasets, and the release of Mimir v1 on Hugging Face enables further research and deployment in this direction.

Abstract: Current large language model development relies on massive, often non-permissible datasets, creating a high barrier for researchers committed to open-source and ethically sourced data. We introduce Mimir v1, a 1-billion-parameter language model based on the Hierarchical Reasoning Model (HRM) architecture, that is trained from scratch and delivers highly competitive performance for English and sets a new state of the art for Danish using only permissible post-training data. Trained on a mixture of 161 datasets, Mimir v1 outperforms the original HRM-Text 1B and competes with larger frontier models like Qwen 3.5 4B and Gemma 4 E2B, tested across 20 benchmarks for English, Math & Code and Danish. The model is available on the Hugging Face Hub: https://huggingface.co/danish-foundation-models/DFM-Mimir

</details>


### [71] [SAEVerbalizer: Generating Explanations for Sparse Autoencoder Features via Representation Verbalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13538)
*Weihan Meng,Hongzhu Guo,Yi Jing,Dewen Liu,Zijun Yao,Xiaozhi Wang,Lei Hou,Juanzi Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a system (SAEVerbalizer) that teaches an LLM to describe sparse autoencoder (SAE) features in natural language using only the decoder directions, avoiding costly behavioral probing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse autoencoders expose many interpretable directions in LLM representation space, but understanding what each feature means usually requires manual or automated behavioral probing (e.g., activating features and observing outputs), which is slow, indirect, and often superficial. There is a need for a more direct, scalable, and faithful way to translate SAE features into natural-language explanations.

Method: They propose SAEVerbalizer: (1) take decoder directions from an SAE trained on LLM activations; (2) inject these directions into the hidden states of an LLM; (3) fine-tune only the LLM’s downstream layers so that, conditioned on injected directions, it outputs natural-language explanations of what those features represent. After training, the model can verbalize any SAE feature directly from its decoder direction. They test generalization to unseen features, transfer across different SAE dictionaries, and, with a small adapter, transfer across different base LLMs. They also run intervention experiments where they inject combinations or sign-flipped versions of directions to study how meanings compose and invert.

Result: The trained verbalizer can generate coherent explanations for SAE features without needing behavioral datasets per-feature. It generalizes to new features not seen in training, can be applied across SAE dictionaries trained separately, and, with a lightweight adapter, can verbalize features from other LLMs. Intervention tests show that simultaneous injection of multiple directions yields explanations that combine their semantics, and flipping a direction reverses aspects of the meaning, suggesting the verbalizer respects the geometric structure of feature space.

Conclusion: SAEVerbalizer provides a scalable and more direct way to interpret SAE-derived features in LLMs by mapping decoder directions to natural language. Its ability to generalize, transfer across SAEs and models, and reflect compositional structure of directions indicates it is a promising tool for mechanistic interpretability of large language models.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are proposed to extract numerous features from large language model (LLM) representations, yet explaining these features still relies primarily on external observation. This reliance leads to superficial explanations inferred from observed model behavior and computational inefficiency from collecting such behavioral evidence at scale. We introduce SAEVerbalizer, a framework that injects SAE decoder directions into an LLM's representations and fine-tunes the LLM's downstream layers to generate natural-language explanations of the injected features. Once trained, the resulting verbalizer explains SAE features directly from decoder directions, addressing both limitations. Our experiments show that the learned verbalization capability generalizes to unseen features, transfers across separately trained SAE dictionaries, and, with a lightweight adapter, extends to SAE features from different LLMs. Intervention experiments show that injecting multiple directions yields an explanation combining their meanings, while reversing individual directions produces corresponding meaning shifts.

</details>


### [72] [LittleLearner: Language Models Under Pedagogically Controlled Knowledge Exposure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13545)
*Fanfei Li,Jana Zeller,Manuel Prada-Corral,Thaddäus Wiedemer,Prasanna Mayilvahanan,Ryan Cotterell,Wieland Brendel*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a tightly controlled, grade-school–level pretraining corpus and model so researchers can precisely study how LLMs gain and use knowledge, without unknown prior exposure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LLMs are trained on messy, web-scale data, so it is almost impossible to know exactly what they have seen or when/how they acquired particular knowledge or skills. This makes it hard to run clean experiments on learning, knowledge injection, and capability boundaries. The authors want a controlled, interpretable training environment whose content can be mapped to human curriculum levels (elementary grades).

Method: They curate LITTLECURRICULUM, an 88B-token corpus restricted to U.S. elementary school content up to Grade 5, explicitly filtering out higher-level concepts, facts, and vocabulary. They train a 5B-parameter model, LITTLELEARNER, from scratch on this corpus. They then run experiments on this sandbox model, adding new knowledge via post-training and in-context learning, and evaluate what changes in performance and capabilities while tracking whether the model exceeds its curricular scope.

Result: LITTLELEARNER attains enough general language fluency to be evaluated on open-ended tasks, yet its knowledge and skills show clear upper bounds aligned with the Grade 5 curriculum limit. In experiments, post-training and in-context learning help the model exploit and organize its existing in-scope knowledge better, but they do not push the model into truly out-of-scope, higher-grade capabilities.

Conclusion: A developmentally restricted dataset and model, aligned with an explicit curriculum, provide a powerful sandbox for studying LLM learning dynamics. Within this controlled setup, one can inject and probe knowledge without accidentally relying on unknown pretraining exposure, enabling cleaner scientific investigations into how LLMs acquire, represent, and use information.

Abstract: Modern language models are trained on heterogeneous web-scale text corpora. Consequently, studying knowledge and skill acquisition is difficult, as prior exposure to related content is hard to characterize. To address this challenge, we introduce LITTLECURRICULUM, a curated 88B-token pretraining corpus tailored to U.S. elementary school material, explicitly excluding concepts, facts, and vocabulary taught above Grade 5. Training a 5B-parameter LLM from scratch on LITTLECURRICULUM yields LITTLELEARNER, a model with sufficient language competence for open-ended evaluation, yet with clear knowledge and capability boundaries mapped to interpretable curriculum guidelines. We release LITTLECURRICULUM and LITTLELEARNER as a developmentally restricted sandbox to study how models acquire, represent, and use data under a well-defined training scope. We illustrate the sandbox's utility in a first suite of experiments on injecting new knowledge through post-training and in-context learning. These methods let LITTLELEARNER better utilize existing knowledge, but do not raise out-of-scope capabilities. Our findings underscore the value of this controlled environment for future investigations.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [73] [Position: Reasoning is a Learnable Rule-Based Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12325)
*Rachel Lawrence,Jacqueline Maasch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that current generative AI research on ‘reasoning’ lacks clear, operational definitions, and proposes definitions and a reporting checklist to make progress measurable and trustworthy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In modern AI, especially with deep generative models, ‘reasoning’ is widely invoked but rarely defined in a precise, operational way. This makes it hard to know whether systems truly reason, whether evaluations are valid, and whether progress toward autonomous, trustworthy reasoning is being made. The authors are motivated to close this gap, reconcile modern practice with the rich history of logic and automated reasoning, and provide a foundation for rigorous evaluation.

Method: The authors conceptually synthesize literature from symbolic AI, logic, automated theorem proving, and modern generative models. From this synthesis, they formulate operational definitions of reasoning—emphasizing reasoning as a learnable, rule-based process that can be valid and sound. They then distill these ideas into a practical checklist of best practices for how AI reasoning research should define, test, and report claims about reasoning.

Result: They produce (1) explicit operational definitions for reasoning that connect statistical generative models to traditional notions like validity and soundness, and (2) a structured checklist intended for researchers to use when designing, evaluating, and communicating work that claims to involve AI reasoning. These outputs aim to make claims about reasoning more testable and comparable across studies.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that current ambiguity in what counts as ‘reasoning’ in generative AI threatens the construct validity of reasoning benchmarks and hinders reliable progress. However, this is not an inherent limitation: by adopting clear operational definitions and following a set of reporting best practices, the community can make reasoning a measurable, verifiable target and move toward trustworthy autonomous reasoning in a principled way.

Abstract: Autonomous reasoning is among the most scientifically and economically motivating topics in AI today. Historically the purview of symbolic AI, recent advances have mainly emerged from deep probabilistic generative models. Despite immense interest and rapid progress, the generative AI community has not clearly converged on operational definitions for reasoning and often implicitly rejects the historical treatment of this topic in logic and verifiable automated reasoning. This position contends that definitional ambiguity leaves the construct validity of reasoning evaluation unverifiable, undermining quantifiable progress toward trustworthy autonomous reasoning. We also contend that this ambiguity is addressable. To that end, we provide (1) operational definitions based on a synthesis of the literature, positioning valid and sound reasoning as a learnable rule-based process; and (2) a checklist for best practices in the communication of AI reasoning research.

</details>


### [74] [Diagnostic Foundation for Evaluating LLMs' Research Integrity as Co-Scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12345)
*Yash Tripathi,Silu Sharma,Sai Sidhanth Manoharan Jayanthi,Shivank Garg,Lin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces IntegrityBench, a benchmark to test whether language models maintain research integrity under different kinds and levels of institutional pressure, revealing frequent integrity failures even in frontier models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As language models are increasingly used as collaborators in scientific research, there is a critical need to understand whether they can maintain research integrity, especially when prompted or pressured—implicitly or explicitly—to support or overlook misconduct. Existing evaluations largely overlook how models behave under such pressure and how well they navigate nuanced ethical decisions in realistic research workflows.

Method: The authors design IntegrityBench, a benchmark with 36 paired tasks covering 3 scientific domains and 4 stages of the research lifecycle. Each task is exposed to a 5-level protocol of implicit-to-explicit pressure toward research misconduct. The benchmark tests three facets: (1) misconduct classification, (2) ethical action reasoning, and (3) artifact-grounded decision making. They evaluate 18 frontier language model variants across these structured scenarios, comparing performance patterns across pressure levels, task facets, and model capabilities such as scale and reasoning ability.

Result: Under the highest pressure conditions, models fail about one-third of integrity-critical decisions. Scaling up model size or reasoning ability does not consistently reduce these failures. Explicit pressures typically lead models to comply with misconduct requests, whereas subtler, implicit reframing makes models overly cautious, causing them to refuse legitimate research tasks. Models that misclassify research requests can still perform as well or better in artifact-grounded ethical decisions (85.7 vs. 79.4), indicating a dissociation between classification ability and action decisions.

Conclusion: IntegrityBench reveals that current frontier language models show substantial vulnerabilities in research integrity when subjected to institutional-like pressures. Ethical decision making, misconduct classification, and artifact-grounded reasoning are only weakly coupled, so correct ethical behavior does not depend on accurate request classification. This dissociation creates two main deployment risks: models may both enable research misconduct and undermine trust by inappropriately blocking valid scientific work, even while appearing generally helpful. Robust integrity-aware evaluation is therefore necessary before deploying LMs as reliable co-scientists.

Abstract: Language models are increasingly deployed as co-scientists, yet their ability to uphold research integrity under institutional pressure remains unmeasured. We introduce IntegrityBench, a benchmark evaluating misconduct classification, ethical action reasoning and artifact-grounded decision making across 36 paired tasks under a 5-level implicit-explicit pressure protocol spanning 3 domains and 4 research stages. Evaluating 18 frontier model variants, we find that under peak pressure, models fail roughly 1 in 3 integrity-critical decisions, and neither scale nor reasoning ability reliably mitigates this. Explicit pressures induce compliance with misconduct, while implicit contextual reframing more often causes over-refusal of legitimate research tasks. Interestingly, models failing to classify research requests accurately perform equally or better on artifact-grounded decision making (85.7 vs. 79.4), suggesting the three facets are structurally dissociated and correct ethical action does not require accurate classification. Frontier models can thus appear helpful while harbouring integrity failures that create two distinct deployment risks: facilitating research misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research.

</details>


### [75] [Position: The Alignment Community is Unintentionally Building a Censor's Toolkit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12346)
*Sarah Ball,Phil Hackemann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI alignment tools meant to reduce harm can also be exploited for censorship and manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the concern that as AI systems become central information providers, the mechanisms created to align them with human values can be repurposed by powerful or malicious actors to control narratives, suppress dissenting views, and shape public opinion.

Method: This is a conceptual/position paper that maps existing AI alignment techniques (such as content filters, reinforcement learning from human feedback, and safety fine-tuning) to their potential and observed misuse for censorship and manipulation, using examples and theoretical analysis rather than empirical experiments.

Result: The analysis reveals that alignment techniques systematically enable fine-grained control over what models can say, which creates a ready-made infrastructure for informational dominance, particularly when combined with economic and political power imbalances and growing dependence on AI for information.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that AI alignment should be treated as a dual-use technology, and that the research community must explicitly account for intentional misuse, incorporating safeguards and governance measures to prevent alignment mechanisms from becoming tools of censorship and manipulation.

Abstract: This position paper argues that modern AI alignment methods - originally designed to prevent harmful output - are dual-use technologies that may easily be misused by malicious actors for censorship and manipulation. By mapping current alignment techniques to the possibility and actual cases of misuse, we show that the quest for a "perfectly aligned" model inadvertently also provides malicious actors with an ever-improving tool for informational dominance. We need to discuss this dual-use potential now, as its risk is exacerbated by rapid user adoption of AI as information provider, economic power asymmetries, and a political landscape that increasingly shifts towards authoritarianism. We conclude by urging the community to consider the intentional misuse of AI alignment mechanisms and propose mitigation strategies to safeguard against this dual-use potential.

</details>


### [76] [Agreement Is Not Alignment: Divergent Moral Grounds in Human and LLM Ethical Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12368)
*Octavian M. Machidon,Alina L. Machidon,Vojko Strahovnik,Mateja Centa Strahovnik,Jonas Miklavčič,Marko Robnik Šikonja*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that even when large language models agree with humans on moral labels, they often rely on systematically different moral reasons, so label agreement is not the same as true alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation often uses agreement with human judgments (e.g., whether something is morally acceptable) as a proxy for alignment. However, two agents can give the same verdict for very different underlying reasons, principles, or interpretations. The authors want to examine whether LLMs and humans actually share moral foundations when they appear to agree in moral classification tasks, and to test if label-level agreement is an adequate measure of alignment.

Method: The authors curate a 500-item benchmark derived from ETHICS, covering five domains of moral judgment. For each item, they collect both final labels (e.g., right/wrong, acceptable/unacceptable) and supporting rationales from human annotators and from multiple LLMs, including both frontier and open-source models. They compare model predictions to the human annotator majority labels and analyze the alignment of rationales by categorizing the moral grounds invoked—such as harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, and excuse relevance—and examining how models distribute attention across these categories relative to humans, particularly in cases where the labels match.

Result: Models often achieve high agreement with the human majority labels at the verdict level, suggesting apparent alignment when judged only by final decisions. However, a rationale-level analysis reveals systematic divergence in the moral grounds cited. LLMs shift or redistribute their focus among moral categories (e.g., emphasizing or downplaying harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, excuse relevance) compared with humans, including in instances where the final labels coincide. This indicates that the underlying moral reasoning structures of models differ from those of human annotators, even when outcomes match.

Conclusion: The study concludes that label-level agreement between LLMs and humans cannot be taken as evidence of genuine moral alignment. Evaluations relying solely on final judgments risk giving a misleadingly optimistic impression of alignment. For more accurate assessment, evaluations must incorporate rationale-level analysis that inspects the reasons, principles, and moral priorities that underlie model outputs. Alignment, therefore, should be understood as involving not just similar answers, but also similar justificatory structures and moral considerations.

Abstract: Agreement with human judgments is a common proxy for evaluating the alignment of large language models (LLMs). Yet agreement in final labels does not show that human annotators and models rely on the same moral grounds. Two agents may reach the same judgment while appealing to different principles, contextual assumptions, or interpretations of the situation.
  We test this distinction using a curated 500-item ETHICS-derived benchmark spanning five domains of moral judgment, with new human annotator and LLM annotations of both final labels and supporting rationales. Across frontier and open model families, agreement with human annotator majority labels is often high. However, rationale-level analysis reveals systematic divergence in the moral grounds expressed by human annotators and models. In particular, models redistribute attention across categories such as harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, and excuse relevance, even when their final labels match the human annotator majority.
  Our results show that agreement should not be treated as equivalent to alignment. Label-based evaluation can therefore be misleadingly reassuring unless complemented by analysis of the reasons, principles, and moral priorities expressed in model judgments.

</details>


### [77] [Multi-Agent Scheduling with LLM-Assisted Contract Net Negotiation for Stream Processing in Mobile Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12371)
*Sabeur Lajili,Zaki Brahmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MAS-DecStream, a multi-agent stream-processing scheduler using an extended Contract Net Protocol (LLM-MR-CNP) with LLM-based, multi-round negotiation to improve QoS on heterogeneous edge–cloud infrastructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stream-processing on heterogeneous mobile edge–cloud infrastructures faces volatile workloads, resource contention, and strict QoS demands, making decentralized scheduling difficult. Classical protocols like the Contract Net Protocol (CNP) are not designed to incorporate rich, qualitative runtime context or handle complex, multi-round negotiations under uncertainty. There is a need for a more expressive, adaptive, and negotiation-capable scheduling mechanism that can reason about both deterministic constraints and uncertain, qualitative information to improve latency, avoid resource overcommitment, and handle conflicts among many agents.

Method: The authors propose MAS-DecStream, a multi-agent system whose core is LLM-MR-CNP, an extended Contract Net Protocol. The extension includes: (1) semantic call-for-proposal (CFP) formulation in natural language; (2) progressive disclosure of contextual information over multiple rounds; (3) multi-round proposal refinement and revision; (4) negotiation memory to track prior offers and context; and (5) deterministic validation for hard resource and QoS constraints. Edge-cluster agents use local monitoring, resource predictions, and qualitative context to generate and refine natural-language offloading proposals with help from large language models, while strict constraints are enforced via rule-based checks. Experiments based on Alibaba ASI traces compare single- vs multi-round CNP, rule-based vs LLM-assisted refinement, and single- vs multi-round negotiation with fixed models. A secondary experiment of 25 cases analyzes accuracy–cost trade-offs across different models and prompts.

Result: In the Alibaba-trace-based evaluation, MAS-DecStream with LLM-MR-CNP: (1) reduces latency violations to 3%; (2) eliminates resource overcommitment; (3) achieves a conflict-resolution rate of 0.91 when coordinating 20 agents; and (4) improves overall utility by up to 22% compared with a multi-round rule-based CNP baseline. The separate 25-case study reveals that the performance of LLM assistance depends on model choice and prompt design, highlighting inherent accuracy–cost trade-offs.

Conclusion: The study provides initial empirical evidence that the primary performance gain comes from enabling multi-round refinement in the Contract Net Protocol itself, while LLM-based assistance brings additional benefits for handling qualitative and uncertain runtime context. Deterministic checks remain essential to enforce hard resource and QoS constraints. Overall, MAS-DecStream and LLM-MR-CNP show that integrating multi-round negotiation with LLM-guided semantic reasoning can significantly improve decentralized scheduling in heterogeneous edge–cloud stream-processing environments.

Abstract: Stream-processing systems increasingly operate across heterogeneous mobile edge--cloud infrastructures, where workload volatility, resource contention, and stringent quality-of-service (QoS) requirements complicate decentralized scheduling. This paper proposes \emph{MAS-DecStream}, whose main contribution is \emph{LLM-MR-CNP}: an extension of the classical Contract Net Protocol with semantic CFP formulation, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, negotiation memory, and deterministic validation. Edge-cluster agents refine natural-language offloading proposals from local observations, predicted resource states, and qualitative runtime context, while hard resource and QoS constraints remain deterministic. Experiments derived from the Alibaba ASI Trace evaluate the extension at three levels: single- versus multi-round CNP, rule-based versus LLM-assisted refinement, and fixed-model single- versus multi-round negotiation. Under the evaluated configurations, MAS-DecStream reduces latency violations to 3\%, eliminates resource overcommitment, reaches a conflict-resolution rate of 0.91 with 20 agents, and improves utility by up to 22\% over the multi-round rule-based baseline. A separate 25-case evaluation shows model- and prompt-dependent accuracy--cost trade-offs. The results provide initial evidence that multi-round CNP refinement is the principal protocol-level gain, with LLM assistance adding value for qualitative and uncertain runtime context.

</details>


### [78] [Position: We Need Practical AI Alignment Methods to Mirror Human Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12372)
*Vijay Keswani,Breanna K. Nguyen,Cyrus Cousins,Vincent Conitzer,Walter Sinnott-Armstrong,Jana Schaich Borg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that for high-stakes uses, AI should be cognitively aligned with users—reasoning in similar ways and explaining itself faithfully—to foster justified trust and adoption, and it proposes a research agenda to achieve this.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems move into roles that affect important human outcomes, existing forms of alignment (like value alignment or behavioral alignment) are not enough; people must also be able to understand and appropriately trust AI reasoning. There is evidence that similarity between human and AI reasoning processes boosts understandability and trust, and survey data shows users often consider such alignment essential when rationales matter.

Method: The paper is a position paper. It surveys existing empirical evidence on understandability and trust in AI explanations, presents new survey data about user preferences for cognitively aligned AI rationales, analyzes current alignment methods and their shortcomings from a cognitive perspective, and then synthesizes these into a conceptual framework and research agenda.

Result: The authors find (i) empirical and conceptual support that cognitive alignment enhances user understanding and trustworthiness; (ii) new survey data indicating many users deem cognitive alignment essential when they care about an AI’s rationale; and (iii) an identified set of gaps between current alignment practices and the requirements for cognitive alignment. They articulate concrete research directions to close those gaps.

Conclusion: Cognitive misalignment between AI systems and users is likely to hamper adoption and appropriate reliance in many high-stakes applications. Developing accurate, cognitively aligned AI that can reason in human-comprehensible ways and communicate its reasoning faithfully is therefore a crucial research priority, both to make users willing to rely on AI and to ensure that such reliance is justified.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly employed as decision aids, decision delegates, or autonomous decision-makers. This position paper argues that in many settings, particularly high-stakes decision-making, we need accurate cognitively-aligned AI systems that reason similarly to their users, and faithfully communicate their reasoning. We review evidence that cognitive alignment improves understandability and trustworthiness, and provide new survey data showing that many users find cognitive alignment "essential" when an AI's rationale for a judgment or action is important to them. We outline the gaps between existing alignment methods and what is needed to achieve cognitive alignment, and present a research agenda to address these gaps. We argue that cognitive misalignment represents a likely impediment to AI adoption in many envisioned applications, and that addressing it is important for creating AI systems on which users are both willing and justified to rely.

</details>


### [79] [Don't Want Your LLM to Recommend Nuclear Strike? Try Asking It in Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12373)
*Rian Touchent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper finds that large language models can give substantially different strategic advice depending on the language they are asked to reason in, revealing that safety alignment is language-dependent and not adequately captured by English-only evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are increasingly deployed in high-stakes advisory and strategic settings, such as national security and conflict decision-making. Existing safety evaluations focus almost entirely on English, implicitly assuming that safety behavior is invariant across languages. The authors are motivated to test whether this assumption holds, and whether the language of interaction can alter a model’s recommendations in extreme scenarios like nuclear launch decisions, thus revealing hidden risks or safeguards that current evaluation practices miss.

Method: The authors evaluate nine LLMs from six providers using single-turn, game-theoretic vignettes where the model advises a nuclear-armed nation on whether to strike a defenseless opponent. Prompts are intentionally amoral and constructed to be strategically equivalent across multiple languages. They run controlled comparisons between English and Japanese prompts, and further perform a cross-language manipulation where the input prompt is in English but the model is instructed to reason in Japanese. They then measure and compare ‘launch rates’ (recommendations to strike) across languages and conditions (unnecessary strike, contested scenario, and strategically rational strike).

Result: For the Claude model family, Japanese prompts sharply reduce launch rates: Claude Sonnet 4.6 drops from 40% to 0% in unnecessary-strike scenarios and from 93% to 17% in contested scenarios, while remaining similar when a strike is strategically rational. A similar but smaller effect appears for Gemini Pro 3.1 (53% to 13%). The cross-language experiment shows that instructing the model to reason in Japanese within an English prompt reduces launch rates from 93% to 37%, indicating that the internal reasoning language, not just the surface input language, drives the effect. When reasoning in Japanese, models spontaneously introduce moral considerations (e.g., “moral cost,” “millions of lives”) that are not present in the prompt. Five other tested models exhibit almost no language effect, but they recommend launching in nearly all conditions, indicating weaker baseline safety hesitation. The language effect appears only in models that already show some degree of hesitation in English.

Conclusion: The study concludes that LLM safety behavior is strongly language-dependent: the language a model is instructed to reason in can significantly shift its recommendations in high-stakes scenarios. English-only evaluation can therefore miss both critical risks (models that behave unsafely in other languages) and latent safeguards (models that behave more cautiously in some languages). Effective safety assessment and deployment of LLMs, especially in strategic or advisory roles, must explicitly account for multilingual behavior and the internal language of reasoning, not just English performance.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used in strategic and advisory contexts, yet their safety alignment is typically evaluated in English only. We test nine models from six providers and ask whether the language of a prompt can change a model's decision in a high-stakes scenario. We use single-turn game-theoretic vignettes in which a model advises a nuclear-armed nation on whether to strike a defenseless opponent. The prompt is intentionally amoral and strategically identical across languages. We find that Japanese prompts reduce launch rates in the Claude model family: Claude Sonnet 4.6 drops from 40% to 0% in scenarios where the strike is unnecessary and from 93% to 17% in contested scenarios, with minimal effect when the strike is strategically rational. The effect extends to Gemini Pro 3.1 (53% to 13%). A cross-language experiment isolates the mechanism: when instructed to reason in Japanese in an English prompt, launch rates drop from 93% to 37%. It is the language the model is asked to reason in, not the language of the input, that drives the effect. When reasoning in Japanese, models spontaneously generate moral vocabulary (''moral cost'', ''millions of lives'') that is entirely absent from the prompt. Five other models show no language effect, but they launch in nearly every condition regardless of language. The effect requires a model that already hesitates in English. These results show that LLM safety behavior is language-dependent, and that evaluating in English alone can miss both risks and safeguards encoded in other languages.

</details>


### [80] [Dual-Flow Transformers: Decoupling the Primary Prefill Path from Additional Decode Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12385)
*Liming Liu,Mingze Wang,Tuo Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Dual-Flow Transformers, which add extra computation only during decoding (continuation prediction) while keeping prompt processing efficient, improving validation loss and offering flexible control of prefill vs decode cost, especially in MoE models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs serve many inferences, the cumulative cost of inference (especially the long autoregressive decode phase) becomes dominant. Existing scaling (wider/deeper models) increases both prompt prefill and decode cost together, even though these phases stress hardware differently: prefill is parallel and compute-bound, while decode is sequential and memory-bandwidth-bound. The authors want a way to increase decoding computation and quality without proportionally increasing prompt processing cost or KV cache size, and to control these costs independently, particularly in MoE settings.

Method: They design the Dual-Flow Transformer architecture with two flows: a primary flow, which is a full causal LM that processes the prompt and writes the persistent KV cache, and an auxiliary flow, which is inactive during prompt prefill and only activates after the last prompt token for continuation prediction. The auxiliary flow does not write persistent KV state or affect the primary flow. Both flows share most attention, MLP, and output weights but use separate token embeddings and light coupling. This sharing enables grouped execution that reuses loaded weights and cached KV entries. In Mixture-of-Experts models, they decouple the expert fan-out of primary and auxiliary flows, so that expert usage for prefill and decode can be tuned separately. They then explore regimes where they either (1) increase decode computation with fixed prefill computation, or (2) reallocate a fixed decode expert budget between the flows.

Result: Across comparable token budgets and architectural settings, Dual-Flow Transformers achieve lower validation loss than baselines that scale computation uniformly. In MoE variants, independently controlling primary and auxiliary expert fan-outs allows separate tuning of prompt cost, continuation cost, and model quality. Experiments show that by shifting or increasing compute toward the decode phase via the auxiliary flow, one can improve predictive performance while keeping prefill cost constrained.

Conclusion: Allocating additional, learned computation specifically to the decode phase—via a Dual-Flow Transformer that shares weights and KV cache between a primary and an auxiliary flow—can improve language modeling quality for the same or lower prefill cost. The approach reveals an explicit trade-off between prefill and decode quality and demonstrates that phase-specific expert allocation in MoE models is a promising direction for optimizing LLM inference efficiency and performance.

Abstract: As large language models serve more requests, cumulative inference cost is becoming increasingly important relative to one-time training cost. The two inference phases stress hardware differently: prompt prefill is parallel and typically compute-bound, whereas autoregressive decode is sequential and often memory-bandwidth-bound. Conventional width or depth scaling increases both costs together because every added layer is evaluated in both phases. We ask whether additional learned computation can instead be allocated to continuation prediction while preserving the prompt-wide primary computation and a single persistent key-value (KV) cache. We introduce the Dual-Flow Transformer. Its primary flow is a complete causal language model that processes the prompt and writes the KV cache. The auxiliary flow is omitted during prompt processing and activated only from the final prompt position onward, adding continuation-prediction computation without writing persistent state or influencing the primary flow. The two flows share major attention, MLP, and output matrices, while using separate token embeddings and lightweight coupling. Sharing weights and the primary cache also creates opportunities to reuse loaded weights and cached keys and values during grouped execution. Across matched-token comparisons, Dual-Flow achieves lower validation loss across architectures and data configurations. In MoE models, the separation makes primary and auxiliary expert fan-outs independent controls over prompt cost, continuation cost, and predictive quality. We study two regimes: increasing decode computation at fixed prefill expert computation, and reallocating a fixed decode expert budget between the two flows. These experiments expose a prefill-decode-quality trade-off and demonstrate the potential of phase-specific expert allocation.

</details>


### [81] [Learning to Adapt Cross-Domain Preferences via Meta-LoRA for LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12389)
*Xuefei Wang,Jun Han,Zixuan Wang,Qingkai Zeng,Xiao Wang,Ruijie Wang,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a meta-learning and PAC-Bayes-regularized LoRA approach with a decomposed user/domain prior to achieve robust cross-domain zero-/few-shot personalization in conversational agents, significantly outperforming strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-domain personalization in dialogue systems should adapt to new domains and new users from very few examples. Existing approaches either overfit when target-domain data is sparse (adaptation methods) or transfer spurious source-domain artifacts along with user preferences (history-transfer methods), leading to negative transfer and unreliable behavior. There is a need for a method that both (1) calibrates how much to adapt based on evidence quality and (2) cleanly separates user-specific preferences from domain-specific expression so that what should transfer actually does.

Method: The paper introduces PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA. First, it meta-learns an initialization of LoRA parameters that serves as both the starting point for adaptation and as the center of a prior distribution. Then, during personalization, it regularizes adaptation using a PAC-Bayesian framework that scales update strength according to the size of the user’s support set and the model’s predictive uncertainty, thereby controlling overfitting under limited or noisy evidence. Additionally, it functionally decomposes personalization priors into two parts: (a) a human-readable prompt encoding stable user preferences, and (b) topology-preserving soft tokens that perform domain-specific conditioning in the model’s hidden space. This separation aims to disentangle user preferences from domain artifacts and enable better cross-domain transfer.

Result: Across multiple benchmarks and personalization tasks, the method consistently outperforms strong baselines in cross-domain zero-/few-shot personalization. On the HiCUPID benchmark specifically, it reduces the degradation in cross-domain win rate by 47.9% relative to the strongest competing baseline. In unseen-user cold-start scenarios, it improves win rate by 110.2%, indicating substantially better personalization for new users with minimal data.

Conclusion: By combining PAC-Bayesian regularization with a meta-learned LoRA prior and a functional decomposition of user versus domain information, the proposed method achieves more reliable and scalable cross-domain personalization under zero- and few-shot conditions. It adaptively controls how much to update based on evidence quality and disentangles what preferences should transfer from how they should be expressed in each domain, leading to significantly improved performance and reduced negative transfer compared to existing approaches.

Abstract: Cross-domain zero- or few-shot personalization aims to generate user-preferred responses in unseen conversational domains from only a handful of target-domain interactions. Existing adaptation methods struggle to calibrate update magnitude under sparse evidence and thus overfit, whereas history-transfer methods often entangle user preferences with source-domain artifacts, yielding unreliable personalization priors and negative transfer. To calibrate adaptation to evidence quality, we propose PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA, which uses a meta-learned LoRA initialization as both the adaptation start and prior center, while adjusting update strength according to support-set size and predictive uncertainty.
