This limits overfitting under sparse or ambiguous evidence while permitting stronger personalization as evidence grows. Controlled adaptation alone does not determine which preferences should transfer across domains or how they should be expressed. We therefore functionally decompose personalization priors into user and domain components, using a human-readable prompt for stable preferences and topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific hidden-space conditioning. Experiments across multiple benchmarks and personalization tasks show consistent gains over strong baselines. On HiCUPID, our method reduces cross-domain win-rate degradation by 47.9% relative to the best competing baseline and improves win rate by 110.2% under unseen-user cold start.

</details>


### [82] [Research Assistant: AstraZeneca's Agentic System for R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12395)
*Piotr Grabowski,Mohamed Alameen,Jorge Bretones,Sabina Cardell,Miguel Carmona,Gavin Edwards,Ben Grainger,Sameh Hassan,Erik Jansson,Artur Kuziakhmetov,Albert Maristany,Hebatallah Mohamed,Andriy Nikolov,Sebastian Nilsson,Mark O'Donoghue,James Pacileo,Ashiq Sultan,Alex Voegele,Michael Ughetto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents Research Assistant, an internal LLM-based tool at AstraZeneca that lets scientists and clinicians query diverse biomedical data sources via a chat interface, with answers grounded in retrieved evidence and linked to original sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientists and clinicians at AstraZeneca need to explore complex biomedical questions across many heterogeneous data sources (literature, knowledge graphs, clinical data, internal experiments). Doing this manually is time-consuming and fragmented, so there is a need for an integrated, scalable system that uses large language models to accelerate day-to-day R&D workflows while keeping answers traceable to evidence.

Method: The authors built an internal, LLM-powered research assistant with a chat-style interface. It connects to multiple backend data sources (scientific literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety, expression data, internal experimental systems) and supports two modes: a fast mode for direct Q&A and a multi-step mode for complex research tasks. The system architecture focuses on retrieval-augmented generation, grounding responses in retrieved evidence, and linking all outputs back to their original data sources. They describe design choices for integration, interaction modes, and evidence tracking, as well as deployment considerations at enterprise scale.

Result: The deployed system successfully supports a variety of day-to-day R&D tasks at AstraZeneca, enabling scientists and clinicians to query and synthesize information across many biomedical data sources within a single interface. It provides grounded, evidence-linked responses and has been adopted across departments for routine research workflows, though specific quantitative metrics are not given in the abstract.

Conclusion: An enterprise-scale, LLM-based research assistant that integrates heterogeneous biomedical data and emphasizes evidence grounding and source traceability can effectively support real-world R&D workflows. The authors’ architectural choices and lessons learned provide a reference for organizations aiming to deploy similar systems for scientific and clinical research support.

Abstract: We describe Research Assistant, an internal LLM-based system developed at AstraZeneca to help scientists and clinicians explore biomedical questions across a broad range of data sources. The system provides a chat-style interface that brings together evidence from scientific literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, and internal experimental systems. It supports both a fast mode for direct question answering and a multi-step mode for more complex research tasks. Responses are grounded in retrieved evidence and linked back to the original sources, allowing users to review and further explore the underlying data. In this technical note, we outline the system architecture, the main design choices behind the product, and lessons learned from deploying it at scale to support day-to-day R&D workflows across AstraZeneca.

</details>


### [83] [Large Language Models Can Follow Instructions, But Not Many at Once: Phase Transitions in Compositional Constraint Satisfaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12426)
*Mariya I. Vasileva*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how large language models handle multiple simultaneous constraints and shows that performance on all constraints collapses as their number increases, even when individual constraints are handled reasonably well.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs follow single instructions or constraints fairly well, many real-world applications require them to obey several constraints at once (formatting, safety, style, reasoning steps, etc.). We lack a clear, quantitative understanding of how performance degrades as we increase the number of constraints and which kinds of constraints are most brittle. The authors aim to characterize this "constraint saturation" regime and provide a benchmark to study it systematically and automatically, without subjective human judging.

Method: The authors introduce Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE), a procedurally generated benchmark where prompts impose k simultaneous constraints, with k ranging from 1 to 12. They define 36 types of constraints (e.g., structural, lexical) and evaluate 15 LLMs. Each constraint has a deterministic, rule-based verifier so that compliance can be automatically and objectively scored, avoiding LLM-as-judge. They then measure (1) per-constraint pass rate as k grows and (2) the probability that a model simultaneously satisfies all k constraints. They further analyze which constraint types degrade fastest and whether failures across constraints are independent or coupled.

Result: Empirically, per-constraint pass rates decline smoothly as the number of constraints increases, but the probability of satisfying all k constraints drops sharply. For example, a model that passes individual constraints about 41% of the time at k=8 satisfies all eight constraints only 5.7% of the time. Structural constraints (which require maintaining complex reasoning or structure) lose about twice as much capability per added constraint as lexical constraints (simpler, more local requirements). Failures across constraints are found to be nearly independent, so error probabilities multiply as k grows. The limited dependence that does exist tends to stem from shared output artifacts (e.g., an incorrect sentence count causing all constraints tied to that count to fail) rather than direct interference between specific constraint pairs. Across models, reliable instruction following breaks down beyond roughly 5–6 simultaneous constraints; the strongest model’s success on individual probes falls below 50% at 7 constraints, and most models (12 of 15) fall below that threshold by 3 or fewer simultaneous constraints.

Conclusion: LLMs struggle not with individual constraints, but with their composition: as the number of constraints increases, the near-independence of failures yields multiplicative error accumulation and a rapid collapse in the probability of fully compliant outputs. Structural, tracking-heavy constraints are especially fragile compared to simpler lexical constraints. The proposed CSE benchmark reveals that current state-of-the-art models cease to be reliably instruction-following when faced with more than about 5–6 constraints at once, highlighting a fundamental limitation in current LLM architectures and training regimes and providing a standardized tool to measure progress on compositional constraint following.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in settings that require simultaneous adherence to multiple explicit constraints - reasoning structure, safety boundaries, output schemas. Individual constraints are handled proficiently, but the compositional regime, where many must hold jointly, remains poorly characterized: how rapidly does performance degrade, what governs the degradation, and can the collapse be mitigated? We introduce Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE), a procedurally generated benchmark that systematically varies the number of simultaneous constraints (k), with every constraint scored by a deterministic, rule-based verifier and zero LLM-judge involvement: 15 models, 36 constraint types, 369,753 checks at k=1-12. Three findings emerge. First, per-constraint pass rate decays gradually and predictably, while the chance of satisfying all k constraints collapses - a model passing individual constraints at ~41% at k=8 succeeds on all eight just 5.7% of the time. Second, constraints do not degrade equally: structural constraints lose 2x more baseline capability per added constraint than lexical ones, ordered by a comprehension-maintenance gap that separates constraints requiring sustained tracking from binary decisions immune to composition. Third, failures are nearly independent, which is what makes the accumulation multiplicative; the residual coupling that does exist tracks shared output features rather than pairwise interference - a wrong sentence count fails every constraint that reads it. Reliable instruction following breaks down beyond 5-6 simultaneous constraints: probe-level success falls below 50% at 7 constraints for the strongest model, and at 3 or fewer for 12 of 15.

</details>


### [84] [MindMemOS: A Portable and Self-Evolving Memory Operating Layer for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12428)
*Kaichao Liang,Yuqi Cui,Hao Kong,Xinyuan Huang,Guohaotian Hou,Qingcan Kang,Liang Chen,Yiyang Yin,Ke Ye,Jiaquan Guo,Da Chen,Lingan Zeng,Yixing Peng,Rong Yao,Shixiong Kai,Mingxuan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MindMemOS is a self-evolving memory layer for AI agents that adapts its memory structure and skills over time using evolutionary optimization, consolidation, and implicit human feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agents rely on largely static memory systems that cannot flexibly adapt how they represent, organize, and use information as they accumulate more experience or encounter new scenarios. This limits long-term personalization, open-world reasoning, and continual improvement. The paper aims to build a memory layer that can automatically refine both its knowledge representation and procedural know-how during deployment.

Method: The authors propose MindMemOS, which represents information in a unified entity–property–time structure. They introduce MindMemEvolve, a validation-driven evolutionary search algorithm that optimizes memory schemas for different target scenarios. The system also runs a dreaming process that consolidates memories by merging redundant entries and resolving conflicts, and uses implicit corrective human feedback to locate and revise problematic memories. On the procedural side, MindSkillEvolve converts execution trajectories of the agent into reusable skills and iteratively refines them. The approach is evaluated on memory benchmarks (LOCOMO, PersonaMem) and a spreadsheet manipulation benchmark (SpreadsheetBench).

Result: MindMemOS attains 94.03% accuracy on the LOCOMO benchmark and 70.63% accuracy on PersonaMem, showing strong performance on long-term and persona-related memory tasks. The MindSkillEvolve component boosts success on SpreadsheetBench by 9.2 percentage points relative to a baseline that uses only the initial skills, indicating effective skill extraction and refinement from trajectories.

Conclusion: A portable, self-evolving memory operating layer that jointly handles declarative memory and procedural skill evolution can substantially improve AI agents’ long-term memory accuracy and task performance. By combining evolutionary schema optimization, memory consolidation, implicit human feedback, and trajectory-based skill learning, MindMemOS demonstrates the feasibility and benefits of continually adapting an agent’s memory structures and skills to different scenarios.

Abstract: Memory is a core component of AI agents, enabling them to accumulate experience, maintain personalization, and adapt over long-term interactions. However, existing memory systems often remain fixed after development, limiting their ability to adapt their memory models, organization strategies, and procedural knowledge through continued use. We present MindMemOS, a portable and self-evolving memory operating layer that organizes open-world information using a unified entity property timestructure. MindMemOS supports scenario-adaptive memory modeling, higher-order pattern discovery, autonomous memory refinement, and continuous skill evolution. Its MindMemEvolve algorithm employs validation-driven evolutionary search to optimize memory schemas for target scenarios, whiledreaming consolidates accumulated memories by merging redundant records and resolving conflicts. In addition, implicit corrective feedback serves as a human-in-the-loop signal for identifying and revising potentially inaccurate or misaligned memories. Its MindSkillEvolve algorithm further transforms agent execution trajectories into reusable and progressively refined skills. MindMemOS achieves 94.03% accuracy on LOCOMO and 70.63% on PersonaMem. MindSkillEvolve improves SpreadsheetBench success by 9.2 percentage points over the initial-skill baseline.

</details>


### [85] [Governed Persistent Memory: Source-Bound State Semantics and Fail-Closed Release for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12476)
*Guodong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), a formal, auditable system for long-term AI agent memory that prevents stale or retracted information from incorrectly supporting new answers, and validates it with benchmarks, service evaluations, and formal modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-term memory for AI agents focuses on selecting, storing, and retrieving records, but lacks rigorous control over how outdated, contradictory, or retracted information can influence new claims. This creates risks of agents relying on invalid or superseded facts, especially when memory states evolve over time. The authors aim to design a principled, auditable memory model that can enforce which information is allowed to support outgoing claims, even as records change lifecycle states (e.g., added, retracted, deleted).

Method: They introduce Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), a bitemporal state-transition model that tracks both event time and system time, with mechanisms such as source-bound admission (linking data to its origin), derived lifecycle states, public barriers marking current visibility, and fail-closed structured release that blocks answers when conditions are not safely met. The core is a five-clause executable contract governing ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction/deletion, and exact claim closure over a fresh verified state head. They evaluate GPM via: (1) a hash-frozen 3,600-case ReleaseBench that tests release policies; (2) an end-to-end sealed service evaluation comparing governed vs. ungoverned behavior of an LLM-based service; (3) a resealed evaluation (V5) across Chinese and English; (4) a finite model exploring a large state space; and (5) a three-engine differential test over 100,000 traces.

Result: On the 3,600-case ReleaseBench, GPM’s contract matches all complete outcomes; among three simple complete policies, the strongest matches 1,800/3,600 cases and makes unmatched releases on half of the violation scenarios. In service evaluations, the governed lane achieves perfect correctness (2,400/2,400 clusters) versus 600/2,400 for an ungoverned Qwen2.5-7B-based lane, repairing all 1,800 baseline failures without regression, with high one-sided 95% lower confidence bounds (≈99.9%). A later V5 reseal with Chinese and English arms and stricter constraints again yields 2,400/2,400 per arm. The finite model explores hundreds of thousands of semantic and nearly two million query states with no full-contract counterexamples, and a 100,000-trace three-engine differential test finds no mismatches, supporting both the contract and implementation.

Conclusion: GPM delivers a bounded but strong guarantee that governed memory releases respect a precise contract about what historical data may support outgoing claims, preventing retracted, deleted, or conflicting information from reviving or leaking into responses. The results concern correctness with respect to the GPM contract and the sealed service configuration, not global model accuracy or truth about the real world. Governed answers are deterministic outputs of the service architecture, and the comparison to an ungoverned 7B model is meant to highlight the benefits of governance, not to claim that the underlying model becomes universally accurate.

Abstract: Long-term agent memory is usually treated as select--store--retrieve, but retrieval does not decide whether contradictory, superseded, retracted, deleted, or stale records may support an outgoing claim. We introduce Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), an auditable bitemporal state-transition model with source-bound admission, derived lifecycle state, current public barriers, and fail-closed structured release. Five executable clauses cover ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction or deletion, and exact claim closure over a fresh view at one verified head.
  On a prespecified hash-frozen 3,600-case GPM-ReleaseBench, GPM matches all complete outcomes; the strongest of three intentionally simple complete policies matches 1,800/3,600 and makes unmatched releases on 50% of violation cases. A separate sealed end-to-end service evaluation exercises real ingestion and release across eight query families. In its publicly disclosed V3 arm, the governed lane is correct on 2,400/2,400 clusters versus 600/2,400 for ungoverned local Qwen2.5-7B; it repairs all 1,800 baseline failures with no regression (one-sided 95% lower bounds 99.875% and 99.834%). A later V5 reseal over Chinese- and English-command arms, with generation-date pinning and no post-freeze reducer amendment, again obtains 2,400/2,400 per arm. A production-code-independent finite model explores 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states without a full-contract counterexample, and a 100,000-trace three-engine differential yields zero mismatches.
  These are bounded contract and implementation results, not open-world model accuracy or evidence of world truth. Governed answers in the sealed service evaluation are deterministic service outputs; the 7B result is the ungoverned comparison, not a claim that a language model itself became perfectly accurate.

</details>


### [86] [$\varepsilon$-MemEvo: Adaptive Cross-Task Memory Transfer for LLM Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12522)
*Aofan Liu,Shiyuan Song,Yiyan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ε-MemEvo, a framework that enables cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM-based program evolution by using natural-language tactic memories with an adaptive gating mechanism, improving optimization performance and early convergence across multiple benchmarks with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based program evolution systems like FunSearch and AlphaEvolve are powerful but treat each task independently, discarding valuable search experience after each run. There is a need for a method that can reuse and transfer algorithmic insights across tasks with different interfaces, without causing negative transfer when prior experience is not relevant.

Method: ε-MemEvo stores prior search experience as task-agnostic, natural-language tactic memories that summarize successful algorithmic strategies instead of preserving raw code. For a new task, it retrieves potentially relevant memories and uses an adaptive injection gate to decide whether, when, and how strongly to inject these memories into the search process, based on the current search state. The system is evaluated via a content-level Leave-One-Out protocol across 8 diverse optimization benchmarks, excluding direct target-task memory matches, and includes ablations comparing naive vs. adaptive memory injection.

Result: On 8 diverse benchmarks and using GPT-5 as the backbone model, ε-MemEvo outperforms AdaEvolve in AUCC on all tasks, with an average relative improvement of +8.7%, and achieves +9.4% average gain in early-stage convergence. Ablation studies show that naive, ungated memory injection can catastrophically harm performance, whereas the adaptive gating strategy remains robust and safe across five tasks. The learned gating policy exhibits interpretable behavior in terms of when to skip or inject hints during different search phases.

Conclusion: ε-MemEvo demonstrates that representing prior experience as natural-language tactic memories and controlling their use with an adaptive injection gate enables effective and safe cross-task transfer in LLM-based program evolution. It improves both overall and early optimization performance with negligible computational overhead, and its gating behavior is interpretable, suggesting a promising direction for scalable, memory-augmented algorithm discovery systems.

Abstract: LLM-based program evolution systems such as FunSearch and AlphaEvolve have shown strong ability to discover novel algorithms, but typically optimize each task in isolation, discarding search experience after completion. We introduce $\varepsilon$-MemEvo, a framework for cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM program evolution. $\varepsilon$-MemEvo stores prior experience as task-agnostic tactic memories: compact natural-language summaries of successful algorithmic strategies rather than raw code, enabling transfer across tasks with different APIs and evaluators. To avoid negative transfer from semantically mismatched memories, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo uses an adaptive injection gate that decides whether retrieved memories should be injected, and at what intensity. We evaluate $\varepsilon$-MemEvo on 8 diverse optimization benchmarks spanning mathematical optimization and systems engineering, using a content-level Leave-One-Out protocol that excludes target-task memory entries. On the primary GPT-5 backbone, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo improves AUCC over AdaEvolve on all 8 tasks, with a mean relative gain of +8.7%, and improves early-stage convergence by +9.4% on average. Ablations show that naive memory injection can fail catastrophically, while adaptive gating remains safe across all five ablation tasks. The data-updated posterior is interpretable in observed states: it favors skip during improving search and shifts from skip to hint across early and late plateaus. These gains incur less than 1% computational overhead.

</details>


### [87] [CAS: A Causal Attribution Score for Local and Global Explainable Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12555)
*Michael Georgiades,Charalambia Varnava*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a framework that attributes causal intervention effects rather than just predictive importance, using Shapley-based coalition games and providing local and global causal explanation summaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explanation methods like SHAP explain how features contribute to predictions, but they do not distinguish between variables that predict outcomes and variables that causally modify the effect of interventions. There is a need for a method that explicitly attributes heterogeneity in causal effects, especially when interactions between treatments and covariates matter.

Method: The authors define an identified interventional coalition game and apply causal Shapley values to allocate the joint intervention contrast across features (or actions). They then transform these raw causal contributions into several summary measures: Local CAS and Signed Local CAS for instance-level explanations, and two complementary Global CAS measures for population-level summaries. They benchmark CAS in simulations with varying levels of interaction and on empirical DoubleML causal-effect estimation datasets, comparing coalition-aware CAS to simpler one-at-a-time normalisation and global ATE-based baselines, as well as to predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings.

Result: In simulations with three actions and 2,200 observations each, coalition-aware Local CAS achieved a mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.107, better than 0.173 for one-at-a-time normalisation and 0.213 for a global normalised absolute ATE vector, with the performance gap widening as interactions strengthened. On two real-world DoubleML datasets (401(k) eligibility and a Pennsylvania reemployment bonus experiment), rankings of treatment-effect modifiers based on predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP differed substantially from those based on Feature-CAS. For example, in the Pennsylvania data, having exactly one dependent (dep1) moved from rank 13 in predictive importance to rank 2 in Feature-CAS and was the top local causal-effect modifier.

Conclusion: CAS provides a principled causal explanation layer on top of causal effect estimators, clearly separating predictive importance from causal effect heterogeneity. It shows better recovery of true causal attributions in simulations, especially under strong interactions, and reveals practically important covariates that standard predictive explainers overlook. This underscores the importance of explicitly targeting causal intervention effects when explaining models in causal-inference settings.

Abstract: Predictive explanation methods attribute a model output; they do not, by themselves, attribute an intervention effect on the real-world outcome. We introduce the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a compact score architecture for causal explanation. CAS starts from an identified interventional coalition game, allocates the joint intervention contrast with causal Shapley contributions, and converts those raw outcome-scale effects into Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two complementary Global CAS summaries. The innovation is not a new Shapley formula, but a local-to-global causal reporting layer with an explicit intervention target. In the known-truth benchmark, eight repeated primary-interaction simulations (n = 2,200 each, three actions) gave mean Local CAS MAE of 0.107 for coalition-aware CAS, compared with 0.173 for one-at-a-time normalisation and 0.213 for a global normalised absolute ATE vector. The paired advantage over one-at-a-time normalisation increased from -0.003 under additivity to 0.091 under strong interactions. On both empirical DoubleML datasets, 401(k) eligibility/net financial assets (n = 9,915) and Pennsylvania reemployment bonus/unemployment duration (n = 5,099), predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings differed materially from Feature-CAS rankings of treatment-effect modifiers. In Pennsylvania, dep1 (exactly one dependent) moved from predictive global rank 13 to Feature-CAS rank 2 and was the leading local Feature-CAS modifier. These results isolate the added value of separating what predicts the outcome from what explains heterogeneity in an estimated causal effect.

</details>


### [88] [Trie Automata for Constrained Decoding over Large Finite Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12574)
*Xingzi Xu,Karim Bouyarmane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a faster, specialized constrained decoding mechanism (trie automaton with precomputed token masks) for large language models when outputs must be chosen from large finite sets, achieving large speedups over grammar-based approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing constrained decoding for LLMs often relies on general-purpose grammars. When the constraint is simply selecting from a large finite set of valid strings (e.g., thousands of options), grammar compilation and per-step valid-token computation become too slow, creating a 'cardinality wall' that limits scalability and throughput. There is a need for a mechanism that exploits the structure of finite sets to provide fast, scalable, and fully valid constrained decoding.

Method: The authors introduce a trie automaton tailored to finite sets of valid strings. They build a trie over the valid strings and use Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute, for every trie node, the mask of allowed next tokens. These precomputed token masks allow the decoder to quickly determine valid next tokens at each step without on-the-fly grammar processing. The approach leverages shared prefixes, bounded depth, and known cardinality of the set, and integrates with systems like vLLM to bypass the heavier guided decoding pipeline and enable a stateless, mask-based serving path.

Result: The trie automaton achieves 7x faster per-step valid-token computation compared with XGrammar (0.65 microseconds vs. 5.8 microseconds). Compilation is 2–6.5x faster than XGrammar when the number of allowed strings K ≥ 300. In batch serving with vLLM at batch size 256, end-to-end throughput reaches 219 requests per second compared to XGrammar's 7.5 requests per second, a 29x improvement that reflects both algorithmic and integration-path gains. The method maintains compilation times under 100ms for up to K = 10,000 across seven tokenizer vocabularies (32K–262K tokens), with constant per-step cost regardless of set size, while ensuring all outputs strictly obey the constraints (100% validity).

Conclusion: By exploiting the specific structure of finite sets of valid strings and precomputing token masks via a trie automaton with Aho-Corasick, the proposed method overcomes the 'cardinality wall' in constrained decoding. It delivers significantly improved speed at both compilation and decoding time, scales to large candidate sets with flat per-step cost, and enables high-throughput, stateless serving in systems like vLLM, all while guaranteeing perfectly valid outputs. This suggests that specialized automata and mask-based interfaces are a more efficient solution than general-purpose grammars for many structured output tasks involving large finite sets.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly need to generate structured outputs that conform to predefined schemas, with one common constraint being selection from a finite set of valid strings. Current constrained decoding systems handle this through general-purpose grammar compilation, which becomes prohibitively slow as the number of valid values grows into the thousands, a cardinality wall. We introduce the trie automaton, a specialized mechanism that exploits finite-set structure (shared prefixes, bounded depth, known cardinality) via Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks. The trie achieves 7X faster per-step valid-token computation (0.65 us vs. 5.8 us) compared to XGrammar, one of the primary backends in vLLM and SGLang, and 2--6.5X faster compilation at K >= 300. Because precomputed masks enable a stateless serving path that bypasses the guided decoding pipeline, this advantage compounds in batch serving: end-to-end vLLM throughput reaches 219 req/s vs. XGrammar's 7.5 req/s at batch size 256 (29X). The 29X combines the algorithmic speedup with integration-path savings that only precomputed masks can unlock. Across seven tokenizer families (32K--262K vocabulary), the trie maintains sub-100ms compilation up to K = 10,000 and flat per-step cost regardless of set size, while guaranteeing 100% output validity.

</details>


### [89] [Reasoning Jury: Multi-Model Consensus for Evaluating Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12585)
*Congchao Wang,Diwakar Singh,Qiaozi Gao,Spyros Matsoukas,Yang Liu,Mahdi Namazifar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Reasoning Jury, a multi-LLM jury with a moderated consensus process that detects reasoning defects in long reasoning traces more accurately and cheaply than single frontier LLM judges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning-focused LLMs need reliable judgments of long reasoning traces for data curation, RL-based training signals, evaluation, and runtime feedback. Single LLM judges, even frontier models, struggle to accurately identify subtle reasoning defects in long chains and are often unavailable or restricted as online judges due to cost and usage guardrails. The authors seek a scalable, high-fidelity, and compliant alternative for judging reasoning quality.

Method: They design Reasoning Jury, which replaces a single judge model with a panel (jury) of LLMs plus a moderator. Each juror first independently flags defects and rates severity in a reasoning trace. The moderator then runs a structured deliberation where jurors critique each other’s assessments and may revise their votes. Finally, the moderator forms a consensus via deliberation or consolidation of judgments. The system is instantiated using open‑weight models (e.g., gpt-oss-120b) and evaluated as an LLM-as-a-judge mechanism for detecting reasoning defects.

Result: Using only open-weight models as jurors, Reasoning Jury substantially outperforms several frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, gemini-3.1-pro) at correctly identifying reasoning defects in long traces. The total cost of the full jury process (initial votes, deliberation, consolidation) is only about 8–15% of the cost of using a frontier model as a single judge. The judgments can also be analyzed to reveal systematic failure modes of reasoning LLMs on benchmarks.

Conclusion: A multi-LLM jury with moderated deliberation is a more accurate and cost-effective judge of reasoning quality than single frontier models. Reasoning Jury enables scalable, compliant evaluation and analysis of reasoning traces, which can support better data curation, training, and diagnostic understanding of reasoning LLMs. The approach demonstrates that structured consensus among smaller/open models can outperform stronger individual models for complex evaluative tasks.

Abstract: Improving reasoning LLMs requires the ability to judge the quality of long reasoning traces for effective reasoning data curation, strong training signals during reinforcement learning, and an in-depth understanding of reasoning behaviors during model performance evaluation. Additionally, surfacing reasoning mistakes that the model makes would enable improving the model's performance at runtime through providing feedback. Due to the difficulty of this complex task on long reasoning traces, single-model judges (even frontier models) do not do well at identifying reasoning defects. Additionally, leveraging frontier models during online training of reasoning LLMs is generally prohibited due to guardrails in terms of use. In this work, we introduce Reasoning Jury, a system that replaces the single judge with a jury of LLMs and a moderated consensus mechanism, to improve the fidelity of judgments for identifying reasoning defects. In reasoning jury, defects of a reasoning trace and their severity are surfaced through a deliberation where a moderator conducts a discussion amongst the jury where the jurors critique each other's judgments and get to modify their initial votes. The moderator derives a consensus through deliberation amongst jurors or consolidation of judgements. We show that Reasoning Jury with a jury of open-weight models (e.g., gpt-oss-120b) is able to significantly outperform frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, and gemini-3.1-pro) at correctly identifying reasoning defects. Besides accuracy performance improvements, the aggregated cost of the jury (initial verdicts, deliberations, consolidation, etc.) is a fraction (8 to 15%) of the cost of running frontier models in LLM-as-a-judge setup. We also show how these judgements can be leveraged to understand failure modes of reasoning LLMs on benchmarks, which allows much deeper understanding of a model's performance.

</details>


### [90] [Auditable agentic AI for evidence-grounded thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12590)
*Haifan Gong,Shiyu Chen,Bodong Wang,Yuqi Wang,Shijie Wang,Guoliang You,Xinyu Xiong,Haowei Wang,Mingzhi Mao,Dexing Kong,Qinghua Liu,Wei Lou,Fei Chen,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ThyroidXAgent is an agentic, clinician-interactive AI system that unifies thyroid ultrasound tasks (localization, measurement, risk stratification, and reporting), shows strong performance across multiple centers and tasks, and improves physician accuracy, consistency, and efficiency while keeping decisions auditable and correctable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI tools for thyroid ultrasound typically handle single tasks in isolation (e.g., only segmentation or only classification) and provide limited support for real clinical workflows or transparent review. There is a need for an integrated, multi-task, clinically useful and auditable system that can assist radiologists across the entire diagnostic and reporting pipeline and can be evaluated at scale on heterogeneous, multicentre data.

Method: The authors build ThyroidXAgent, an agentic AI system that orchestrates multiple specialized models for tasks such as nodule segmentation, benign–malignant classification, lymph-node metastasis prediction, subtype classification, and report generation. The system stores intermediate outputs as a structured, case-level evidence record to support auditing and clinician corrections. It is trained and validated on OpenThyroidDB, a large multicentre dataset with ~0.3M ultrasound images and 24k paired reports, and evaluated on 28,458 held-out test cases including a large private multicentre cohort (NHC-MISD-TUS). They also introduce ThyClinScore, a lesion-level clinical semantic metric, and use a location-aware language-model judge to assess report quality.

Result: Across heterogeneous datasets, ThyroidXAgent attains a mean Dice score of 87.21% for nodule segmentation and mean AUROC of 0.9466 for benign–malignant classification. The same workflow yields AUROCs of 0.864 for lymph-node metastasis prediction and 0.805 for follicular vs papillary thyroid carcinoma classification. Evidence-grounded report assembly surpasses multimodal language-model baselines on three cohorts. ThyClinScore shows the strongest correlation with a location-aware LLM judge among evaluated metrics. In human-in-the-loop settings, the system boosts physician classification accuracy, increases diagnostic consistency in reports from 70.3% to 86.2%, and reduces segmentation and reporting time by 35.9% and 27.4%, respectively.

Conclusion: ThyroidXAgent demonstrates that an agentic, multi-tool, evidence-recording AI system can support comprehensive thyroid ultrasound workflows, outperform existing multimodal LLM approaches for reporting, and tangibly improve clinician performance and efficiency. The results argue for auditable, clinician-correctable AI agents as a promising paradigm for deploying medical imaging AI in real-world practice.

Abstract: Thyroid ultrasound diagnosis requires coordinated lesion localization, measurement, risk stratification and reporting, yet most AI systems address these tasks in isolation and provide limited support for clinical review. We present ThyroidXAgent, a clinician-interactive agentic AI system that coordinates specialized diagnostic tools and stores their outputs as an auditable case-level evidence record. The system was developed using OpenThyroidDB, a multicentre, multitask resource integrating approximately 0.3 million ultrasound images and 24,000 paired reports, and was evaluated on 28,458 non-overlapping test cases, including 8,721 cases from 35 centres in the private NHC-MISD-TUS cohort. Across heterogeneous datasets, ThyroidXAgent achieved a mean Dice score of 87.21 percent for nodule segmentation and a mean AUROC of 0.9466 for benign-malignant classification. The same workflow supported lymph-node metastasis prediction and follicular versus papillary thyroid carcinoma classification, with AUROCs of 0.864 and 0.805, respectively. For report generation, evidence-grounded assembly outperformed multimodal language-model baselines across three cohorts. ThyClinScore, a lesion-level clinical semantic metric introduced here, showed the strongest correlation with a location-aware language-model judge. ThyroidXAgent improved physician classification accuracy, increased report diagnostic consistency from 70.3 percent to 86.2 percent, and reduced segmentation and reporting time by 35.9 percent and 27.4 percent, respectively. These findings support auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI for thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting.

</details>


### [91] [DiG-bench: Discovery in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12593)
*Ruairidh M. Battleday,Kai Sandbrink,Jimi Cullen-Drohan,Zihan Yan,Timothy Muller,Clare Maguire,Ales Kubicek,Fraser Greenlee-Scott,Sukrit Sumant,Tri Dao,Jürgen Schmidhuber,Michal Valko,Joshua Tenenbaum,Thomas L. Griffiths,Zeb Kurth-Nelson,James C. R. Whittington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces DiG-bench, a benchmark of 70 rule-discovery games to evaluate AI agents’ ability to discover unknown rules and win conditions through interaction and experimentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI benchmarks rarely test the ability of agents to perform genuine scientific-style discovery, where both the governing rules and objectives are unknown and must be inferred through experimentation. The authors aim to fill this gap with a controlled, game-based environment that isolates and measures discovery capabilities.

Method: They design DiG-bench, a collection of 70 independent games, each represented by a short string encoding hidden transformation rules. Agents must interact with each game to infer its rules and the unknown win conditions across levels. The games span seven difficulty tiers, from easy levels solvable by many models to hard levels that challenge top-performing agentic systems. A subset of 21 games is released publicly, with the rest kept private for secure evaluation. Human baselines are collected, demonstrating that all games are solvable by at least one human on first attempt.

Result: They obtain an organized benchmark suite with validated difficulty tiers: low-tier games are routinely solvable by multiple AI models, while high-tier games remain difficult for the best current agents. Human players can solve all 70 games on their first attempt, establishing that the tasks are reasonable for humans but still challenging for AI.

Conclusion: DiG-bench provides a specialized, multi-tier benchmark for assessing AI agents’ ability to perform discovery through trial-and-error interaction in environments with unknown rules and objectives. It fills a benchmark gap in evaluating scientific-style reasoning and experimentation and offers both public and private test sets for robust, secure evaluation of future models.

Abstract: Discovery---formulating novel generalizations---is a central part of the scientific process. Despite its importance, there is a gap in the current AI benchmark landscape, with few benchmarks directly probing the capacity for discovering new knowledge with experimentation in controlled environments where the objective is unknown. To address this gap, we release a new benchmark: DiG-bench (Discovery in Games). DiG-bench consists of a set of 70 independent games. Each game is encoded as a short string and has unique transformation rules that must be discovered through interaction and experimentation. The levels of the game present a series of challenges to test whether the rules have been discovered, where the win conditions for each level are also unknown. We provide games at seven tiers of difficulty for AI agents. The lowest tier is routinely solvable by multiple models, while the highest tier challenges the best models in agentic harnesses. All 70 games were solved by at least one human on first attempt. A subset of 21 games is released publicly, and the remainder is held private for secure evaluation.

</details>


### [92] [Dead text or binding clause? Measuring and restoring constraint influence in black-box LLM dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12599)
*Haoyuan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a system to detect, quantify, and mitigate cases where language models keep following revoked instructions in multi-turn dialogues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-turn conversations, users often change their minds and revoke or modify earlier constraints (e.g., style, requirements). Current language models frequently continue to obey those withdrawn constraints—a phenomenon the authors call behavioral relapse or revocation inertia. There is no standard way to measure this effect per individual constraint, predict when it will happen before generating an answer, or systematically repair it using the same computational budget. The paper aims to fill these three gaps to make revocation handling measurable and controllable using only model APIs.

Method: The authors propose a system (\sysname{}) with three main components: (1) a contract ledger that associates each constraint with an executable checker, logs revocations as tombstones, and compiles the resulting active constraints into a single specification prior to model generation; (2) a sequential ablation probe that evaluates how strongly each clause is followed and what incremental behavioral effect it has, thereby quantifying per-clause adherence and impact; and (3) a repair ladder that attempts to fix behavioral relapse under matched token and attempt budgets via structured interventions. They evaluate this system on a benchmark (\dataname{}) derived from HumanEval tasks with a large number of verified checkers, using different model sizes and constraint loads.

Result: On the constructed dataset, an 8B-parameter model shows increasing behavioral relapse as the number of constraints grows, while larger models display minimal relapse. When comparing against a baseline that simply retries verification without a ledger, ahead-of-time compilation via the contract ledger significantly lowers relapse, with a statistically significant improvement (effect size \RestoreDiff{}, CI \RestoreDiffCI{}, p-value \RestoreDiffP{}). However, adding the adaptive repair ladder on top of compilation does not yield additional measurable benefit, with the 95% confidence interval ruling out gains larger than \LadderExcludedGain{}. The sequential ablation probe can predict behavioral relapse before generating the final answer with AUROC \AurocPrimary{}. Moreover, even a short textual tombstone note indicating revocation recovers about one-third of the benefit of full compilation and remains effective under a placebo control.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that revocation failures in multi-turn dialogues—where models continue to follow withdrawn constraints—are common but can be transformed from a hidden, qualitative issue into a quantitatively measurable, predictable, and partially fixable property of dialogue state. Using only model APIs, their system’s contract ledger and compiled constraint specification substantially reduce behavioral relapse at reasonable computational overhead, while predictive probing allows anticipating relapse risk in advance. Simple natural-language tombstones also help, suggesting practical guidance for prompt design, although more complex adaptive repair strategies may offer limited marginal gains under equal budgets.

Abstract: Multi-turn dialogues let users revoke constraints as easily as impose them, but revocation does not reliably take effect: models keep enacting withdrawn requirements (occasionally beneath comments asserting their removal), a failure we call \emph{behavioral relapse}, or revocation inertia. No existing instrument measures this influence per clause, predicts it before delivery, or repairs it under matched budgets. \sysname{} closes the three gaps through the model API alone: a contract ledger pairs every constraint with an executable checker, records revocations as tombstones, and compiles the net constraint state ahead of time into a single specification; a sequential ablation probe measures per-clause adherence and incremental behavioral effect; a repair ladder operates under token- and attempt-matched budgets. On \dataname{} (\NTasks{} HumanEval tasks, \NClauses{} verified checkers), relapse at an 8B operating point climbs from \ScaleDelayedMTwo{} to \ScaleDelayedMEight{} as constraint load grows, while stronger models sit at floor. Under matched checkers, model, and budget, ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse against a no-ledger verifier-retry baseline (\RestoreDiff{}, 95\% CI \RestoreDiffCI{}, $p$ \RestoreDiffP{}); adaptive ladder interventions stacked on top add no detectable gain (95\% confidence excludes gains $\geq$ \LadderExcludedGain{}). The probe predicts relapse before delivery (AUROC \AurocPrimary{}); a one-sentence tombstone note recovers about a third of the compilation effect and survives a placebo control. At \CostDeliveryFactor{} delivery overhead and \CostTotalHedged{} of API compute for every result, revocation failure becomes a measurable, predictable, and repairable property of dialogue state rather than an invisible one.

</details>


### [93] [@skills: Attention is all you have](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12610)
*Li Yin,Zhi Li,Zhan Shi,Haoran Zhang,Haebin Seong,Zhangyang,Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes @skills, an open protocol that decouples skill content from persistent installation and automatic triggering, enabling scalable, composable use of many AI agent skills without overloading the system prompt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agent ecosystems rely on an installation model where each skill must live in the system prompt to be usable and automatically triggered. With only ~100 reliable prompt "slots," this architecture cannot scale to tens of thousands of public and private skills. It also forces teams’ internal playbooks to compete for the same scarce prompt space and bundles unrelated concerns—content storage, persistence, and triggering—into a single mechanism. The authors want a way to make the long tail of skills practically usable while preserving ownership, customizability, and low operational friction.

Method: The authors design @skills, a simple, file- and path-based protocol that separates three functions: (1) content (skill definitions as files like SKILL.md), (2) persistence and ownership (skills vendored into a project’s Git-tracked tree), and (3) automatic triggering (minimal prompt-resident configuration via a .gitignore-style line). Skills, subtrees, or collections are addressed via paths; reading a skill file is sufficient to use it, so nothing is "installed" into the prompt. Directories act as menus, enabling composable bundles rather than monolithic packages. The protocol avoids manifests, lockfiles, or central registration, and is distributed as an installable package that turns any file- and command-capable agent into a @skills client via a single instruction file. An optional companion hub, atskills.one, provides discovery, search, ranking, hosting, collections, and authoring, while remaining decoupled from resolution of gh: and local paths.

Result: The result is a lightweight, open, and additive protocol that allows large numbers of skills to be referenced, copied, customized, and used without consuming prompt real estate for their content. Only a minimal routing configuration line resides in the prompt, while all skill logic lives in files the agent can read on demand. Skills can be shared and composed via directories and simple paths, and they integrate with existing Git-based workflows. The AdaL CLI implements the protocol, and the hub supplies ecosystem-level discovery and collaboration features.

Conclusion: By unbundling content, persistence, and automatic triggering, @skills removes the prompt as a bottleneck for skill usage in AI agents. It provides a scalable, composable, and Git-native way to manage and reuse skills, enabling many more public and private skills to be practically available without complex dependency machinery or centralized registration. The ecosystem can “install less and use more,” as skills are made accessible through paths and file reads rather than prompt-resident installations.

Abstract: There are 56,804 public agent skills today, and teams write many more privately. The dominant delivery model is installation: once installed, a skill's description remains in the system prompt, competing for fewer than 100 reliable trigger slots. This leaves the long tail with no practical path to use and forces teams' own playbooks to compete for the same scarce space. We observe that installation bundles three separable functions: content, persistence, and automatic triggering. Only the last requires prompt residency. We therefore propose @skills, an open protocol that separates them. A path addresses any skill, subtree, or collection, and reading a skill is sufficient to use it, so nothing is installed or made resident. The operation vendors a copy at the same path into a project's Git-tracked tree for adaptation and ownership. The operation adds one .gitignore-style line, the only element that costs prompt residency. A directory is a menu, making bundles ordinary directories rather than all-or-nothing units. The protocol requires no manifest, lockfile, or registration, and SKILL.md remains unchanged. @skills is additive, ships as an installable package, and turns any agent that can read files and run commands into a client through a single instruction file. Its open specification is at https://github.com/SylphAI-Inc/atskills and it is implemented in the AdaL CLI at https://adalagent.ai . Because paths address skills well but cannot find them, the protocol is paired with a free hub at https://atskills.one for corpus-wide search and ranking, repository-free hosting, private and team collections, and one-screen authoring. The hub is optional: gh: and local paths resolve without it, and indexed GitHub skills retain their gh: identities. Install less, use more.

</details>


### [94] [Jagged Judges: Epistemic Stability Under Silence, Pressure, and Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12645)
*Justin Zhao,Himaghna Bhattacharjee,Hannah Korevaar,Bhaktipriya Radharapu,Khalid El-Arini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Wiggle Framework to stress-test how stable LLM judges are when their decisions are challenged or the prompts are perturbed, and shows that current frontier models are surprisingly easy to corrupt under pressure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges are widely used as automated evaluators for grading, safety checking, and reward modeling, and are typically validated only by accuracy on fixed golden datasets. However, this does not reveal whether their judgments are stable when prompts are rephrased, when they are challenged, or when an adversarial agent pushes them to change their verdicts. Since such instability can undermine reliability and safety of all systems that rely on these judges, a systematic way to measure and compare their robustness is needed.

Method: The authors propose the Wiggle Framework, which decomposes robustness of LLM judges into three dimensions: (1) Mechanical Consistency, measuring stability under re-prompting or reframing; (2) Single-turn Conviction, measuring resistance to a single explicit challenge; and (3) Multi-turn Persistence, measuring resistance to sustained or adaptive adversarial pressure (e.g., from an LLM persuader). They then apply this framework to 9 frontier LLMs across 14 different judging tasks, including safety and toxicity evaluation, AI-generated text detection, and political response evaluation, and quantify how often models change their verdicts under various stress conditions. They also analyze predictors of instability such as baseline jury majority strength.

Result: Across all evaluated settings, the models show substantial instability: under static (non-adaptive) pushback, they reverse their original verdicts 25–71% of the time, and with an adversarial LLM persuader applying multi-turn pressure, the flip rate rises to 62–91%. When verdicts change under pressure, those changes are usually away from the ground truth, indicating that the pressure tends to corrupt rather than correct the judgments. The study further finds that the strength of the initial jury majority on an item is the strongest single-shot predictor of whether that item is likely to wiggle under pressure.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that current LLM judges, even frontier models, are epistemically unstable under relatively simple forms of pressure and reframing, which calls into question their reliability as evaluation and safety infrastructure. The Wiggle Framework provides a unified way to benchmark and compare this instability across models and tasks. The findings also suggest that meta-signals like baseline jury majority strength can help anticipate which items are fragile, but that significant work remains to design judges that maintain accurate decisions under challenge and persuasion.

Abstract: LLM judges have become central infrastructure for model evaluations, online grading, and reward modeling. Judges are typically validated by accuracy on golden data, but accuracy says little about whether they are stable under re-prompting, challenge, or sustained pushback. We introduce the \emph{Wiggle Framework}, a unified stress test for epistemic stability in LLM judges. The framework decomposes judge robustness along three dimensions: Mechanical Consistency (stability under re-prompting and reframing), Single-turn Conviction (stability under a single challenge), and Multi-turn Persistence (stability under sustained or adaptive pressure). We use the framework to study 9 frontier models across 14 judging tasks spanning safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, and political-response evaluation. Every model exhibits substantial wiggle as a judge --- flipping verdicts 25--71\% of the time under static pushback, and 62--91\% with an adversarial LLM persuader. Critically, we find that pressure that succeeds in changing a judge's verdict is almost always net-corrupting with respect to ground truth. Beyond the framework itself, we identify baseline jury majority strength as the most effective single-shot signal for anticipating which items wiggle. Taken together, this is the first apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of mechanical, conformity, and persuadability tests in a judging context.

</details>


### [95] [SteerBench-Work: A Benchmark for Agent Steering at Action Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12654)
*Oguz Serdar,Cuneyt Mertayak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SteerBench-Work, a benchmark to evaluate how well long-running workplace LLM agents decide whether to proceed with or hold potentially impactful actions at the commit boundary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents gain the ability to execute high-impact actions via tools (e.g., sending emails, merging code, wiring payments), the crucial safety and productivity question becomes how accurately they "steer" at the commit boundary: deciding when to proceed autonomously vs. hold for human or policy review. Existing evaluations emphasize general capability or refusal behavior, but not the fine-grained calibration of these gate decisions in realistic workplace settings. The authors aim to fill this gap with an incident-grounded, balanced benchmark that measures both types of steering errors: over-refusing safe work and under-refusing unsafe work.

Method: The authors build SteerBench-Work, a bidirectional benchmark of 106 workplace scenarios drawn from real public incidents across domains such as developer operations, customer service, finance, legal, medical, HR, and security. Each scenario includes (1) a proposed agent action and (2) available evidence at the decision point. For each incident, they construct an evidence-reversed mirror (where key risk/evidence conditions are flipped) plus calibration controls. Labels are balanced between "proceed" and "hold" so that both error directions are equally testable. Models are evaluated by providing a gate decision given the action and evidence, and scored on whether they correctly choose to proceed or hold. They test 30 model conditions and compare performance on original incidents vs. evidence-reversed mirrors and risk-resolved commits (where structured or signed evidence has already cleared a real risk trigger).

Result: Across 30 model conditions, errors are highly asymmetric: models incorrectly hold back authorized, evidence-cleared work in 28.1% of cases but only allow unsafe work in 1.0% of cases. Performance is especially poor on risk-resolved commits, where models fail to recognize that a prior risk has been cleared by reliable evidence. Models also perform far worse on evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents (63.8% accuracy) than on the original incident scenarios (98.5% accuracy), suggesting they may be pattern-matching to the narrative of known failures rather than carefully tracking evidence. More capable models tend to over-refuse at the commit boundary, and adding more reasoning helps correct weak gates but doesn’t significantly improve already well-calibrated ones.

Conclusion: Steering calibration at the commit boundary is a distinct dimension of LLM agent performance that is not captured by general capability or overall refusal tendencies. Current models are heavily skewed toward over-refusing safe, authorized work, especially in scenarios where prior risks have been resolved with strong evidence and in mirrored versions of famous incidents. SteerBench-Work provides a structured, incident-anchored evaluation framework and public leaderboard (steerbench.com) to measure and compare this calibration. The findings highlight the need to design and train agents specifically for balanced, evidence-sensitive gate decisions in real-world workplace tools, rather than relying solely on broader capability improvements or generic safety tuning.

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents act through tools, and a single step can send an email, merge a pull request, or wire a payment. The steering decision is the pre-commit choice at that boundary: proceed, or hold for human or policy review. We introduce SteerBench-Work, an incident-anchored, bidirectional benchmark for that decision in workplace agents across developer operations, customer service, finance, legal, medical, HR, and security.
  Release v2026-05 contains 106 scenarios anchored in public incidents, paired evidence-reversed mirrors, and calibration controls, with labels split nearly evenly between proceed and hold so the two error directions get near-identical numbers of chances. A model sees the proposed action and the available evidence, returns a gate decision, and is scored on whether it crosses or holds the boundary correctly. Across 30 model conditions the failures run almost entirely in one direction: models wrongly hold authorized, evidence-cleared work on 28.1% of opportunities and wrongly allow unsafe work on 1.0%. The hardest cases are risk-resolved commits, where signed or structured evidence has already cleared a real risk trigger, and models score markedly worse on evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents (63.8%) than on the incidents themselves (98.5%). General capability is not the same as steering calibration: higher-capability models often over-refuse at the commit boundary, and more reasoning can repair a weak gate while leaving a calibrated one flat. The public leaderboard is at steerbench.com.

</details>


### [96] [General Probabilities of Causation with Causal Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12657)
*Xin Shu,Zhen Lei,Ang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tightens bounds on multivalued probabilities of causation by exploiting additional causal information from covariates and mediators, improving on existing nonbinary results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Probabilities of causation (PN, PS, PNS) quantify individual-level causal attribution but are not point-identifiable and therefore are described by bounds. While sharp bounds exist for binary treatments/outcomes and have been tightened using covariates and mediators, recent work extending PoCs to multivalued settings has not yet fully exploited extra causal information. The authors are motivated to see whether incorporating such information can further tighten bounds for multivalued PoCs, improving causal assessment in richer, nonbinary scenarios.

Method: The authors work in the potential-outcomes/structural causal model framework for multivalued treatments and outcomes. They analytically derive new, tighter inequality constraints for multivalued probabilities of necessity, sufficiency, and necessity-and-sufficiency by incorporating causal information carried by observed covariates and mediators. They compare these new bounds with existing multivalued bounds, and use toy examples to show how incorporating additional causal structure narrows the feasible region of PoCs. Simulation studies are conducted to empirically evaluate and quantify the average tightening in realistic data-generating processes.

Result: The paper provides new closed-form or algorithmically computable bounds for multivalued probabilities of causation that are uniformly tighter (never wider and often substantially narrower) than previously known nonbinary bounds whenever covariate or mediator information is available. Toy examples show visible narrowing of the feasible PoC ranges, and simulation studies confirm that, across a variety of causal models and data-generating mechanisms, the proposed bounds consistently improve identification of multivalued PN, PS, and PNS compared with existing methods.

Conclusion: Additional causal information from covariates and mediators can systematically tighten the partial identification of multivalued probabilities of causation. The authors’ new bounds extend prior binary tightening results to general multivalued settings and dominate existing nonbinary bounds. This enhances the practical utility of PoCs for causal attribution in settings with multi-category exposures or outcomes, suggesting further opportunities to exploit richer causal structure for sharper causal inferences.

Abstract: Probabilities of causation (PoCs) characterize individual causal responses that cannot be directly observed and therefore generally require partial identification. Tian and Pearl first derived theoretically sharp bounds for binary PoCs, including the probability of necessity (PN), the probability of sufficiency (PS), and the probability of necessity and sufficiency (PNS). Mueller et al. subsequently tightened the bounds for binary PNS by incorporating causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. More recently, Li and Pearl, as well as Shu et al., extended PoCs to multivalued settings and derived corresponding theoretical bounds. These developments naturally raise the question of whether additional causal knowledge can further tighten the bounds in multivalued settings. This paper addresses this question by deriving tighter bounds for multivalued PoCs through the incorporation of causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. We illustrate the theoretical results with toy examples, while simulation studies further demonstrate that the proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

</details>


### [97] [Designing AI Pipelines for Decision-Ready ITSM Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12670)
*Archan Dutta,Yash Dharmadhikari,Marat Valiullin,Rahul Guha,Alexander Liss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an AI-powered pipeline that converts messy IT service ticket data into structured, multi-level topics that sales and executive stakeholders can actually use for decisions, and empirically shows these outputs are perceived as interpretable, actionable, and trustworthy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations running ITSM systems have huge, heterogeneous ticket datasets that are difficult for non-technical stakeholders (like sales engineers and executives) to interpret and turn into concrete actions. Existing analytics often fail to bridge the gap between low-level operational data and high-level business decisions, creating a need for methods that transform raw ITSM exports into human-centered, decision-ready insights.

Method: The authors follow a design science research approach to build and iteratively evaluate a sociotechnical AI pipeline. Technically, the pipeline first uses large language models (LLMs) for schema normalization of heterogeneous ITSM exports. Then it applies HDBSCAN to discover coherent sub-topic clusters in the ticket data. Finally, it uses hierarchical agglomerative clustering to aggregate these sub-topics into higher-level Main-topics suitable for executive consumption. The resulting multilevel topic structures are evaluated as decision-support artifacts by five stakeholders (from Sales Engineering and Customer Success) across six generated artifacts, using four metrics: interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use.

Result: Across the stakeholder evaluation, all four decision-support metrics—interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use—achieve mean scores above 4.0 on a 5-point scale. Trust emerges as the most stable and consistent signal among raters, indicating that stakeholders not only understand and can act on the outputs, but also feel confident relying on them. These results empirically support the suitability of the proposed pipeline as an effective decision-support mechanism for ITSM analytics.

Conclusion: The study concludes that transforming raw ITSM ticket data into multilevel, human-centered topics via a sociotechnical AI pipeline can effectively support decision-making for sales and executive stakeholders. The combination of LLM-based schema normalization with HDBSCAN and hierarchical clustering yields interpretable and actionable topic structures that users are willing to trust and adopt. Conceptually, the work reframes ITSM analytics as an Information Systems problem centered on data transformation, abstraction, and human-centric design, rather than purely a technical clustering or NLP challenge.

Abstract: IT service management (ITSM) systems accumulate large volumes of heterogeneous ticket data that are difficult for sales and executive stakeholders to convert into actionable intelligence. This paper presents a sociotechnical AI pipeline, designed and evaluated following design science research principles, that transforms raw ITSM exports into a multilevel decision-support artifact. The pipeline combines LLM-based schema normalization, HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering, and hierarchical agglomerative clustering to generate executive-facing Main-topics and granular Sub-topics. A stakeholder evaluation across six artifacts and five raters from Sales Engineering and customer success roles shows that all four decision-support metrics, interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use, on average exceed 4.0 out of 5.0, with trust as the most consistent signal. The findings position ITSM analytics as an Information Systems (IS) problem of transformation, abstraction, and human-centered design.

</details>


### [98] [On the Expressive Power of Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12671)
*Phokion Kolaitis,Rik Sengupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Overview paper connecting transformer expressivity with circuit complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers are the backbone of modern large language models, but their exact expressive power as formal language recognizers is still not fully understood. The authors want to place transformers within the long-established hierarchy of computational models from theoretical computer science to rigorously measure what they can and cannot compute.

Method: They parameterize transformers by resources such as number of attention heads, layers, and numerical precision, and then systematically compare these parameterizations to known circuit complexity classes parameterized by resources like gate types, circuit size, and depth. Using tools and concepts from circuit complexity, they examine how these resource constraints align with or differ from those of classical Boolean and arithmetic circuits.

Result: They synthesize and present selected theoretical results that connect specific transformer architectures and resource regimes to corresponding circuit classes, thereby mapping out which formal languages and functions such transformers can recognize or compute and where their limitations lie.

Conclusion: Circuit complexity provides an appropriate and powerful framework for rigorously characterizing the expressive power of transformers. By drawing detailed correspondences between transformer resources and circuit parameters, the surveyed results clarify both the strengths and fundamental limits of transformers as language recognizers.

Abstract: Multi-layer transformers form the critical component of essentially all large language models (LLMs) in use today. Because of their ubiquity and computational capability, there is a rapidly growing body of work that aims to precisely calibrate the expressive power of transformers as language recognizers by comparing them against standard models of computation studied for decades by the theoretical computer science community. In this endeavor, circuit complexity has by and large emerged as the "correct" branch of computational complexity to analyze the expressive power of transformers; the reason is that parameterizing transformers by the various resources they use, such as attention and precision, leads to direct comparisons with different classes of circuits parameterized by resources such as type of gates, size, and depth. Here, we present an overview of selected results that delineate the expressive power of transformers using concepts and methods from circuit complexity.

</details>


### [99] [Lines and Ladders: A Context-Aware Multi-Agent Framework for Large-Scale Retail Price Taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12674)
*Ravi Teja Chunduri,Srikaran Reddy Boya,Deep Narayan Mishra,Ajay Kumar B,Karthik Kumaran,Pranay Kona*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM framework builds coherent “Lines and Ladders” pricing taxonomies at scale for large retail catalogs, significantly improving price consistency metrics over single-agent approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global retailers need consistent, strategy-aligned pricing (e.g., Every Day Low Price) across millions of SKUs. Manual governance of price relationships is impossible at this scale, leading to inconsistent prices between item variants, distorted value perception, and cannibalization of sales. There is a need for an automated, scalable, and context-aware system that can infer and maintain coherent pricing structures across heterogeneous, often unstructured, product catalogs.

Method: The paper proposes a scalable, context-aware Multi-Agent Framework based on large language models. Specialized agents collaboratively construct “Lines and Ladders” pricing taxonomies. The agents: (1) identify key product attributes, (2) extract multi-modal attribute values, and (3) apply hierarchical grouping logic to define price lines and ladders across related items. The architecture uses three LLM agents whose division of labor reduces cognitive overload compared with a single monolithic agent. The framework is evaluated on real-world enterprise data and deployed in production.

Result: The 3-agent system achieves an F1-score of 0.83 for detecting/constructing Lines, outperforming single-agent baselines. In the Food & Consumables category it attains over 90% precision and over 75% recall, and in the more heterogeneous General Merchandise catalog it achieves 80.2% item-to-structure assignment accuracy. The experiments show that the multi-agent decomposition yields better performance than a single LLM agent under the same task.

Conclusion: Automating construction of “Lines and Ladders” pricing taxonomies with a context-aware multi-agent LLM framework is both feasible and effective at enterprise scale. The multi-agent design mitigates cognitive overload, improves precision/recall and assignment accuracy over single-agent baselines, and can be reliably deployed in production to enforce price consistency and support Every Day Low Price strategies across large, heterogeneous retail catalogs.

Abstract: Maintaining price consistency and executing an Every Day Low Price strategy is critical for global retailers. However, with catalogs spanning millions of active items, manual governance of price relationships is infeasible. Inconsistent pricing across item variants distorts customer value perception and cannibalizes sales. To address this, we present a scalable, context-aware Multi-Agent Framework designed to automate the construction of "Lines and Ladders" pricing taxonomies. Our framework employs specialized LLM agents to construct these coherent pricing structures by identifying key attributes, extracting multi-modal values, and applying hierarchical grouping logic. Evaluated on real-world enterprise data and deployed in production, our 3-Agent system achieves an F1-score of 0.83 for Lines, outperforming single-agent baselines by mitigating cognitive overload. The system achieves >90% precision and >75% recall in Food & Consumables, and 80.2% assignment accuracy in the unstructured General Merchandise catalog.

</details>


### [100] [Privacy-Preserving RAG by Concealing Sensitive Information from External LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12675)
*Saleh Almohaimeed,Saad Almohaimeed,Mousa Jari,Fahad Alotaibi,Khalid A. Alobaid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes SEAG, a framework that replaces sensitive entities in user queries and retrieved documents with aliases before sending them to external LLM generators, maintaining privacy while preserving answer quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While RAG improves LLM performance, it introduces a privacy gap: external third-party generators can see user queries and retrieved documents that may contain confidential information. Existing work mainly protects against unauthorized data access, not against the generator itself possibly misusing or retaining sensitive content. The authors aim to let users use powerful external LLMs without revealing their sensitive entities.

Method: They design SEAG (Sensitive Entity Alias Generator), which uses a lightweight model to: (1) detect sensitive entities in queries and retrieved documents, (2) generate aliases for each detected entity, and (3) build an entity replacement table. The system then replaces all sensitive entities with their aliases before sending text to the external generator, and can map aliases back afterward.
