They construct two datasets: one for fine-tuning SEAG models on entity replacement table generation, and another for evaluating the full framework’s privacy and utility. They test multiple base models (e.g., Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, Phi-4) as SEAG components.

Result: In user-centric evaluation (“User” metric), all SEAG models exceeded 80% accuracy, meaning users still received correct answers while sensitive information remained hidden from the external generator. In an entity-hiding evaluation on documents, SEAG running on Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 achieved total accuracies of 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91% respectively in masking all sensitive entities.

Conclusion: SEAG effectively enables privacy-preserving use of third-party LLMs within RAG by aliasing sensitive entities in both queries and retrieved documents. It balances privacy and utility: it hides most sensitive entities while still enabling high answer accuracy, demonstrating that lightweight, local preprocessing can mitigate the privacy risks inherent in sending raw RAG context to external generators.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is widely used to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in answering user queries. Existing privacy research on RAG has focused on preventing unauthorized users from accessing sensitive data. However, another important problem that is often overlooked in RAG privacy research is that external generators have access to the query and the retrieved documents, which may contain confidential information that could potentially be misused or accessed for unintended purposes. In this paper, we introduce the Sensitive Entity Alias Generator (SEAG), a privacy-preserving framework that empowers users to utilize powerful third-party generators without disclosing sensitive information. SEAG introduces a lightweight model that locates sensitive entities, generates corresponding aliases, and constructs an entity replacement table. The table is used to replace sensitive words in the user's query and in the retrieved documents before they are forwarded to an external generator. For this purpose, two datasets were constructed: one for fine-tuning SEAG models to generate entity replacement tables, and another for evaluating the entire SEAG framework. The experimental results demonstrate the success of the SEAG framework. As for the User metric, which measures the ability of the model to provide a correct response to the user while hiding sensitive information from the external generator, all SEAG models achieved over 80% accuracy. Additional analysis further evaluated the ability of SEAG models Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 to hide all sensitive entities within given documents. The results show good performance with total accuracies of 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91%, respectively.

</details>


### [101] [The Role of Natural Language Understanding in Multimodal Video-Based Dengue Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12677)
*Danial Sharifrazi,Saadat Behzadi,Julakha Jahan Jui,Mojtaba Mohammadi,Nouman Javed,Roohallah Alizadehsani,Prasad N. Paradkar,Asim Bhatti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a YOLO+CLIP vision-language system to distinguish videos of dengue-infected vs. uninfected mosquitoes and achieve near-perfect classification, showing VLMs are useful for infection-related behavior analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatically detecting subtle, infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes from video is hard due to their small size, fast/irregular motion, and environmental noise (background, lighting, shadows). Traditional feature engineering and standard vision models struggle with robustly extracting informative features under these conditions, especially when the goal is to distinguish infected from uninfected individuals. There is a need for a framework that can leverage semantic, biologically meaningful descriptions of behavior to improve discrimination and interpretability.

Method: They propose a vision-language framework combining YOLO for detecting and cropping mosquito regions from video frames with CLIP for joint image-text representation learning. First, YOLO isolates mosquitoes from complex backgrounds. Then, video frame crops are encoded by the CLIP visual encoder, while biologically meaningful textual prompts describing mosquito infection states/behaviors are encoded by the CLIP text encoder. They fine-tune this multimodal model on their labeled dataset using supervised bidirectional contrastive learning in the shared embedding space so that frames of infected vs. uninfected mosquitoes align with their corresponding textual prompts. Classification is performed by image-text similarity in the embedding space, and they also compare to a vision-only model and conduct ablations on fine-tuning and representation choices. Temporal aggregation of frame-level predictions is used for video-level decisions.

Result: At the frame level, the proposed model achieved 98.54% accuracy and 99.91% sensitivity in distinguishing DENV2-infected from uninfected mosquito flight frames. After aggregating predictions temporally across frames, the system reached perfect performance at the video level. Ablation studies showed that both fine-tuning the VLM and using CLIP-based multimodal representations were critical to achieving high performance in this domain. However, the textual (language) branch mainly contributed semantic alignment between images and text and interpretability, without providing a clear accuracy gain over a well-tuned vision-only baseline.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that a YOLO+CLIP-based vision-language framework can very accurately classify infection status of mosquitoes from flight videos and that such models are well-suited to capturing infection-related behavioral differences under challenging imaging conditions. Fine-tuned CLIP representations are important for performance, and while the language branch improves semantic alignment and interpretability, raw accuracy gains over vision-only models are limited. Overall, vision-language models appear to be a promising tool for automated, biologically informed analysis of infection-related behaviors in video data.

Abstract: Detecting infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes from video data is challenging because mosquitoes are small, move rapidly and irregularly, and are affected by environmental factors such as background, lighting, and shadows, which can make reliable feature extraction difficult. In this study, a YOLO- and Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP)-based vision-language framework is proposed to classify mosquito flight frames of uninfected and Dengue virus serotype 2 (DENV2)-infected mosquitoes. First, YOLO is used to isolate mosquito regions from the background. Then, visual features extracted from video frames are aligned with biologically meaningful textual prompts in a shared embedding space. The multimodal model was fine-tuned using supervised bidirectional contrastive learning and evaluated through frame-level image-text similarity-based classification. The results show that the proposed method achieved 98.54% accuracy and 99.91% sensitivity at the frame level. After temporal aggregation of frame-level information, the model achieved complete video-level performance. The ablation results showed that fine-tuning and CLIP-based representations were essential for this domain, while the textual branch provided semantic image-text alignment rather than an accuracy advantage over the vision-only model. These findings suggest that vision-language models can provide a useful framework for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data.

</details>


### [102] [Beyond the Best Guess: Improving LLM Solution Coverage with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12679)
*Conor F. Hayes,Elliot Meyerson,Kajetan Schweighofer,Roberto Dailey,Babak Hodjat,Risto Miikkulainen,Xin Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares Reinforcement Learning (RL) and Evolution Strategies (ES) as post-training methods for LLMs in discovery tasks, showing ES yields higher pass@k and broader solution coverage, improving performance on math benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs used in math and science are typically evaluated by a single best-guess answer, but discovery tasks benefit from generating many diverse candidate solutions (high pass@k). Standard RL post-training tends to narrow the model’s output distribution around high-reward responses, reducing diversity and harming solution coverage. The authors seek a post-training method that preserves or enhances diversity while improving performance.

Method: The authors apply Evolution Strategies, a gradient-free, population-based optimization method, to fine-tune LLM weights via random perturbations and fitness evaluation. They compare ES-based post-training against RL-based post-training on discovery-style tasks, measuring pass@k and analyzing the resulting output distributions. Benchmarks include standard math tasks where diverse solution coverage is important.

Result: ES consistently achieves higher pass@k scores than RL on the evaluated tasks and maintains a broader output distribution. This broader distribution corresponds to greater coverage of the solution space, leading to better performance on standard math benchmarks compared with RL-post-trained models.

Conclusion: ES is a more suitable post-training approach than RL for LLMs in discovery domains where diverse solution coverage is essential. By directly optimizing in weight space and preserving distributional breadth, ES supports better pass@k performance and improved benchmark results, making it a stronger foundation for post-training in math, science, and similar exploratory tasks.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in discovery domains such as math and science. The usual approach is to present the problem to the model and use its answer as the proposed solution. However, beyond this best guess, discovery can be enhanced by increasing test-time compute. In a process called pass@k, the model is allowed to explore the solution space and generate diverse candidate solutions. Unfortunately, the standard approach to post-training LLMs through Reinforcement Learning (RL) may limit pass@k: the model's output distribution narrows around high-reward outputs, causing the solution coverage to collapse. The alternative is to use Evolution Strategies (ES), a population-based, gradient-free post-training method that optimizes directly in weight space through random perturbations. As this paper shows, ES achieves consistently higher pass@k than RL and produces a broader output distribution with greater solution coverage. This coverage in turn makes it possible to achieve better results in e.g. standard math benchmarks. Thus, ES provides a better foundation for post-training in discovery problems and other domains where diverse solution coverage is critical.

</details>


### [103] [Spatial Memory Agent: Experience-Grounded Procedure Memory for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12743)
*Haokai Zhang,Yuhang Ding,Yunshu Zhou,Xinze Du,Shengtao Zhang,Zhiyue Zhao,Yuling Xi,Hao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Spatial Memory Agent (SMA), a parameter-update-free framework that lets frozen vision-language models (VLMs) improve spatial reasoning by storing and reusing verified spatial experiences as lessons, without external spatial tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods to enhance spatial reasoning in vision-language models either (1) modify model parameters via post-training (SFT, RL) or (2) rely on external spatial tools (depth estimators, 3D recon). Both have drawbacks: retraining is costly and model-specific, and external tools add complexity, latency, and tool-dependence at inference time. The authors want a third route: let a frozen VLM agent self-improve its spatial reasoning through experience during interaction, without changing its weights or calling expert tools, making improvement cheaper, more general, and easier to deploy.

Method: They introduce Spatial Memory Agent (SMA), an experience-grounded runtime framework that sits on top of a frozen VLM. In a verifiable spatial environment, SMA repeatedly: (1) queries the VLM for an answer, (2) obtains a reward via an environment verifier, and (3) performs verifier-guided reflection to turn each (state, answer, reward) into a compact, transferable lesson describing spatial reasoning patterns. Every lesson receives a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS), initialized uniformly. Over time, SMA updates TRS using evidence from how often lessons lead to successful transfers in later tasks. At deployment, in read-only mode (no more writing/updating of lessons), SMA retrieves relevant lessons using semantic filtering and a combined similarity+TRS ranking; the retrieved lessons are provided as contextual guidance to the frozen VLM to improve its spatial predictions.

Result: Across five diverse spatial benchmarks and four different base VLMs, SMA consistently improves spatial reasoning performance. It attains the highest macro average performance for each base-model group and achieves the top accuracy in most of the 20 evaluation settings when compared to other methods, showing that its parameter-update-free, tool-free self-evolution is effective across models and environments.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that frozen VLM agents can substantially enhance their spatial reasoning through parameter-update-free self-evolution, using only a runtime memory of distilled spatial lessons and no external spatial tools. Spatial Memory Agent provides a practical and general framework for experience-grounded improvement that transfers across benchmarks and model scales, indicating a promising direction for scalable, low-cost enhancement of spatial intelligence in embodied and multimodal agents.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence is becoming a foundation for embodied agents, robotic planning, and multimodal assistants. To improve the spatial reasoning ability of VLM agents, existing work has mainly followed two lines. One line uses post-training methods, such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Another line adopts an agentic paradigm in which the model calls external spatial tools, such as depth estimation and 3D reconstruction tools, to gather intermediate spatial evidence. We study a complementary and underexplored route: Can a frozen VLM agent improve its spatial reasoning through \textbf{parameter-update-free self-evolution}, without depending on external expert spatial tools at inference time? We present \textbf{Spatial Memory Agent (SMA)}, an \textbf{experience-grounded runtime framework} that converts verified spatial experience into reusable transferable lessons. In a verifiable spatial environment, SMA queries the frozen VLM, obtains a predicted answer and reward, and uses \textbf{verifier-guided reflection} to distill compact transferable lessons from spatial experience. SMA further assigns each lesson a \textbf{Transfer Reliability Score (TRS)}, which is initialized uniformly and calibrated from later retrieval outcomes as visit evidence of future transfer reliability. During \textbf{read-only deployment}, SMA retrieves lessons by semantic filter and similarity-TRS combined ranking, allowing the retrieved memory to guide frozen model inference. Across five representative spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and the best accuracy among the evaluated methods in most of the 20 evaluations, establishing a practical parameter-update-free path for spatial self-evolution across the evaluated frozen model scales and environments.

</details>


### [104] [Correct Is Not Governed: Provenance Integrity in Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12761)
*Jesus Salas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Matrix, a system for governed execution that tracks provenance, authority, and dependencies in agentic workflows to ensure actions are auditable, properly authorized, and robust to change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of agentic workflows focus on whether they reach correct outcomes, which is inadequate in institutional settings. In such environments, an action can look correct but be invalid because it relies on the wrong authority, lacks supporting evidence for completion, or becomes outdated due to subsequent changes. Institutions need mechanisms that make agentic work auditable, attributable to proper authority, and resilient to evolving states.

Method: The authors define the concept of governed execution, where every decision, claim of completion, and response to change is backed by inspectable provenance. They implement Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that: (1) records authority and fact dependencies for each workflow step, (2) verifies evidence that a task is truly complete, and (3) selectively invalidates only the work affected by later changes. They run controlled comparisons between governed workflows using Matrix and direct (ungoverned) workflows, and then design a role-separated transfer challenge to test how a strict completeness contract behaves when artifacts are moved across contexts.

Result: In controlled experiments, both governed and direct workflows frequently produced the same final outcomes. However, the governed workflows using Matrix consistently preserved governing evidence, avoided accepting unsupported task completion, and limited recovery efforts to only those tasks causally dependent on invalidated work. In the role-separated transfer challenge, a deterministic completeness contract in Matrix caused severe over-blocking when synthetic packets were generated outside of the original authoring context, revealing limits and failure modes of strict governance in cross-context use.

Conclusion: Matrix should not be seen as a general mechanism to improve accuracy of agentic workflows. Instead, its core value is as an institutional integrity layer that makes agentic work auditable, attributable to appropriate authority, and independently verifiable. The system enforces provenance-aware execution, robust completion checking, and principled invalidation in response to change, addressing institutional needs around compliance, accountability, and trustworthy automation rather than raw task performance.

Abstract: Agentic workflows are commonly evaluated by whether they reach the correct outcome. That is insufficient in institutional settings, where a correct action may rely on the wrong authority, an unsupported completion claim, or work made stale by a later change. We define governed execution as work whose decisions, completion, and response to change are supported by inspectable provenance. We present Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that records authority and fact dependencies, verifies completion evidence, and selectively invalidates affected work. Across controlled comparisons, governed and direct workflows often reached the same outcomes, but only the governed path consistently preserved governing evidence, refused unsupported closure, and limited recovery to dependent tasks. A role-separated transfer challenge then failed: a deterministically enforced completeness contract severely over-blocked synthetic packets produced outside its authoring context. These results do not establish Matrix as a general accuracy enhancer; they support its primary role as an institutional integrity layer for making agentic work auditable and independently verifiable.

</details>


### [105] [PROVE-RT: Generating Mechanized Theorem Prover Scripts for Real-Time Systems using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12762)
*Sadat Shahriyar,Shareef Ahmed,Abdullah Al Arafat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents PROVE-RT, an LLM-assisted framework that generates PROSA/ROCQ mechanized proofs for schedulability analysis, significantly improving success rates over direct LLM prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing schedulability analyses for real-time systems rely on pen-and-paper proofs, which are hard to scale, validate, and maintain. Mechanized verification with PROSA/ROCQ is rigorous but requires deep expertise and heavy manual proof engineering. While LLMs are promising for automating proof generation, they lack PROSA-specific knowledge and often fail when used via naive prompting, creating a need for a targeted, guided approach.

Method: The authors design PROVE-RT, a framework that assists LLMs in generating correct PROSA/ROCQ scripts. The method includes: (1) dependency-aware informal proof sketches to structure the reasoning; (2) retrieval of relevant processed PROSA documentation to inject domain-specific knowledge; (3) staged generation of proof skeletons before full proofs; and (4) a proof-completion phase. They also build a large mechanization-oriented corpus from 1,191 real-time systems papers, containing 13,134 informal sketches with dependency annotations, which supports the retrieval and guidance components.

Result: On a curated evaluation set of schedulability analyses, directly prompting state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably produce valid PROSA mechanizations, often resulting in incorrect or incomplete proofs. In contrast, PROVE-RT achieves a 44.7% success rate in generating valid mechanized proofs, demonstrating a substantial improvement over unguided LLM baselines.

Conclusion: PROVE-RT shows that combining retrieval of specialized documentation, dependency-aware proof sketches, and staged proof generation allows LLMs to more effectively mechanize schedulability analyses in PROSA/ROCQ. The evidence suggests that domain-structured, retrieval-guided and staged assistance is a promising path for automating mechanized verification in real-time systems and potentially other formal methods domains.

Abstract: Schedulability analysis is essential for certifying real-time systems, but existing tests are often developed through pen-and-paper proofs that are difficult to scale, validate, and maintain. Mechanized verification in PROSA/ROCQ offers a rigorous alternative, yet manually constructing such proofs requires substantial domain expertise and proof-engineering effort. Recent successes of large language models (LLMs) across a wide range of tasks make them promising candidates for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts for mechanized theorem provers. However, state-of-the-art LLMs often lack the PROSA-specific knowledge required to correctly use its modeling abstractions and proof patterns.
  This paper introduces PROVE-RT, an LLM-assisted framework for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts to mechanize schedulability analyses in real-time systems literature. PROVE-RT guides generation through dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion. We construct a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1, 191 real-time systems papers, containing 13, 134 informal sketches with dependency information. On a curated evaluation set, direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably generate valid PROSA mechanizations, whereas PROVE-RT achieves a success rate of 44.7%. These results show that retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can improve automated mechanization of schedulability analysis in PROSA/ROCQ.

</details>


### [106] [ARAC: Benchmarking Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness on End-to-End Researchs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12788)
*Jiale Cui,Yueyao Yuan,Kaixi Zhong,Xiaogang Xu,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARAC-Bench is an evaluation framework for auto-research systems that focuses on how closely their research process matches human researchers, rather than only judging final answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of autonomous research systems mostly compare final outcomes or answers, ignoring whether the intermediate reasoning process, experimental planning, and synthesis resemble rigorous human research behavior. With the rise of Auto-Research, there is a need for a benchmark that measures alignment with human-like research trajectories, logical coherence, and completeness across the full research pipeline.

Method: The authors design ARAC-Bench, a Researcher-Mimicking Evaluation framework composed of two main parts: (1) an Academic Cognition Skills system that converts expert reviewer knowledge into explicit, stage-specific quantitative rubrics, and (2) a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol that breaks research into modular, independently evaluated stages: Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis. They then apply this benchmark to 11 state-of-the-art auto-research frameworks and compare the benchmark’s model rankings with rankings of Ph.D. candidates.

Result: When 11 SOTA auto-research frameworks are evaluated with ARAC-Bench, the best system achieves only 67.9 out of 100 on alignment with human research processes, exposing a considerable performance gap. Correlation analysis between ARAC-Bench scores and Ph.D. candidate rankings yields a high correlation coefficient of 0.8141, indicating that ARAC-Bench’s evaluation closely tracks human judgments about valued research skills.

Conclusion: ARAC-Bench effectively captures key dimensions of research quality that humans care about, including alignment with human methodology, logical coherence, and completeness across the proposal–experiment–synthesis pipeline. It serves both as a detailed diagnostic tool for analyzing strengths and weaknesses of auto-research systems and as a scalable, process-level reward signal to guide training of future autonomous research agents.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of Auto-Research has surfaced a fundamental evaluation challenge: how can we measure the alignment, logical coherence, and evolutionary completeness of its research trajectory with human research behavior? We propose Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness, ARAC-Bench: a Researcher-Mimicking Evaluation framework that shifts the objective from matching final answers to reproducing high-quality human research processes. The framework operates through two synergistic components: the Academic Cognition Skills system, which is the first to transforms implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated, quantifiable rubrics; and a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol, which decomposes the research process under strict modular constraints into three traceable, mutually independent dimensions: Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis. Systematic evaluation of 11 SOTA frameworks yields a best alignment score of only 67.9 of 100, revealing a significant gap in simulating rigorous human methodology. Validation against Ph.D. Candidates rankings shows a strong correlation of 0.8141, confirming that ARAC-Bench reliably reflects the dimensions researchers truly value. ARAC-Bench provides not only a fine-grained diagnostic tool but also a scalable reward signal for training the next generation of autonomous research systems.

</details>


### [107] [CABS+: Efficient and Scalable Model Merging via Conflict-Aware Sparsification and Adaptive Weight Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12842)
*Yuchen Liu,Zongzhen Yang,Binhang Qi,Hailong Sun,Xiang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CABS+, an improved model merging framework that mitigates parameter conflicts across tasks while being more efficient and robust than prior methods like CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing model merging methods such as CABS can construct multi-task models without retraining but suffer from parameter conflicts, exponential-time grid search for scaling coefficients, and an optimization objective dominated by strong tasks, which harms overall multi-task performance. There is a need for a more efficient, fair, and principled way to merge models and to quantify when models are even mergeable.

Method: CABS+ extends CABS with three main components: (1) Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA), which uses a gradient-free search strategy to optimize scaling/merging coefficients, avoiding exponential-time grid search; (2) an asymmetric fitness function that emphasizes balanced improvements across tasks instead of letting high-performing tasks dominate; and (3) a systematic empirical study of merging factors plus the introduction of Relative Synergy Score (RSS), a metric to measure how mergeable models are and to guide model selection before merging.

Result: Across 27 datasets and 5 models (covering LLMs, smaller language models, and vision models), CABS+ outperforms state-of-the-art merging methods. It improves average performance by 16.97% over AdaMerging and 12.93% over WUDIMerging, is more stable and robust to task/model variations, uses under 25% of the GPU memory required by AdaMerging, and delivers roughly a 4x speedup in merging time compared with WUDIMerging.

Conclusion: CABS+ is an effective and efficient extension of CABS for model merging, providing better multi-task performance, fairness across tasks, and practical advantages in memory and speed. The introduced RSS metric further offers a principled way to assess model mergeability and select suitable base models before merging.

Abstract: Model merging has recently attracted significant attention as a promising paradigm for constructing unified multi-task models without requiring additional retraining. However, parameter conflicts and knowledge interference across tasks often degrade merged-model performance. Prior work introduced Conflict-Aware and Balanced Sparsification (CABS), which reduces parameter interference through structured pruning and sequential masking. However, CABS relies on grid search to determine scaling coefficients, resulting in exponential time complexity, while its optimization objective can be dominated by high-performance tasks, leading to suboptimal overall performance. To address these limitations, we extend CABS and propose CABS+. Specifically, Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA) optimizes merging coefficients via a gradient-free search scheme to reduce time complexity, while an asymmetric fitness function promotes more comprehensive performance gains across tasks. Moreover, we conduct a systematic empirical study of key factors influencing model merging performance and propose Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify model mergeability and guide model selection. We compare CABS+ with state-of-the-art model merging methods, including CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging, across 27 datasets and 5 models covering large language, small-scale language, and vision models. Extensive experiments verify the effectiveness and efficiency of CABS+. Compared with AdaMerging and WUDIMerging, CABS+ improves overall performance by 16.97% and 12.93%, respectively, exhibits stronger stability and robustness across varying task numbers and model architectures, uses less than 25% of the GPU memory required by AdaMerging, and achieves nearly a 4x speedup in merging time over WUDIMerging.

</details>


### [108] [Beyond Retrieval: Query-Conditioned Reuse of Long-Horizon Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12847)
*Yifei Li,Heng Wang,Lingling Zhang,Muye Huang,Xinyu Zhang,Jiashuai Liu,Hang Yan,Rongman Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to best reuse retrieved long-horizon trajectories for agents, proposing an evaluation framework and a simple query-conditioned reuse (QCR) memory format that outperforms raw trajectory injection while using fewer tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval systems can surface relevant past trajectories, but they do not specify how an agent should effectively reuse those trajectories when task details or environment states differ. Directly injecting full past trajectories becomes inefficient and brittle as trajectories get longer or conditions change, creating a bottleneck in long-horizon memory. The authors want to isolate and rigorously evaluate this post-retrieval reuse step, separating it from retrieval quality itself.

Method: The authors introduce an evaluation framework that keeps retrieval, target state, base model, decoding strategy, and tool budget fixed and instead varies how retrieved experience is formatted and presented as support to the agent. Within this framework they instantiate Query-Conditioned Reuse (QCR), a simple, target-bound note format that encodes a reusable procedure along with variable bindings, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. They compare QCR against full trajectory injection and other baselines across multiple benchmarks (WebArena, WorkArena, AppWorld), and analyze performance by trajectory length and degree of source-target binding shift. They also study summary reranking to select the most relevant reusable memory for each target.

Result: Across 2,391 target instances, QCR achieves an average success rate of 62.3%, which is 10.7 percentage points higher than using the full retrieved trajectory, while also reducing online token usage by 48.9%. Their summary-reranking mechanism is able to select an appropriate reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, leading to end-task success that is within 1.8 percentage points of an oracle selector. Analyses indicate that full trajectory injection degrades significantly as trajectories lengthen or when specific bound values differ between source and target, whereas QCR-style target-bound notes retain a larger portion of the potential benefit.

Conclusion: The work shows that the main bottleneck in using long-horizon trajectory memories is not just retrieval quality but the post-retrieval reuse mechanism. Their framework makes it possible to evaluate this reuse step independently. The proposed QCR format, while deliberately simple and not claimed as universally optimal, demonstrates that concise, target-bound, explicitly conditioned support can outperform naive full-trajectory injection in both effectiveness and efficiency. Thus, transforming retrieved experience into structured, reusable guidance is key to safely and effectively leveraging past trajectories for new tasks.

Abstract: Retrieval can identify a past trajectory that may matter, yet it does not specify how an acting agent should use that trajectory after users, entities, constraints, or environment state have changed. We identify this post-retrieval reuse step as a distinct bottleneck for long-horizon trajectory memory and formulate an evaluation framework that holds candidate retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget fixed while varying the support delivered to the agent. We instantiate the framework with query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a deliberately simple target-bound note that records a reusable procedure, bindings to recover, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. QCR serves to test the reuse hypothesis rather than to claim a universally preferred memory format. Across 2,391 target instances in WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld, QCR reaches 62.3% average Success, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, while using 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selects a reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, placing end-task Success within 1.8 points of an oracle reusable selector. Analyses by trajectory length and source--target binding shift show that direct trajectory injection loses much of its utility as traces grow longer or source-specific values change, whereas target-bound support preserves a larger share of the measured gain. The resulting framework separates retrieval quality from the problem of turning retrieved experience into safe, useful support for a new task.

</details>


### [109] [Practice Makes Unsafe: Skill Misevolution in Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12851)
*Xutao Mao,Liangjie Zhao,Xiang Zheng,Cong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how self-improving LLM agents can “evolve” unsafe skills from past unsafe but successful trajectories, proposes a benchmark and framework to measure this lifecycle risk, and introduces a safety wrapper that mitigates it with minimal utility loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-improving LLM agents store successful trajectories as reusable skills or state. If an agent succeeds in a task using an unsafe method (e.g., harmful instructions), that unsafe behavior can be encoded into its persistent memory and later reused in different tasks, even when the original triggering input is gone. Existing safety evaluations mainly check current behavior or static models and cannot attribute or track risks that arise as skills are authored, stored, retrieved, and executed over time. The authors want to expose and quantify this lifecycle risk of "skill misevolution" and investigate safety mechanisms that control how persistent skill state is written and reused.

Method: 1) Define "skill evolution" as distilling operational trajectories from LLM agents into executable, transferable, inspectable procedures that can be reused across tasks and sessions. 2) Introduce SkillMisevo-Gym, a lifecycle-aware harness that versions and tracks skill state across multiple agent frameworks, enabling measurement at different stages: authoring, retrieval, and execution. 3) Introduce SkillMisevo-Bench, a benchmark consisting of a predesigned set of malicious exposure tasks and benign carryover tasks, along with concept-aligned benign tasks and nine metrics that capture safety and behavior across the lifecycle. 4) Conduct a large-scale evaluation over 25 agent-method configurations, each evaluated on 525 tasks across 25 episodes, to see how often unsafe artifacts are authored, retrieved, and cause harm in fresh sessions. 5) Propose SafeEvolve, a wrapper mechanism that (a) repairs or filters unsafe content during skill creation and (b) governs how evolved skills can be retrieved and reused, with the goal of reducing unsafe reuse while preserving task performance.

Result: Empirically, all 21 configurations that include skill evolution produce unsafe artifacts at the authoring stage, showing that skill misevolution is pervasive. However, only 15 of these configurations cause actual harm in fresh sessions, indicating that not all unsafe artifacts propagate to realized unsafe behavior. In the exposure sweep, introducing just three malicious tasks more than doubles carryover attack success rate (ASR), from 16.0% to 35.3%, demonstrating that a small number of unsafe exposures can significantly contaminate persistent skills. SafeEvolve, applied to representative skill evolution methods, reduces unsafe retrieval by 26.7 percentage points and reduces fresh-session harm by 17.3 percentage points, while reducing benign-task performance by only 0.4 points on average, suggesting strong safety gains with minimal utility loss.

Conclusion: Self-improving LLM agents are vulnerable to "skill misevolution," where unsafe but successful experiences become persistent, reusable unsafe skills. Safety cannot focus solely on immediate outputs or static models; it must regulate the entire lifecycle of skill evolution—what gets written into persistent state and how later agents retrieve and execute these skills. The proposed SkillMisevo-Gym and SkillMisevo-Bench enable systematic, lifecycle-aware evaluation of these risks, and the SafeEvolve wrapper shows that it is feasible to substantially mitigate unsafe skill reuse with negligible impact on benign performance. The overarching message is that persistent adaptation mechanisms in agents need dedicated safety governance at the write and reuse stages to prevent long-lived unsafe behavior.

Abstract: Self-improving LLM agents convert successful trajectories into persistent cross-task state. An unsafe success can thereby become reusable policy after its triggering input disappears. Skill evolution makes this failure measurable by distilling operational trajectories into executable, transferable, and inspectable procedures. Because evolution optimizes task outcomes rather than procedure safety, compromised experience can cause skill misevolution. Existing benchmarks measure current behavior or static artifacts but cannot attribute risk across authoring, retrieval, and later execution. To expose this lifecycle, we introduce SkillMisevo-Gym, a lifecycle-aware harness that versions skill state across agent frameworks, and SkillMisevo-Bench, a frozen design from malicious exposure to carryover tasks, with concept-aligned benign tasks and nine lifecycle metrics. We also introduce SafeEvolve, a wrapper that repairs unsafe content and governs subsequent reuse. Across 25 agent-method configurations, each covering 525 tasks in 25 episodes, all 21 evolved configurations author unsafe artifacts, while only fifteen lead to fresh-session harm. In the exposure sweep, three malicious tasks raise carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. Across representative skill evolution methods, SafeEvolve reduces unsafe retrieval and fresh-session harm by 26.7 and 17.3 percentage points, respectively, while mean benign utility changes by only 0.4 points. Together, persistent-adaptation safety must govern what updates write and what future executors reuse. Code is available at https://github.com/henrymao2004/misevolve.

</details>


### [110] [AI and Consumer Rights in India Working Paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12863)
*Omir Kumar,Sriya Sridhar,Vibhav Mithal,Balaraman Ravindran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper assesses how well India’s Consumer Protection Act, 2019 can handle harms caused by AI products and services and finds it partially suitable but with important gaps, especially around causation and allocating liability in complex AI value chains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With AI increasingly embedded in consumer products and services, Indian law must determine who is responsible when AI causes harm. The Consumer Protection Act, 2019 is the central statute for consumer harms, but it was not written with AI in mind. The paper is motivated by the need to evaluate whether this existing framework can be stretched to cover AI incidents and whether liability is fairly distributed across the many actors involved in creating and deploying AI.

Method: The paper undertakes a doctrinal and conceptual legal analysis of the Consumer Protection Act, 2019. It interprets the Act’s key definitions—product liability, harm, deficiency—and systematically maps them onto common AI‑related harms and the AI value chain (data providers, model developers, deployers, users). It then identifies mismatches and implementation problems, especially regarding proof of causation and role classifications.

Result: The analysis finds that the Act’s broad, technology‑neutral language is in principle capable of covering a wide range of AI‑related harms, including physical injury, psychological harm, biased or discriminatory outputs, and loss of control. However, the paper identifies major practical gaps: (i) establishing causation between an AI system’s design or behaviour and consumer harm is technically and evidentially difficult, and (ii) the Act’s tripartite structure of manufacturer–seller–service provider does not correspond well to the overlapping and distributed responsibilities in AI ecosystems, leaving multi‑actor harms under‑addressed.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that while India’s Consumer Protection Act, 2019 can formally apply to AI products and services due to its broad definitions, it is not yet well‑equipped to handle complex, multi‑stakeholder AI harms in a proportionate way. Effective enforcement will require clarifying how AI actors fit within existing legal categories, addressing causation challenges, and coordinating with sector‑specific regulations to avoid gaps and overlaps in liability.

Abstract: As AI systems proliferate in consumer facing applications, questions about liability for AI related harms remain unresolved. This working paper examines whether India's Consumer Protection Act, 2019, adequately addresses harm caused by defective AI products and services, and whether it proportionately allocates liability across the AI value chain.
  The Act's broad definitions of product liability, harm, and deficiency appear technology agnostic and potentially applicable to AI related incidents including personal injury, psychological harm, biased outputs, and loss of control. However, significant gaps remain. Proving causation between AI defects and consumer harm presents a technical challenge, as AI failures often stem from design choices rather than discrete defects. Additionally, the Act's framework assumes distinct roles for manufacturers, sellers, and service providers, yet the AI value chain involves overlapping responsibilities among data providers, model developers, deployers, and users that do not neatly map to these categories. Current liability frameworks lack proportionate mechanisms to effectively address complex, multistakeholder AI harms. While the Act may cover AI entities, enforcement requires clarification on sector specific overlaps.

</details>


### [111] [ReflectFact: Self-Reflective Agents for Improving Comprehension and Reasoning in Multi-Hop Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12877)
*Runze Zhao,Zixin Tang,Xiaoshuai Hao,Leyuan Chang,Xiaopeng Fu,Boyu Qiao,Dongyang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReflectFact is a self-reflective single-agent framework that plans, verifies, and reflects on multi-hop reasoning paths to improve evidence-grounded fact verification, achieving new SOTA on HOVER and EX-FEVER.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-hop fact verification on social media is hard because it requires reasoning over multiple evidence pieces. Existing multi-agent approaches decompose tasks across specialized agents, but each agent often lacks awareness of the global verification goal and can be misled by its own parametric knowledge instead of the given evidence, leading to reasoning drift and incorrect verdicts.

Method: ReflectFact uses a single self-reflective agent with three explicit tasks. (1) Explicit Reasoning Path Planning: resolve implicit entities in the claim, decompose the claim into sub-questions, and chain verified sub-facts into a final verdict, forming an explicit multi-hop reasoning path. (2) Evidence-Drift Verification: detect when an answer is overly influenced by parametric priors rather than evidence and force a re-answer that must explicitly quote supporting evidence, thereby calibrating the reasoning back to the provided documents. (3) Reasoning Reflection Verification: review each reasoning step from a global task perspective, identify inconsistencies such as location or replacement biases, and regenerate flawed steps. The agent then aggregates validated reasoning chains to produce robust fact-verification decisions.

Result: On two benchmark datasets, HOVER and EX-FEVER, ReflectFact achieves state-of-the-art multi-hop fact verification performance. It surpasses the strongest prior baseline by 3.32 percentage points on HOVER and 2.78 percentage points on EX-FEVER, indicating improved robustness and accuracy in evidence-grounded reasoning.

Conclusion: A self-reflective single-agent framework that explicitly plans reasoning paths, checks for evidence drift, and reflects on reasoning steps can more effectively handle multi-hop fact verification than existing multi-agent methods. ReflectFact improves both comprehension of evidence and reliability of reasoning, leading to new SOTA performance on standard benchmarks and suggesting that self-reflection and global-task awareness are key to robust fact verification systems.

Abstract: Multi-hop fact verification, which verifies claims by reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence, is critical for combating misinformation on social media yet remains highly challenging. Recent methods primarily rely on multi-agent collaboration to decompose fact verification into specialized subtasks. However, these methods face two critical limitations: (1) agents may perform individual subtasks without sufficient awareness of the global verification objective, causing their reasoning to deviate from the intended direction; and (2) conflicts between parametric knowledge and the provided evidence may undermine evidence-grounded reasoning and lead to incorrect verdicts. To address these challenges, we propose ReflectFact, a novel self-reflective agent framework for multi-hop fact verification. ReflectFact introduces three key tasks. Explicit Reasoning Path Planning builds an evidence-grounded reasoning path by resolving implicit entities, decomposing the claim into sub-questions, and integrating the verified facts into a verdict. Evidence-Drift Verification makes the agent re-answer by quoting the supporting evidence when a grounded answer merely echoes its parametric prior, thereby calibrating evidence deviation to ensure grounded comprehension. Reasoning Reflection Verification re-examines each reasoning step and regenerates it once an inconsistency is detected, correcting reasoning flaws such as location bias and replacement bias through a global task perspective. Subsequently, the agent aggregates validated reasoning chains to yield reliable verdicts. Extensive experiments on HOVER and EX-FEVER demonstrate that ReflectFact effectively remedies the comprehension and reasoning defects of existing methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance and respectively outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.32\% and 2.78\% on the two datasets.

</details>


### [112] [Predictive Memory Localization: Forecasting Selective Intervention Paths from Internal Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12892)
*Jinhao Jing,Tian Zeyu,Lucas Qingyang Fang,Zhisheng Chen,Shuang Chen,Yuhao Luo,Qiannian Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Predictive Memory Localization (PML), a method to evaluate and exploit how activation-steering directions in language models cause selective, controllable changes in model behavior while limiting side effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing activation-steering and memory-localization approaches identify directions in activation space that appear meaningful, but they do not show whether these directions have a reliable, selective regime of operation (i.e., a range of steering strengths where they improve target behavior without causing excessive collateral damage). The authors want a framework that connects localization to predictive, falsifiable claims about intervention outcomes and that can support risk-aware deployment of activation steering.

Method: They define Predictive Memory Localization (PML), which treats the entire intervention path over different steering strengths as the object of study. PML decomposes the effects of steering into (1) desired target movement, (2) movement toward semantic neighbors, and (3) general capability damage. It compares directions found by static localization and supervised geometry against random baselines using a calibrated, low-dose causal response regime. Using a frozen model, they evaluate roughly 30k record–direction–layer combinations and 210k strength evaluations across 3k records, nine datasets, and fourteen domains, focusing particularly on responses at small steering strengths (|α|=0.1) and how well they predict behavior at higher strengths (|α|∈{0.25,0.5}). They also train predictors that, given a record and direction, either choose a steering coefficient or abstain, aiming to improve utility and reduce side effects relative to fixed-strength policies.

Result: Geometry-derived directions (e.g., RFM/AGOP at layer 7) significantly outperform random directions, achieving 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any rates—3.6 and 3.4 percentage points above random, respectively, under bootstrap evaluation. Low-dose responses (|α|=0.1) provide the strongest predictive signal for outcomes at higher, disjoint strengths (|α|=0.25,0.5) across splits by record, dataset, and domain. The learned predictor-based selector on held-out records can choose when and how strongly to intervene, improving utility and reducing semantic-neighbor damage versus a fixed-strength, train-tuned baseline, while also avoiding most of the evaluations required by a dense strength scan. Across three base models matched by residual norm, learned directions preserve selective-path advantages, and low-dose responses reach 0.801–0.828 macro AUROC on record-held-out evaluation.

Conclusion: PML reframes memory localization as a predictive, testable framework: instead of just identifying directions, it forecasts margin-level, selective behavioral outcomes and guides risk-aware decisions about whether and how much to intervene. The approach shows that geometry-derived steering directions can have reliably selective operating regimes, that low-dose responses are informative predictors of higher-strength behavior, and that learned selectors can exploit this to improve control while limiting collateral semantic and capability damage.

Abstract: Activation steering turns localized representations into control directions, but localization alone does not reveal whether a direction has a selective operating regime. We introduce Predictive Memory Localization (PML), which treats the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object of memory localization. PML separates random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage, and compares static localization and supervised geometry with a strength-disjoint low-dose causal response. Our frozen study covers 3,000 records from nine datasets and fourteen domains, yielding 30,000 distinct record-direction-layer paths and 210,000 distinct path-strength evaluations. At layer 7, the geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction reaches 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6 and 3.4 percentage points under a record-paired bootstrap. Across record-, dataset-, and domain-grouped splits, responses at $|α|=0.1$ are the strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths $|α|\in\{0.25,0.5\}$. On held-out records, a predictor-driven selector chooses a coefficient or abstains, improves utility and reduces semantic-neighbor damage relative to a train-tuned fixed-strength policy, and avoids most evaluations in a dense scan. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, learned directions retain selective-path gains and low-dose responses yield 0.801-0.828 record-held-out macro AUROC. PML therefore turns memory localization into a falsifiable forecast of margin-level selective outcomes and a risk-aware intervention decision.

</details>


### [113] [Agent Behavioral Contracts II: Certifying Compositional Reliability Without Assuming Independence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12895)
*Varun Pratap Bhardwaj,Garima Singh,Arun Pratap Bhardwaj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that standard independence-based reliability estimates for multi-agent AI systems can be dangerously over-optimistic, provides empirical evidence of strong positive dependence between agents sharing a model, proves that fitting dependence models and bootstrapping can degrade guarantees, and introduces linear-program-based, finite-sample reliability certificates that remain valid without assuming a dependence structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliability analysis for multi-agent systems usually multiplies component reliabilities, assuming conditional independence of failures. This assumption is typically untested, yet if it is violated (especially via positive dependence), system-level reliability can be severely overestimated, which is critical for safety assurance of AI systems. Existing alternatives that avoid independence either become too loose (vacuous) or rely on fitted dependence models and bootstrapping whose guarantees can quietly worsen with more data. The paper aims to test the independence assumption empirically, understand its failure modes, and design sound, finite-sample reliability certificates that do not rely on strong dependence assumptions.

Method: 1) Empirical evaluation: Run 18,000 missions involving two-agent handoffs using identical models to estimate co-failure rates, with outcomes scored by deterministic, non-LLM code in a preregistered setup. Compute association statistics (log odds ratio, phi) and compare co-failure dependence when changing models and vendors.
2) Theoretical analysis: Show that naive use of fitted dependence models and bootstrap methods leads to reliability certificates whose coverage degrades with increasing sample size because the identification gap (model misspecification) remains O(1), while the bootstrap correction shrinks as O(n^{-1/2}).
3) Certificate construction: Formulate a linear program over the joint distribution of agent outcomes, constrained only by confidence intervals (Bonferroni–Clopper–Pearson bounds) on observed co-execution moments. Use this to compute worst-case and best-case system reliability consistent with the data. Analyze monotonicity and sharpness with respect to added moment constraints and extend to an anytime-valid version that controls type-I error under optional stopping.

Result: 1) Empirical: Two instances of the same model show extremely strong positive dependence in failures: 90% of missions where at least one fails see both failing; association statistics are very large (log OR ~6.66, phi ~0.916). Replacing the model reduces this dependence in all six tested contrasts, while switching vendors (where the model already differs) does not materially change dependence; the registered hypothesis about vendor effects is not supported.
2) Theoretical: Independence-based reliability bounds are shown to substantially over-credit redundancy when agents share a model, because positive dependence inflates joint failure probability beyond the product of marginals. Certificates based on fitted dependence models with bootstrap adjustments lose coverage as data grows, with no observable external indicator of degraded validity.
3) Certificates: The proposed LP-based certificates are sound and tight given the available moment information and are monotone with respect to the richness of the moment family. Increasing the number of moment functionals from 10 to 14 shrinks the width of the identified reliability interval by 85.7% and raises the certified lower bound from 0.2455 to 0.4116. An anytime-valid variant maintains type-I error at approximately 0.0471 under optional stopping.

Conclusion: Standard compositional reliability analysis that assumes conditional independence between agents is empirically violated and can severely overestimate the benefits of redundancy when agents share the same model. Model-sharing induces strong positive dependence in failures; simply switching vendors without changing the underlying model does not resolve this. Approaches that fit a dependence model and rely on bootstrap-based uncertainty quantification can yield reliability certificates whose nominal guarantees secretly worsen with more data due to persistent identification gaps. In contrast, the paper’s LP-based, finite-sample certificates provide sound, sharp, and data-monotone reliability guarantees without making structural assumptions about dependence, and can be extended to anytime-valid settings. These methods offer a more trustworthy basis for certifying multi-agent AI systems, especially in safety-critical applications.

Abstract: Compositional reliability bounds for multi-agent systems multiply component reliabilities, a step licensed by a conditional-independence assumption that is routinely stated and rarely tested. We test it. Two instances of one model, in a two-agent handoff, co-fail on 90.0% of the missions on which either fails (log OR 6.66, 95% CI [6.38, 7.00]; phi 0.916), in a preregistered evaluation of 18,000 missions scored by deterministic code with no LLM judge. Substituting a different model reduces the association in six of six contrasts; substituting a different vendor, model already different, does not -- a registered hypothesis reported as a null.
  The error is signed and runs against the operator: positive dependence inflates joint failure above the independence product, so redundancy is over-credited exactly when components share a model. The assumption-free alternative is often vacuous, and fitting a dependence model is worse: we prove a bootstrap bound on a fitted model's functional loses coverage of the truth as n grows, the identification gap being O(1) while the bootstrap haircut is O(n^{-1/2}). More data makes such a certificate worse, with no visible symptom.
  We give a finite-sample certificate assuming no dependence structure: a linear program over the joint, over a Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson box around measured co-execution moments. It is sound, sharp for the information supplied, and monotone in the moment family. Enriching ten moment functionals to fourteen narrows the identified interval by 85.7% and lifts the certified floor from 0.2455 to 0.4116. A companion anytime-valid certificate holds type-I error at 0.0471 under optional stopping.
  Common dependence statistics are marginal-bounded and can reverse an apparent ordering of conditions when the compared agents fail at different rates. Contracts, scoring code, analysis scripts, and the preregistration are released.

</details>


### [114] [Polish Medical Visual Question Answering: Vision-Language Models Underutilize Visual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12928)
*Jakub Pokrywka,Łukasz Grzybowski,Antoni Lasik,Marek Kubis,Jeremi Ignacy Kaczmarek,Wojciech Kusa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build and release a Polish-language medical VQA benchmark from specialist exam questions, evaluate multiple vision–language models, and show the task is still hard, with models often underperforming humans and exploiting answer-choice biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There was no high-quality, domain-specific Polish medical VQA benchmark, which is needed to rigorously evaluate and improve multimodal models for Polish-speaking clinicians across many specialties. Existing VQA datasets are mostly in English and often synthetic or simpler than real exam questions, and they do not clearly separate the contributions of images, questions, and answer options.

Method: They construct a benchmark from Polish Board Certification Examination questions for physicians and dentists, selecting those that contain images and also compiling a text-only QA control set. They annotate questions by the importance of the image, and design experimental conditions that provide (1) full input (image + question + options) and ablated variants without the image, without the question, or without both. They then evaluate several categories of models—Polish-focused models, general open-weight vision–language models, and commercial models—on both VQA and text-only QA, measuring accuracy and comparing to approximate human performance.

Result: The best model reaches 79.0% accuracy on the full VQA set. On the subset with candidate responses and human reference data, only GPT-5.6 slightly surpasses human-level performance; all other evaluated models lag behind humans. Models gain more from question text than from the image and perform noticeably worse on questions where the image is crucial. However, even when key components (image or question) are removed, models still perform above chance by exploiting patterns in the answer choices alone, revealing non-trivial shortcut behavior and dataset biases.

Conclusion: Polish medical VQA based on real board exam questions is challenging for current models, with most still below human performance. Vision–language models rely more on textual cues than on images, and can game the task using biases in answer options, so simple accuracy metrics may overestimate their true multimodal and clinical reasoning capabilities. The new benchmark provides a more realistic and language-specific testbed to spur development of stronger, better-grounded medical VQA systems for Polish healthcare.

Abstract: We introduce a Polish-language medical visual question answering (VQA) benchmark, built from Polish Board Certification Examination questions for licensed physicians and dentists pursuing specialist certification. The benchmark comprises image-containing questions spanning diverse medical specialties and visual domains, together with a text-only question answering (QA) control set. We evaluate Polish-oriented, general-purpose open-weight, and commercial vision-language models. The task remains challenging: the best model achieves 79.0\% accuracy on the full VQA set, and only GPT-5.6 surpasses the approximate human reference on the subset with available candidate responses; all other evaluated models perform worse than humans. To assess visual grounding, we compare complete inputs with configurations omitting the image, the question, or both, and categorize questions by image importance. Models derive more useful information from the question text than from the image and perform worse on image-dominant questions. Across both QA and VQA, they nevertheless achieve above-chance accuracy from the answer choices alone, showing that non-trivial performance can persist even when key task components are missing.

</details>


### [115] [FlashDrive: Flash Vision-Language-Action Inference for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12932)
*Zekai Li,Yihao Liang,Hongfei Zhang,Jian Chen,Yesheng Liang,Zhijian Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlashDrive is an algorithm-system co-design that accelerates vision-language-action models for autonomous driving by addressing four inference bottlenecks simultaneously, achieving ~4.7x lower latency with similar or better driving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language-Action models can provide powerful end-to-end reasoning for autonomous driving, but their inference is too slow for real-time control because latency comes from multiple cascaded bottlenecks across vision, language reasoning, and action generation. Existing optimizations usually target only one stage, leaving others as limiting factors. There is a need for a holistic approach that accelerates all major stages while preserving control quality.

Method: The paper introduces FlashDrive, which co-designs algorithms and systems optimizations across four stages: (1) visual encoding is sped up via streaming KV-cache reuse across temporally overlapping frames; (2) language-model prefill is reduced by carrying over and reusing context across timesteps; (3) low-entropy, highly correlated driving-domain reasoning tokens are accelerated using a non-autoregressive diffusion-based drafter for speculative decoding; and (4) flow-matching denoising is sped up via adaptive step caching that allocates more compute to high-velocity endpoint regions and less to flatter middle regions. These are combined with CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion, and applied on a quantized (W4A8) Alpamayo 1.5–10B VLA model.

Result: On Alpamayo 1.5–10B, FlashDrive cuts end-to-end inference latency from 717 ms to 151 ms (about 4.7x) and boosts control frequency from 1.4 Hz to 6.6 Hz on a single GPU. Accuracy is essentially preserved or slightly improved: minADE6@6.4s changes only by 0.08 m, minADE1 improves, and in closed-loop simulation both collision and off-road rates decrease.

Conclusion: Jointly optimizing all major inference stages of a VLA model via tailored algorithmic shortcuts and system-level engineering can dramatically reduce latency without sacrificing, and even marginally improving, autonomous driving performance. FlashDrive demonstrates that a 10B-parameter reasoning model can operate near real-time on a single GPU, bringing end-to-end VLA-based driving closer to practical deployment.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models promise to bring end-to-end reasoning to autonomous driving, but their computational cost remains far too high for real-time control. The core challenge is structural: VLA inference is not a single bottleneck but a cascade of four. Visual encoding wastes compute on overlapping video frames; language-model prefill recomputes context that could be carried over from the previous timestep; reasoning tokens are generated serially despite low entropy; and flow-matching denoising applies uniform compute to a non-uniform velocity field. Addressing any one stage in isolation leaves the others untouched. We propose FlashDrive, an algorithm-system co-design framework that targets all four stages simultaneously. Our key insight is that each bottleneck admits a distinct, lightweight algorithmic shortcut: temporal overlap enables streaming KV-cache reuse across frames; the low per-token entropy and strong intra-block correlations of driving-domain reasoning make a non-autoregressive diffusion drafter highly effective for speculative decoding; and the velocity field's structure---sharp at the endpoints, flat in the middle---permits adaptive step caching that concentrates compute where it matters. Layered on system-level CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion, these techniques compound. Applied to Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization, FlashDrive reduces end-to-end latency from 717ms to 151ms (4.7x) while leaving accuracy essentially unchanged: minADE6@6.4s shifts by only 0.08m, minADE1 improves, and closed-loop collision and off-road rates improve in simulation. By raising a 10B-parameter reasoning VLA from 1.4~Hz to 6.6~Hz on a single GPU, FlashDrive moves end-to-end autonomous driving substantially closer to real-time deployment.

</details>


### [116] [Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, and Fragility in Perturbation Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12935)
*Lei You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose DECAF, a method that decomposes perturbation-based model responses into evidence, contradiction, and fragility, better aligning explanations with observed model behavior than raw response magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing perturbation-based explanations only look at how much a model’s prediction changes when inputs are altered, but the same magnitude can mean very different things: it can support the final prediction, oppose it, or appear transiently and vanish. This makes standard magnitude-based attributions ambiguous and sometimes misleading. The authors want a way to distinguish qualitatively different types of responses that have the same overall size but different semantic roles in the model’s decision process.

Method: They progressively reveal paired inputs along a perturbation path and track how the contrast between predictions develops over that trajectory, using the final prediction contrast as an anchor to interpret earlier changes. They introduce DECAF (Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, And Fragility), which classifies response components as aligned with the final contrast (evidence E), opposed to it (contradiction C), or strong along the path but absent at the endpoint (fragility F). The method is defined so that the sum of these nonnegative components equals the usual absolute response magnitude, Abs = E + C + F, and the decomposition is uniquely determined by endpoint-relative axioms. They evaluate it on synthetic vision and tabular tasks, as well as large-scale image models, and compare to gradient and general-purpose attribution baselines.

Result: In controlled experiments on vision and tabular datasets, E, C, and F correlate with independently measured behavioral properties of the models, indicating that the decomposition captures meaningful distinctions. In a large audit over 72 ImageNet-9 models, they find many cases where standard response magnitudes are nearly identical across inputs, yet models behave differently under independent tests; in 96.4% of such cases, the largest DECAF component matches the independently observed behavior, versus only 35.0% agreement when using magnitude alone. They show that changing only the reveal path can increase total response by ~80%, primarily by inflating the fragility component (>4× increase) while leaving evidence nearly unchanged. On FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k, short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform several general-purpose attribution methods. On a large 1B-parameter DINOv2 model, a short DECAF trajectory reaches the performance of a strong gradient-based baseline at 4.75× lower wall-clock time and 2.36× lower peak memory usage.

Conclusion: DECAF provides a principled, unique decomposition of perturbation responses into evidence, contradiction, and fragility that preserves total magnitude while exposing qualitatively different model behaviors that magnitude alone obscures. This decomposition aligns much more closely with independently measured behaviors, helping distinguish supportive, opposing, and unstable contributions to a decision. It is practical at scale—matching or surpassing existing attribution baselines in performance while being more efficient on large vision models—suggesting it is a useful tool for more reliable and interpretable model explanations.

Abstract: Perturbation methods explain model decisions by measuring prediction changes under altered inputs, but response magnitude tells us only how much a model reacts, not what that reaction means. The same magnitude can support the final factual-counterfactual difference, oppose it, or arise strongly along the perturbation path yet vanish at the endpoint. We therefore track how the contrast develops as paired inputs are progressively revealed, using the final contrast to interpret the trajectory. We introduce DECAF (Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, And Fragility), which routes aligned, opposed, and endpoint-null responses into evidence E, contradiction C, and fragility F. The decomposition preserves ordinary magnitude exactly, Abs = E + C + F, and is unique under endpoint-relative axioms. Across controlled vision and tabular settings, the three components track independently measured behavior. In a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit, we compare cases with nearly identical response magnitude but different independently measured behaviors. The largest DECAF component agrees with an observed behavior in 96.4% of cases, compared with 35.0% for magnitude alone. Changing only the reveal path increases total response by nearly 80%, yet evidence barely changes while fragility grows by more than 4x. On FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k, short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform the tested general-purpose attribution baselines. On a 1B-scale DINOv2 model, a short trajectory matches a strong gradient-based baseline with 4.75x lower wall time and 2.36x lower peak memory.

</details>


### [117] [Moose: Latent concept learning with reasoning-shortcut awareness in $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12961)
*Olga Mashkova,Asaad Mohammedsaleh,Fernando Zhapa-Camacho,Robert Hoehndorf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic method called Moose compiles OWL 2 EL ontologies into Sentential Decision Diagrams to enable differentiable reasoning and latent concept learning under partial supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OWL 2 EL is widely used in large, real-world ontologies, but existing neuro-symbolic learning methods mainly handle propositional or Datalog inputs and do not address reasoning shortcuts or partial supervision in ontology-based settings. There is a need for a method that can leverage OWL EL reasoning in a differentiable way while coping with its limited expressivity.

Method: The authors compile an EL++ TBox together with a finite ABox into a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD), which serves as a differentiable weighted-model-counting layer. They extend the compiled theory with additional closure clauses outside the strict EL++ profile for explicitly declared exhaustive concept families to mitigate expressivity limits under partial supervision. They formally prove termination, soundness, completeness, and polynomial bounds on intermediate sizes, validating the proofs in Lean. They then define a partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task over OWL EL ontologies and use the SDD-based model to learn per-individual classifiers from observed ABox literals.

Result: The approach, named Moose, is implemented and evaluated on two benchmarks: MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo. Moose outperforms propositional neuro-symbolic baselines, fuzzy-logic methods, and ontology-embedding approaches on the defined latent concept learning tasks. It also enables the first systematic analysis of reasoning shortcuts in an OWL EL ontology setting.

Conclusion: Moose demonstrates that EL++ ontologies can be compiled into tractable SDDs that support differentiable learning with formal guarantees, while extended with closure clauses to better handle partial supervision. This yields superior performance over several baseline approaches and provides a new framework for studying reasoning shortcuts in OWL EL-based neuro-symbolic learning tasks.

Abstract: The OWL 2 EL profile is used in some of the largest production ontologies, including the Gene Ontology and SNOMED CT. Existing neuro-symbolic (NeSy) learning methods accept propositional theories or Datalog, and reasoning-shortcut (RS) awareness has not been investigated in ontology settings. We present Moose, a method that compiles an $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ TBox and finite ABox to a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD). The SDD acts as a differentiable weighted-model-counting layer, and we add closure clauses outside the $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ profile on declared exhaustive families to overcome the limited expressivity of $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ under partial supervision. We show termination, soundness, completeness, and polynomial intermediate sizes, and validate the proofs in Lean. We then define the first formal partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task over an OWL EL ontology, i.e., learning per-individual classifiers for latent concepts from observed ABox literals, and evaluate Moose on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo. Moose improves over propositional-NeSy, fuzzy-logic, and ontology embedding baselines, and presents the first reasoning-shortcut analysis in an OWL EL setting.

</details>


### [118] [OGR-MARL: Option-Guided Residual Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Heterogeneous USV Cooperative Pursuit in Constrained Port Waterways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12995)
*Mao Jiayang,Wang Lanfeng,Peng Zhao-Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose OGR-MARL, a rule-guided residual multi-agent RL framework for heterogeneous USV cooperative pursuit in constrained ports, and show it improves capture rate, rule compliance, and transfer to real port maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative interception of an evading vessel by multiple heterogeneous USVs in real port waterways is hard because agents must obey navigation/traffic/role rules while coordinating in continuous, constrained environments. Pure MARL from scratch explores inefficiently and struggles with safety and rule compliance, and existing methods are often tied to a specific algorithm and don’t generalize well to real ports.
