Method: Design OGR-MARL, an option-guided residual multi-agent RL framework that sits on top of arbitrary continuous-control MARL algorithms. It uses: (1) a shared belief over the evader to inform all agents; (2) role-conditioned option targets that encode rule-based baseline behaviors per vessel role; (3) adaptive penalties for rule violations; and (4) residual policy learning, where the MARL policy learns corrective actions on top of the rule-based options instead of learning from scratch. They instantiate this with multiple MARL backbones (MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, MASAC) to obtain corresponding OGR variants and test them in a simulated port scenario.

Result: In the Xiazhimen port-waterway simulation, the OGR-based methods enable better coordination and rule-compliant interception than their vanilla counterparts. Among them, OGR-MASAC performs best, achieving a 75% capture rate while maintaining high rule compliance and effective heterogeneous coordination across USVs. The results show that integrating rule-guided options with residual learning significantly improves performance in constrained maritime pursuit tasks.

Conclusion: OGR-MARL is an effective, algorithm-agnostic framework for rule-guided multi-agent control of heterogeneous USVs in constrained port environments. By combining shared evader belief, role-based options, adaptive rule penalties, and residual learning, it enhances capture performance, coordination, and rule compliance. Its successful zero-shot transfer from abstract simulation to a QGIS/AIS-informed real port map suggests good generalization potential for more complex, realistic port scenarios.

Abstract: Heterogeneous USV cooperative pursuit in constrained port waterways requires evader interception under navigation, traffic, and role constraints. This paper proposes OGR-MARL, an option-guided residual multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that is decoupled from a specific MARL algorithm. OGR-MARL integrates shared evader belief, role-conditioned option targets, adaptive rule penalties, and residual policy learning, allowing different MARL algorithms to learn corrective actions on top of rule-guided behaviors rather than exploring constrained port environments from scratch. We instantiate OGR-MARL with representative continuous-control MARL backbones, including MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, and MASAC, yielding OGR-MADDPG, OGR-MATD3, OGR-MAPPO, and OGR-MASAC. Experiments in an abstract Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario show that the OGR-MASAC instantiation achieves a 75.0% capture rate, promising mission-effective rule compliance, and the best heterogeneous coordination among the tested methods. Without retraining, zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed Xiazhimen map achieves promising results, demonstrating the generalization potential of OGR-MARL in more complex port scenarios.

</details>


### [119] [Foundations of MT-PDCL: Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13018)
*Costin Bădică,Amelia Bădică*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes MT-PDCL, a measure-theoretic probabilistic logic programming framework that supports exact inference over continuous domains without discrete grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing probabilistic logic programming systems require grounding to discrete propositional forms, limiting exact inference to finite domains and discrete distributions and causing combinatorial bottlenecks. The authors want a principled framework that handles continuous variables and measure-theoretic semantics while keeping the declarative flavor of logic programming.

Method: They define Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic (MT-PDCL), where stochastic variables are defined over bounded index domains with standard Borel σ-algebras, enabling logical variables to range over continuous measurable spaces. Based on Continuous Distribution Semantics, they treat probabilistic rules as independent causal events and replace finite Boolean-circuit aggregation with Lebesgue integration over the continuous measure space. They introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator that jointly performs integration of continuous priors and evaluation of continuous observations, yielding algebraic and differentiable inference procedures.

Result: Conceptually, they show that inference can be characterized as exact Lebesgue integration rather than discrete grounding and circuit evaluation. Technically, they obtain a unified operator for continuous inference that avoids combinatorial blowup and produces structurally differentiable inference suitable for continuous probabilistic reasoning.

Conclusion: MT-PDCL lifts probabilistic logic programming to a fully measure-theoretic setting, removing the finite-domain restriction and supporting continuous variables while preserving the declarative syntax of definite clause logic. This trades discrete combinatorial complexity for high-dimensional geometric complexity but provides the expressive power of continuous probabilistic models with principled, exact semantics.

Abstract: Standard probabilistic logic programming frameworks typically rely on grounding logic programs into discrete propositional representations. This operational requirement restricts exact inference to finite domains and discrete probability distributions. In this paper, we introduce Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic (MT-PDCL), a generalized foundational framework that eliminates this finite-domain restriction. By explicitly defining stochastic variables over bounded index domains and equipping the interpretation space with standard Borel $σ$-algebras, MT-PDCL allows logical variables to operate natively over continuous measurable spaces. Building on Continuous Distribution Semantics, MT-PDCL models probabilistic rules as mutually independent causal events. However, rather than aggregating these derivations via finite boolean circuits, declarative entailment is formally defined through exact Lebesgue integration over the continuous measure space. We introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator that unifies the integration of continuous prior distributions with the evaluation of exact continuous observations. We demonstrate that this approach replaces the combinatorial bottleneck of discrete grounding with exact, algebraic, and structurally differentiable inference. While this transition trades discrete combinatorics for the geometric curse of dimensionality, it achieves the expressive power of continuous probabilistic models while preserving the pure declarative syntax of definite clause logic.

</details>


### [120] [From Local Mismatch to Global Impact: Optimizing Cache Reuse Policy for Efficient Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13043)
*Xichen Ye,Yifan Wu,Zhikang Xie,Xiangyu Yue,Cheng Jin,Weizhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces GCache, a cache-based acceleration framework for diffusion models that achieves substantial speedups while improving generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models deliver state-of-the-art visual generation but are computationally expensive at inference. Existing cache-based acceleration methods choose when to reuse cached computations using local similarity heuristics along the denoising trajectory. These heuristics are poorly aligned with final image/video quality because errors propagate non-uniformly through the steps, causing either over-conservative or harmful reuse decisions. A method is needed that accounts for global impact on final quality when deciding where to save or spend computation.

Method: The authors analyze error propagation in diffusion-model denoising and derive a theoretical upper bound on how cache-induced errors accumulate along the trajectory. Because this bound is too conservative for complex, non-convex models, they reparameterize the propagation exponent using a Bernstein polynomial form, which flexibly models how errors at different timesteps affect final quality. They then cast cache policy design as a bilevel optimization problem: the inner optimization searches for the optimal cache reuse pattern under a given error-weighting function, while the outer optimization learns the error-weighting function to best match actual generation quality loss. This yields a global-impact-aware caching policy (GCache) that prioritizes computation at timesteps with the largest influence on visual fidelity.

Result: Across extensive experiments on image and video diffusion models, GCache consistently outperforms prior caching strategies, delivering faster inference and better quality. On the strong Wan2.1 video diffusion model, GCache attains a 2.17x speedup while substantially improving perceptual quality, with LPIPS dropping from 0.1095 to 0.0316, demonstrating that carefully learned global error weighting can both accelerate and enhance generation compared to heuristic caching.

Conclusion: GCache provides a theoretically grounded and empirically validated framework for cache-based acceleration of diffusion models that explicitly accounts for the global impact of step-wise errors on final generation quality. By combining an analytically derived error bound, a flexible Bernstein reparameterization, and bilevel optimization over reuse policies and error-weighting, it resolves the misalignment of local heuristics and delivers simultaneous speedups and quality gains for both image and video generation.

Abstract: Diffusion models have achieved dominant performance in visual generation but suffer from substantial inference overhead. While cache-based acceleration has emerged as a promising solution, existing policies rely on local similarity heuristics, which we identify as being significantly misaligned with final generation quality. This discrepancy stems from the non-uniform propagation and accumulation of errors along the denoising trajectory. To address this, we propose Global-Impact Cache (GCache). We first establish a rigorous theoretical characterization of the error propagation upper bound. Recognizing that this bound can be overly conservative for complex, highly non-convex diffusion models, we further reparameterize the propagation exponent with a Bernstein form and reformulate cache policy search as a bilevel optimization problem. In detail, GCache identifies an optimal reuse policy in the inner objective while aligning the error-weighting function with generation quality loss in the outer objective. This framework effectively reconciles theoretical rigor with empirical performance, learning to prioritize computation where it most impacts visual fidelity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GCache consistently outperforms prior caching strategies on both video and image generation. Notably, on the state-of-the-art Wan2.1 video diffusion model, GCache maintains a 2.17x speedup while significantly enhancing generation quality, reducing LPIPS from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

</details>


### [121] [BoardroomAI: Dependency-Aware Human-Steerable Multi-Agent Deliberation through Evolving Decision Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13046)
*Sanjeev Manivannan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BoardroomAI is a multi-agent decision-support framework where humans remain active participants who can intervene during deliberation, with a graph-based representation and efficient update mechanism to handle evolving evidence and constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional multi-agent decision systems take a static problem description, reason internally, and output a final answer, leaving humans unable to dynamically adjust assumptions, constraints, or evidence mid-process. Real organizational decisions, however, evolve over time and require continuous human involvement. The paper seeks to design a system where humans and AI agents can co-create and iteratively revise complex decisions while maintaining structure, traceability, and efficiency.

Method: The authors propose BoardroomAI, which structures decision-making as a typed decision graph capturing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, dependencies, and specialist responsibilities. A human-facing intervention layer compiles user actions (e.g., challenging assumptions, modifying constraints) into precise graph updates. A dependency-aware propagation algorithm identifies only the affected parts of the graph, preserving unaffected portions and selectively reactivating relevant specialist agents. An evaluation framework measures how well the system handles interventions in terms of impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation cost, and final decision validity.

Result: In synthetic experiments involving 600 decision-DAG interventions, the dependency-aware propagation achieved the same impact coverage as exhaustive recomputation while only needing to inspect about 14.59% of the nodes. In a small exploratory study on 12 synthetic decision cases, selective repair recomputed roughly 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all nodes that were known to be unaffected, and generated valid updated decisions in six cases while abstaining in the other six when context was insufficient.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that structured, graph-based, human-in-the-loop multi-agent deliberation is feasible and can efficiently localize and repair the impact of human interventions while preserving unaffected reasoning. However, the system sometimes lacks enough contextual information to synthesize updated decisions even when interventions are correctly routed, highlighting the need for a notion of "decision-sufficient context closure"—ensuring that all necessary context for a sound decision is present and accessible. The results are preliminary, synthetic, and prototype-level, indicating promising directions rather than definitive performance guarantees.

Abstract: Organizational decisions are co-created while evidence, constraints, and human priorities continue to evolve. In conventional transcript-based multi-agent systems, humans typically provide an initial problem, agents deliberate internally, and the system returns a final response. BoardroomAI instead treats the human as a persistent participant who can intervene by challenging assumptions, modifying constraints, changing priorities, introducing evidence, or redirecting the decision process. We operationalize this human--agent coexistence through four components: (i) a typed decision graph representing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, semantic dependencies, and specialist responsibility; (ii) an intervention compiler that converts confirmed human actions into explicit graph updates; (iii) dependency-aware propagation that identifies affected subgraphs, preserves unaffected artifacts, and selectively reactivates relevant specialists; and (iv) an evaluation framework measuring intervention impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation, and decision validity. Across 600 generated decision-DAG interventions, propagation matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes. In a 12-case exploratory pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid updated decisions in six cases while abstaining in the remaining six. These abstentions show that correct intervention routing may still provide insufficient context for synthesis, motivating a \emph{decision-sufficient context closure} for human-steered multi-agent deliberation. All results are synthetic and prototype-level.

</details>


### [122] [DMDIntel: Interpreting Large Language Models via Dynamic Mode Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13048)
*Amogh Joshi,Animesh Mukherjee,Sergey Utyuzhnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DMDIntel makes LLM classifications interpretable by decomposing hidden states with Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD) and ranking input tokens by their contributions, outperforming common attribution baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing interpretability methods for LLM classification, such as PCA-based visualization, integrated gradients, and SHAP, have limitations in faithfully capturing which input tokens drive model predictions. There is a need for an attribution framework that is better aligned with the internal dynamics of LLM hidden states and provides more accurate, robust token-level explanations.

Method: The paper proposes DMDIntel, an interpretability framework that applies Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD) to the sequence of hidden states produced by an LLM during a classification task. It decomposes these hidden states into dominant dynamical modes and uses the projection of each input token’s representation onto these modes to compute an importance score. Tokens are then ranked according to their projection-based attribution. The method is evaluated against baselines like PCA, Integrated Gradients, and SHAP across multiple datasets and LLM families.

Result: Across three datasets and three different LLM families, DMDIntel’s token attribution rankings are consistently more faithful and informative than those produced by PCA, Integrated Gradients, and SHAP. The experiments demonstrate substantial improvements in standard attribution evaluation metrics, indicating that DMDIntel more accurately identifies the tokens most responsible for model predictions.

Conclusion: DMDIntel establishes Dynamic Mode Decomposition as a powerful tool for LLM interpretability in classification tasks. By leveraging the dynamical structure of hidden states, it yields superior token-level attributions compared to existing state-of-the-art methods. The approach suggests that mode-based analyses of hidden states are a promising direction for building more transparent and trustworthy LLMs.

Abstract: In this work, we introduce DMDIntel which uses dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) to make the predictions made by LLMs in a classification task interpretable. It develops an input attribution pipeline, that first decomposes the hidden states of an LLM into prominent patterns, also known as modes, and then associates ranks to the input tokens based on the projection values on those modes. Rigorous experiments across three datasets and three model families consistently show that the ranked attribution of input tokens obtained using DMDIntel by far outperforms state-of-the-art techniques such as principal component analysis, integrated gradients and SHAP.

</details>


### [123] [VALG: An Agentic System for ML Theory Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13060)
*Dechen Zhang,Xuan Tang,Xinxiang Yin,Xingwu Chen,Jian Qian,Difan Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VALG is an autonomous, agent-based system for doing machine-learning theory, organizing how conjectures, problem formulations, and proofs are iteratively refined and verified.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In ML theory, solving an open problem is not just about proving a theorem; it requires jointly designing the problem formulation, the exact theorem statement, and a proof strategy under a precise mathematical setup (data model, oracle access, loss, metric, randomness, etc.). Human researchers already do this iteratively: they pose hypotheses, test partial proofs or experiments, then revise assumptions or statements. The paper asks whether this iterative, structured research process can be systematized and automated as an agentic workflow for ML theory specifically, rather than just for coding or empirical ML tasks.

Method: The authors build VALG, an agentic system for ML theory research that integrates: (1) multi-level verification, (2) adaptive formulation of learning-theory problems, and (3) graph-structured proof development. For each “source-relative” theorem branch (i.e., a branch tied to a specific source problem), VALG fixes a precise mathematical specification and maintains a typed proof-dependency graph that tracks lemmas and their relationships. It checks the consistency and composition of the theorem-level structure and then constructs local proofs in dependency order. When a proof attempt fails, VALG diagnoses whether the issue is with a local derivation, the higher-level proof structure, or the theorem’s formulation itself. Depending on this diagnosis, it either tries a different derivation, restructures the proof, or modifies the theorem (e.g., relaxes assumptions, changes the result’s scope), while keeping an explicit link between the new variant and the original source problem.

Result: VALG is tested on nine subproblems derived from five open problems from COLT 2026. In two cases, VALG autonomously arrives at internally finalized theorem candidates that match the original scope of the given problem descriptions. In the remaining seven, it does not fully solve the original tasks but produces restricted-method theorems, special-case results, or conditional statements. Across all runs, VALG maintains clear distinctions between full-scope matches, relaxations of the source tasks, conditional results, and dead-end branches, demonstrating structured, traceable exploration rather than opaque trial-and-error.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that the practice of ML theory research can be partially organized into an autonomous agentic workflow. VALG can manage precise mathematical specifications, systematically develop and verify proof graphs, and adapt theorem formulations while preserving their relationship to the original open problems. Although it does not completely solve most tasks, it yields meaningful theoretical variants and clearly separates successful, relaxed, conditional, and blocked directions. This suggests that agentic systems can act as structured collaborators in learning-theory research and that the proposed verification–formulation–graph framework is a promising foundation for future ML theory automation.

Abstract: Machine learning theory studies learning procedures through mathematical setups in which the data model, training protocol, oracle access, loss, metric, and randomness define the phenomenon that a theorem is meant to explain. Solving an open problem therefore requires the problem formulation, theorem target, and proof mechanism to be developed in concert. Researchers formulate hypotheses, test them through preliminary theoretical or empirical analysis, and refine both assumptions and proofs. We investigate whether this process can be organized as an autonomous agentic workflow for ML theory research.
  We develop VALG, an agentic system that combines multi-level Verification, Adaptive formulation of Learning-theory problems, and Graph-structured proof development. Within each source-relative theorem branch, VALG maintains a fixed mathematical specification, checks the theorem-level composition of a typed proof-dependency graph, and constructs and reviews local proofs in dependency order. When a proof attempt fails, VALG identifies whether the obstruction lies in a derivation, the proof structure, or the theorem formulation and routes the next attempt accordingly. Formulation-level obstructions initiate an explicitly related variant or relaxation, preserving the mathematical relation between the resulting theorem and the source problem.
  We evaluate VALG on nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems. Two runs produce internally finalized theorem candidates that match the scope of their source briefs; the remaining seven yield restricted-method results, special cases, or conditional theorems. These case studies show how VALG keeps source-scope matches, relaxations, conditional results, and blocked attempts mathematically distinct. VALG is open source at https://github.com/DechenZhang/VALG-ML-Theory-Agent.

</details>


### [124] [Uniform Herding: Exemplar Replay with Representation Refresh](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13061)
*Krishna Subedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Uniform Herding, a method for exemplar replay in class-incremental learning that better preserves past classes under changing feature representations, improving accuracy and reducing forgetting compared to iCaRL under a specific protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In class-incremental learning, models must learn new classes over time while retaining performance on earlier classes. Replay methods rely on a limited exemplar memory, and as the feature representation of the network changes, old exemplars may become suboptimal, causing forgetting. Existing methods like iCaRL do not fully address how to optimally allocate and refresh exemplars under a fixed memory budget, motivating a more principled strategy.

Method: The authors propose Uniform Herding, which (1) uniformly allocates the active exemplar set across all observed classes, and (2) uses a bounded candidate pool at each step to refresh which exemplars are stored for each class under the current feature representation. The method is evaluated with a ResNet-18 backbone on CIFAR-100 split into ten class-incremental tasks, using an active memory budget of M=2,000 exemplars and a retrieval budget of b=64. Ablation studies replace components such as NME classification, herding, and distillation, and vary retrieval and active budgets within the same protocol.

Result: On CIFAR-100 with ten incremental tasks, ResNet-18, M=2,000, b=64, and three random seeds, Uniform Herding achieves a final average accuracy of 44.00±0.51% and forgetting of 17.22±0.43%, compared to iCaRL’s 42.33±1.20% accuracy and 24.87±1.11% forgetting under an end-to-end comparison. Within the Uniform Herding protocol, alternatives to NME or herding reduce final accuracy, and removing distillation increases forgetting. Varying the retrieval budget has relatively minor impact versus changing the active budget.

Conclusion: Uniform Herding provides a more effective strategy for managing a bounded replay memory in class-incremental learning by uniformly distributing exemplars across classes and periodically refreshing them with a candidate pool under the current representation. Under the tested protocol, it improves final accuracy and reduces forgetting compared with iCaRL, and ablations highlight the importance of NME, herding, and distillation. However, the gains are demonstrated only within this specific experimental setup, and the end-to-end comparison does not isolate the contribution of the exemplar refresh mechanism from other protocol differences.

Abstract: As the feature representation changes, replay must preserve the earlier classes. However, only a bounded active exemplar set can be replayed. We propose Uniform Herding, which allocates the current active set across observed classes and uses a bounded candidate pool to refresh their chosen exemplars in the current representation. On CIFAR-100 with ten class-incremental tasks, a ResNet-18 backbone, active budget $M=2{,}000$, retrieval budget $b=64$, and three seeds, Uniform Herding obtains $44.00\pm0.51\%$ final average accuracy and $17.22\pm0.43\%$ forgetting, compared with $42.33\pm1.20\%$ and $24.87\pm1.11\%$ for iCaRL. Within the Uniform Herding protocol, final accuracy decreased when NME or herding was replaced with the tested alternatives, while forgetting increased when distillation was removed. Changing the retrieval budget has a smaller effect across the tested range than changing the active budget. The comparison with iCaRL is end-to-end. It does not isolate the effect of refresh from the other protocol differences. These results are limited to the tested protocol.

</details>


### [125] [Explanatory Engagement Under Rare Anomalous Failure: Asymptotic Rarity in Model Behavior (or: The Asymptotic AI)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13063)
*Sam Mao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study of how LLMs change their explanatory behavior as failures in a tool-using workflow become very rare, across models, failure rates, and elicitation styles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand not just whether LLMs notice anomalies, but how their length, specificity, and self-reported confidence of explanations change when operating in mostly-reliable workflows, where failures are rare and controllable.

Method: Implemented a local test harness on three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) performing a repeated tool-call task where one tool call fails with probability p. Swept p across eight values from 0.2 to 0.0001 and tested five different elicitation conditions (from immediate forced explanation to no prompting). Measured explanation length, specificity, and confidence, and also ran a companion guaranteed-failure experiment (72 cells) to distinguish anomaly recognition from engagement level once anomalies are noticed.

Result: Pooled data seemed to show a flat monotonic decrease in explanation length as failures become rarer, contradicting the initial hypothesis. However, disaggregating by elicitation condition revealed different patterns: in the immediate_forced condition, explanation length increases as failures become rarer then plateaus (rather than collapsing), and confidence generally rises from ~53% to 70–90%. In grouped_runs, no collapse appears. In passive_unprompted, overall engagement is low but a logging gap exposed that llama3.1:8b spontaneously produces structured confidence reports and updates them over time, unlike the other models. The guaranteed-failure runs show models differ in anomaly recognition, separate from how they engage once an anomaly is recognized.

Conclusion: Elicitation structure (how and when we ask the model to explain failures) is a key moderator of whether any “collapse” in engagement is observable. Models exhibit different anomaly-recognition capabilities and self-monitoring styles, and aggregate metrics can hide condition-specific effects. More continuous sampling of failure rates is needed to fully characterize behavior between discrete probability points.

Abstract: Prior work on LLM behavior under anomalous conditions asks whether a model notices anomalies. We ask a narrower question: once a model sits in a workflow with a low, controllable failure rate, does its explanatory engagement - length, specificity, self-reported confidence - change as failure grows asymptotically rarer? We built a local, zero-cost harness on three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) running a repeated tool-call task where one call fails at probability p, swept across eight rates from 0.2 to 0.0001, under five elicitation conditions from immediate prompting to none. We hypothesized a rise in engagement as failures grew rarer, then a collapse near a detectability threshold. Pooled across conditions this appeared false: length fell in a flat, monotonic pattern. Splitting by condition overturned that. Under immediate_forced, where the model must explain every failure instantly, the predicted rise is confirmed but followed by a plateau, not a collapse: length peaks at 28.4 words at p=0.05, settles to 17.4-19.0 words at the rarest rates, and confidence rises unevenly from about 53% to the 70s-90s. Under grouped_runs, explanation batched to run-end, no collapse appears. Under passive_unprompted, aggregate magnitude is a floor artifact, but a recovered logging gap revealed real, model-specific self-monitoring: llama3.1:8b volunteers structured confidence reports unprompted, sometimes eroding its own confidence as trials accumulate; the other two do so only once, as boilerplate. Elicitation structure is a first-class moderator of collapse observability. A companion guaranteed-failure run (72 cells, backfilling rates where random sampling gave zero real failures) shows models differ in whether they recognize an anomaly, distinct from engagement once recognized. Limitation: discrete rate points cannot capture behavior between them, a direction for future work.

</details>


### [126] [Behavioral Reprogramming of Open-Weights Models: Cognitive Plasticity and Alignment Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13069)
*Lucia Malíčková*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically studies how to reprogram open-weight LLMs from passive/sycophantic assistants into proactive, Socratic agents under tight compute constraints, identifying concrete PEFT and DPO settings that enable efficient cross-lingual behavioral modification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLMs are aligned to be passive, agreeable assistants, which is suboptimal for use cases that require proactive, question-asking, Socratic behavior. At the same time, many practitioners operate under strict HPC and compute budgets and need principled, parameter-efficient ways to alter model behavior, including across languages. The authors aim to rigorously test how far open-weight models can be behaviorally reprogrammed, what PEFT limits exist, and how well such behavior transfers cross-lingually, all while remaining compute-efficient.

Method: They perform a large, parallel hyperparameter sweep of 405 jobs under constrained HPC conditions to explore parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) configurations, focusing on LoRA rank and training epochs. They mathematically characterize bounds for PEFT and run epoch ablations to find optimal training windows. They scale models up to 14B parameters to measure perplexity changes. After base PEFT, they apply Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to disentangle the desired assertive conversational behavior from surface-level syntax. Finally, they conduct rigorous cross-lingual stress tests to evaluate zero-shot persona transfer across related and morphologically distant languages, analyzing robustness and degradation patterns.

Result: They find a clear architectural threshold at LoRA rank r=16 for effective behavioral reprogramming. Epoch ablation shows that generalization performance reliably converges optimally within 2–3 training epochs, depending on dataset density, reaching a minimum validation loss of 0.919. Scaling the model to 14B parameters further reduces localized evaluation perplexity to 1.414, indicating improved modeling of the target behavior. DPO is shown to effectively decouple assertive/Socratic behavior from specific syntactic patterns. Cross-lingual stress tests reveal strong behavioral alignment in closely related language families and predictable degradation in morphologically distant languages, mapping structural limits of zero-shot persona transfer.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that LLMs exhibit substantial cognitive plasticity and can be reprogrammed from passive to proactive, Socratic conversational styles using carefully tuned, compute-efficient PEFT and DPO pipelines. It establishes practical bounds on LoRA rank and training epochs for effective behavioral modification under HPC constraints and shows that these behavioral changes can transfer cross-lingually with high robustness in related languages but with clear structural limitations in distant ones. Overall, it provides a rigorous, empirically grounded recipe for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral alignment of open-weight LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are predominantly aligned to function as passive, sycophantic assistants. We challenge this default paradigm by empirically evaluating the cognitive plasticity of open-weight architectures when subjected to rigorous behavioral reprogramming. Our objective is to induce a proactive, Socratic conversational framework, characterized by high-frequency question generation under strictly constrained high-performance computing (HPC) conditions. Through a massively parallelized hyperparameter sweep comprising 405 HPC jobs, we define precise mathematical bounds for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT). We identify an architectural threshold at LoRA rank $r=16$ and demonstrate via extensive epoch ablation that generalization capacity strictly reaches its optimal convergence within an optimized training window of $e \in [2, 3]$ depending on dataset density (minimum validation loss of 0.919). Furthermore, scaling model capacity to 14B parameters yielded a lower localized evaluation perplexity (1.414). Subsequent Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) successfully decoupled the underlying assertive behavior from localized syntax, while rigorous cross-lingual stress testing reveals both the capabilities and the structural boundaries of zero-shot persona transfer, demonstrating robust alignment in closely related linguistic families alongside identifiable degradation pathways in morphologically distant targets. These findings establish a rigorous empirical framework for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification.

</details>


### [127] [EEG-PRIME: Prototype-Aligned Representation Learning with Multi-Level Conditioning for EEG Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13072)
*Shuailei Zhang,Muyun Jiang,Wei Zhang,Jinbo Chen,Zhiwei Guo,Yong Li,Yi Ding,Cuntai Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EEG-PRIME is a two-stage EEG foundation model that uses masked pretraining and instruction tuning with prototype alignment to enable subject-invariant, cross-dataset, multi-task EEG decoding and strong zero-shot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG decoding models typically fail to generalize across datasets and subjects because of domain shifts from different recording setups and individual neurophysiological differences. There is a need for a foundation-style EEG model that can handle multiple tasks and datasets with minimal or no target-domain calibration while remaining robust to subject variation.

Method: The authors design EEG-PRIME, a two-stage framework. In the pretraining stage, an EEG encoder is trained with a masked reconstruction objective, enhanced by frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation, to learn transferable EEG representations. In the instruction-tuning stage, they introduce instruction-aware conditioning that captures task semantics, dataset-specific characteristics, and subject-invariant information. This conditioning modulates a Q-Former via Layer-wise Query Modulation. For prediction, frozen text embeddings of class labels are used as prototypes, and classification is done by cosine similarity between EEG features and these prototypes, enabling operation across heterogeneous label spaces.

Result: Across sixteen EEG datasets spanning multiple paradigms (motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, mental workload), EEG-PRIME outperforms state-of-the-art baselines and previous EEG foundation models in cross-subject settings. On two additional held-out datasets that are not used in training or tuning, EEG-PRIME attains balanced accuracy on par with within-session calibration models, without any optimization, calibration, or linear probing on the target domain, indicating strong zero-shot generalization.

Conclusion: EEG-PRIME effectively mitigates domain shifts in EEG decoding by combining masked pretraining with instruction-aware, prototype-aligned tuning. It yields robust, subject-invariant representations that generalize across datasets, tasks, and label spaces, achieving state-of-the-art cross-subject performance and promising zero-shot transfer without target-domain adaptation.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) decoding models often generalize poorly across datasets and subjects due to domain shifts in acquisition protocols and individual neurophysiology. We propose EEG-PRIME, a two-stage EEG foundation model for cross-dataset multi-task decoding. EEG-PRIME combines masked pretraining with prototype-aligned instruction tuning to enable instruction-aware and subject-invariant decoding across diverse BCI paradigms. During pretraining, an EEG encoder learns transferable representations through masked reconstruction with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation. During instruction tuning, EEG-PRIME incorporates task-semantic, dataset-specific, and subject-invariant conditioning. The resulting conditioning signal modulates the Q-Former through Layer-wise Query Modulation, while frozen text embeddings of class labels serve as prototypes for cosine-similarity-based prediction across heterogeneous label spaces. Experiments on sixteen datasets covering motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, and mental workload show consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines and prior EEG foundation models under cross-subject settings. On two additional held-out datasets, EEG-PRIME achieves balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration models without target-domain optimization, calibration, or linear probing, demonstrating promising zero-shot transfer capability.

</details>


### [128] [SPADE: Speculative Decoding for Precise and Low Cost Distributed Edge Cloud Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13076)
*Divya Jyoti Bajpai,Kishan Kumar Upadhyay,Manjesh Kumar Hanawal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a distributed edge–cloud framework using speculative decoding to run LLM inference cheaply and quickly while maintaining the accuracy of a large cloud model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are powerful but expensive to run. Running them fully in the cloud is accurate but incurs high per-token cost and latency, while running small models on the edge is cheaper and faster but significantly less accurate. There is a need for a deployment method that preserves the accuracy of large LLMs while reducing both cloud cost and inference time, without retraining.

Method: The authors design a distributed inference framework that combines a small draft model on the edge with a large verifier model in the cloud via speculative decoding. The edge model rapidly proposes candidate tokens; the cloud model validates these tokens in parallel. Tokens accepted by the verifier are kept, while rejected ones trigger corrections computed by the large model. The architecture is plug-and-play, works with existing LLMs, and is evaluated on multiple NLP tasks (SpecBench and CNN/DailyMail).

Result: Across evaluated tasks, the framework reduces the number of calls to the cloud-based large model by 76% while maintaining the same task accuracy as running the full large model alone. This leads to lower latency and reduced cloud computation cost without sacrificing performance.

Conclusion: Integrating speculative decoding across edge and cloud enables a practical, scalable, and cost-efficient way to deploy LLMs. By offloading most computation to a small edge model and using the large cloud model only to verify and correct, the system retains the accuracy of the big model while significantly cutting cloud usage, inference time, and deployment cost, and requires no retraining of existing models.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in natural language understanding and generation, but their deployment is constrained by high computational demands. Deploying smaller LLMs directly on the edge can circumvent this, but with degraded accuracy. Deploying smaller cloud-based big LLMs preserves performance, but at the cost of expensive per-token computation. We present a distributed inference framework, \our{}, that integrates speculative decoding (SD) across edge and cloud. A compact draft model deployed on the edge generates candidate tokens rapidly, and a large verifier model on the cloud validates these tokens in parallel. Accepted tokens are retained, while only rejections trigger verifier correction, substantially reducing the number of cloud queries. Our plug-and-play design shifts the bulk of computation to the edge, significantly lowers inference time and cloud cost, and preserves the accuracy of the big model without any retraining requirement. Our approach demonstrates a practical path toward scalable, cost-efficient, and accurate deployment of LLMs in real-world environments. Experimental results across multiple Natural Language Processing tasks using SpecBench and CNN/Dailymail datasets demonstrate that \our{} reduces the cloud model calls by $76\%$ with zero loss in accuracy as compared to the full model.

</details>


### [129] [Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging: A Semantic Superposition and Contextual Lamination Framework for Malpractice-Resilient Online Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13100)
*Gupta Lovi Raj,Kaur Kamalpreet,Dama Sri Ram,Parani Prajithaa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new mathematical framework (MCCT) is proposed to hide online exam content in plain sight using semantic camouflage so humans can read it but automated extraction tools face high uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing online exam security tools (browser lockdown, webcams, behavior analytics) can be bypassed because attackers can still capture the actual assessment content via screenshots, screen sharing, OCR, and automated scraping. There is a need for a principled, content-centric defense that protects the rendered exam text itself rather than only controlling the environment.

Method: The authors extend the prior Multi-dimensional Spatio-Temporal Context Camouflaging Model (MSCCM) in the MARS suite by defining the Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT). They build a mathematical model of how exam content is rendered and how an adversary might extract it via an explicit extraction-channel operator. They introduce six coupled constructs: (1) Context Inversion Operator, (2) Contextual Lamination Operator, (3) Separation Channel, (4) Human Readability Functional, (5) Computational Ambiguity Functional, and (6) Context Camouflage Tensor. Using conditional entropy, they derive a closed-form expression for computational ambiguity, and an exact filtering identity that allows legitimate users to recover the true content. They also define theoretical properties for ambiguity, camouflage density, semantic preservation, leakage under multiple observations, and temporal multiplexing, and provide a rendering algorithm with complexity analysis and an evaluation protocol.

Result: The paper yields a general mathematical framework that formalizes semantic camouflage for assessment content, including closed-form measures of ambiguity faced by attackers, guarantees for exact recovery by legitimate users, and properties describing how camouflage density and temporal multiplexing affect security and readability. It also delivers an implementable rendering algorithm along with its computational complexity and a predefined protocol for empirical evaluation.

Conclusion: MCCT offers a rigorous theoretical basis for securing the visual representation of online assessment content through multi-layer semantic camouflage. It shows that it is possible to retain human readability for legitimate candidates while inducing quantifiable uncertainty for automated or unauthorized extraction tools, enabling behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware, and computationally robust online testing systems.

Abstract: Contemporary online assessment systems rely primarily on browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, and behavioural analytics, yet remain vulnerable to attacks that extract the assessment content itself through screenshots, screen sharing, optical character recognition, and automated scraping. This paper extends the Multi-dimensional Spatio-Temporal Context Camouflaging Model (MSCCM) within the MARS (Multi-modal Assessment Resilience Suite) by introducing the Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT), a mathematical framework that protects rendered assessment content through semantic superposition. Authentic assessment content and synthetically generated camouflage are represented as a unified rendering while remaining recoverable only by legitimate candidates. The framework models the adversarial extraction process through an explicit extraction-channel operator and develops six coupled constructs: the Context Inversion Operator, Contextual Lamination Operator, Separation Channel, Human Readability Functional, Computational Ambiguity Functional, and Context Camouflage Tensor. Computational ambiguity is formulated using conditional entropy, yielding a closed-form expression that quantifies uncertainty during unauthorized extraction, while legitimate recovery is guaranteed through an exact filtering identity. We further establish theoretical properties governing ambiguity, camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing, and present a rendering algorithm with computational complexity and a pre-registered evaluation protocol. MCCT provides a mathematically rigorous foundation for behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware, and computationally resilient digital assessment by securing rendered assessment content while preserving readability for legitimate users.

</details>


### [130] [Robust Dempster-Shafer Evidence Fusion with Chaos-Conflict Measurement and Historical-Experience Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13108)
*Huiyu Li,Weibo Liu,Xinru Xu,Dongchen Gao,Meng Zhang,Junhua Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified Dempster-Shafer evidence fusion framework that jointly measures conflict and uncertainty, leverages historical performance to weight sources contextually, and adaptively combines evidence to improve classification performance over existing DST and boosting baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Dempster-Shafer based fusion struggles with two gaps: (1) conflict measures typically handle inter-evidence conflict and intra-evidence uncertainty separately, leading to partial or biased assessments of evidence quality; and (2) most fusion rules treat evidence sources based only on current observations, ignoring how reliable they have been across different decision scenarios historically. This limits robustness and adaptability in real-world multi-source decision-making tasks.

Method: The authors design (1) a chaos-conflict measurement that simultaneously captures cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity, backed by five proven properties for consistency; (2) a historical experience-based weighting strategy that first partitions the decision space using spectral clustering, then uses regret theory to estimate context-dependent reliability of each source from past fusion performance; (3) a hybrid combination rule that adaptively trades off between preserving uncertainty and enforcing weighted consensus based on the overall conflict level; and (4) a belief-interval based decision rule that performs classification while explicitly retaining epistemic uncertainty rather than forcing overconfident decisions.

Result: On 16 real benchmark datasets, the proposed framework achieves an average F1 score of 85.78 and mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight existing DST fusion baselines and three gradient boosting methods. An ablation study further shows that each major component (chaos-conflict metric, historical weighting, adaptive combination, belief-interval decision) contributes positively to performance.

Conclusion: A unified, adaptive evidence reasoning framework under Dempster-Shafer theory that integrates a new conflict-uncertainty measure, context-aware historical weighting, and an adaptive fusion/decision mechanism can significantly improve multi-source decision-making performance and robustness compared with standard DST approaches and strong machine learning baselines.

Abstract: Multi-source evidence fusion under Dempster-Shafer theory faces two persistent challenges: existing conflict measures assess inter-evidence inconsistency and intra-evidence uncertainty independently, yielding incomplete evaluations, and current fusion methods evaluate evidence sources exclusively through instantaneous comparisns without exploiting their long-term reliability across diverse decision contexts. This paper proposes a unified evidence reasoning framework that addresses both limitations. Specifically, a chaos-conflict measurement is introduced to jointly quantify cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity, with five formally proven properties ensuring consistent assessment. A historical experience driven weighting scheme partitions the decision space via spectral clustering and applies regret theory to compute context-specific reliability profiles from past fusion outcomes. These mechanisms feed into a hybrid combination rule that adaptively balances uncertainty preservation against weighted consensus, controlled by the global conflict level, followed by a belief-interval decision strategy that enables robust classification without discarding epistemic uncertainty. Experiments on 16 real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves an average F1 score of 85.78 and a mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods. Ablation analysis confirms the contribution of each component we proposed. The framework offers an effective approach for adaptive evidence fusion in multi-source decision making.

</details>


### [131] [SkillEvo: Self-Renewing Evolution Gradients from Multi-Turn Interaction Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13120)
*Qianxi Yan,Chunrong Chen,Jiuzhou Zhao,Min Zhang,Yongzhou Xu,Xiaochuan Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SkillEvo, a framework for continuously improving LLM-based agent skills using multi-turn feedback and an explicit governance layer, outperforming existing self-reflection and QA-based evolution methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent skills are either manually written or generated once by an LLM, and they lack a closed feedback loop from real interaction failures. Prior attempts to close this loop rely on single-turn QA evaluation, which quickly saturates: after obvious single-turn gaps are patched, deeper multi-turn defects no longer receive clear learning signals. Governance is similarly crude, using a single scalar score to accept or reject candidates without localizing or fixing structural problems. The authors aim to enable sustained, reliable evolution of skills by providing richer, trustworthy feedback and better control over how evolution proceeds.

Method: The authors propose SkillEvo, which has two main components. First, they transform multi-turn user simulation from a final evaluation step into an iterative feedback generator: simulated follow-up questions are used to reveal defects layer by layer over multiple turns, and each round of skill revision both uses existing feedback and generates new, deeper feedback. Second, they add an independent governance layer that does more than just accept or reject a skill candidate. This layer actively repairs factual errors and structural bloat introduced during evolution, constraining the direction of the evolution gradient so that skills do not drift or degrade over many iterations. Together, these mechanisms create a sustained, closed-loop optimization process for skill evolution.

Result: In experiments spanning six categories of cloud services, 9 real production skills, and 98 associated skill-reference files, SkillEvo achieves substantial performance gains. Quantitatively, it outperforms evolution based on self-reflection by 23.0 points and evolution driven by single-turn QA evaluation by 15.4 points, suggesting that its multi-turn feedback and governance mechanisms provide a stronger and more reliable improvement signal.

Conclusion: SkillEvo demonstrates that the key to long-term improvement of LLM agent skills is not just the ability to edit skills or run many iterations, but to maintain a steady supply of trustworthy, structured feedback and to govern how that feedback drives changes. By turning multi-turn interaction into a feedback engine and adding an active governance layer that repairs and constrains skill changes, the framework avoids the saturation and degradation issues of prior methods and delivers significantly better evolved skills across diverse, real-world cloud service domains.

Abstract: Agent Skills are today either hand-authored or produced in a single LLM generation pass, and consequently possess no closed loop through which they might improve from the interaction failures they actually cause. Recent work does close this loop, but derives its feedback from single-turn question-answering evaluation. The consequence is a sharp asymmetry: once the first round has patched the gaps that a single exchange can reveal, the evolution gradient decays, the defects that surface only across multiple turns remain invisible, and evolution stalls. Governance in these systems is likewise driven by an end-to-end verification score, a scalar gate that can reject a degraded candidate but can neither localize nor repair its structural cause. We argue that the binding constraint on sustained skill evolution is neither editing capability nor the number of iterations, but whether the evaluation feedback keeps supplying trustworthy evolution gradients. We introduce SkillEvo, in which trustworthy feedback generates the gradient and controllable governance constrains its direction. The first component recasts multi-turn user simulation from an evaluation endpoint into a feedback generator: follow-up questions expose defects layer by layer, so that every round of revision both consumes feedback and produces new feedback. The second replaces the passive rejection of a scalar gate with an independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat, preventing the gradient from drifting as degradation accumulates. Across six categories of cloud services, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files, SkillEvo surpasses self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single- turn-QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points.

</details>


### [132] [Numeracy in Large Language Models: Fundamental Limitations and Paths to Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13129)
*Aoxin Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper surveys why large language models fail at basic numerical tasks despite good performance on math reasoning, introduces a framework (NGF) to categorize numerical abilities and failures, organizes existing benchmarks and methods, and gives practical recommendations to improve numerical reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs perform well on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, they are surprisingly unreliable at simple numerical tasks such as comparing magnitudes, doing large integer arithmetic, handling fractions, or working with scientific notation. This gap matters for any application that depends on trustworthy numeric behavior. There is a need to conceptually separate and systematically study basic numeracy, understand its failure modes and structural causes in current architectures and training pipelines, and consolidate fragmented benchmarks and mitigation strategies into a coherent picture.

Method: The authors propose the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF), which divides numeracy into two core components: Representational Grounding (RG), i.e., correctly mapping numeral strings to values, magnitudes, and equivalent numeric forms; and Procedural Grounding (PG), i.e., correctly executing arithmetic operations according to their formal definitions. Using this framework, they categorize and analyze diagnostic benchmarks, observed failure patterns, hypothesized structural causes (tokenization, positional encodings, embedding geometry, and data distribution), and mitigation techniques. They also empirically apply NGF to evaluate three state-of-the-art LLM families on multiple benchmarks (Number Cookbook, NumericBench, GSM-Symbolic), contrasting performance on isolated numeric skills (atomic), numeracy within context, and numeracy supported by reasoning steps or tools.

Result: The survey finds that current LLMs have systematic weaknesses in both representational and procedural grounding, especially for larger numbers and less frequent formats. Architecture-level changes such as digit-aware tokenization and specialized numeric embeddings (e.g., Abacus Embeddings) can reliably enhance numeracy when models are trained from scratch. However, such modifications are not typically accessible to end users of already-pretrained models. For those users, the most practical and effective improvements come from supervised fine-tuning on numerical tasks, using reasoning scaffolds (e.g., chain-of-thought or decomposition prompts), and integrating external computational tools.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that basic numerical understanding is a distinct and underdeveloped capability in LLMs compared to high-level mathematical reasoning. The Numerical Grounding Framework helps clarify where and why models fail and how different interventions target representational vs. procedural aspects of numeracy. For deployment, the authors recommend combining prompt-based reasoning support, task-specific fine-tuning, and tool integration to reduce numerical errors in current systems, while advocating for future research on architecture and training strategies—such as better tokenization and numeric embeddings—that yield more intrinsically grounded numerical behavior in foundation models.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve strong results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks yet remain unreliable on elementary numerical tasks, including magnitude comparison, large-integer arithmetic, fractions, and scientific notation. This survey examines basic numerical understanding as a capability distinct from high-level mathematical reasoning. We propose the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF), which decomposes numeracy into Representational Grounding (RG), mapping numeral forms to value, magnitude, and equivalent representations, and Procedural Grounding (PG), executing arithmetic operations in accordance with their mathematical definitions. Using NGF, we organize recent diagnostic benchmarks, failure modes, structural explanations, and mitigation strategies. We review evidence concerning tokenization, positional encoding, embedding geometry, and pretraining-data distribution. We also apply NGF in a coordinated evaluation of three frontier model families across Number Cookbook, NumericBench, and GSM-Symbolic, comparing atomic, contextual, and reasoning-assisted numeracy. Architectural interventions such as digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings can improve models trained from scratch but are generally unavailable to users of pretrained systems, for whom supervised fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools are more practical. We conclude with deployment recommendations and research directions for more reliable numerical behavior in foundation models.

</details>


### [133] [Rethinking Normalization Placement for LLMs: Post-Norm under Curriculum Depth Growing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13156)
*Sheng Ren,Yadong Wang,Naiqiang Tan,Jiangang Kong,Jun Fang,Rui Liu,Jun Wang,Kai Chen,Lipeng Liang,Xiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how the choice between pre-norm and post-norm Transformers interacts with a curriculum that grows model depth during distillation, finding that post-norm becomes better specifically under curriculum depth growth and that normalization placement and training curriculum should be designed together.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-norm has become the default normalization placement in Transformer architectures because it eases optimization of deep models under standard joint training. However, when model depth is not fixed from the start but instead increased gradually through a curriculum, each new block is conditioned on boundary representations from already-trained layers. This may change how normalization impacts optimization and representation scaling. The authors want to know whether the usual preference for pre-norm still holds when depth is introduced via a curriculum in knowledge distillation, and to understand the mechanisms behind any differences.

Method: The authors perform a controlled distillation study using a Qwen3-8B model as teacher and a 9-layer Transformer as student. They compare pre-norm vs post-norm normalization placement under two regimes: (1) standard joint training of the full-depth student, and (2) curriculum depth growth where layers are appended over time, with new blocks receiving boundary representations from a trained prefix. They also include several controls: a post-joint control matched by active-layer tokens to rule out pure compute differences; single-block and freeze controls to separate effects of shallow-block quality and retraining; and diagnostics of residual scales, token-wise scale drift, and block behavior (e.g., near-identity mappings) at curriculum boundaries.

Result: Under standard joint training of the full 9-layer student, pre-norm and post-norm achieve almost identical validation cross-entropy (difference of only 0.0004). Under curriculum depth growth, however, post-norm outperforms pre-norm significantly, improving validation cross-entropy by 0.0328—an order of magnitude larger effect. A compute-matched post-joint control still underperforms the post-grow (curriculum) setup, showing that extra compute is not the sole driver. During the curriculum, the performance ranking of pre- vs post-norm crosses over, with post-norm becoming superior once new blocks are appended. Analysis shows that post-norm yields stable residual scales, while pre-norm exhibits structural-token scale drift, and the final pre-grow block in the pre-norm setup behaves nearly as an identity map on a fixed batch.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that normalization placement (pre-norm vs post-norm) and training curriculum (especially curriculum depth growth in distillation) interact in a non-trivial way and should be considered jointly. While pre-norm and post-norm are equivalent under standard joint training for the studied setting, post-norm becomes clearly superior when depth is grown via a curriculum. Diagnostic evidence suggests that boundary-scale conditioning when appending new blocks—manifested in residual scale stability vs drift and near-identity behavior—explains the advantage of post-norm in this regime. Designers of distilled Transformer students that grow in depth should therefore treat normalization placement and training schedule as coupled design decisions.

Abstract: Pre-norm is the standard normalization placement in modern Transformers because it facilitates joint optimization of full-depth models. We ask whether this preference persists when depth is introduced through a curriculum. In curriculum depth growth, each appended block receives the boundary representation produced by a trained prefix, making normalization placement relevant to forward conditioning. We therefore test whether placement and training curriculum interact. In a controlled distillation study with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, pre-norm and post-norm are indistinguishable under joint training, differing by $0.0004$ validation CE, while post-norm improves over pre-norm by $0.0328$ under curriculum growth, an order of magnitude larger. A post-joint control matched by student active-layer tokens remains worse than post-grow, which rules out compute as the sole explanation. The ranking crosses over during the curriculum: post-norm takes the lead once blocks are appended. Single-block and freeze controls localize the ranking change to block appending rather than shallow-block quality or retraining. Boundary diagnostics associate post-norm with stable residual scales and pre-norm with structural-token scale drift; on a fixed batch, the final pre-grow block is also nearly identity-mapped. Together with the phase-wise crossover, these observations are consistent with boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks are appended. The results motivate treating normalization placement and training curriculum as coupled design choices in this distillation setting.

</details>


### [134] [SkillShapley: Boundary-Adaptive Shapley Valuation for Skill Step Attribution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13173)
*Chang Liu,Yuqi Zhang,Yiman Zhong,Boyi Liu,Hengjun Wang,Shuyue Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SkillShapley, a Shapley value-based method to attribute performance contributions to individual steps in language agent skills, enabling better understanding and optimization of long procedural skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language agents rely on multi-step skills to perform complex tasks like coding and document processing, but current skills are handcrafted or derived from traces without a principled way to measure how much each step helps or hurts task performance. This lack of step-level attribution makes it hard to refine, debug, or automatically improve skills. The authors aim to provide a rigorous, quantitative framework to assess the contribution of each step within a skill to overall task success.

Method: The authors formulate step-level attribution as a cooperative game and use Shapley value theory to estimate each step’s contribution to the final performance. They propose SkillShapley, which works in two phases: (1) it identifies informative coalitional regions of step subsets by leveraging the fact that benchmark rewards are discretized and show sharp performance cliffs; (2) it adaptively samples new step coalitions in those regions to gather reusable marginal evidence, exploiting that step interactions are mostly additive and not strongly synergistic. This design aims to approximate Shapley values efficiently while preserving attribution fidelity.

Result: On skills from SkillsBench, a standard benchmark for language agent skills, SkillShapley can reliably distinguish high-value from low-value steps, doing so more efficiently than naive Shapley estimation. The framework surfaces which steps are critical for success and which are redundant or even detrimental, demonstrating practical utility for analyzing and improving skills in real agent pipelines.

Conclusion: SkillShapley offers an effective and computationally efficient framework for step-level attribution in language agent skills by leveraging Shapley value concepts and empirical properties of benchmarks. It enables systematic identification of valuable and non-valuable steps, providing actionable insights for designing, pruning, and refining agent skills. This contributes a principled tool toward more interpretable and optimized language-agent workflows.

Abstract: Agent skills are crucial external instructions that enable language agents to execute long procedural tasks such as coding or document processing. Existing agent skills are primarily created through human manual crafting or agent execution traces, with limited understanding of how each step contributes to overall skill performance on specific tasks; i.e., there remains an open problem in quantifying the contribution of individual steps within an agent skill. To address this issue, we first model skill-step attribution as a Shapley value-based contribution estimation problem, and then propose SkillShapley, a step-level attribution framework for agent skills. Notably, SkillShapley operates in two phases, motivated by key empirical insights, i.e., discretized benchmark rewards that create sharp performance cliffs, and step interactions that are largely additive rather than synergistic. Specifically, it first identifies informative coalitional regions, and then adaptively samples new coalitions that can yield reusable marginal evidence. Experiments on skills from the widely adopted SkillsBench demonstrate that our SkillShapley can effectively and efficiently identify high- or low-value skill steps, providing several key takeaways for agent skill creation.

</details>


### [135] [Teach the Magnitude, Not the Direction: Verifier-Bounded Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn Multi-step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13179)
*Zechuan Wang,Siyuan Lu,Hongxuan Zhang,Linjian Mo,Chenyi Zhuang,Leilei Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CrEST is a hierarchical credit assignment method that combines verifier-bounded RL with dense token-level guidance, improving multi-turn tool-use agents, especially on long, complex trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) provides strong, trustworthy performance guarantees because rewards are based on an external verifier, but it only delivers a single trajectory-level reward, which mixes good and bad decisions across turns and harms learning, especially for multi-turn tool-use agents. On-policy distillation, on the other hand, gives dense token-level supervision but is limited by the teacher’s quality or leads to optimization pathologies like gradient concentration collapse. There is a need for a method that keeps RL’s verifier-bounded performance ceiling while gaining the dense credit assignment benefits of distillation, without inheriting its downsides.

Method: The paper proposes CrEST, a hierarchical credit assignment framework. First, it decomposes RL’s trajectory-level rewards into turn-segmented verified advantages, addressing inter-turn dilution by assigning credit to specific turns within multi-turn trajectories. Second, within each turn, it uses a privileged self-teacher’s token-level signals, modulated via an entropy-gated mechanism, to refine which tokens contributed positively, thus handling intra-turn credit assignment. The self-teacher does not dictate the direction of updates (as in standard distillation) but only scales the magnitude of updates, retaining the verifier as the ultimate signal provider while leveraging dense supervision.

Result: On the BFCL V3 and WildToolBench benchmarks, across two model scales, CrEST consistently outperforms both pure RL (RLVR) and distillation-based baselines. The improvements are largest for long trajectories and strict session-level evaluation metrics, where credit assignment is especially challenging. This empirically supports that hierarchical credit assignment with verifier-grounded advantages plus self-teacher modulation leads to better tool-use performance.

Conclusion: CrEST shows that it is possible to combine the verifier-bounded performance guarantees of RLVR with dense, token-level credit assignment without falling back to teacher-bounded performance. By restricting the teacher’s influence to modulating update magnitudes while keeping update directions governed by verifier-derived advantages, the method unlocks more effective learning for multi-turn tool-use agents, particularly on long, complex interaction sequences.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) offers a verifier-bounded performance ceiling for training multi-turn tool-use agents, yet its trajectory-level credit assignment conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes into a single reward signal. On-policy distillation provides dense per-token supervision but is either teacher-bounded or prone to gradient concentration collapse. We introduce $\textbf{CrEST}$, a hierarchical credit assignment framework that retains RL's verifier-bounded ceiling while incorporating dense token-level signals from a privileged self-teacher. $\textbf{CrEST}$ resolves credit at two levels: turn-segmented verified advantages address inter-turn dilution, while entropy-gated self-teacher modulation refines intra-turn token contributions. Experiments on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show that $\textbf{CrEST}$ consistently outperforms both RL and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long-trajectory and strict session-level metrics. Our work demonstrates that the teacher's role in policy optimization can be reduced from determining update directions to modulating update magnitudes, unlocking dense credit assignment without sacrificing the verifier-bounded ceiling.

</details>


### [136] [TsuGO: Probing Search Efficiency in LLM Reasoning via Go Life-and-Death Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13221)
*Shunwen Bai,Ziping Ma,Chaoyang Zhang,Yarong Wang,Jiale Liu,Zhen Qin,Qingpei Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes TsuGO, a benchmark using Go life-and-death puzzles to evaluate how LLMs organize and allocate resources in their reasoning search, beyond just answer correctness or CoT length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM reasoning evaluation focuses on final answers or superficial chain-of-thought properties like coherence and redundancy, which do not reveal how models plan search, choose candidate moves, backtrack, or allocate tokens. Many benchmarks involve single-objective tasks solvable by static abilities, so they cannot isolate and measure search organization. The authors want a way to study and quantify search efficiency and reasoning-resource allocation as distinct capabilities of LLMs.

Method: They construct TsuGO, a process-level benchmark based on Go life-and-death (tsumego) problems, which naturally require adversarial search and exploration of move branches. The benchmark constrains the solution space so that chain-of-thought traces can be parsed into explicit search trees. They define metrics including Search Efficiency and Token Efficiency, and provide diagnostics and visualizations that measure where and how models generate candidates, check responses, compare branches, and backtrack during reasoning. They then run various LLMs on these problems and compare their search behavior to both naive search procedures and a strong Go engine (KataGo) as a neural-guided search reference.

Result: Empirical results show that current LLMs are far from reliably solving tsumego. Stronger LLMs tend to succeed when they quickly find promising candidates and maintain focus on productive branches, but most models behave similarly to unguided search, rather than exhibiting the structured, guided search patterns of systems like KataGo. The analysis also shows that longer chain-of-thoughts or higher token efficiency do not necessarily correlate with better search quality or problem-solving performance.

Conclusion: Search organization and reasoning-resource allocation are critical but underexplored dimensions of LLM reasoning. TsuGO demonstrates that current LLMs lack stable, efficient search behavior on structured adversarial tasks like Go life-and-death problems. Process-level evaluation with constrained, verifiable search spaces is necessary to move beyond answer accuracy and CoT superficialities toward a deeper understanding and improvement of LLM reasoning processes.

Abstract: The evaluation of LLM reasoning is moving from final-answer accuracy to process-level assessment, yet existing methods still fail to capture how models plan reasoning paths and allocate reasoning resources--that is, how they organize search. Prior process-level methods focus on the coherence and redundancy of chain-of-thought (CoT), and most benchmark tasks have a single objective solvable by static capabilities such as derivation and tool use, leaving search organization unmeasured. We introduce TsuGO, a process-level reasoning benchmark for evaluating Search Efficiency in LLM reasoning through Go life-and-death problems.
