These problems provide closed and verifiable solution spaces with an inherent adversarial structure, making candidate generation, response checking, branch comparison, and backtracking necessary parts of reasoning rather than incidental trace patterns. By constraining the solution space, TsuGO disentangles domain knowledge from search organization, parses CoT into a structured search tree, and reports Search Efficiency together with Token Efficiency and other diagnostic metrics and visualizations. Experiments show that current LLMs remain far from stable tsumego solving: stronger models succeed by finding the correct candidate earlier and sustaining effort on productive branches, but most models still behave much closer to unguided search algorithms than to neural-guided KataGo. Longer CoT or higher Token Efficiency does not necessarily imply better search. Our results identify search organization and reasoning-resource allocation as missing dimensions in LLM reasoning evaluation.

</details>


### [137] [Capability Sheaves for Compositional Agent-Harness Repair: Controlled Quotients and a Real-Repository Stress Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13228)
*Saveliy Batruin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a cohomology-based framework (capability sheaves) to analyze and debug complex agent systems, shows in a controlled setting that it correctly factors out irrelevant hidden state, and in a real code-repair benchmark finds no significant performance gain over strong non-cohomological baselines, only invariance and identifiability benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern agent systems (retrieval, routing, tools, verification, etc.) combine many components that must share state; in practice, different "locally good" components can disagree about this shared state, causing failures that are hard to diagnose. The authors want a principled, mathematically grounded way to model such systems, detect inconsistencies, and separate meaningful structure from nuisance variation (like hidden mediator states or stale representations) without assuming that agents are perfectly synchronized or that symbolic reasoning always wins.

Method: They formalize an agent harness as a finite capability sheaf, where each stalk encodes a typed behavior signature for a component, restriction maps capture shared fields between components, and valid system executions correspond to global sections consistent across all overlaps. Acceptance of runs is cast as an exact finite constraint satisfaction problem (CSP). On top of this, they construct a linearized relative cohomology class that summarizes how local states fail to glue into a global state; this class becomes both a diagnostic signal and a way to search or rank configurations. They first run a controlled experiment on 20 synthetic or semi-synthetic task clusters with hidden mediators whose internal states act as nuisance variables, and analyze how quotienting by coboundaries (identifying states differing only by mediator fluctuations) changes the search budget. Then they apply the same machinery to a realistic benchmark constructed from the SWE-bench Multilingual pool (PatchFuseBench): 160 software issues over 20 repositories with 875 candidate patches and thousands of fine-grained edit atoms. They build two cohomological constructions: a pool-level class over all candidates, and a finer, candidate-indexed repair signal. They compare these to matched non-cohomological selectors and to strong baselines, including abstention gates and anchors, using issue resolution counts and sign-flip tests across repositories to assess statistical support.

Result: In the controlled mediator experiment, many raw candidate configurations differ only in hidden mediator state. By quotienting out coboundaries (treating such differences as equivalent), they can cut the candidate budget per cluster from 2,000 to 1,000 without losing performance; when they additionally align the hidden state explicitly, the apparent performance gap disappears. Exact CSP reasoning and the cohomological quotient yield the same accepted runs, indicating that the main contribution is invariance to stale or redundant representations rather than improved logical power. On the PatchFuseBench discovery split, the first, coarse pool-level cohomology class is constant (since [b−Dx]=[b] in coker D), so it cannot rank candidate configurations at all. A refined, candidate-indexed construction produces a nontrivial signal on 848 of 875 candidates and varies within 120 of 160 issues. Using it as a selector resolves 118 issues, versus 116 for a carefully matched non-cohomological selector; this 2-issue edge is not statistically supported by a repository-wise sign-flip test (p=0.75). A more conservative, leave-one-repository-out abstention gate based on the same machinery reaches 127/160 issues, tying a strong anchor baseline and beating its own matched gate by a single issue, which again is not statistically significant (p=1.0). The pre-registered confirmatory split is thus not opened, since the discovery-phase gating result fails to show real support.

Conclusion: Capability sheaves and the associated relative cohomology classes provide a rigorous way to model multi-component agent systems, detect and factor out nuisance variation (such as differences in hidden mediator states), and correct identifiability problems. In controlled settings, the cohomological machinery reproduces exact CSP outcomes while being invariant to stale or redundant representations, confirming the intended invariance and state-alignment benefits. However, when tested on a realistic software repair benchmark, the approach does not exhibit a statistically significant performance advantage over strong, non-cohomological baselines: the coarse pool-level construction is trivial, and the finer candidate-indexed signal yields at best marginal, non-robust gains. The paper’s main contribution is therefore methodological clarity and an invariance mechanism, not a demonstrated real-world performance boost from cohomological reasoning itself.

Abstract: Agent harnesses combine retrieval, routing, state, provenance, and verification, but locally successful components may disagree on shared state. We model this failure with a finite \emph{capability sheaf}: stalks encode typed behavior signatures, restriction maps retain shared fields, and accepted runs are useful global sections. An exact finite constraint-satisfaction problem (CSP) defines acceptance, while a linearized relative cohomology class provides a diagnostic and search feature.
  A controlled experiment over 20 task clusters introduces hidden interior mediators whose raw states are nuisance variables. Quotienting their coboundaries reduces the candidate budget from 2,000 to 1,000 per cluster; aligning the hidden state removes the gap. Exact CSP matches the quotient, so the result demonstrates invariance to stale representatives, not superiority over exact reasoning.
  We then test the method on a discovery split from the SWE-bench Multilingual pool of PatchFuseBench: 160 issues from 20 repositories, 875 real candidate patches, 2,579 source-aware edit atoms, and 153 newly executed patches. A first pool-level construction is constant because $[b-Dx]=[b]$ in $\operatorname{coker}D$ and therefore cannot rank configurations. A candidate-indexed repair is nontrivial on 848/875 candidates and varies within 120/160 issues. It resolves 118 issues versus 116 for a matched noncohomological selector, but the difference is not supported across repositories (exact sign-flip $p=0.75$). A leave-one-repository-out abstention gate reaches 127/160, tying the strong anchor and exceeding its matched gate by one issue ($p=1.0$). The discovery gate therefore fails and the confirmatory split remains sealed. The study supports the controlled invariance mechanism and an identifiability correction, but not a real-world cohomological advantage.

</details>


### [138] [vToken: Token-Level Virtualization for Reclaimable KV Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13263)
*Yuanhang Gao,Xiangrui Yang,Yuanfeng Chen,Hongjia Chen,Qianru Lv,Wenfei Wu,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces vToken, a token-level virtualization layer for KV cache management that reduces memory fragmentation and improves throughput in LLM serving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM serving systems suffer from a memory bottleneck because KV cache memory grows with sequence length and batch size. PagedAttention reduces allocator-level fragmentation using fixed-size blocks, but modern eviction algorithms work at token granularity, creating intra-block fragmentation and making much of the KV memory unreclaimable. There is a need for a mechanism that aligns fine-grained eviction with block-based memory management without breaking existing kernels or requiring large code changes.

Method: The authors design vToken, a lightweight token-level virtualization layer. vToken decouples logical token liveness from physical KV block placement using a token-table indirection that provides a stable logical token view. It performs physical memory reclamation by asynchronously repacking live tokens into blocks, while preserving compatibility with existing PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graphs. vToken is implemented in vLLM and integrated with several eviction policies (H2O, Random, Scissorhands).

Result: In experiments across models and eviction policies, vToken reduces the number of retained KV blocks per request by 27.2%–72.3% versus a Naive-Evict baseline. This leads to up to 1.37× higher throughput under an SLA constraint. With a fixed active-KV budget, vToken can double the maximum feasible concurrency. It also cuts the per-policy integration code from over 500 lines to under 50, simplifying adoption.

Conclusion: vToken effectively bridges the mismatch between token-level eviction and block-based KV cache management. By virtualizing tokens and enabling asynchronous repacking, it significantly reduces intra-block fragmentation, improves throughput and concurrency under memory constraints, and simplifies integrating various eviction policies into LLM serving systems, all while remaining compatible with existing PagedAttention infrastructure.

Abstract: Large language model serving faces a critical memory bottleneck: the KV cache grows with sequence length and batch size. PagedAttention uses fixed-size memory blocks to reduce allocator-level fragmentation, but recent KV eviction algorithms operate at a token granularity finer than block-level management. This mismatch causes intra-block fragmentation, leaving a large fraction of allocated KV memory unreclaimable. We present vToken, a lightweight token-level virtualization layer that decouples logical token liveness from physical block placement. vToken maintains a stable logical token view through token-table indirection and realizes physical reclamation by repacking live tokens asynchronously. The design preserves PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graph compatibility. We implement vToken in vLLM and evaluate it with H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models. Compared with a paired Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2\%--72.3\% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37$\times$. Under a constrained active-KV budget, it extends the maximum feasible concurrency by up to 2$\times$, while reducing the per-policy integration footprint from 500+ lines to under 50.

</details>


### [139] [Sovereign by necessity? Frontier AI export controls, cyber security, and the limits of national AI capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13272)
*Alan Woodward,Andrew Rogoyski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how control over frontier AI models is becoming a lever of national cyber defense and explores what AI sovereignty can realistically mean for small, middle, and large powers. It proposes a layered strategy to mitigate dependence on a few states and firms that dominate cutting-edge AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier AI development is highly concentrated in a few firms located in two states, whose governments can and do restrict global access to the most advanced models. A 2026 US licensing requirement that forced the sudden global withdrawal of leading models, combined with the first autonomous AI-driven cyber espionage campaign and changing cyber-attack/defense economics, reveals that frontier AI access is now entangled with national security and cyber defense. Many countries fear strategic vulnerability if their access to such models can be revoked.

Method: The paper uses qualitative policy and market analysis. It examines regulatory actions (like US export-style controls), real-world incidents (AI-run cyber espionage), and economic data on training costs, compute concentration, and national AI support programs. It then reasons from these empirical observations to assess the feasibility of AI sovereignty for different categories of states and to derive a set of strategic policy options.

Result: The study finds that: (1) Access to frontier AI models is de facto part of national cyber defense posture. (2) This access is contingent and can be withdrawn quickly for political or security reasons. (3) Building fully sovereign frontier capabilities is currently feasible only for a small number of major powers, due to high training costs, concentrated compute, and dependence on a few firms and infrastructures. (4) Open‑weight models offer a partial hedge but carry more capability and political exposure than many policymakers assume. (5) Near-term systemic risk stems less from the absolute performance of models and more from how and where they are deployed, governed, and contained.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that complete AI sovereignty at the frontier level is unrealistic for most states in the near term. Instead, governments should pursue a layered strategy: negotiate access guarantees with model-controlling states and firms; ensure sovereignty at the inference layer (control over deployment environments and usage); use open‑weight frontier or near‑frontier models as a strategic hedge; develop pooled regional capabilities; invest in long-term talent pipelines; and strengthen baseline cyber resilience. Proper deployment, governance, and containment of capable models are more urgent for risk reduction than marginal performance improvements in the models themselves.

Abstract: A small number of firms based in two states produce the most capable frontier AI models. The governments of those states have shown both the legal power and the political will to decide which other countries may use these systems. In June 2026 the United States required a leading developer to obtain licences before releasing its most advanced models to any foreign person, including foreign nationals resident in the United States. The affected models were withdrawn worldwide at short notice, partly because the restriction proved impractical to administer. This followed within months of the first documented case of a largely autonomous, AI-run cyber espionage campaign, and coincided with mounting evidence that frontier models alter the economics of both cyber attack and cyber defence. This article examines how these two developments interact, and situates them within the unusual market dynamics now driving large-scale AI development. It argues that access to frontier AI is becoming part of national cyber defence, that such access can be revoked, and that the obvious remedy of sovereign capability remains only partly feasible for all but a handful of states. Drawing on evidence about training costs, the concentration of computing power and the support offered by national AI programmes, it asks what sovereignty can realistically mean for small and middle powers, and for large powers as well. The article proposes a layered strategy: negotiated access guarantees, sovereignty at the level of inference, hedging with open-weight models, pooled regional capability, sustained talent development and continued investment in basic cyber resilience. The open-weight hedge proves at once more capable and more politically exposed than is commonly assumed. Much of the near-term risk lies in how capable models are deployed and contained rather than in their apparent performance.

</details>


### [140] [Towards Context-Aware Clinical Motion Understanding in Daily Living at Home: Freezing of Gait Detection with Egocentric Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13283)
*Vayalet Stefanova,Diwas Lamsal,Margot Genbrugge,Maxim Yudayev,Christian Schlenstedt,Moran Gilat,Bart Vanrumste,Benjamin Filtjens*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how adding egocentric video context can improve understanding and detection of freezing of gait (FOG) in Parkinson's disease during daily living, comparing pretrained vision and time-series models with an IMU-based TCN baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Similar inertial motion patterns in activities of daily living may indicate very different underlying causes, such as intentional stopping, interacting with objects, or pathological impairment like freezing of gait. Traditional IMU-only methods lack task context and may misinterpret such signals. Egocentric (first-person) video can provide rich contextual information about the environment and tasks, potentially improving clinical motion understanding and FOG detection in real-world home settings.

Method: The authors collect synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMU data, and expert-annotated freezing of gait labels from 13 people with Parkinson's disease in their homes. They evaluate frozen representations from pretrained egocentric video foundation models (e.g., V-JEPA2) and pretrained time-series foundation models, and compare them to an IMU-based temporal convolutional network (TCN) trained from scratch. Performance is assessed under leave-one-subject-out cross-validation using event-detection metrics (F1, AUROC). Qualitative analyses explore what contextual information ego-video captures that might be complementary to IMUs.

Result: The IMU-based TCN trained from scratch achieved the best quantitative performance for FOG event detection, with 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC. Pretrained ego-video features (V-JEPA2) achieved lower but still meaningful performance (32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC), showing above-chance discrimination of FOG. Ego-video appeared to capture FOG-relevant contextual information that is not redundant with IMU signals, as suggested by qualitative analyses.

Conclusion: While IMU-based models currently outperform ego-video alone for at-home FOG detection, egocentric vision provides nontrivial, complementary contextual information about daily living activities and environment. Pretrained ego-video representations are therefore promising for augmenting wearable-sensor-based clinical motion analysis, potentially leading to more accurate and context-aware understanding of pathological movements such as freezing of gait in real-world settings.

Abstract: Understanding motion in daily living requires context beyond kinematics, because similar inertial patterns during activities of daily living (ADLs) can reflect intentional stopping, object interaction, or pathological movement impairment. Egocentric vision provides task-related context that may help disambiguate these cases. We investigate this challenge through freezing of gait (FOG) detection in Parkinson's disease (PD), a symptom strongly influenced by contextual factors during ADLs. Using synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels collected from 13 PD participants in their homes, we evaluate frozen representations from pretrained ego-video and time-series foundation models, alongside an IMU-based TCN trained from scratch, under leave-one-subject-out evaluation. The IMU-based TCN achieved the strongest event-detection performance, reaching 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC, compared with 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC for V-JEPA2 ego-video features. Although ego-video alone did not outperform IMU-based sensing, it showed above-chance discrimination, and qualitative analyses suggest that egocentric vision may capture FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs. Together, these results support the use of pretrained ego-video representations to add contextual information to wearable-sensor-based clinical motion understanding in daily living.

</details>


### [141] [NAS-Driven Hardware Accelerator Exploration for Edge AI and Quantization Effects on the Pareto Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13293)
*Eleftherios Mylonas,Angelos Kouprizas,Michael Birbas,Alexios Birbas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They study how applying 4‑bit post‑training quantization affects NAS-discovered architectures and present a three-stage pipeline that links NAS, quantization, and CGRA-based hardware exploration, finding that an FP32 surrogate predicts the quantized Pareto front better than a dedicated INT4 surrogate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge AI needs models that are accurate, efficient, and easily deployable on hardware. Hardware-aware NAS addresses this but becomes complex, especially when quantization is integrated in-loop. Post-training quantization is a simpler alternative, but its impact on NAS-derived Pareto fronts is poorly understood, and there is no integrated framework that connects quantized architectures to reconfigurable accelerators with automated design-space exploration.

Method: They propose a three-stage pipeline: (1) a hardware-agnostic Pareto-ranking surrogate frontend trained on NAS-Bench-201 to estimate accuracy vs. cost trade-offs; (2) a quantization "bridge" that applies INT4 post-training quantization, performs Pareto-aware filtering, and feeds back information on quantization effects; and (3) an evolutionary Domain Space Exploration (DSE) backend targeting the CGRA4ML reconfigurable accelerator to find optimal hardware mappings. They also carry out an exhaustive empirical analysis of all 15,625 NAS-Bench-201 architectures, computing formal stability metrics to quantify how INT4 PTQ perturbs the Pareto space and comparing an FP32 zero-shot surrogate to an INT4-trained surrogate across two search strategies.

Result: The INT4 post-training quantization causes measurable but characterisable perturbations of the Pareto front defined on NAS-Bench-201, and these are quantified via stability metrics over the full 15,625-architecture space. Surprisingly, the FP32 zero-shot surrogate model provides better coverage of the quantized (INT4) Pareto space than a surrogate trained specifically on INT4 data, across two standard NAS search strategies. The full three-stage pipeline successfully links NAS, quantization, and CGRA-based hardware mapping within an automated flow.

Conclusion: Post-training quantization can be systematically integrated with NAS and hardware mapping without embedding quantization inside the NAS loop, preserving a simpler design flow while still enabling hardware-aware optimization. The behavior of INT4 PTQ on NAS-Bench-201 Pareto fronts is now empirically characterized, and an FP32-based zero-shot surrogate is shown to be more effective than an INT4-trained surrogate for covering the quantized Pareto space, simplifying surrogate modeling for quantized deployment on edge accelerators like CGRA4ML.

Abstract: Edge AI deployment demands neural architectures that are simultaneously accurate, computationally efficient, and hardware-deployable - a challenge addressed by hardware-aware Neural Architecture Search (NAS). While recent works incorporate quantization directly into the NAS loop, these approaches expand search complexity and tightly couple architecture and quantization design. The simpler post-search quantization strategy has received little analytical attention: the effects of Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) on the NAS-discovered Pareto structure remain uncharacterised, and no framework combines quantized architecture mapping onto reconfigurable accelerators with automated hardware exploration. This paper addresses both gaps. First, a three-stage pipeline is proposed: a hardware-agnostic Pareto rank surrogate frontend on NAS-Bench-201, a quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control, and an evolutionary Domain Space Exploration (DSE) backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Second, an empirical study characterises how INT4 PTQ perturbs the NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space through formal stability metrics on ground-truth data for all 15,625 architectures, and demonstrates that an FP32 zero-shot surrogate outperforms a dedicated INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto space coverage across two standard search strategies.

</details>


### [142] [StateBridge: Training-free Hidden-state Alignment for Latent Communication in LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13317)
*Yanwen Peng,Delvin Ce Zhang,Xi Wang,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes StateBridge, a training-free method for latent (non-text) communication between large language model agents, using an orthogonal transformation to align hidden states between sender and receiver and achieving strong performance across several reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-agent systems built from large language models, agents usually communicate via text tokens. This discretization step throws away information contained in the sender’s continuous hidden states and creates a bottleneck. Existing latent communication methods either modify models layer-by-layer or rely on trained projection modules, which hurts portability and simplicity. A method is needed that preserves rich continuous information while remaining training-free, simple, and compatible with off-the-shelf models.

Method: The authors introduce StateBridge, a latent communication mechanism where a sender’s final-layer hidden states are mapped directly into the receiver’s input embedding space. They compute a closed-form orthogonal transformation to align the two spaces, avoiding any gradient-based training. To make the transformed states match the distribution expected by the receiver’s input layer, they apply lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring. The resulting aligned hidden states are then prepended as a continuous prefix to the receiver’s standard tokenized input, enabling the receiver to consume this extra context seamlessly.

Result: Across math reasoning, code generation, and question answering tasks, using four LLMs from two architecture families, StateBridge outperforms or ties the best competing communication methods on 22 of 26 model–task combinations. It consistently beats the strongest baseline approaches to latent or text-based communication, indicating that the training-free orthogonal alignment and simple calibration techniques are effective in practice.

Conclusion: StateBridge demonstrates that training-free, orthogonal alignment of hidden states can enable effective latent communication between LLM agents without modifying their internal layers or learning additional projectors. By prepending calibrated continuous prefixes to the receiver’s inputs, agents can share richer information than is possible through discrete tokens alone, leading to better performance on a range of reasoning and generation tasks and offering a portable solution for multi-agent LLM systems.

Abstract: Large language model based multi-agent systems usually communicate in text, i.e., using discrete tokens. However, text introduces a discrete bottleneck. Converting the sender's continuous hidden states into discrete tokens discards information that token identities alone cannot capture. Recent work proposes latent communication as an alternative, where agents transmit hidden representations directly without converting them to text. However, existing latent methods either inject working memory layer by layer across the transformers, or require trained projectors that limit portability. We propose StateBridge, a training-free latent communication approach that aligns the sender's final-layer hidden states to the receiver's input space via a closed-form orthogonal transformation. Lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring ensure compatibility with the pretrained input distribution. The aligned states are prepended to the input of the receiver agent as a continuous prefix. We evaluate StateBridge on math reasoning, code generation, and question answering with four models from two families. StateBridge achieves the best or tied-best score on 22 out of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [143] [LLM-Guided Graph Generation for Structure-Based Local Improvement Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13333)
*Hai Xia,Vaidyanathan Peruvemba Ramaswamy,Stefan Szeider*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a problem-agnostic pipeline that uses an LLM to generate graph structures from MiniZinc models, then use these graphs to guide structure-based local search and configuration selection, beating Gurobi and fixed configurations on many problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large neighborhood search (LNS) typically chooses variables randomly, but good performance often relies on hand-crafted, problem-specific variable selection strategies that exploit structural properties. This is labor-intensive and not portable across problem domains. The authors want an automatic, problem-agnostic way to extract useful structure from diverse MiniZinc problems so that local search and algorithm selection can be improved without manual expert design.

Method: 1) Define semantic prompting guidelines for an LLM so it can read a MiniZinc problem and produce a graph generator: a mapping from any instance of that problem type to a uniform weighted graph whose nodes are decision variables and edges encode constraint relationships. 2) Use this graph as input to a structure-based local improvement framework (SLIM), which uses graph structure to guide variable selection in large neighborhood search. 3) Because all instances map to a common weighted graph representation, they can extract consistent graph features across instances. 4) Use these features for configuration/algorithm selection over a portfolio of solvers/configurations. 5) Evaluate the overall pipeline on instances from 20 MiniZinc competition problems against a strong baseline (Gurobi) and against single best configurations, and run ablation studies on configuration space and feature sets.

Result: Across 20 MiniZinc competition problems, their algorithm-selection framework guided by LLM-generated graphs achieves a 39.5% average problem-weighted win rate against a one-shot Gurobi baseline, more than doubling the win rate of the best single configuration (19.3%). Further ablation and tuning of configurations and features increases the win rate to 44.0%. These results indicate that the automatically generated structural representations significantly improve variable selection and algorithm selection in constraint optimization.

Conclusion: Using LLMs with carefully designed semantic prompts can automatically generate problem-agnostic structural graph representations from MiniZinc models. These representations support both structure-aware local improvement (SLIM) and effective algorithm/configuration selection across heterogeneous constraint optimization problems. The approach outperforms strong baselines and manually chosen single configurations, showing that automated structure extraction and feature construction is a viable path to scalable, domain-independent performance gains in constraint optimization.

Abstract: Large neighborhood search normally selects a random subset of decision variables for iterative optimization. For efficiently solving different problems, researchers tend to design variable selection strategies by taking into account structural features from different domains. In this paper, we build an automatic pipeline that is problem-agnostic to all problems in the MiniZinc format. By prompting an LLM with our semantic guidelines, we guide the LLM to produce a graph generator that maps any instance of a problem type to a uniform weighted graph, where nodes represent decision variables and edges represent constraint relationships. These problem-agnostic graphs guide our structure-based local improvement framework (SLIM) in variable selection. Meanwhile, the weighted graph enables all problem instances to share the same generic graph representation, from which the same graph features can be extracted and used for configuration selection. We evaluated our pipeline on instances across 20 MiniZinc competition problems, finding that algorithm selection achieves a 39.5% average problem-weighted win rate against a one-shot Gurobi baseline, more than doubling the best single configuration (19.3%). Configuration and feature ablation boost the performance further to 44.0%, demonstrating that LLM-based semantic generation enables effective automated structure extraction and feature extraction for constraint optimization.

</details>


### [144] [LongEarth-R1: Benchmarking and Aligning Vision-Language Models for Long-Horizon Earth Observation Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13344)
*Yupan Ding,Jing Xiao,Zhenyuan Zhang,Chaofeng Chen,Liang Liao,Gui-Song Xia,Mi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new benchmark and model suite, LongEarth and LongEarth-R1, are proposed for long-horizon Earth observation QA over multi-frame satellite image sequences, improving reasoning about spatiotemporal changes and future prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing remote sensing vision-language models mainly operate on single images, pairs, or short sequences, which makes them poor at handling long-horizon reasoning: tracking geographic evolution, localizing spatial changes across many frames, detecting long-term anomalies, and inferring future states. There is a lack of standardized benchmarks and models tailored to this kind of extended temporal reasoning over Earth observation data.

Method: The authors create LongEarth-Bench, a large QA benchmark built from Earth observation image sequences averaging about 15 frames and up to 30 frames, with 12 task types spanning evolution summarization, spatial reasoning, anomaly identification, and logical prediction. A 30k-sample subset includes explicit structured reasoning traces that connect key frames and changed regions to final answers. They then train LongEarth via supervised fine-tuning using explicit sequence identifiers and the provided structured chain-of-thought traces. On top of LongEarth, they develop LongEarth-R1, which applies group relative policy optimization with specially designed rewards that enforce correct output format and encourage better temporal and spatial reasoning.

Result: LongEarth-Bench offers around 120k QA samples from 117k unique images with diverse long-sequence tasks and reasoning annotations. Models trained as LongEarth and further optimized into LongEarth-R1 demonstrate strong performance, with LongEarth-R1 achieving state-of-the-art results on all 12 long-sequence tasks while also remaining competitive on conventional remote sensing benchmarks that do not require such long-horizon reasoning.

Conclusion: The work shows that dedicated benchmarks and training methods for long-sequence, spatiotemporal reasoning in Earth observation significantly enhance vision-language model capabilities. By leveraging structured reasoning traces and RL-style optimization with temporal and spatial rewards, LongEarth-R1 improves grounding in relevant frames and regions and sets a new performance standard for long-horizon remote sensing QA tasks while not sacrificing performance on standard tasks.

Abstract: Long-horizon Earth observation reasoning requires models to organize multi-stage geographic evolution, localize spatial changes, detect temporal anomalies, and infer future from extended image sequences. However, existing remote sensing vision-language models mainly focus on isolated images, image pairs, or short sequences, limiting reliable grounding in the relevant frames and regions. We introduce LongEarth-Bench, a benchmark containing approximately 120k question-answering samples derived from 117k unique images. Its sequences average 15.14 frames and extend to 30 frames, covering 12 tasks across evolution summarization, spatial reasoning, anomaly identification, and logical prediction. A 30k-sample subset further provides structured reasoning traces linking key frames and changed regions to final answers. We develop LongEarth through supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and structured chain-of-thought supervision. Building on LongEarth, LongEarth-R1 applies group relative policy optimization with format, temporal, and spatial rewards. LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results on all 12 long-sequence tasks while remaining competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks.

</details>


### [145] [Rules or Character? Scaling Laws for AI Safety Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13345)
*Satoshi Takahashi,Nobuji Kouno,Masaaki Komatsu,Ryuji Hamamoto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds a simple quantitative model to study how to optimally split safety effort between “character shaping” during training and “rule enforcement” at inference as AI systems are deployed at larger scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI safety practice uses both behavioral training (RLHF, Constitutional AI) and inference-time filters, but there is little formal theory for how to balance these two levers, especially as systems are deployed more widely and encounter distribution shifts. The authors want to understand, in a structured way, when to invest more in character shaping versus rule-based filters, and how deployment scale and various failure modes affect this choice.

Method: They construct a stylized comparative-statics model in which a single design variable alpha in [0,1] represents how much safety resource is allocated to character shaping versus rule enforcement. The model includes: (i) scale-dependent degradation of filters as deployment grows, (ii) common-mode failures that break multiple safeguards simultaneously, and (iii) “character fragility,” i.e., the probability that trained safe behavior fails under novel conditions. Assuming a multiplicative Pareto model of damage severity, they derive closed-form expressions for expected harm. They then perform Monte Carlo simulations to evaluate tail risk via Conditional Value at Risk (CVaR) under different parameter scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic) and deployment scales T.

Result: In all considered scenarios, the optimal allocation alpha* (minimizing harm) is either inside the interior of [0,1] or at the rule-enforcement-only boundary; it never moves to a character-only corner. As deployment scale T increases, the optimal alpha* shifts only weakly toward more character shaping, with changes ranging from very small (+0.01) to more noticeable (+0.21), depending on scenario. The baseline character fragility rate p^(0)_frag is the key driver: varying it across its plausible range can change the optimal alpha* by about 0.50, which is much larger than the effect of other parameters like tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure probability. At large deployment scales, the alpha* that minimizes expected harm and the one that minimizes CVaR tend to coincide.

Conclusion: The balance between training-based character shaping and inference-time rule enforcement in AI safety architectures is driven more by how robust shaped behavior is under distributional shift than by deployment scale itself. If character shaping is fragile, the model favors heavier reliance on rules/filters; if it is robust, more weight can optimally be placed on character shaping. Scale amplifies risks but does not fundamentally change which lever is more valuable; the decisive factor is the reliability of character shaping in novel conditions.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) safety systems combine character shaping (e.g., Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback [RLHF], Constitutional AI), which modifies behavioral distributions at training time, with rule enforcement (e.g., output filters, safety classifiers), which blocks harmful outputs at inference time, yet little formal analysis exists on how their optimal balance should change as deployment scales increase. We introduce a stylized comparative-statics model that parameterizes safety design as a resource allocation alpha in [0,1] between these two approaches, incorporating scale-dependent filter degradation, common-mode failures, and character fragility -- the risk that shaped behavior degrades or collapses under novel conditions. Under a multiplicative Pareto damage model, we derive closed-form expected harm and supplement it with tail-risk (CVaR) analysis via Monte Carlo simulation. Across three scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic), the optimal alpha* is interior or at the rules-only boundary and shifts weakly toward character shaping as deployment scale T grows, from negligible (Delta alpha* = +0.01) to pronounced (Delta alpha* = +0.21) depending on scenario. The dominant parameter is the baseline character fragility rate p^(0)_frag, which shifts alpha* by 0.50 across its range -- far exceeding the effect of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure probability. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large T. These results suggest that safety architecture decisions depend less on deployment scale per se than on the reliability of character shaping under distributional shift.

</details>


### [146] [TopoIntent: Compiling Security Intent into Executable, Compliance-Checked Network Topologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13389)
*Xiaokang Qu,Jianliang Ma,Zao Fan,Tianshu Chu,Tianlong Fan,Linyuan Lü*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces TopoIntent, a system that automatically compiles high-level, natural-language security requirements into executable, compliance-checked enterprise network topologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing enterprise security topologies from business and regulatory intent is complex and largely manual. Existing NetOps automation tools assume a fixed topology and focus on post-design configuration, providing little support for translating underspecified natural-language requirements into structured, compliant security architectures. There is also no public benchmark for evaluating systems that perform this requirement-to-topology translation.

Method: TopoIntent uses a schema contract to constrain topology generation. It retrieves reference architectures from a curated template library using dense-vector (semantic) search, then aligns user intent with retrieved templates through a staged fusion process and performs security completion. The system checks generated topologies against CIS Controls v8.1.2 that are visible at the topology layer, flags unresolved or ambiguous cases for manual review, and repairs structural gaps via additive, schema-preserving edits. Finally, it exports the resulting topology into Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs for executable reachability and policy testing. For evaluation, the authors construct a dataset derived from reference security architecture diagrams, splitting it into a retrieval set and a held-out set across multiple scenarios, including finance and government.

Result: On held-out finance and government scenarios (not present in the retrieval set), the additive repair process raises topology-visible CIS control satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in under 1.5 repair rounds on average. With one feedback round, the pass rate of post-ACL security policy tests improves from 0.78 to 0.88. The reference template library consists of 22 templates and 44 synthetic intents for five scenarios in the retrieval set, and 7 templates plus 14 intents in the held-out set.

Conclusion: TopoIntent effectively bridges the gap between high-level, natural-language security intent and concrete, executable enterprise network topologies. By combining schema-constrained generation, template retrieval, staged fusion, and automated compliance checking and repair, it can achieve full coverage of topology-visible CIS v8.1.2 safeguards on held-out scenarios and significantly improve policy test pass rates with minimal feedback rounds, demonstrating the viability of intent-to-topology compilation for security architecture design.

Abstract: Enterprise security topology design requires translating business intent, regulatory requirements, and risk assumptions into zones, boundary devices, inter-zone paths, and access-control policies. Existing NetOps automation tools mainly operate after this design is fixed, providing limited support for generating structured security topologies from underspecified natural-language requirements. We present TopoIntent, a system that compiles security intent into executable, compliance-checked network topologies. It uses a schema contract to constrain generation, retrieves reference architectures from a curated template library via dense-vector search, and applies staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion. The generated topology is checked against CIS Controls v8.1.2 safeguards visible at the topology layer, while unresolved cases are marked for manual review. Structural gaps are repaired through additive schema-preserving edits. The final topology is exported to Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs, enabling executable reachability and allow/deny tests. Because no public benchmark exists for this requirement-to-topology task, we construct an evaluation set from reference security architecture diagrams. The retrieval set contains 22 templates and 44 synthetic intents across five scenarios, while the held-out set contains 7 templates and 14 intents from finance and government scenarios excluded from retrieval. On the held-out set, additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in fewer than 1.5 rounds on average, and one feedback round raises the post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88.

</details>


### [147] [Jointly Predicting Courses and Grades Using a Transformer-Based Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13409)
*Paul Savala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a transformer-based model that jointly predicts which courses a student will take next semester and the grades they will receive, modeling courses at the semester level rather than as a simple sequence, and significantly improves grade prediction accuracy over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most learning analytics models treat a student’s academic history as a linear sequence of courses, ignoring that multiple courses are taken concurrently within each semester. This loses important information about course load, difficulty interactions, and co-enrollment patterns, which can degrade prediction accuracy—especially for students taking many or challenging courses. The authors aim to build a model that better reflects how coursework is actually organized, and thus improves early identification of students who may be at risk academically.

Method: The authors design TRACE, a transformer-based model tailored to student records. Instead of modeling individual courses as a flat sequence over time, they encode courses on a per-semester basis to explicitly capture concurrency effects. TRACE performs joint prediction: (1) it predicts the set of courses a student is likely to enroll in for the next semester, and (2) it predicts the grade for each of those courses. They introduce a new loss function that combines course-set prediction loss with grade prediction loss, encouraging the model to learn both enrollment patterns and performance simultaneously. The architecture is compared against an identical transformer that predicts grades only, as well as LSTM-based sequential models and graph neural network approaches. Student attributes can be fed into the model as additional inputs.

Result: Using ten years of institutional data, TRACE achieves nearly a 50% reduction in mean absolute error compared with an otherwise identical architecture that predicts only grades (without joint course-set prediction). It also outperforms traditional LSTM-based sequence models and graph neural network-based methods on the same prediction tasks. The experiments show that including course-set prediction during training materially boosts grade prediction quality, validating the proposed joint modeling and semester-level encoding of concurrency.

Conclusion: The study concludes that modern transformer architectures, when adapted to represent semesters and course concurrency and trained to jointly predict course enrollment and grades, can meaningfully improve academic performance prediction. Predicting which courses students will take is not just a side task but a key technique that enhances grade prediction accuracy. TRACE yields interpretable outputs, can integrate student attribute data, and is portable across institutions via retraining and recalibration. The authors note its potential role as a core component in early detection and intervention systems in higher education, helping identify students who may need support in upcoming semesters.

Abstract: Existing predictive models in learning analytics often treat student academic history as a simple sequence, overlooking the concurrent nature of courses taken within a semester. This simplification can lead to inaccurate performance predictions, particularly for students with heavy or challenging course loads. This paper introduces a TRansformer for Academic Course-grade Estimation (TRACE) that addresses this limitation by jointly predicting both the set of courses a student will take and their corresponding grades for an upcoming semester. Our approach encodes courses on a per-semester basis to capture the effects of course concurrency and utilizes a novel loss function combining course-set prediction with grade prediction. We demonstrate that predicting courses taken in addition to the grades in those courses leads to significant improvements in prediction quality. Trained on ten years of institutional data, our joint prediction model reduces mean absolute error by nearly 50% compared to an identical architecture that predicts grades alone. The model also outperforms traditional LSTM-based sequential models, as well as graph neural network-based approaches, and offers natural ways to incorporate student attribute data. This work demonstrates the utility of modern neural architectures for creating interpretable models that can be adapted to new institutions via retraining and recalibration, as well as the importance of key techniques, such as predicting courses taken during training. We discuss how this model could be incorporated into early detection systems at institutions of higher education.

</details>


### [148] [MARC v1: An Open-Source Multi-Agent Framework for Clinical AI Reasoning and Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13476)
*Saisha Shetty,Satvik Tripathi,Austin Lin,Colin Zhao,Theodore Kim,Don Enwerem,Jacinta Arnold,Shahriar Faghani,Tessa S Cook*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MARC is an open-source, deterministic multi-agent framework for clinical reasoning with LLMs, replacing single big prompts with coordinated specialized agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monolithic prompts for clinical reasoning with LLMs are hard to control, debug, and adapt. Clinicians and domain experts without programming skills need an interpretable, configurable, and model-agnostic way to structure complex reasoning workflows without manual prompt engineering.

Method: They design MARC, a multi-agent orchestration framework, where different role-specialized agents handle extraction, reasoning, answer generation, and evaluation. Agents pass context explicitly, and all intermediate steps are logged for transparency and error tracing. They also add a Decomposer module that, given a plain-language task description, automatically generates task-specific prompts for each agent, removing the need for manual prompt design. The system is configured through YAML files and works with both API-based and local CPU-compatible models, without changing code.

Result: MARC provides a working, open-source system that operationalizes clinical reasoning as a deterministic multi-agent pipeline, supports multiple deployment modes, and lets users configure and inspect all stages. It enables stage-wise failure attribution and removes the need for low-level programming or manual prompt engineering by clinical experts.

Conclusion: A deterministic, YAML-configurable multi-agent framework like MARC can make LLM-based clinical reasoning more interpretable, debuggable, and accessible to non-programmers, while remaining model-agnostic and easy to deploy in different environments.

Abstract: We present Multi-Agent Reasoning and Coordination (MARC), an open-source framework that replaces monolithic LLM prompting with deterministic multi-agent orchestration for clinical reasoning. MARC coordinates role-specialized agents for extraction, reasoning, answer generation, and evaluation, with explicit context passing and traceable intermediate outputs, enabling stage-wise failure attribution. We additionally introduce a Decomposer module that generates task-specific agent prompts from a plain-language description, eliminating manual prompt engineering. The framework supports both API-based and local CPU-compatible deployments and is entirely configurable via YAML, without code modifications. MARC is designed to be model-agnostic, interpretable, and accessible to clinical domain experts without programming expertise. The full framework is available at https://github.com/Penn-RAIL/MARC-v1.

</details>


### [149] [Who Speaks Matters: Authority-Aware Multi-View RAG over Italian Parliamentary Proceedings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13410)
*Mirko Tritella,Riccardo Pozzi,Matteo Palmonari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ParliamentRAG is a specialized RAG system for Italian parliamentary transcripts that models topic-dependent speaker authority to generate more balanced, faithful, and expert-grounded multi-perspective summaries than a strong baseline (Google NotebookLM).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parliamentary proceedings are vast, fragmented, and hard to explore from multiple perspectives, which hinders access for citizens, journalists, and researchers. Naively applying generic RAG to this domain risks over-amplifying frequent speakers, ignoring topical expertise, and misattributing quotations in politically sensitive contexts. There is a need for a system that can surface diverse, topic-relevant expert voices while maintaining citation faithfulness.

Method: The authors build ParliamentRAG, a RAG pipeline over transcripts of the Italian Chamber of Deputies. They design a topic-dependent authority model that, given a user query, estimates each MP’s authority on that topic using interpretable features such as profession, education, and prior interventions. The system retrieves relevant speech chunks, scores speakers by topic authority across parliamentary groups, selects topic-relevant experts, and then uses a generative model to synthesize a summary that highlights and quotes these experts. They evaluate the system against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics using both automatic metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts, focusing on coverage, quotation faithfulness, and qualitative preferences.

Result: ParliamentRAG outperforms Google NotebookLM on several source- and representation-focused metrics: it achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs 0.95) and perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs 0.95). Domain experts prefer ParliamentRAG on source-related aspects, such as how well it represents different groups and maintains accurate citations. However, Google NotebookLM still produces superior text on prose-related dimensions like fluency and style.

Conclusion: A domain-specific RAG system that explicitly models topic-dependent speaker authority and uses interpretable features can mitigate key risks in applying RAG to parliamentary data—namely dominance of frequent speakers, lack of expertise weighting, and misattribution. ParliamentRAG provides more balanced, expert-grounded, and faithful multi-perspective summaries of parliamentary debates than a strong generic baseline, though there is still room to improve generative prose quality.

Abstract: Parliamentary proceedings are a primary record of democratic deliberation, yet their volume and fragmentation make multi-perspective access difficult for citizens, journalists, and researchers. Applying Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to parliamentary transcripts introduces three specific risks: dominance of the most frequent speakers, inability to weight speakers according to topical expertise, and citation misattribution in politically sensitive text. We present ParliamentRAG, a RAG system for the Italian Chamber of Deputies that addresses these risks jointly. Its core contribution is a topic-dependent authority model that estimates each speaker's authority as a function of the current query, combining interpretable components such as profession, education, and previous interventions. Given a user query, the system retrieves relevant speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates a summary synthesizing their perspectives, accompanied by supporting quotations. ParliamentRAG is evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics via a two-level protocol combining automated metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts. The system achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions, while NotebookLM remains stronger on prose-oriented dimensions.

</details>


### [150] [OmniScientist: An Omni-Modal Omni-Discipline AI Scientist](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13558)
*Bobo Li,Hao Fei,Tianjie Ju,Mong-Li Lee,Wynne Hsu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniScientist is an omni-modal AI scientist that goes from raw, heterogeneous data all the way to a finished manuscript, showing that direct perception over full lifecycle evidence substantially improves scientific reasoning and outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current AI scientist systems only operate on text, code, or precomputed summaries and scalar features, which strips away critical scientific evidence encoded in spatial, temporal, cross-channel, and procedural relations. This gap prevents agents from accessing the full richness of experimental data on which real scientific discovery depends. The authors are motivated to build an AI system that can reason directly over raw, heterogeneous evidence across many modalities and disciplines, and to demonstrate that such lifecycle-wide perception materially improves the rigor and quality of automated science.

Method: The authors propose OmniScientist, an end-to-end AI scientist built around an omni-modal perception layer plus three autonomous agents specialized for ideation, experimentation, and writeup. These agents interact via a deterministic pipeline so that raw observations influence hypotheses, experimental design and execution, and final claims. The system encodes checks for novelty, statistical rigor, execution provenance, and numerical traceability as executable code-based tests (idea, rigor, and claim checks). It ingests heterogeneous raw data – including images, time-series signals, audio, video, 3D structures, trajectories, tables, formulae, and graphs – and uses a reference reasoning backbone to generate, test, and report scientific findings. OmniScientist is evaluated on 36 real-data research cases across 5 discipline families and 4 evidence families, comparing the full omni-modal system to a blind variant that only sees precomputed scalar features.

Result: On 36 real-world cases, OmniScientist successfully completes the entire scientific workflow from raw data to a compiled manuscript in every instance. Using the reference reasoning backbone, the produced papers achieve a mean overall score of 6.3 (on the evaluation scale used by the authors). In head-to-head comparisons against a blind baseline model that only has access to precomputed scalar features, the omni-modal version with direct perception improves performance on all 7 evaluation dimensions and is preferred in 85% of pairwise judgments.

Conclusion: Lifecycle-wide, omni-modal perception over raw scientific evidence is crucial for robust, evidence-grounded automated scientific discovery. By tightly integrating perception with ideation, experimentation, and writeup, OmniScientist can autonomously produce full research manuscripts of competitive quality across diverse disciplines and data types. The findings suggest that future AI scientists should be designed as end-to-end, omni-modal systems with built-in checks for novelty, rigor, provenance, and traceability, and that giving such systems direct access to raw data substantially increases the quality and reliability of their scientific output.

Abstract: Recent advances in foundation models have enabled AI scientists to automate increasingly complete research workflows, from hypothesis generation and code execution to manuscript preparation. Yet workflow coverage alone does not provide access to the full evidence on which scientific discovery depends. Existing systems typically reason over text, code, labels, or precomputed summaries, leaving scientifically decisive spatial, temporal, cross-channel, and procedural relations unavailable to the agent. We introduce OmniScientist, an end-to-end, omni-modal AI scientist that conducts multidisciplinary research directly from heterogeneous raw evidence. A perception layer and 3 autonomous agents for ideation, experiment, and writeup operate within a deterministic pipeline, allowing observations to shape research questions, experimental decisions, and final claims throughout the research lifecycle. By running idea, rigour, and claim checks in code, the system enforces novelty screening, statistical validity, execution provenance, and numerical traceability. We evaluate OmniScientist on 36 real-data cases spanning 5 discipline families, 4 families of scientific evidence, and modalities including images, signals, audio, video, 3-D structures, trajectories, tables, formulae, and graphs. The system completes the full path from raw data to a compiled manuscript in all 36 cases and achieves a mean overall paper score of 6.3 with the reference reasoning backbone. In paired comparisons against a blind variant that receives only precomputed scalar features, direct perception improves all 7 evaluation dimensions and wins 85% of head-to-head judgments. These results show that lifecycle-wide perception is essential for evidence-grounded scientific discovery and provides a practical path toward broadly capable AI scientists.

</details>


### [151] [Beyond Final Scores: A Systematic Evaluation of Agents for Long-Horizon AI Research and Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13417)
*Yiwei Li,Wanli Yang,Hexiang Tan,Xiangzhou Huang,Zhengyu Chen,Ziran Li,Borun Chen,Shanglin Lei,Huaisheng Zhu,Hao Tian,Fei Sun,Xunliang Cai,Jingang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well current autonomous AI agents conduct long-horizon experimentation, going beyond final task scores to analyze within-run behavior and experience reuse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Final performance scores on long-horizon tasks do not reveal how autonomous agents actually behave during problem solving, where their progress comes from, or whether they effectively reuse experience over time. As agents are increasingly used to autonomously improve models and systems, there is a need for a more systematic, process-level evaluation framework.

Method: The authors design a new evaluation framework with rule-based metrics that decompose agent behavior within each run into three phases: Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control. Using this framework, they systematically evaluate seven frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks, and perform controlled comparisons to study how agents reuse experience within the same task and across different tasks.

Result: They find that current agents resemble engineering optimizers more than fully autonomous researchers. Agents can frame and implement workable solutions, but their performance is highly variable across runs. Their strongest solutions typically adapt or recombine existing techniques rather than generating genuinely novel methods. The analysis also uncovers multiple process bottlenecks that can lead to similar final scores, mixed effects of experience reuse that can either improve or mislead later decisions, and a strong influence of harness (experimental setup) design on performance stability.

Conclusion: The study concludes that while current autonomous agents are competent at practical optimization, they are limited in robustness and methodological creativity. Process-level metrics expose important gaps not visible from final scores alone. The authors argue that improving agent capabilities will require advances in model training, better inference-time strategies, more reliable experience management mechanisms, and carefully designed evaluation harnesses that support stable, informative measurements of long-horizon behavior.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly capable of improving models, systems, and other technical artifacts through long-horizon experimentation. To understand the current state of this capability, however, evaluation must go beyond final scores, which neither reveal where progress is gained or lost nor indicate whether accumulated experience improves later decisions. We therefore present a systematic evaluation of seven frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks based on a new framework that uses rule-based metrics to characterize within-run behavior through Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control and controlled comparisons to assess experience reuse within and across tasks. The results show that current agents operate more like engineering optimizers than fully autonomous researchers: they can formulate and implement practical solutions, but their performance varies substantially across runs, their strongest solutions mainly adapt or combine established techniques, and genuine methodological novelty remains rare. Detailed analysis reveals that observed performance is shaped by multiple factors, including distinct process bottlenecks behind similar final outcomes, experience reuse that can help or mislead subsequent decisions, and harness designs that affect performance stability. These findings suggest concrete directions for improving model training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design.

</details>


### [152] [Enhancing Virtual Agents through SLMs and Edge-Computing: An Exploratory Evaluation of Think and Memory Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13420)
*Aimilios Hadjiliasi,Louis Nisiotis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents and evaluates an edge-deployed, SLM-driven prototype of a Cognitive Embodied Agent Architecture (CEAA) “brain” for virtual/Metaverse agents, focusing on thinking and memory processes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied virtual agents in complex virtual and Metaverse environments must be persistent, adaptive, and context-aware, but building cognitively capable agents is both conceptually and technologically difficult. Existing approaches lack a practical, implementation-oriented framework that can run efficiently at the edge, close to where agents operate, especially given resource constraints and the need for low-latency cognition and memory in interactive worlds.

Method: The authors adopt the Cognitive Embodied Agent Architecture (CEAA) as the conceptual framework and target its “Think” and “Memory” components. They design and implement an edge-based virtual agent gateway system that uses Small Language Models (SLMs) to realize parts of these cognitive processes. The system is deployed on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX device and instantiated using Qwen2.5 models of various sizes. Through simulation experiments, they test how well the system processes service requests and supports memory-driven conversations, evaluating routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency.

Result: The experiments show that the SLM-based gateway can successfully route service requests, retrieve and use memory to conduct conversations, and operate within acceptable latency bounds on an edge device. Different Qwen2.5 model sizes exhibit trade-offs between accuracy and latency, but overall the prototype demonstrates that meaningful portions of CEAA’s cognitive processes can be realized with SLMs at the edge.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates the feasibility of using SLMs, deployed at the edge, to partially implement CEAA’s “Think” and “Memory” processes for embodied virtual agents. This provides a practical path toward scalable, context-aware, and efficient cognitive “brains” for agents in immersive virtual worlds, though it remains a partial implementation and further work is needed to cover the full CEAA and improve performance and capabilities.

Abstract: Embodied intelligent virtual agents are expected to operate as persistent, adaptive, and context-aware entities within complex virtual and Metaverse worlds. However, implementing cognitively capable agents in such environments is conceptually and technologically challenging. Among a range of blueprints and development approaches, the Cognitive Embodied Agent Architecture (CEAA) has been developed as an implementation-oriented framework for architecting components of perception, memory, reasoning, planning, and embodied action. Considering the recent advances in edge computing and generative AI language models, this paper explores the use of Small Language Models (SLMs) to support edge-based operation of selected CEAA components, focusing on "Think" and "Memory" as processes central to cognitive orchestration and persistence of virtual agents in interactive virtual worlds. An edge-based virtual agent gateway system was developed and evaluated on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX using Qwen2.5 models of different sizes, exploring the system's capability to process service requests and handle memory-driven conversations. A series of simulation experiments evaluated routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency, demonstrating an SLM-driven prototype agent system that partially implements selected CEAA processes to support the development of embodied agents whose cognitive "brain" can operate efficiently and contextually for interactive experiences in immersive virtual worlds.

</details>


### [153] [RAIL: An Automatic Classifier of the Artificial Intelligence Readiness Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13428)
*Juan Irving Vasquez,Juan Terven,Laura-Ivoone Garay-Jimenez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified, AI-specific readiness scale and an LLM-based assessment procedure to automatically and consistently rate the maturity of AI projects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI readiness and maturity frameworks (Technology Readiness Levels, MLTRL, and AI/data readiness dimensions) are heterogeneous, hard to compare, and difficult to apply automatically. Some lack AI-specific criteria, others require internal artifacts that are often unavailable, and their scales do not map cleanly onto each other. This creates problems for investors, project managers, and policymakers who need consistent, automatable measures of AI technology maturity from limited, often text-only descriptions.

Method: The authors first design the Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL), a nine-level ordinal scale that merges three existing approaches. AIRL is based on an environmental evidence ladder and is constrained by caps along several dimensions: specification, data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, and algorithmic maturity. It also uses a generality-anchoring rule and explicit assignment disciplines to make it possible to determine a readiness level from a natural-language description alone. Second, they create RAIL (Readiness Assessment via Independent LLM-experts), an automated classifier that uses a panel of specialized LLM agents: one evaluates evidence while six separate agents evaluate each readiness dimension. Their individual judgments are combined via a deterministic minimum rule, and a chief expert LLM reviews and can confirm or lower (but not raise) the score beyond the dimensional caps.

Result: The proposed AIRL scale and RAIL assessment procedure were applied to several AI research works. The experiments indicate that the multi-agent panel approach produces consistent readiness assessments and mitigates the tendency of single, monolithic LLM classifiers to overestimate maturity levels.

Conclusion: By unifying disparate readiness frameworks into AIRL and operationalizing it through the multi-agent RAIL system, the paper provides a practical, automatable, and conservative way to assess AI technology maturity from textual descriptions alone. This helps standardize evaluations across projects and stakeholders, reduces overestimation risk, and supports more reliable investment, project, and policy decisions involving AI systems.

Abstract: Assessing the maturity of artificial intelligence technologies is essential for investment decisions, project management, and policy monitoring, yet the available readiness frameworks are heterogeneous and difficult to apply automatically: the adaptation of Technology Readiness Levels to AI lacks AI-specific gating criteria, the Machine Learning Technology Readiness Levels presuppose access to internal process artifacts, and AI/data readiness dimension models employ scales that resist direct comparison. This paper makes two contributions. First, we unify these three frameworks into the Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL), a nine-level ordinal scale built on an environmental evidence ladder and complemented by dimensional caps (covering specification, data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, and algorithmic maturity) together with a generality-anchoring rule and explicit assignment disciplines, so that a readiness level becomes decidable from a natural-language description of the work alone. Second, we propose RAIL (Readiness Assessment via Independent LLM-experts), a panel-of-experts classifier that operationalizes the scale: one evidence agent and six independent dimension agents, each a large language model with a narrowly scoped mandate, deliver verdicts that a deterministic minimum rule aggregates and a chief expert reviews under asymmetric authority, confirming or lowering the panel's recommendation but never raising it above the caps. The method was tested in the analysis of several research works showing consistency and avoiding overestimation from monolithic LLM classifiers.

</details>


### [154] [Academic League of Artificial Intelligence - An Integrative Perspective of Teaching, Research, and Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13447)
*Alison R. Panisson,Maria Eduarda W. M. Vianna,Italo Firmino da Silva,Heitor Henrique da Silva,Rafaela Fernandes Savaris,Bernardo Pandolfi Costa,Martin Augusto Gagliotti Vigil,Jim Lau,Agenor Hentz,Andréa Sabedra Bordin,Alexandre Leopoldo Gonçalves,Roberto Rodrigues-Filho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes and illustrates an organizational framework for an Academic League of Artificial Intelligence that integrates teaching, research, and extension via student-centered, project-based activities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Academic leagues are increasingly important in complementing formal curricula and in connecting universities with society, but there is a lack of structured, replicable models showing how these student organizations can systematically integrate teaching, research, and extension, especially in AI and engineering/computing fields.

Method: The authors describe and analyze the organizational framework used by the Academic League of Artificial Intelligence at UFSC, emphasizing democratic governance, collaborative learning, dynamic project organization, and student-centered, project-based practices. They present and discuss representative initiatives—competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, and socially oriented AI applications—as concrete instantiations of the framework.

Result: The described initiatives successfully operated under a unified organizational structure and fostered technical skills, transversal competencies (such as leadership and collaboration), scientific production, community engagement, and knowledge preservation. The experience shows that diverse educational, research, and extension projects can coexist and reinforce each other within the proposed framework.

Conclusion: The organizational framework of the AI academic league at UFSC is flexible and replicable, effectively integrating teaching, research, and extension in engineering and computing education. It offers practical guidance and a reference model for academic leagues and similar student-centered organizations seeking to structure their activities around democratic governance, collaborative learning, and project-based work.

Abstract: Academic leagues have become important mechanisms for promoting extracurricular education and strengthening the integration between universities and society. This paper presents the organizational framework adopted by the Academic League of Artificial Intelligence (LIA) at the Federal University of Santa Catarina (UFSC), designed to integrate teaching, research, and university extension through a student-centered, project-based approach. The framework combines democratic governance, collaborative learning, and dynamic project organization to foster both technical and transversal competencies. The framework is illustrated through representative initiatives, including competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, and AI-powered applications with social impact. These projects demonstrate how diverse educational, scientific, and extension activities can be developed within a common organizational structure while promoting leadership, scientific production, community engagement, and knowledge preservation. The reported experience indicates that the proposed framework provides a flexible and replicable model for integrating the three university pillars into engineering and computing education, offering practical guidance for academic leagues and similar student organizations.

</details>


### [155] [A Unifying Perspective on Causal World Models: From Observations to Representations to Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13456)
*Avinash Kori,Fabrizio Russo*

Main category: cs.AI
