TL;DR: The paper defines and formalizes Causal World Models (CWMs), world models that explicitly encode causal structure—entities, their properties, and interactions—to support prediction, explanation, and decision-making beyond training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World Models are promising for intelligent agents that need to predict and act out-of-distribution, but most current models are mainly generative and lack explicit causal structure. Without causal representations of entities and their interactions, agents struggle with explanation, generalization, intervention, and robust planning. The authors aim to put world models on a solid causal footing, linking them to established causal inference frameworks and clarifying what can and cannot be learned from data.

Method: The authors take a conceptual and theoretical approach rather than proposing a specific algorithm. They: (1) analyze world models at multiple abstraction levels, from raw perceptual data to high-level conceptual structures; (2) argue that useful WMs must represent entities, their properties, and their interactions with each other and the environment; (3) introduce a formal definition of Causal World Models (CWMs) in terms of the downstream tasks they should support; (4) connect this definition to existing work in causal representation learning, object-centric learning, structural causal models, and model-based decision-making; and (5) examine identifiability conditions for recovering components of a world model from data, specifying relevant equivalence classes.

Result: The main results are: (1) a formal task-grounded definition of Causal World Models that goes beyond purely generative latent-variable models; (2) a unifying conceptual framework linking world modeling with several strands of causal and object-centric learning literature; and (3) theoretical clarification of identifiability: under what assumptions the components of a CWM (such as entities and their causal relations) can be recovered from observational and/or interventional data, and up to which equivalence relations. The paper systematizes conditions under which causal structure in world models is learnable.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that world models intended for robust prediction, explanation, and planning must be explicitly causal, representing entities and their interactions rather than only modeling joint distributions. Their proposed definition of Causal World Models and the associated identifiability analysis provide a principled bridge between world modeling and causal inference, grounding future research in representations and structures that better support causal reasoning and informed decision-making.

Abstract: World Models (WM) are increasingly seen as a foundation for intelligent agents that can predict, plan, and act beyond their training distribution. In this paper, we study WMs from a causal perspective across multiple levels of abstraction, ranging from perceptual observations to building a conceptual representation of the structure governing the environment dynamics. We argue that useful WMs must go beyond generative capabilities alone: they should also capture entity properties, entity-to-entity interactions, and entity-to-environment interactions that determine and explain the dynamics of a system. We provide a formal definition of Causal WMs (CWMs) grounded in the tasks they are intended to support, connecting world modelling with existing work in causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, structural causal models, and model-based decision-making. Finally, we relate CWMs to the literature on identifiability, clarifying when the components of a WM can be recovered from data and up to which equivalence. With this, we ground WMs in representations and structures that support causal reasoning and informed decision-making.

</details>


### [156] [AlayaWorld: Interactive Long-Horizon World Modeling - Full Technical Report (v1.1)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13492)
*AlayaWorld Team,Kaipeng Zhang,Chuanhao Li,Yifan Zhan,Yongtao Ge,Yuanyang Yin,Jiaming Tan,Kang He,Liaoyuan Fan,Mingliang Zhai,Ruicong Liu,Xiaojie Xu,Xuangeng Chu,Zhen Li,Zhengyuan Lin,Zhixiang Wang,Zian Meng,Zihui Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Improved AlayaWorld refines how conditioning signals are represented and fed into the video generation model so they better match the latent and temporal structure of the generated content, leading to more coherent, controllable videos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The previous AlayaWorld kept the core architecture and data but suffered from a mismatch between conditioning inputs (e.g., images, camera info, spatial memory) and the internal latent/temporal structure of the generated videos. This mismatch limited coherence over time, motion consistency, and precise control of viewpoint and scene evolution. The motivation is to redesign conditioning so that all external signals live in the same latent space and temporal organization as the model’s outputs, thereby improving controllability, temporal consistency, and utilization of spatial memory.

Method: The method keeps the backbone and training setup but overhauls conditioning. It introduces a streaming 3D point-cache renderer to replace depth-warping spatial memory, providing a more faithful, temporally continuous spatial representation. Visual conditions (such as input images and re-rendered memory) are encoded into the same causal VAE latent space used for video tokens, with matching temporal statistics. Concretely, the method: (1) replaces static image-based conditioning with motion-aware latent conditioning; (2) causally encodes spatial memory as a continuous latent sequence; (3) aligns the temporal memory window in pixel space with the generated frames; (4) uses hard memory dropout that removes memory tokens instead of zeroing them; (5) unifies VAE encode/decode procedures across training and inference; and (6) removes a dedicated camera AdaLN conditioning branch, shifting viewpoint control entirely to the re-rendered spatial condition.

Result: Although the abstract does not report explicit numerical metrics, it claims a substantially revised conditioning design that better matches latent and temporal structures. The implied result is improved temporal coherence, more accurate use of spatial memory, more reliable motion representation, and more predictable viewpoint control compared to the prior AlayaWorld version.

Conclusion: Aligning conditioning signals with the model’s latent and temporal structure is crucial for effective video generation. By moving to a streaming 3D point-cache renderer, encoding all visual conditions in the same causal VAE latent space, restructuring temporal memory, improving memory regularization, and simplifying camera conditioning, the improved AlayaWorld achieves a cleaner and more principled conditioning mechanism that should yield more coherent and controllable generated videos without changing the backbone or data.

Abstract: This report presents an improved version of AlayaWorld. While the backbone architecture, chunk-wise autoregressive generation scheme, and training data remain unchanged from the previous release, we substantially revise how conditioning signals are represented and integrated into the model. The new design is guided by a simple principle: conditioning signals should match the generated content as closely as possible in both latent representation and temporal structure. To this end, we make two major changes. First, we replace the previous depth-warping-based spatial memory with a streaming 3D point-cache renderer. Second, we redesign the conditioning pipeline so that visual conditions are encoded in the same causal-VAE latent space, with temporal statistics consistent with those of the generated video. Concretely, the new version introduces six modifications: (1) replacing static-frame image conditioning with motion-aware latent conditioning; (2) causally encoding re-rendered spatial memory as a continuous sequence; (3) aligning the temporal-memory window in pixel space; (4) adopting hard memory dropout that removes memory tokens rather than zeroing them; (5) unifying the VAE encoding and decoding protocol across training and inference; and (6) removing the camera AdaLN branch, such that viewpoint control is provided entirely through the re-rendered spatial condition.

</details>


### [157] [QuoteBench: How Matched Scores Can Hide Command-Path Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13547)
*Shangao Li,Yao Zhang,Volker Tresp,Yuanyuan Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QuoteBench evaluates how sensitive LLM coding agents are to parsing/transport errors between generation and Bash execution, showing that small boundary changes can massively alter success rates and reorder model rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM coding agents mostly use matched execution scores, which cannot tell whether failures come from the model’s command generation or from how its outputs are serialized, parsed, and executed. In practice, agents often pass through wrappers, templates, and parsers that can subtly break shell commands (e.g., quoting and escaping issues), but current benchmarks don’t isolate or quantify this boundary effect. The paper aims to create a benchmark and methodology to measure how much performance loss or recovery is due specifically to the interface/transport layer versus the underlying model, and to show that treating scores as intrinsic model properties is misleading.

Method: The authors introduce QuoteBench, a benchmark with 56 one-shot Bash tasks grouped into 14 families derived from real-world incidents involving quoting/escaping bugs. They construct an “added parser” that deliberately introduces one unescaped interpolation boundary, thereby creating a controlled mismatch between the model’s generation contract and the execution transport. They then: (1) run models in eight configurations (same context window but different transports/parsing setups); (2) replay identical model replies through the added parser to measure how much success is lost purely from transport changes; (3) disclose this boundary to the model and let it adapt its generation to compensate; and (4) use exact final-state validation of the filesystem or environment to determine success. By comparing raw-path and parser-path outcomes, and with/without disclosure, they separate pure generation quality from boundary adaptation capability.

Result: Replaying identical replies through the added parser causes large drops in success—55.4 to 73.2 percentage points across configurations—showing that transport/parsing can severely damage performance even when the model outputs are unchanged. When the added boundary is disclosed, models can often adapt: six configurations recover 30.4 to 60.7 percentage points, while two show no or slightly negative recovery. At the current frontier, raw command generation performance is nearly saturated, so differences between strong models mostly come from their ability to adapt to the boundary. For example, GPT-5.6-sol has a small net matched performance gap of -3.6 points, which conceals -64.3 points of damage from the parser and +60.7 points of recovery via adaptation. Deployment choices (parser, transport, configuration) can reorder model rankings; among 26 comparable pairs, at least one ranking reversal is clear and four more are borderline on single tasks.

Conclusion: Command-issuing LLM evaluations must no longer treat a single matched execution score as an intrinsic property of the base model. Instead, they should explicitly report and consider the full deployment stack: model configuration, generation contract (what the model thinks it must output), execution path (how outputs are serialized and parsed), operating point, and final-state validator. The work shows that boundary effects and a model’s ability to adapt to them can drastically change success rates and model ordering, especially now that raw generation quality is near-saturated. Benchmarks like QuoteBench are needed to separate generation quality from interface-induced errors and to design more robust agent systems.

Abstract: LLM coding agents issue Bash commands through interfaces that may serialize, wrap, and reparse model output. Matched execution scores alone cannot distinguish command-generation errors from failures introduced after generation. QuoteBench measures this boundary with exact final-state validation on 56 one-shot tasks from 14 incident-derived families, crossing the generation contract with the execution transport around one deliberately unescaped added parser. Escaping at the interpolation point reproduces each replayed reply's raw-path outcome, so any recovery under a disclosed boundary must come from the model changing its generation. Across eight same-window configurations, replaying the same reply through the added parser lowers success by 55.4 to 73.2 percentage points; disclosure recovers 30.4 to 60.7 points for six configurations, and zero or slightly negative for the other two. Raw generation is nearly saturated at the frontier; boundary adaptation is what still separates models. GPT-5.6-sol's matched gap of -3.6 points hides -64.3 points of damage and +60.7 points of compensation. The deployment configuration reorders models: one reversal among 26 comparable pairs is unambiguous and four more sit on single-task margins. Evaluations of command-issuing agents should report the model configuration, generation contract, execution path, operating point, and final-state validator rather than treat a matched score as an intrinsic model property.

</details>
